# Sūrah al-Tawbah (Chapter 9) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Sūrah With Two Names and One Purpose**

Sūrah al-Tawbah, the ninth chapter of the Qur'an, is unique in the Book in more ways than one, and nearly all of its uniqueness converges on a single theme: the dividing line between loyalty and treachery — toward God, toward His Messenger, and toward the community of believers. Its most famous name, *al-Tawbah* ("The Repentance"), is drawn from the invitation to repentance that stands at the head of its treaty-annulment passage in verse 3, though many commentators also connect the name to God's "relenting" toward the Prophet and the believers in verses 117–118, where the Arabic verb *tāba* appears again — the same verb that gives the sūrah its title. "Repent" and "relent," as the classical lexicons note, render one and the same verb; the difference lies only in the preposition that follows it. A human being *turns back* to God in repentance; God *turns toward* His servant in acceptance. The sūrah's very name is thus a miniature of its message: no matter how stern its judgments, the door of turning never closes.

Its other famous name, *al-Barāʾah* ("The Repudiation" or "The Dissociation"), comes from the first word of its first verse — the announcement that God and His Messenger are free of obligation toward the idolaters who had violated their treaties. Al-Qurṭubī records several further names by which the early community knew this chapter, each one a sermon in itself: *al-Fāḍiḥah*, "the Exposer," because it laid bare the secrets of the hypocrites; *al-Baḥūth*, "the Searcher," because it searched out the hidden corners of hearts as an investigator searches a house; and *al-Muqashqishah*, "the Remover of Disease," because, in the words of the commentators, it separated the believers from the hypocrites as the physician's lancet separates the healthy flesh from the infected. A sūrah known by such names cannot be read casually: it is a surgical chapter, and its instrument is light.

**The Sūrah That Begins Without the Divine Name**

Every sūrah of the Qur'an begins with the basmalah — *In the Name of God, the Compassionate, the Merciful* — except this one. The classical tradition preserved the questions this raised. It is reported that Ibn ʿAbbās asked ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib why the sūrah opens without the formula of mercy. ʿAlī replied that the basmalah is a word of security and covenant, while this sūrah opens with the severing of a covenant and a declaration of conflict — and the two do not travel together. When it was objected that the Prophet ﷺ had sent letters to hostile rulers beginning with the basmalah, ʿAlī answered that those letters were *invitations* to God, which lead toward peace, whereas this is a *rescinding* of a pact, which was understood, even in pre-Islamic Arab diplomacy, to be a message of a different order. Commentators note that in the diplomacy of the Arabs it was a recognized custom to omit the divine invocation from a communication that broke a treaty — the omission itself was the signal.

Others read the absence of the basmalah as a literary stitch binding this sūrah to the one before it. Sūrah al-Anfāl ends with a summons to separate from those who refuse the truth; al-Tawbah begins that separation in earnest. Some Companions, among them — in one account — ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, wondered aloud whether al-Anfāl and al-Tawbah were in fact two halves of a single sūrah, since no other sūrah follows another without the basmalah between them; and the traditional answer is that the Prophet ﷺ himself directed the placement of every sūrah and verse, and that he ordered them placed side by side *without* the separating formula — whether as a signal of their continuity or of the difference of opinion among the Companions, the scholars have discussed at length. The accepted view, by consensus, is that the arrangement of the muṣḥaf was itself revelation-guided, and that al-Tawbah stands as its own sūrah, its missing basmalah a permanent reminder that there are moments in the life of a community — and in the life of a soul — when the softening word must wait until the accounting is done.

**The Historical Hour of the Revelation**

Al-Tawbah is a Madinan sūrah of the year 9 AH — after the conquest of Makkah, after the Battle of Ḥunayn, and centered upon the expedition to Tabūk in the far north. It is, by the majority report, among the last complete sūrahs to be revealed; Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have said that it was the last sūrah to come down as a whole, and that nothing of it was abrogated. Its 129 verses constitute the Qur'an's most sustained reflection on war and treaty, on the hypocrites who weakened the community from within, on the alms that bind a society together, and on repentance as the one remedy that outlasts them all. The sūrah announces the annulment of violated treaties (vv. 1–15); addresses the idolaters, then the People of the Book (vv. 16–37); chronicles the Tabūk campaign and the excuses of those who stayed behind (vv. 38–106); dismantles the "mosque of harm" built to divide the believers (vv. 107–110); and closes with the divine "purchase" of the believers' lives, the repentance of the Prophet, the Emigrants, the Helpers, and the three who were left behind (vv. 111–118), and a final summons to truthfulness, remembrance, and trust (vv. 119–129).

The reader should hold the whole in view while reading the parts. This is a sūrah in which armies move and mosques are demolished, yet its deepest laws are laws of the heart: what you love most (v. 24), what you conceal (v. 78), whether you can weep from longing to serve (v. 92), and where you turn when the whole earth has narrowed around you (v. 118). It is a sūrah of edges — the edge of the covenant, the edge of the community, the edge of the crumbling bank on which a false building stands (v. 109) — and it asks of every generation the same question it asked of that first one: on which side of the line do you actually stand?

**How to Read What Follows**

The commentary that follows treats each of the 129 verses as an independent discourse, in strict verse-by-verse order, exactly as the workflow mandates: every verse is quoted individually, then expanded through the Arabic language, the classical commentators — al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Rāzī, al-Thaʿlabī and others — authenticated Prophetic reports with their collections and narrators identified, the historical circumstances of the Tabūk era, and the logical and spiritual structure of the verse's teaching as it bears on modern life. Verses the source commentary groups together (for example 9:1–4, 9:14–15, or 9:44–45) are here separated and given full individual treatment, as the method requires. Nothing is compressed because the chapter is long: from the first word *barāʾah* to the final *rabb al-ʿarsh al-ʿaẓīm*, each verse is given the time it deserves, and each is retained as it is completed until all are gathered into this one final file.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:1

> **A repudiation from God and His Messenger to those idolaters with whom you made a treaty.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**One Word That Ends an Era**

The sūrah opens with the single noun *barāʾah* — a repudiation, a declaration of innocence, a cutting of ties. Its root, *b-r-ʾ*, carries the sense of being free of something, disassociated from it, washed clean of any connection to it; a person *abraʾa* from a debt is one discharged of it, and *baraʾa min fulān* means he declared himself innocent of any entanglement with such a person. Here the word is a legal and political instrument: the existing state of treaty obligation between the Muslim polity and certain idolatrous tribes is declared at an end. What makes the opening so arresting is its authorship: the repudiation comes *from God and His Messenger* jointly. This is not a commander-in-chief's communiqué drafted in a chancellery; it is revelation itself descending in the language of diplomacy. The Qur'an here enters, deliberately and precisely, into the most technical register of inter-communal law, and in doing so sanctifies a principle: treaties are serious things before God, and so is their lawful dissolution.

Ibn Kathīr observes that regarding the exact circumstances of this verse there is "considerable disagreement" among the early authorities — a candid admission that is itself a lesson in humility for anyone who imagines that the meaning of these verses is simple. One line of interpretation connects the repudiation to the breach of the Treaty of Ḥudaybiyah: that ten-year truce, concluded in 6 AH and described in Sūrah al-Fatḥ as "a manifest victory," had bound not only the Prophet ﷺ and Quraysh but also their respective allies — among them the Banū Khuzāʿah on the Muslim side and the Banū Bakr on the Qurashī side. The Banū Bakr, exploiting an old vendetta and armed, it was reported, with quiet Qurashī assistance, fell upon the Khuzāʿah by night and killed men within the sacred precincts. That treachery nullified the treaty and set in motion the conquest of Makkah. On this reading, the "repudiation" addresses the Makkans whose own allies had already torn the document.

**The Majority View: A Proclamation After Makkah**

The majority of commentators, however, hold that these verses were revealed after the conquest of Makkah in 8/630, and that they concern the patchwork of tribes — some bound by treaty, some not — across the Arabian Peninsula. The legal question the verse settles is which treaties ended, and when. One opinion holds that any treaty with more than four months left was reduced to four months, and any treaty with less was extended to four; others restricted the four-month rule to those with a short treaty or none at all, leaving faithfully observed long treaties intact; still others held that the directive touched only those who had never had a treaty. Ibn ʿAbbās, as al-Rāzī relays, noted that the Muslims had a treaty with the clan of Ḥayy ibn Kinānah that still had nine months to run at the time of the announcement, and that they continued to honor it in full. The very texture of this disagreement — careful, clause-by-clause, refusing to paint all idolaters with one brush — refutes, before the argument can even begin, the later stereotype that this passage is a blank check for indiscriminate hostility. Al-Ṭabarī insists explicitly that the passage cannot mean that after the sacred months the believers were free to kill any idolater whatsoever.

**How the Announcement Was Delivered**

The manner of the proclamation is itself prophetic history. In the year 9 AH the Prophet ﷺ sent Abū Bakr to lead the pilgrimage, and then sent ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib after him to be the herald of the sūrah. It is recorded in the Ṣaḥīḥ collections from al-Barāʾ ibn ʿĀzib that ʿAlī proclaimed at Minā four matters:

> *"No idolater shall approach the Sacred Mosque after this year; no one shall circumambulate the House naked; none shall enter the Garden but a believing soul; and whoever has a treaty with the Messenger of God — his treaty stands until its term."* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim from al-Barāʾ ibn ʿĀzib)

The commentators note why ʿAlī was chosen for the task: by Arab custom, a treaty could only be dissolved by the contracting party himself or by a near kinsman. For the annulment to be unambiguous — for no tribe to be able to say that an underling had exceeded his brief — the Prophet sent the man closest to him by blood and by standing. The detail is worth pausing on, because it shows a scrupulousness operating *inside* the act of rupture: even the ending of a covenant was made to observe the forms of covenant. A community that honors the grammar of treaties even while dissolving one is a community whose dissolutions can be trusted.

**Why a Public Notice Rather Than a Surprise**

There is a logic of mercy in the very form of verse 1 that the modern reader can easily miss. A power preparing betrayal simply attacks; it does not advertise the termination of its obligations. The repudiation is announced — read aloud before the assembled pilgrims of the greatest ḥajj — so that every hearer knows exactly where he stands and what time remains. Revelation could have authorized pursuit of the treaty-breakers without warning; instead it chooses the instruments of law: declaration, notice period, exception clauses, and an open door of repentance in the very next verses. Throughout this passage the believer is being taught that force in Islam is a legal instrument with a moral purpose, not an appetite with a license. That distinction, as the coming verses will show, is written into the grammar of the text itself.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:2

> **So travel freely throughout the land for four months, and know that you cannot thwart God, and that God shall disgrace the disbelievers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Grace Period Written in Revelation**

Verse 2 turns the blunt instrument of annulment into a measured, dated thing: *fa-sīḥū fī al-arḍi arbaʿata ashur* — "travel freely in the land for four months." The verb *siyāḥah* ordinarily denotes free movement, roaming unhindered; here it functions as a guarantor of safe passage. The treaty-holders who had lived under Muslim protection are not seized, ambushed, or expelled: they are given a season of total security in which to move, trade, settle their affairs, weigh their options, and — the door remains open — repent. The commentators disputed the calendar mechanics with characteristic precision. If the announcement of verse 3 fell on the Day of Sacrifice, then the "sacred months" of verse 5 would end some fifty days later (the rest of Dhū al-Ḥijjah plus Muḥarram), while the "four months" of verse 2 would run to mid-Rabīʿ al-Thānī; some reconciled the two periods by assigning the fifty days to the treaty-less tribes and the four months to the treaty-holders; others held the sūrah revealed at the start of Shawwāl so that the two windows closed together. The arithmetic matters less than the principle it serves: whatever the exact dates, every affected tribe received a known, announced, and generous runway.

Notice, too, whom the notice protects. The verse addresses people who, by the majority account, had either broken their word or never given it — and yet the Qur'an's first word to them is not a threat but a safe-conduct. This is the pattern the sūrah will repeat at every level: judgment held open long enough for choice to operate. A notice period is not softness toward treachery; it is a final extension of moral agency to the treacherous. Modern law recognizes the same idea in the notice requirements of terminated contracts and the grace periods of defaulting debtors; the Qur'an embedded the ethic at the level of revelation fourteen centuries earlier.

**"You Cannot Thwart God": The Theology of Futility**

The middle clause is the verse's engine: *wa-iʿlamū annakum ghayru muʿjizī Allāh* — "and know that you cannot thwart God." The root *ʿ-j-z* behind *muʿjiz* means to escape beyond pursuit, to outrun the one chasing you; the image is of a fugitive straining to break through a net that has no edge. The treaty-breakers are invited to *know* — an epistemic verb, not merely an emotional one — that no maneuver, alliance, or deception can place them outside the reach of the One who owns the geometry of the earth they are invited to roam. There is a quiet irony in the juxtaposition: travel the whole land, use its entire breadth — and understand that its breadth is Mine. The same argument from inescapability appears across the Qur'an:

> *"Whithersoever you turn, there is the Face of God."* (Qur'an 2:115)

The psychological function of the clause is to dissolve the illusion of exit that makes deception attractive. People betray covenants only because they believe there is somewhere to hide the betrayal — a private corner where cause and effect do not reach. The verse removes the corner first, before any consequence arrives, because a threat without metaphysics is merely shouting.

**Disgrace as the Fate of Bad Faith**

The closing clause — *anna Allāha mukhzi al-kāfirīn*, "God shall disgrace the disbelievers" — adds the register of honor and shame to the register of consequence. The root *kh-z-y* denotes public humiliation, the collapse of face. In the honor-conscious world of seventh-century Arabia this was no small threat, and its deeper point survives the culture that first heard it: treachery, when it is finally seen, strips a people of the one asset that no arms can restore — credibility. Ibn Kathīr notes the chastening historical footnote that within a generation of this announcement, the political order that had lived by shifting oaths had vanished, and the precincts once defiled by the blood of treaty-breaking were filled with pilgrims whose word needed no enforcement. Disgrace, in the Qur'anic idiom, is simply truth catching up with a reputation.

For the believer reading today, verse 2 quietly asks a question it never states: when your own obligations begin to pinch, do you look for the loophole or honor the ledger? The four months of grace were given to people who had broken faith; we, who will never face that specific deadline, are given something longer — a whole lifetime of free movement through the land — with the same two certainties attached at the end of it: the net has no edge, and the truth eventually stands up in public. The wise use the grace period before the grace period ends.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:3

> **And an announcement from God and His Messenger to the people on the day of the greater ḥajj: that God and His Messenger have repudiated the idolaters. So, if you repent, it would be better for you. And if you turn away, then know that you cannot thwart God. And give the disbelievers glad tidings of a painful punishment,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Public Address on the Day of Maximum Attendance**

The word *adhān* — here rendered "announcement" — is a summons made aloud, a formal proclamation; it is the same noun the Qur'an and the Islamic tradition use for the call to prayer, and its presence gives verse 3 the character of a civic decree issued at the widest possible audience. The "day of the greater ḥajj" (al-ḥajj al-akbar) is debated: al-Ṭabarī reports the two main readings — the Day of Sacrifice at Minā, or the Day of ʿArafah — and either way the choice of occasion is strategic. The pre-Islamic pilgrimage assembled the tribes of Arabia in one place, under a general truce of the sacred season, at the one calendar moment when a message could reach every clan that mattered. God could have communicated His repudiation privately to the Prophet; instead He orders it broadcast, at high noon of the Arabian information year. There is a principle of transparency here that later Muslim governance would inherit: the state of peace or war between the community and others is public knowledge, publicly declared, never a matter of whispered cabals. ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib, as the reports of al-Barāʾ ibn ʿĀzib relate, discharged exactly this duty at Minā, crying the terms of the new dispensation to the assembled pilgrims until, as some accounts add, the valleys echoed the proclamation back.

**The Invitation Tucked Inside the Divorce**

What follows the proclamation is one of the sūrah's most characteristic reversals: *fa-in tubtum huwa khayrun lakum* — "so if you repent, it would be better for you." The very verse that announces the end of the old order opens, in its second breath, with an offer of mercy to those the order has just expelled. The verb *tāba* — to turn back, to return — is the sūrah's name-word, and it appears here for the first time, before any battle has been fought, before any punishment has landed. The sequence deserves close attention: declaration of freedom from obligation (verse 1), safe-conduct (verse 2), public notice (verse 3a), and then — immediately — an open door (verse 3b). Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī both read the clause as addressed to every idolater within earshot of the proclamation: the treaty arrangement is dead, but *you* are not; the political order has been repudiated, but the human being beneath it has not. This is the Qur'an separating, with surgical consistency, the system from the soul.

**Irony as an Instrument of Revelation**

The verse ends with a sting engineered into its syntax: *wa-bashshiri alladhīna kafarū bi-ʿadhābin alīm* — "and give the disbelievers glad tidings of a painful punishment." The verb *bashshara* is the standard word for announcing joy — the same root that gives the Prophet his title *bashīr*, bearer of good news. Applying it to the announcement of agony is deliberate, unsettling irony, the rhetorical equivalent of handing a condemned man a congratulatory scroll. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the Arabs used the verb here because the news, however grim, arrives with such certainty and such clarity that it shares the one essential feature of good news: it is *true*, and its hearer can act on it. The irony also performs a theological audit on the listener: it forces the question — what do you actually consider good news? To a heart still soft, the verse's sarcasm is a jolt toward repentance; to a heart calcified, it is the first taste of the humiliation promised in verse 2. One verse, two effects, sorted by the condition of the receiver — a device the sūrah will later make an explicit law (compare verses 124–125, where the same revealed sūrah increases the believers in faith and the diseased in defilement).

**Why the Offer Precedes the Ultimatum**

The verse's order — mercy offered *before* the finality of "if you turn away" — encodes a consistent divine pedagogy found throughout the Qur'an:

> *"God calls to the Abode of Peace, and guides whom He wills to a straight path."* (Qur'an 10:25)

God's first word to a straying humanity is customarily invitation; His second, only after refusal, is warning. Al-Rāzī observes that the sequence mirrors the way a wise physician proceeds: the prescription is explained before the prognosis, so that no patient can later claim he was never offered the cure. For the modern believer the verse carries a discipline for every act of correction — of a child, a colleague, a community: the exit and the remedy belong inside the rebuke itself, stated first and stated plainly. A warning that contains no door is a threat; the Qur'an does not deal in threats, only in choices with their prices posted openly. And for the one being warned, the verse sets a clock: the "better for you" of repentance is not an eternal standing offer; it lives inside the four months, inside the fifty days, inside a lifespan. The sūrah will return to this urgency at its end, when the window of turning is measured against the sudden coming of God's command.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:4

> **save for those idolaters with whom you have made a treaty, and who thereafter commit no breach against you, nor support anyone against you. So fulfill the treaty with them for its duration. Truly God loves the reverent.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Exception Clause That Reads the Whole Passage**

Immediately after the sweeping repudiation, the Qur'an carves out its first exception, and the exception is breathtakingly precise: treaties in good standing remain in force. *Illā alladhīna ʿāhadtum mina al-mushrikīna thumma lam yanquṣūkum shayʾan wa-lam yuʿāhilū ʿalaykum aḥadan* — "except those idolaters with whom you made a treaty, who then neither defaulted against you in anything nor backed anyone against you." Three conditions, each a small legal treatise: no direct breach, no indirect support of enemies, no change of circumstance that turns neutrality into hostility. Only a contract that has failed on its own terms may be treated as ended. The verse thereby settles, in a single breath, the interpretation of everything around it: the repudiation of verse 1 is *not* a general divorce from the idolaters as idolaters, but the termination of specific, already-broken arrangements. Whoever reads verses 1–3 without verse 4 has amputated the passage from its own conscience.

Al-Ṭabarī underlines the point with the report that the Muslims continued to honor, to its full term, a treaty with Ḥayy ibn Kinānah that still had nine months to run at the time of the proclamation. Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī transmit the various juristic positions on how the four-month window and the standing treaties interacted, but the spectrum of disagreement itself proves the shared assumption: a faithful treaty is binding until it ends by its own clock. This assumption the Qur'an had already stated as a standing command:

> *"Fulfill the covenant of God when you have undertaken it, and do not break oaths after they have been confirmed, having made God a surety over you."* (Qur'an 16:91)

**Fulfillment "To Their Term": The Grammar of Trust**

The command *fa-atimmū ilayhim ʿahdahum ilā muddatihim* — "so fulfill the treaty with them for its duration" — uses the verb *atamma*, to bring to completion, the same verb used for completing a prayer or a fast. The idiom teaches that a treaty is not an inconvenience to be endured but an act of worship to be consummated. Ibn Kathīr notes the astonishing asymmetry this creates: the Muslim side is commanded to impeccable fidelity even toward partners whose confederates have already demonstrated, at Ḥudaybiyah's expense, what their signatures are worth. The community that keeps its word to the untrustworthy is not being naive; it is keeping its word for the sake of the Witness, not the counterparty. The Prophet ﷺ embodied this so thoroughly that during the agreed ten years of Ḥudaybiyah he refused to respond to provocation until the breach was manifest, and on his deathbed, as Abū Bakr's caliphate would reaffirm, every treaty outstanding was honored to its letter — including those with tribes that would later wish otherwise.

**God Loves the Reverent: The Refrain of the Passage**

The verse closes with the formula that also closes verse 7: *inna Allāha yuḥibbu al-muttaqīn* — "truly God loves the reverent." It is worth noticing *who* is being praised for *what*. The ones who fulfill treaties on schedule are called *muttaqīn*, the same word the Qur'an elsewhere reserves for the highest grade of God-consciousness — the word used of those who believe in the Unseen and spend of what God gave them (2:3). Treaties, in the Qur'an's accounting, are not a morally neutral zone of realpolitik where sincerity may be suspended; they are a theater of taqwā precisely because that is where honesty costs something and no one but God sees the difference between the letter and the escape. The sūrah will shortly say of the opposite type that "they please you with their mouths, while their hearts refuse" (9:8); verse 4 stands as that verse's antidote: reverence is what remains when enforcement is absent.

**A Charter for Every Kind of Covenant**

For the modern reader, the verse's application radiates outward from the seventh-century parchment to every instrument of trust a life contains: employment contracts, marriage covenants, loans, promises made in private, terms of service a person agreed to and later finds inconvenient. The Qur'anic standard is unambiguous — performance to the term, absent breach by the other side, is not a strategy but an act loved by God. Businesses that honor warranties nobody checks, spouses who keep promises nobody audits, citizens who obey laws they could quietly evade — all of them are operating inside verse 4. And the verse supplies its own motivation, one more durable than reputation or reciprocity: the Surety over the covenant is God, who loves those who keep their footing on this narrow, profitable path. The treaty-breakers of verse 1 lost everything; the treaty-keepers of verse 4 — idolaters though they were in creed — were owed protection by name. In the divine accounting, faithfulness across religious lines is not merely tolerated; it is written into revelation and honored with a declaration of divine love.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:5

> **Then, when the sacred months have passed, slay the idolaters wheresoever you find them, capture them, besiege them, and lie in wait for them at every place of ambush. But if they repent, and perform the prayer and give the alms, then let them go their way. Truly God is Forgiving, Merciful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Reading the Verse Inside Its Own Sentences**

Verse 5 is the most quoted — and most misquoted — verse of this sūrah, famous in the later juristic literature simply as *Āyat al-Sayf*, "the Sword Verse." Honest commentary must begin by insisting that the verse be read entire, in order, in its context, because every one of its harsh clauses is immediately bounded by clauses of law and mercy. The verse opens with a time condition: *fa-idhā insalkhati al-ashur al-ḥurum* — "when the sacred months have passed." Al-Ṭabarī records the discussion of what "sacred months" means here: either the four traditional sacred months (Rajab, Dhū al-Qaʿdah, Dhū al-Ḥijjah, Muḥarram), or — as Ibn Kathīr transmits — the word *ḥurum* ("forbidden") refers to the four-month safe-conduct of verse 2, during which the believers were *forbidden* from fighting. On either reading, the sword is not unsheathed until a fully announced, publicly known grace period has expired. Then come four verbs of war — slay, capture, besiege, ambush — and then, in the same breath, the escape hatch: *fa-in tābū wa-aqāmū al-ṣalāta wa-ātū al-zakāta fa-khallū sabīlahum* — "but if they repent, establish the prayer, and give the alms, then let them go their way." And the verse, having spoken of killing, signs off not with a war-cry but with two names of mercy: *inna Allāha Ghafūrun Raḥīm*, "Truly God is Forgiving, Merciful."

**What the Fight Was Actually About**

The critical question is what condition triggers the fighting. Some early jurists read the mere fact of idolatry as the cause; but that reading collides with the passage's own text. Verse 4 has just guaranteed full treaty rights to idolaters who keep faith; verse 6, coming next, will order protection and safe escort for any idolater who seeks asylum to hear God's word; and verse 13 states the rationale of this whole campaign in plain words: *"Will you not fight a people who broke their oaths, and intended to expel the Messenger, and opened hostilities against you first?"* The fought-against party is defined by treachery, aggression, and a demonstrated intent to destroy the community — not by creed as such. The source commentary puts the historical logic well: after years in which treaties were made and broken as weather changes, the idolatrous confederations of Arabia had become, structurally, a standing military alliance against Madinah, and the political reality after Ḥunayn was that they could not be left in a position to menace the community in future. Conversion was not the purpose of the fighting; it was, however — on the plain sense of the verse — the way an individual removed himself from the state of war, since joining the community of prayer and alms meant renouncing the hostile confederacy. Al-Ṭabarī says explicitly that this passage cannot possibly mean that after the sacred months the believers were free to kill any idolater; and the Prophet's own conduct in year 9 — the safe-conduct of verse 2, the honored treaties of verse 4, the asylum of verse 6 — is the verse's first commentary.

**The Ḥadīth That Is Always Half-Quoted**

Discussions of this verse almost always import a famous ḥadīth, usually truncated, so its full text belongs here. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"I have been commanded to fight the people until they testify that there is no god but God and that Muḥammad is the Messenger of God, establish the prayer, and give the alms. If they do that, their lives and their property are safe from me except by the right of Islam — and their reckoning is with God, exalted and mighty."* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī (25) and Muslim (22) from Ibn ʿUmar)

The final clause is routinely amputated by both those who wield the ḥadīth as a weapon and those who cite it as an accusation: *wa-ḥisābuhum ʿalā Allāh* — "and their reckoning is with God." The Prophet's arms end where the heart begins; faith that is compelled or simulated is not faith, and the Prophet ﷺ was explicitly told that his duty was proclamation, not coercion — *"Remind, for you are only a reminder; you are not a keeper over them"* (88:21–22). The "commanded to fight" of the ḥadīth, read whole, is the fighting of this sūrah's context: the dismantling of a war-making order whose exit door — the two testimonies, prayer, alms — was open to every individual inside it.

**The Place of This Verse in the Law of War**

Later jurists debated whether verse 5 abrogated the treaty verses, and the mature legal tradition largely answered no: verses 4, 6, 7 and the entire apparatus of amān (safe-conduct), dhimmah (covenant protection) and muʿāhadah (treaty) remained living law, practiced by the Prophet ﷺ and his successors. The classical codifications of jihād that grew from this passage built around it one of history's earlier bodies of regulated warfare: non-combatants — women, children, the elderly, monks, and those who do not fight — protected by explicit prophetic instruction; treaties binding; safe-conduct honored even for a single seeker. None of that regulation is intelligible if verse 5 abolished restraint; all of it is intelligible if verse 5 ended a specific, dangerous, and treaty-shattered status quo. And the ending of that status quo, history records, did not end in extermination: the tribes that entered the covenant of prayer and alms — which is to say, nearly all of them — were not killed but married, traded with, governed, and absorbed into the community as brethren, exactly as verse 11 will announce.

**The Verse as a Mirror for Every Age**

The abuse of verses like this one comes from two directions, and both are corrected by the same discipline. Extremist readers sever verse 5 from verses 4, 6, and 13 and mint from it a perpetual license, forgetting that the Prophet ﷺ to whom it was addressed understood it as the terminus of a specific, betrayed truce. Hostile readers perform the same amputation from the other side, presenting the severed verse as Islam's essence, forgetting that the passage's own God — who immediately orders protection for the asylum-seeker and closes the verse with "Forgiving, Merciful" — is the same God they accuse of bloodlust. The verse's permanent teaching is quieter and harder: that evil organized under a flag may not always be answered with patience, that patience with aggression is itself a decision on behalf of its victims, and that even a war meant to end a treacherous order must carry its exit door inside itself — repentance received, the penitent released, "let them go their way." Any era's warfare that cannot state its own ending conditions has already abandoned the standard this verse set.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:6

> **And if any of the idolaters seek asylum with thee, grant him asylum until he hears the Word of God. Then convey him to his place of safety. That is because they are a people who know not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Protection for the Enemy Who Asks**

Coming immediately after the Sword Verse, verse 6 performs one of the most striking tonal reversals in the Qur'an. The very population toward whom force has just been authorized now includes a person who *seeks protection* — *istajāraka*, from the root j-w-r, the vocabulary of *ijārah* and *jiwār*, the near-neighbor's covenant of safety — and the answer is commanded before the request is finished: *fa-ajirhu* — "grant him asylum." Not "interrogate his motives," not "detain him pending review": grant. Two obligations follow. First, he must be allowed to *hear the Word of God* — enough of it, the commentators say, to know what Islam actually teaches; al-Rāzī records the discussion of whether "the Word" means the whole Qur'an or this sūrah, which contains what an idolater most needs to hear about his situation. Second, when the hearing is done — whether he believes or not — *fa-ablighhu maʾmanah* — "convey him to his place of safety." He is escorted home. He may return to his tribe having rejected everything he heard, and the believers are nonetheless his armed escort on the way. The verse thus guarantees, in the middle of a state of war, three rights that the modern world would only much later call asylum, audience, and safe return.

**The Legal Edifice Built on One Verse**

Islamic law built an entire institution on this verse. The *mustaʾmin* — the non-Muslim who enters Muslim territory under safe-conduct — became a protected person from the moment of the grant, and the scholars held that the amān could be granted even by a single adult Muslim, even, according to many jurists, a woman, so seriously did the tradition take the binding force of the word "you are safe." Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī transmit the discussion of whether verse 6 was abrogated by the prisoner-policy verse of Sūrat Muḥammad (*"free them graciously or ransom them until the war lays down its burdens"*, 47:4), and the dominant answer, as the source commentary notes, is that neither verse abrogated the other: the Prophet ﷺ held from the beginning the full set of options — fight, capture, release — and verse 6 governs the specific case of the person who comes over voluntarily seeking to hear and to be safe. A verse this generous was not retired; it was institutionalized.

**"A People Who Know Not": The Diagnosis Beneath the Decree**

The verse's own explanation of its mercy is the clause *bi-annahum qawmun lā yaʿlamūn* — "that is because they are a people who know not." This is not an insult; it is an etiology. The idolaters' hostility is traced not to evil essence but to epistemic deprivation — they fight what they have never heard, swear against a Book no one has read to them. The asylum-seeker's ignorance is precisely the justification for giving him an *audience* with the Word. The Qur'an elsewhere makes the same move about whole epochs of human history:

> *"We never punished until We had sent a messenger."* (Qur'an 17:15)

Accountability tracks knowledge; mercy supplies knowledge before judgment. The verse thereby quietly forbids the believer's contempt. A person who has never truly encountered the message is not an enemy by nature but an audience by circumstance, and the community's duty is to complete the missing delivery — by escort if necessary.

**The Modern Face of an Ancient Command**

The contemporary resonance of verse 6 needs no laboring. Every asylum regime on earth is judged by exactly the three elements this verse commands: admission, a genuine hearing, and non-refoulement — safe return or safe resettlement rather than delivery back to peril. The Qur'an reached the destination in one verse. And the verse speaks equally to a quieter register of daily life: the coworker who argues against Islam from caricatures absorbed from hostile media, the relative who mocks a faith no one has ever actually presented to him — the verse's diagnosis covers them: *a people who know not*. The commanded response is not fury but the patient provision of a hearing, with the outcome left to God. The believer who can neither grant an audience nor guarantee the questioner's dignity in the exchange has not yet caught up to a command revealed in the hardest year of the community's external conflict. Mercy of this order is not the softening of a later age; it is the original text, standing with unsheathed context on either side of it.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:7

> **How can the idolaters have a treaty with God and with His Messenger, save for those with whom you made a treaty at the Sacred Mosque? If they remain true to you, remain true to them. Truly God loves the reverent.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question That Is Its Own Answer**

Verse 7 opens with the interrogative *kayfa* — "how?" — and the rhetorical force depends on hearing it rightly. The verse is not asking for information; it is exposing an absurdity. How can there be a standing covenant relationship between God, His Messenger, and people whose record is oath-breaking as a way of life? Ibn Kathīr notes that the question carries the sense: "by what right, after all that has passed?" The interrogative grammar of moral exposure is a recurring Qur'anic instrument — *a-wa-lam yaʿlamū* ("do they not know?"), *am ḥasibtum* ("did you suppose?") — and here it invites the listener to run the ledger himself: the broken pledges, the night-raid on the Khuzāʿah, the alliances assembled to expel the Messenger. Al-Ṭabarī reads the verse as a denial that any general, open-ended covenant obligation remains toward the idolaters as a class, *after* the events that had dissolved it — while the exception clause, again, preserves every covenant earned by conduct.

**Who Are the Excepted at the Sacred Mosque?**

The exception — *illā alladhīna ʿāhadtum ʿinda al-masjidi al-ḥarām* — occasioned careful historical detective work among the commentators. Some, including al-Ṭabarī in his preferred reading, identified them as those clans of the Banū Bakr who had been allied to Quraysh under Ḥudaybiyah's terms but refused to take part in the treachery of their kinsmen; their treaty, unbroken by them, remained unbroken by God. Others proposed the Banū Khuzāʿah, the Prophet's own wronged allies; others, certain Qurashī individuals who had been granted the four-month window and embraced Islam before it closed — an account al-Thaʿlabī records. What every reading shares is the operative principle in the verse's second half: *fa-mā istaqāmū ʿalaykum fa-staqīmū ʿalayhim* — "if they keep straight with you, keep straight with them." The verb *istaqāma* — to stand upright, hold a straight course — makes reciprocity the engine of the relationship: fidelity answers fidelity, measure for measure, for exactly as long as it lasts. Covenant in the Qur'an is never a one-time artifact; it is a living posture, renewed or forfeited by conduct.

**God's Treaty, Not Just the Prophet's**

The framing "a treaty with God and with His Messenger" teaches the theology that makes the whole passage cohere. When the Prophet ﷺ concluded treaties, God counted Himself a party. This elevation of statecraft into covenant appears most explicitly elsewhere:

> *"Truly those who pledge allegiance to thee pledge allegiance only to God; the Hand of God is above their hands."* (Qur'an 48:10)

The practical consequence is double-edged. For the believers, it means their signatures are given in the presence of the Surety of all witnesses, and breaking them is not diplomacy but sacrilege. For the counterparties, it means that treachery toward the community is recorded as treachery toward God — which is why verse 7 can speak as though the idolaters owed their treaties to God directly. The verse thereby dissolves the modern fiction that international commitments are morally weightless chess moves. In the Qur'anic frame, the parchment has a divine co-signatory.

**The Refrain Returns Where It Belongs**

For the second time in five verses the passage closes: *inna Allāha yuḥibbu al-muttaqīn* — "God loves the reverent." The repetition is structural, not decorative. Verse 4 taught that treaty-keeping is taqwā toward the other party; verse 7 teaches that reciprocity is taqwā toward God. Between the two occurrences stands the Sword Verse — and the placement is the point. The same passage that contains the fiercest language of war in the Qur'an brackets it, twice, with a declaration of divine love for those who keep faith. Any reading of 9:5 that cannot pass through 9:4 and 9:7 is, by the Qur'an's own architecture, a misreading. The believer navigating conflict today — marital, commercial, political — receives here a compact ethic for the fog of dispute: ask first whether your adversary's conduct has actually forfeited the covenant (v. 7a); honor every covenant not yet forfeited, to its term, regardless of creed (v. 7b); and remember that your own straightness, not his, is what God loves (v. 7c). Reverence, not victory, is the terminal value of the passage.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:8

> **How, since if they prevail over you, they will not observe any kinship or treaty with you? They please you with their mouths, while their hearts refuse. And most of them are iniquitous.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Thought Experiment That Indicts**

Verse 8 supplies the proof behind verse 7's rhetorical question, and it does so with a hypothetical of forensic cruelty: *in yaẓharū ʿalaykum* — "if they prevail over you." Put them in the position of power, the verse says, and watch the treaty evaporate. The argument is empirical, not theoretical: the community had already seen what these confederates did with a decade's worth of assurances. The verse's logic dismantles a persistent moral confusion: that honoring agreements with the weak is proof of good faith. Anyone can be scrupulous when the balance of power enforces it; character is only visible from the side of advantage. God proposes the test no treaty can survive failing — *would they keep it if they could break it safely?* — and the answer, written by their own record, is no. The lesson travels far beyond war: the measure of any party's covenant is their conduct in the season of their strength.

**Three Bonds, All Discarded**

The verse names what would be violated under that hypothetical: *lā yarqubū fīkum illan wa-lā dhimmah* — "they will not observe any kinship or treaty." The first term, *ill* (from the root meaning to watch, tend, respect), spans blood-tie and sworn alliance — the *ḥilf* of the pre-Islamic Arabs, the adopted kinship by oath that made strangers into brothers. The second, *dhimmah*, is the covenant-obligation itself: al-Rāzī glosses it as "that which sets up an obligation," the binding charge on a person's honor. The Qur'an is noting that the enemy would discard both kinds of bond at once — the natural and the voluntary, the tie they were born with and the tie they chose. A people willing to burn both ledgers is not a partner for peace; they are a weather system. Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī read the verse as the revelation's own verdict that the general treaty relationship with these tribes was, by their demonstrated nature, void — the exception of verse 7 remaining for those whose conduct had differentiated them.

**Mouths and Hearts: The Anatomy of Duplicity**

The middle clause is a psychological x-ray: *yurʿibūnaka bi-alsinatihim wa-taʾbā qulūbuhum* — "they please you with their mouths, while their hearts refuse." The verb *raʿaba* is to soothe, to set at ease — the same root as the divine name *al-Raʾūf*? No: the root here is *r-ʿ-b*, the language of lulling a child. Their speech is a sedative administered to your vigilance. Meanwhile the heart — the seat of intention in Qur'anic anthropology — *taʾbā*, refuses, recoils. The split between uttered word and inner disposition is the exact pathology the sūrah will anatomize at length in the hypocrites of Madinah; here it appears at the inter-tribal level. The Qur'an's realism deserves notice: it does not treat words as cheap in themselves — covenants *are* words, and binding — but it refuses to let fluency masquerade as fidelity. Speech is honored precisely because it can be false, and the community is told plainly that a class of counterparties had converted language itself into a weapon. The remedy is not cynicism but verification — the Qur'an's own repeated instruction to test claims against conduct.

**"Most of Them": The Qur'an's Refusal to Totalize**

The final clause — *wa-aktharuhum fāsiqūn*, "and most of them are iniquitous" — contains a surprising mercy in its very quantifier. Not "all of them." The commentators flag the restraint: some among the idolaters kept faith — they are the excepted of verses 4 and 7 — and the Qur'an declines to erase the exception even while indicting the pattern. Al-Qurṭubī notes that since all disbelief is iniquity in the general sense, the word *most* here must be doing specific work: most, though not all, of them lie and will fail their agreements. The grammatical honesty is a standing rebuke to every mind that wants its enemies monolithic. Judgment of collectives in the Qur'an always leaves room for the individuals who opt out of the collective's sin — a principle the believer needs urgently in every age of mass blame.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:9

> **They have sold the signs of God for a paltry price, and have turned from His way. Evil indeed is that which they used to do,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Ledger Metaphor the Qur'an Keeps Reusing**

Verse 9 compresses the spiritual diagnosis of the treaty-breaking leadership into a single commercial image: *sharaū bihi thamanan qalīlan* — "they sold it for a paltry price." The thing sold is *āyāt Allāh*, the signs of God — His revealed verses, and by extension every evidence of truth they had witnessed in two decades of prophethood. The price is *thaman qalīl*, a paltry consideration: the tribal prestige, trade advantage, and political convenience that fidelity to their oaths would have cost them. The Qur'an recurs to this transactional metaphor so often that it amounts to a doctrine: people do not reject truth neutrally; they *trade* it, consciously or not, for perceived benefit:

> *"Those who conceal what We sent down of the clarifications and guidance after We made it clear to mankind in the Book — they are the ones God curses, and the cursers curse them — save those who repent... Those are the ones whose purchase is loss."* (Qur'an 2:159–162)

> *"And purchase not My signs for a small price."* (Qur'an 5:44)

And the Qur'an does not leave the metaphor one-sided. The same marketplace language is inverted for the believer, whose "selling" is the only profitable trade: *"O you who believe, shall I show you a commerce that will save you from a painful punishment? That you believe in God and His Messenger and strive in God's way with your wealth and your lives — that is better for you, did you but know"* (61:10–11). Every human life, in the Qur'an's accounting, is always trading; the only question is the commodity and the price.

**What Made Their Price "Paltry"**

The adjective deserves scrutiny, because what was at stake was not objectively small. The Quraysh leadership were defending an entire economic order — the pilgrimage economy of the idols, their custodianship of the Kaʿbah, their hegemony over the caravan routes. The Qur'an's answer is that no price anchored in *this world's duration* can be other than paltry when weighed against what is being sold: the truth of the One who owns the world and the Hereafter. The word *qalīl* is the sūrah's own calibration: a few decades of regional dominance, set against the everlasting, is a rounding error. This is the same arithmetic the sūrah will make explicit in verse 38 — *"the enjoyment of the life of this world, compared with the Hereafter, is but a little"* — and in verse 111, where God names the only transaction that is not a loss. The tragedy of verse 9 is not villainy but *valuation*: they were not too evil to see the truth; they were too invested to keep it.

**Turning Away as a Double Motion**

The second charge, *wa-ṣaddū ʿan sabīlih*, is rendered "and have turned from His way," but the verb *ṣadda* — to turn aside, to bar — carries a deliberate ambiguity the commentators explore: it means both to turn away oneself *and* to divert others from the path. Like a man who, finding a road blocked to himself, drags thorn-brush across it behind him, they converted their own rejection into an obstacle for everyone downstream of their influence. Ibn Kathīr reads both senses here, and the double meaning matters for the verse's moral weight: private unbelief is one thing; unbelief that funds and organizes obstruction of the path — the boycotts, the propaganda, the coalitions — has made itself other people's problem too. It is the difference between leaving a door and welding it shut behind you.

**The Verdict Clause and Its Quiet Logic**

The verse closes: *biʾsa mā kānū yaʿmalūn* — "evil indeed is that which they used to do." The imperfect verb *kānū yaʿmalūn* ("they used to do") frames the selling not as one bad transaction but as a *practice* — a settled pattern of trading principle for advantage. Ibn Kathīr notes the consistency with which the Qur'an judges patterns rather than moments: a single lapse can be repented; a character that has monetized truth has entered a different category. For the modern reader the verse is less about seventh-century Qurashīs than about the everyday auctions of conscience it describes: the professional who shades a finding because the client is paying, the friend who stays silent about an injustice because speaking costs a seat at the table, the community leader who recalibrates his principles to the donor's comfort. Each is a sale. The verse's question — *what was your price?* — is asked in the same tone for every seller, and the adjective has never once changed: whatever it fetched, it was *paltry*.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:10

> **observing neither kinship nor treaty with any believer. And it is they who are the transgressors.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**One Verse to Complete the Indictment**

Verse 10 is among the shortest in the sūrah, and its brevity is its rhetoric. Having described the treaty-breakers' conduct toward the Muslim community in general (v. 8), the verse now particularizes the charge with surgical economy: *lā yarqubūna fī muʾminin illan wa-lā dhimmah* — "they observe neither kinship nor treaty with *any believer*." The move from "you" (plural, the community) to "any believer" (distributive singular) matters juridically: no individual believer, however weak, however isolated, could count on the restraint these people owed to the powerful. The bond of *ill* — kinship, alliance — and the bond of *dhimmah* — sworn obligation — both collapse before the believer as such. Ibn Kathīr reads the verse as the completion of verses 7–9: after the general analysis comes the itemized bill. And the closing identification — *wa-ulāʾika humu al-muʿtadūn*, "and it is they who are the transgressors" — stamps the file closed with the Qur'an's own classification.

**Transgression as Boundary-Collapse**

The word *muʿtadūn* (from *iʿtidāʾ*, transgression) is one of the Qur'an's broadest moral terms: to overstep a boundary, to take what lies outside one's allotment. The classical lexicons derive it from *ʿadā* — to pass beyond, as a runner passes a mark. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the term spans both theological transgression (disbelief itself, which oversteps the boundary of the soul's rightful submission) and practical transgression (aggression, treaty-breaking, injustice in force). The Qur'an uses the same family for the ethics of retaliation:

> *"So whosoever transgresses against you, transgress against him in the manner he transgressed against you."* (Qur'an 2:194)

What distinguishes the people of verse 10 is not that they fought — fighting, in that world, was ambient — but that they fought *across* every line that fighting was supposed to respect: the line of blood, the line of oath, the line of the sanctuary months, and now, explicitly, the line that marks the believer as a covenant-partner. Transgression, in its deepest Qur'anic sense, is contempt for limits as such; and the verse warns every reader that a person who routinely negotiates his boundaries is not flexible but lawless, whatever banner he flies.

**Why the Believer Is Named as the Protected Class**

It is worth pausing on why the verse names *the believer* as the party to whom kinship and treaty are owed. In the tribal logic of pre-Islamic Arabia, obligation tracked lineage and alliance, not creed; a man honored his oath to his cousin and his confederate regardless of what either worshipped. Verse 10 records that this old calculus had been corrupted into something darker: obligation now tracked *power and convenience*, and the believer — the member of the vulnerable Madinan polity — had become the safe category to betray. The verse quietly inverts the corruption: from God's side, the believer is precisely the one whose covenant cannot be dishonored with impunity, because the believer's treaty is, as verse 7 established, a treaty with God and His Messenger. The Qur'an thereby creates a protected status not by granting privilege to the believer but by declaring the cost of violating him. Later Islamic law would inherit this logic in the doctrine that the blood of the muʿāhad (covenant-holder) is sacrosanct — and the Prophet ﷺ would state its violation in terms that chill every honest reader:

> *"Whoever kills a person under covenant (muʿāhad) will not smell the fragrance of Paradise, though its fragrance is found from a journey of forty years."* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī (6914) from ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr)

**The Short Verse's Long Shadow**

For all its brevity, verse 10 completes a case file that the sūrah will keep consulting: a people who sold the signs (v. 9), whose mouths and hearts disagreed (v. 8), who honored no bond toward the weakest party (v. 10) — *they* are the transgressors. The definitive article in "it is *they*" performs the sūrah's favorite operation: separating identities. Not the idolater as such — verse 4 has already blessed the faithful treaty-holder — but this formed character: the oath-shredder who preyed specifically where retaliation was weakest. In an age when every community debates who its enemies are, the verse offers a discriminator better than creed or flag: watch how a party treats those too weak to retaliate, and you have God's own classification of them. Transgression is not what a people believe; it is what they do to the believer-sized gaps in their accountability.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:11

> **But if they repent, and perform the prayer and give the alms, then they are your brethren in religion. And We expound the signs for a people who know.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Exit Door Stated in Legal Form**

Verse 11 restates the mercy-clause of verse 5, but where verse 5 spoke in the language of war ("let them go their way"), verse 11 speaks in the language of *belonging*: *fa-ikhwānukum fī al-dīn* — "then they are your brethren in religion." The conditions are identical and instructive: *tābū* (they repented — abandoning the idolatry and the hostile confederacy it anchored), *aqāmū al-ṣalāt* (they established the prayer), *ātū al-zakāt* (they gave the alms). Ibn Kathīr glosses the repentance here as the abandonment of *shirk*; and the pairing of prayer and alms — the vertical and the horizontal obligations — recurs throughout the Qur'an as the two public markers of a life reoriented to God. The verse thereby completes the architecture begun in verse 1: repudiation, grace period, exception for the faithful, asylum for the seeker, and now full brotherhood for the returning. The door at the end of the corridor does not open onto a second-class corridor; it opens into the family room.

**The Blood of the People of the Qiblah**

The classical jurists built a towering doctrine on this verse and its siblings. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī record the principle that verse 11 "makes sacrosanct the blood of the people of the qiblah" — those who turn their faces in prayer toward the Kaʿbah — meaning that no separate treaty is needed to establish peace between Muslims: membership in the prayer itself *is* the treaty. In later centuries, when multiple Islamic polities coexisted, they concluded inter-state treaties as equals, but the underlying doctrine of an automatic, creed-based peace remained the ummah's legal floor. The Prophet ﷺ had already made the signs of membership and protection explicit in the ḥadīth of the five pillars:

> *"Islām is built upon five: the testimony that there is no god but God and that Muḥammad is the Messenger of God, the establishment of the prayer, the giving of the alms, the pilgrimage, and the fast of Ramadan."* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī (8) and Muslim (16) from Ibn ʿUmar)

And on the prayer's status as the boundary-marker of the covenant community, he ﷺ said:

> *"The covenant between us and them is the prayer; whoever abandons it has disbelieved."* (Recorded by al-Tirmidhī (2621), from Anas ibn Mālik; graded ṣaḥīḥ)

**Brotherhood as a Juridical Fact, Not a Sentiment**

The word *ikhwān* (brethren) in this verse is not emotional garnish; it is a status with consequences. Qur'anic brotherhood creates mutual rights and duties — inheritance in the early Madinan period before the revelation of shares, mutual defense, the prohibition of one believer's despoiling another, and the obligation of mutual counsel and cover that the sūrah will detail in verses 71 and 119. The verse is among the most radical social legislations in history: it replaces the brotherhood of blood — the entire organizing principle of Arabian society — with the brotherhood of shared orientation. A Qurashī who yesterday fought at Ḥunayn becomes the brother of an Abyssinian and a Persian he has never met, on the strength of two prostrations and an alms. Al-Qurṭubī marvels at the speed: one verse, and the category "stranger" is abolished for everyone who walks through it.

**"For a People Who Know": The Verse's Own Theory of Its Audience**

The closing clause — *wa-nufaṣṣilu al-āyāti li-qawmin yaʿlamūn* — "and We expound the signs for a people who know" — attaches a condition to divine explanation itself. The Qur'an repeatedly ties the *detail* of its exposition to the *quality* of its audience: those who reflect, those who know, those of understanding. The implication is that clarity is not merely broadcast; it is a collaboration. The signs are expounded for minds prepared to receive connection — and no verse in this opening passage rewards knowing reflection more than the sequence just completed: that the God who ordered the sword at verse 5 is the same God who opened the treasury of brotherhood at verse 11, with nothing between them but repentance, prayer, and alms. The one who *knows* sees the design; the one who quotes verse 5 without verse 11 has been defeated by his own scissors. For every generation of readers, the verse sets the entrance exam for the rest of the sūrah: bring a mind that wants the whole picture, and the whole picture will be given.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:12

> **But if they renege on their oaths after having made their treaty, and vilify your religion, then fight the leaders of disbelief—truly they have no oaths—that they might desist.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Escalating Breaches**

Verse 12 governs the final case in the sūrah's treaty architecture: the party that, *after* the general settlement, compounds its betrayal. The Arabic itemizes the escalation with legal precision: *in naqadū aymānahum min baʿdi mīthāqihim* — "if they unravel their oaths after their treaty." The verb *naqada* is the unravelling of a rope: the covenant was a twisted cord, and breach pulls it apart strand by strand. The second clause, *wa-ṭaʿanū fī dīnikum* — "and vilify your religion" — uses the verb *ṭaʿana*, literally to stab; verbal assault on the faith is figured as a knife-work, and al-Qurṭubī hears in the metaphor the recognition that words can wound a community's coherence as surely as weapons. Ibn Kathīr reads the two conditions together: the material breach (arms) plus the ideological assault (propaganda designed to detach the weak from the community) — a combination that in the sūrah's context describes the ringleaders who kept the frontier tribes inflamed even after the four months had settled the general question.

**Why the Leaders Are Named**

The command that follows is aimed with deliberate narrowness: *f-qātilū aʾimmata al-kufr* — "fight the *leaders* of disbelief." Some commentators, al-Rāzī among them, hold that the leaders are named because they are the decisive element — the generation and funding of hostility — while the rank and file are followers whose hostility dissolves with their chiefs; others, like Ibn Kathīr, note that although the verse came about figures like the Makkan war-leadership, it states a universal rule for all who fit the description. The Qur'an's own narratives repeatedly show that a people's rejection of its prophet is voiced and driven by its notables — *"the leading ones among his people said..."* is the refrain of the perished nations (7:60, 66, 75, 88). The verse's sociology is cold-eyed: movements of aggression have organizational charts, and accountability that ignores the chart wastes itself on the implement while sparing the hand. Modern law reached the same insight at Nuremberg; the Qur'an stated it at Tabūk.

**"They Have No Oaths"**

The clause *inna lahum uḥūman* — rendered "truly they have no oaths" — records the legal effect of their conduct: by unravelling every strand themselves, they have dissolved the instrument that would have shielded them. Al-Ṭabarī explains: since they broke the oaths, it is *as though* the oaths never existed — the protection lapses not by the believers' choice but by the oath-breakers' own act. The verse ends with the purpose-clause that restrains the whole command: *laʿallahum yantahūn* — "that they might desist." The stated goal of the fighting is the cessation of the aggression and vilification — a cessation which, the grammar implies, could arrive at any moment and would instantly re-sheathe the sword. The Qur'an embeds in the war's stated objective its own termination condition, and the Prophet's entire career of accepting mid-battle surrender, treaty, and profession of faith is this clause made history.

**Vilification, Law, and the Limits of Force**

The juristic discussion around this verse is among the most careful in the tradition, and honest commentary must present its range. Al-Qurṭubī records that many jurists held that covenant-holders (ahl al-dhimmah) who reviled the Prophet forfeited protection, while Abū Ḥanīfah demurred, reasoning that their unbelief already contained an implicit denial worse than any insult, and that the verse's own two-condition structure (breach *plus* vilification) sets a high bar for the death penalty. He further records the exquisite early case of Abū Bakr, who, enraged by a man's insult of the Prophet ﷺ, was asked whether he intended execution, and answered: *"No one has that right after the Messenger of God."* The deeper principle the source commentary distills is that the classical treatment of vilification was framed by *incitement* — speech that organized violence, like the campaign of Kaʿb ibn al-Ashraf — rather than by offense taken. The Prophet ﷺ himself endured, without retaliation, the insults the Qur'an itself records: that he was a madman, a poet, a teller of ancient fables; he lived at peace with neighbors who never professed his message. The believer reading verse 12 today inherits both halves: the recognition that organized defamation of the faith can be an act of war — and the Prophet's own refusal to convert personal grievance into punitive theology. Both halves are sunnah; neither may be amputated.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:13

> **Will you not fight a people who broke their oaths, and intended to expel the Messenger, and opened [hostility] against you first? Do you fear them? For God is worthier of being feared by you, if you are believers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Counts in One Indictment**

Verse 13 is the sūrah's own legal brief for the campaign it has been ordering, and al-Rāzī observes that its structure is deliberate: three charges are listed, *any one of which* would suffice. First, *naqaḍū aymānahum* — they broke their oaths: the unravelling of Ḥudaybiyah and its allied web of pledges. Second, *hamma bi-ikhrāji al-rasūl* — "they intended to expel the Messenger": most commentators read this of the plot to drive the Prophet from Makkah that preceded the Hijrah — the council where the Quraysh resolved on assassination or expulsion — while some extend it to the later campaigns, Uḥud and the Trench, whose aim was to dislodge him from Madinah. Third, *wa-hum badaʾūkum awwala marrah* — "and they opened hostilities against you first": the first blow, the initiative in aggression, is theirs — a point the Qur'an never lets the community forget, because the right to respond is rooted in the fact of having been attacked (cf. 22:39: *"Leave is given to those who are fought, because they have been wronged"*). The three counts together — perjury, intent to destroy the leadership, first aggression — answer in advance the question every generation asks of every war: *why?* The Qur'an does not answer with destiny or glory; it answers with a charge sheet.

**The Interrogative That Confronts Hesitation**

The verse opens with *a-lā tuqātilūna* — "Will you not fight...?" — a negative interrogative, which in Arabic rhetoric is the strongest form of urging. It presupposes an audience that *could* fight and is holding back. The sūrah is speaking, before the event, to the very reluctance that the Tabūk expedition would expose: the believers who would rather stay with the harvest. And the verse diagnoses the reluctance's engine in its most startling clause: *a-takhafūnahum* — "Do you fear them?" The fear named here is not cowardice in the abstract but a *mis-located* fear: dread of the creature that has displaced dread of the Creator. The corrective follows instantly: *fa-innā Allāha aḥaqqu an takhafūhu in kuntum muʾminīn* — "God is worthier of being feared by you, if you are believers." The comparative *aḥaqqu* (worthier, more entitled) treats fear as a finite resource being misallocated: you have a budget of awe; the enemy is drawing what belongs to God. The Qur'an returns to this audit repeatedly:

> *"That is only Satan frightening his partisans; so fear them not, and fear Me, if you are believers."* (Qur'an 3:175)

And the Qur'an had already generalized the principle at the start of the Madinan legislation: *"...fear them not, and fear Me"* (5:3), and *"fear Me" is the seal upon the qiblah verse* (2:150).

**Fear Management as a Doctrine of Faith**

The psychological insight in "God is worthier of being feared" repays slow reading. Fear is not commanded to be *eliminated* — the Qur'an knows no stoic fantasy of fearlessness — but to be *ranked*. The believer feels fear; the believer refuses it the chair of final authority. Al-Ṭabarī notes that the verse ends with *in kuntum muʾminīn* — "if you are believers" — making the correct allocation of fear a *criterion* of faith itself: not its height, but its direction. This is the same doctrine the Prophet ﷺ lived before the community at Ḥunayn's near-rout and at Tabūk's brutal march: the believers' tactical fear was real, and it was mastered by a larger reference. Modern life rarely demands the battlefield version, but the structure is identical in every situation where a person silences conscience because of what some human might do — the manager who fears the CEO more than the truth, the student who fears mockery more than God. The verse's question — *whom do you actually fear?* — is a portable audit, and the answer, honestly given, is the most accurate spiritual x-ray a believer can take.

**The Verse as the Passage's Missing Preface**

Read canonically, verse 13 does something quietly decisive for the whole opening of the sūrah: it supplies the *reason* that verses 1–12 could only gesture at through context. The people named by the repudiation are oath-breakers, would-be expellers, and first-strikers — a triple qualification that no merely polytheistic neighbor satisfied. Anyone in any century who lifts verses 1–12 out of this frame is therefore contradicted not by modern apologetics but by the thirteenth verse of the very sūrah he is misusing. And for the believer, the verse completes a moral education in two lines: fighting is never an identity, always a response; and the only fear that may veto a duty is the fear of God. When those two convictions govern a community, it can be entrusted with strength precisely because strength will never become its god.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:14

> **Fight them and God will punish them by means of your hands and disgrace them, and He will grant you victory over them and heal the breasts of a believing people,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**God Punishing by Human Hands: The Theology of Instruments**

Verse 14 contains one of the Qur'an's most compressed theologies of history: *qātilūhum yuʿadhdhibhumu Allāhu bi-aydīkum* — "Fight them, and *God* will punish them *by your hands*." The grammar assigns the punishment to God and the instrumentality to the believers simultaneously; the verse later repeats the doctrine of itself: the enemy is told to await *"a punishment from Him, or by our hands"* (9:5 — cf. 9:52). The Qur'an's worldview refuses the two errors that hollow out human responsibility: the determinism that says God does everything so nothing is ours to do, and the atheism that says events are ours alone so nothing is God's. Instead the community is made a *pen* in the hand that writes history. The doctrine carries two disciplines at once: humility, because the victory belongs to Another ("You did not slay them, but God slew them; and you threw not when you threw, but God threw" — 8:17); and seriousness, because the instrument is accountable for the use it is put to. The believers at the sūrah's center are not fate's privileged bullies; they are employees in an enterprise whose audit is divine and exacting.

**Healing Breasts: The Psychology of Justice**

The verse's fourth promise is its most tender: *wa-yashfī ṣudūra qawmin muʾminīn* — "and He will heal the breasts of a believing people." Al-Ṭabarī identifies the believing people concretely: the Banū Khuzāʿah, whose men had been cut down in the treacherous night-raid that shattered the truce — a community carrying what we would now call untreated collective trauma. The Qur'an names their wound with the word *ṣadr* (breast, chest) — the seat of grief, resentment, and the pressure of unredressed wrong — and it names the remedy not consolation but *healing* (*shifāʾ*), and locates that healing in the *doing of justice*. The verse quietly teaches a psychological truth that modern trauma studies labors to articulate: some wounds do not close by time or by talk, because they are wounds of *witnessed injustice*; they close when the wrong is named and its machinery dismantled. The verse does not say the believers' rage is sinful; it implies the opposite — the rage is a vital sign, and God proposes to treat it at its source. But note the agency: *God* heals, *through* an outcome He Himself authors. Vengeance privately seized heals nothing; it merely relocates the wound. That distinction — between justice administered within God's order and revenge seized outside it — is the entire ethics of this clause, and the sūrah will keep enforcing it.

**Disgrace and Victory: The Two Directions of the Same Event**

The promises cluster in deliberate symmetry: God punishes them, God disgraces them, God grants you victory, God heals you. Punishment addresses their deeds; disgrace, their standing; victory, your position; healing, your interior. One event, four consequences, two per direction — the Qur'an's characteristic insistence that divine action is never one-dimensional. The word for disgrace (*yukhzihim*) picks up verse 2's warning that "God shall disgrace the disbelievers": the prophecy is now attached to a specific instrument and a specific people. And the promise of victory (*yanaṣurakum ʿalayhim*) echoes the sūrah's refrain — help belongs to God, who gives it to whom He wills (3:160; 30:5) — keeping the instrument from pride: the hands are yours; the triumph is His.

**What This Verse Asks of the Modern Believer**

Stripped of its battlefield, verse 14 leaves a permanent structure of thought. First, the believer's engagement against genuine evil is not obstruction of God's plan but its appointed channel. Second, the outcome of that engagement is determined by God, which converts zeal into patience and stamina: the community that knows the victory is authored can endure defeats that would shatter an enterprise sustained by self-belief alone. Third, justice is medicine — for the doer's community above all — and must be pursued *as* medicine, with dosage, precision, and the goal of health, never with the appetite to wound. The believer who campaigns against an injustice, dismantles a corrupt system, or simply stands witness when standing is costly, is standing inside this verse's promise: work with your hands, and let the healing that follows be attributed — accurately — to the One who works through them.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:15

> **and will dispel the rage within their hearts. And God relents unto whomsoever He will, and God is Knowing, Wise.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**God Treating the Emotion Itself**

Verse 15 continues the healing of verse 14 and descends one level deeper: from the breast to the *rage* within it — *wa-yudhhib ghayẓa qulūbihim* — literally, "and He will remove the anger in their hearts." The word *ghayẓ* denotes hot, seething wrath, the kind that pressurizes a community after treachery; and the verb *adhbaba* is the verb of *taking away* — as one removes a burden from a back. The theological statement is remarkable: God's remedy does not stop at external justice; it reaches inside the injured and un-knots the emotion itself. The Qur'an thus dignifies anger at injustice as real — it does not command the Khuzāʿah to *feel* nothing — while refusing to leave them in the furnace. Wrong done, redressed by God's own action, closes the emotional file; wrong left to fester, or avenged by private hands, keeps it open forever. There is a whole pastoral theology in the sequence: God punishes (v. 14), therefore your rage may be released (v. 15). Forgiveness that precedes justice is often repression; forgiveness that *follows* God's justice can be genuine rest.

**The Open Door Placed After the Sentence**

Then, in a transition the reader must not slide past, the verse turns from the wounded believers to the perpetrators themselves: *wa-yatūbu Allāhu ʿalā man yashāʾ* — "and God relents unto whomsoever He will." Even now — after perjury, aggression, the sentence of verse 14 — the verb of the sūrah's title returns, and its object is *whomever God wills*: the door out of the condemned category stands open to the last hour. Ibn Kathīr notes the resonance with verse 27, where the same clause follows the punishment of Ḥunayn. The verse thereby performs the sūrah's signature move — judgment and invitation held together without either weakening the other. History's sequel confirms it: the very tribes named in these verses would, most of them, walk through that door within months; some of their sons would become reciters of the Qur'an and governors of provinces. The believer is being taught to pronounce judgment on systems of aggression while keeping the door of the sentence unlocked for the persons inside them — because God keeps it unlocked, and He relents *toward whom He wills*, which is a knowledge no human holds.

**Knowing and Wise: The Signatures on the Judgment**

The verse closes with the divine pair *ʿAlīmun Ḥakīm* — Knowing, Wise. The pairing is the guarantee beneath everything the passage has said. That God *knows* answers the question the punishment raises — does He know what He is destroying?
