He knows the oaths, the night-raids, the councils; nothing in the charge sheet of verse 13 is circumstantial to Him. That God is *wise* answers the question the mercy raises — is this relenting arbitrary? Wisdom means the door opens and closes according to a plan whose totality we do not see. The two names together teach the reader how to hold the sūrah's severity and its mercy without reducing either: the severity is informed (Knowing), and the mercy is governed (Wise). Al-Rāzī remarks that this closing formula is placed precisely after the most emotionally charged promise — the removal of rage — because the reader most needs to remember God's knowledge and wisdom when his own feelings are loudest.

**A Verse for the Angry**

For the modern believer, verse 15 may be the most personally applicable verse of the entire opening movement. Everyone carries a Khuzāʿah somewhere: a betrayal, an injustice witnessed or suffered, an anger that has outlived its usefulness and begun to tax the one who keeps it. The verse's teaching sequence is a therapeutic protocol: bring the case to God rather than the sword; trust His management of the outcome; and ask Him specifically for the miracle named here — not the strength to suppress the rage, but its *removal*, its being carried off like a load. The Prophet ﷺ taught exactly this transfer of the emotional burden in supplication: among his words was the prayer for refuge from anxiety and grief, and the Qur'an promises here that the God who heard Ḥudaybiyah's casualties is still in the business of emptying hearts. The believer who cannot let go of an old rage has not yet fully believed that God saw the wrong; the verse's cure for vengeance is, finally, the doctrine of omniscience — He knows, He is wise, and He is quite able to run the accounting without your oversight.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:16

> **Or did you suppose that you would be left alone, while God had yet to know whom among you strove and took no friend apart from God, His Messenger, and the believers? And God is Aware of whatsoever you do.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Exam You Did Not Know You Were Sitting**

Verse 16 changes register from command to metaphysics: *am ḥasibtum* — "or did you suppose...?" — the Qur'an's recurring formula for unmasking a false assumption. The assumption named is that the community could simply *be left alone* — *an tutrakū* — that faith might be held quietly, untested, without ever being called upon to prove its ranking of loyalties. The supposition is refuted by the structure of the world: God *had yet to know* — in the idiom of the Qur'an's "knowing by manifestation" (cf. 2:143) — who among you would strive, and who would take as *walījah* an intimate apart from God, His Messenger, and the believers. The word *walījah* is precise: al-Qurṭubī glosses it as the confidant, the one taken into the inner councils of the heart, the private ally whose advice governs decisions. The verse is not forbidding courtesy to outsiders; it is exposing the *inner cabinet* of the soul: whose voice, at the moment of conflict, actually weighs most? The trial is the audit that answers the question — not to inform God, who is ʿAlīm, but to translate the invisible allegiance into visible history, where it can be seen, rewarded, and understood.

**The Curriculum of Trial**

The verse belongs to a family of "did you suppose" passages that together form the Qur'an's doctrine of testing:

> *"Did mankind suppose that they would be left to say 'We believe' and be tried not?"* (Qur'an 29:2–3)

> *"Or did you suppose that you would enter the Garden, while there had not yet come to you the like of what came to those who passed away before you?"* (Qur'an 2:214)

The logic is consistent: faith makes claims — God first, Messenger next, believers as family — and claims that are never costed remain claims. The sūrah is about to put the community's ranking to its most expensive test: the march to Tabūk in the killing heat of late summer, with the harvest on the trees and the Byzantine empire at the end of the road. Verse 16 tells them, in advance, what the march *is*: not a military necessity alone, but a revealing instrument. And the verse is candid about what it expects the instrument to expose — some will strive, and some will quietly take another walījah: the comfort of home, the opinion of kin, the ledger-book of the harvest. The verse does not say the hypocrites are known to God and therefore may be purged by men; it says the trial will *know* them, and leaves the sorting to God — a restraint the sūrah will make explicit in verse 101, where the Prophet ﷺ is told of hidden hypocrites: *"you know them not; We know them."*

**The Trinity of Loyalty**

The positive side of the verse names the sanctioned inner cabinet: God, His Messenger, and the believers. The ordering is itself doctrine. God alone is the ultimate object of trust and fear (v. 13); the Messenger is the concrete, living embodiment of God's claims upon a life; and the believers are the extended body through which loyalty to God and His Messenger is actually practiced — visited, fed, defended, financed. Ibn Kathīr hears in the phrase a definition of the community's social architecture: the believer's confidence is drawn from these three wells, and any well outside them, made into a *walījah*, becomes a leak. The sūrah will enforce the same architecture in verse 23 ("take not your fathers and brothers as protectors if they prefer disbelief") and verse 24 (the eight loves that must not outweigh God, His Messenger, and striving). Verse 16 is the premise of them all: there is an inner ring of allegiance, and life is designed to reveal what sits in it.

**God Aware: The All-Seeing Signature**

The verse closes: *wa-Anhā bimā taʿmalūna Ṣabīr* — "and God is Aware of whatsoever you do." The signature is the point of the paragraph: the trial is not for God's information but the world's, and every actor is acting before a permanent witness. For the believer, this two-layer picture — an all-knowing God who nonetheless lets history *enact* what He knows — resolves the puzzle of why an omniscient God tests: it is the same reason a teacher who knows the material gives the exam. The knowing is His; the *knowing by you*, the earned, embodied, witnessed knowing, is what the exam bestows. And for the hesitant believer of every era, verse 16 delivers the uncomfortable mercy at its core: the disturbances, the demands, the campaigns that interrupt a settled religious life are not interruptions of the plan; they *are* the plan. "Left alone" is the one thing the sincere were never promised.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:17

> **It is not for the idolaters to maintain the mosques of God, bearing witness of disbelief against themselves. They are those whose deeds have come to naught, and in the Fire shall they abide.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Custody of Sacred Space**

Verse 17 turns from the treaty question to the question of religious legitimacy: *mā kāna lil-mushrikīna an yaʿmirū masājida Allāh* — "it is not for the idolaters to maintain the mosques of God." The verb *ʿamara* (to populate, build up, tend) is the root of ʿimārah — cultivation, settlement, maintenance: the comprehensive word for keeping a place alive. The mosque in the Qur'an is not a building that happens to host worship; it is the institutional heart of the tawḥīd it houses — a place of prostration (from *sajada*, the root of *masjid*), of assembly, of the community's public life. The verse rules that the custodianship of that heart belongs only to those whose creed is the reason the heart beats. The reason is stated with the Qur'an's surgical directness: *shāhidīna ʿalā anfusihim bil-kufr* — "bearing witness of disbelief against themselves." Their very presence at the door testifies against them: they maintain a house whose foundational claim they deny.

**Works That Come to Naught**

The verdict clause — *ḥabiṭat aʿmāluhum* — uses the verb *ḥabaṭa*, the collapse of a building or the invalidation of a legal act: their deeds are voided. The Qur'an attaches this voiding specifically to disbelief and to deeds done for others than God:

> *"And it was already revealed to you and to those before you: if you associate, your deeds will surely come to naught, and you will be among the losers."* (Qur'an 39:65)

> *"Those who disbelieve... their deeds are like a mirage in a desert plain..."* (Qur'an 24:39)

The theological principle — much debated between the schools — is stated here at its bluntest in application to the custodianship question: a deed's *external* form can be impeccable while its *axis* is false. The idolaters of Makkah did maintain the Kaʿbah — rebuilt it, roofed it, fed its pilgrims, garbed it. The verse looks at that entire religious economy and files it under "witness against themselves," because the service was rendered to a God they had partitioned among rivals. Ibn Kathīr notes the poignancy: the mosque cannot be honored by those who dishonor the One it names.

**The Verse's Place in a Longer Argument**

Read in sequence, verse 17 is the first move of a four-verse argument (17–20) about comparative religious merit: who deserves to serve God's houses (17–18), whether venerable ritual services can rank with faith and striving (19), and who stands highest in degree (20). The argument has a context: at the conquest of Makkah, the custodial privileges of the old order — the *siqāyah* (watering the pilgrims) held by al-ʿAbbās's family, the *ḥijābah* (keys) held by the Banū ʿAbd al-Dār — did not vanish; they were re-founded on a new footing. The verse is the constitutional principle for that transition: sacred institutions pass to the people of the creed, not the people of the keys. Notably, the Qur'an does not command the stripping of the families — history records the Prophet ﷺ returning the keys to ʿUthmān ibn Ṭalḥah with the words *"Take it, O Banū Abī Talḥah, as a trust eternal"* — but the *principle* of legitimacy is redefined forever: custody is now a function of taqwā, and the ancient families retain their office by entering the new covenant.

**Institutions Without the Creed, Then and Now**

The verse's question has never stopped being asked. Every generation produces people who love religious architecture, ambience, heritage — the mosque's beauty, the chant's comfort, the ethics' respectability — while declining the Lordship that grounds them all. The verse is gentle to tourists and hard to custodians: it does not bar the seeker at the door (verse 6 ordered an audience for the idolater himself), but it reserves *custody* — the right to define, fund, and direct the house — for those who witness to the God of the house. Al-Qurṭubī draws the practical line for his own age: a community's institutions must not be governed, at the decisive level, by those who would tear up the foundation if it inconvenienced them. Modern boards, endowments, schools and pulpits all stand before verse 17's question, and the answer the Qur'an gives is structural, not merely sentimental: the maintenance of the mosques of God is a creedal office, because the mosque itself is nothing but creed made architectural.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:18

> **Only they shall maintain the mosques of God who believe in God and the Last Day, perform the prayer and give the alms, and fear none but God. Such as these may be among the rightly guided.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Four Qualifications of Custody**

Where verse 17 stated the exclusion, verse 18 states the qualification: *innamā yaʿmiru masājida Allāhi man āmana billāhi wa-l-yawmi al-ākhir* — "only they shall maintain the mosques of God who believe in God and the Last Day." Then follow three further marks: *wa-aqāma al-ṣalāta wa-ātā al-zakāta* — "and perform the prayer and give the alms" — and *wa-lam yakhsha illā Allāha* — "and fear none but God." The structure is a complete anatomy of the worthy custodian. Creed supplies the foundation (God and the Last Day); the two pillars of practice supply the ongoing life (prayer vertical, alms horizontal — the same pair that sealed conversion in verse 11); and the final clause supplies the spine — fear reserved for God alone, the same audit verse 13 imposed before battle. Al-Ṭabarī observes that the qualifications are listed cumulatively and none is optional: a philanthropist without creed fails at the first clause, a believer who hoards fails at the second, and a worshipper who trembles before donors and despots fails at the last.

**The Beautiful "May Be" of the Qur'an**

The verse closes on a note that stirred the grammarians: *fa-ʿasā ulāʾika an yakūnū mina al-rāshidīn* — "such as these may be among the rightly guided." The particle *ʿasā* in ordinary Arabic expresses hope or likelihood; but al-Ṭabarī reports the established rule among the commentators that when *ʿasā* issues from God in the Qur'an, it carries the force of certainty and promise — God's "perhaps" is His "shall be." Why, then, the softer form? The commentators answer: it is the etiquette of grace. God names the reward in the register of hope to keep the servants striving, humility intact, the gift never mistaken for a wage owed. The one who meets all four qualifications is *promised* right guidance, but hears it spoken as an encouraging "may well be" — and that tone itself trains the ear of the believer: assurance without arrogance, confidence without entitlement. Rāshid — from rushd, the rightly directed, sound judgment — is the last word of the verse, and it fits: the custodians of the houses of guidance are themselves first guided.

**Fear of None but God: The Custodian's Spine**

The fourth qualification deserves its own reflection, because it names the specific temptation of institutional religion. Whoever holds custody of sacred space will be pressured — by wealth, by power, by the crowd — to adjust the message of the house to the comfort of the moment. The verse's answer is not a strategy but a character: the custodian must have *no other fear*, so that no other lever works. Ibn Kathīr cites the application to the believers of Makkah who kept their faith under boycott and torture, and al-Qurṭubī extends it to every imam and trustee who must tell a patron an unwelcome truth. The phrase also quietly completes the verse's parallel with verse 13: before the community may wield the sword, it must pass the fear-audit; before a person may tend the mosque, the same audit applies. The Qur'an's deepest offices all sit behind the same door.

**A Charter for Every Volunteer and Trustee**

For the modern believer, verse 18 is the job description behind every mosque committee, school board, charity directorship, and even the quiet custodianship of a household's religious life. It asks four questions of anyone who would serve the houses of God: Is the creed whole? Is the practice alive — prayer and alms both, the God-ward and the human-ward duties together? And is there any person whose displeasure could buy a compromise? The verse's realism is bracing: it does not ask custodians to be scholars or saints of the highest rank, but to be *structurally sound* — foundation, pillars, spine. A community that elects its custodians by these four marks will find, by God's promising "perhaps," that its houses are indeed among the rightly guided; a community that elects by wealth, lineage, or charisma alone has rebuilt, at the level of personnel, the very arrangement verse 17 dismantled.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:19

> **Or do you consider giving drink to the pilgrims and maintaining the Sacred Mosque to be like those who believe in God and the Last Day and strive in God's way? They are not equal in the Eyes of God. And God guides not wrongdoing people.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Debate Near the Pulpit**

The asbāb — circumstances — of verse 19 are reported by Ibn Kathīr, al-Ṭabarī, and al-Qurṭubī with two converging accounts. In one, al-ʿAbbās, the Prophet's uncle and the custodian of the *siqāyah* — the honored office of watering the pilgrims — was conversing with ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib, pressing the merit of his service; ʿAlī, who had fought at Badr and Uḥud, replied that he had striven with his life since the first hour, while al-ʿAbbās had only lately come to prayer. In the other account, Muslims were debating among themselves which deed was best — some saying the watering of pilgrims, some the maintenance of the Kaʿbah, some the striving in God's way — and were rebuked by ʿUmar for raising their voices beside the Prophet's pulpit, whereupon the verse came down. Al-Qurṭubī offers the judicious possibility that the verse may have been *cited* into the moment rather than first revealed by it: a previously revealed answer applied to a live dispute. Either way, the dispute is eternal: religious people have always ranked liturgical comfort against costly commitment, and the Qur'an here sets its weights on the scale.

**Ritual Service Versus Total Stakes**

The verse's comparison is deliberately lopsided. On one side: *siqāyatu al-ḥājji* (giving drink to the pilgrims) and *ʿimāratu al-masjidi al-ḥarām* (maintaining the Sacred Mosque) — genuinely honorable services, ancient and venerated, the religious infrastructure of a whole peninsula. On the other side: *āmana billāhi wa-l-yawmi al-ākhir wa-jāhada fī sabīli Allāh* — faith in God and the Last Day *plus* striving in God's way. And the verdict: *mā yastawūna ʿinda Allāhi* — "they are not equal in the Eyes of God." The inequality is not because the services are bad — the verse never says that — but because the second party has put everything at stake, *anfus* and *amwāl*, life and property, while the first has given from the surplus of an otherwise unconverted order. Ibn Kathīr states the principle directly: the deeds of service are only weighty when they stand upon the deeds of creed. It is the difference between maintaining the stadium and playing in the match with your body.

**"Do You Consider...?": The Verse as Scales in Every Believer's Hands**

The interrogative *a-jaʿaltum* — "do you consider/make...?" — hands the scales to the listener. The Qur'an repeatedly demands that believers *judge weights* in exactly this comparative grammar: *"Say: are those who know and those who know not equal?"* (39:9); *"Is he who was dead and We gave him life... equal to him who is in darkness?"* (6:122). Verse 19 trains a permanent faculty: the ranking of goods. Not all religious activity is equal, and sentiment is a poor scale. The ranking criterion the verse teaches is *costliness aligned with creed* — what did it require of your faith, your safety, your wealth, your self? A hundred services that never inconvenience the soul do not outweigh one act that risked everything for God. Al-Rāzī adds the social reading: this is a community at war for its existence, and the verse refuses to let the comfortable offices hide the ones bleeding at the front — a refusal aimed not at humiliating the servers but at honoring, in public revelation, those whose service could not be performed safely.

**Wrongdoing and the Loss of Proportion**

The verse's closing clause is startlingly apt: *wa-Anhā lā yahdī al-qawma al-ẓālimīn* — "and God guides not wrongdoing people." Why call a mistaken ranking *ẓulm*? Because ẓulm is precisely the misplacement of a thing — putting the lesser where the greater belongs. To rank the comfortable service above the total stake is to commit a misplacement in the moral universe, and misplacements, the verse warns, cascade: the person who cannot tell which deeds are greater soon cannot tell which loves are greater (v. 24 will press the same audit inward) or which fears are greater (v. 13). For the modern believer, verse 19 is a yearly review form: What did my religious activity cost me? Where in my service was anything actually at stake — my comfort, my reputation, my money, my time's best hours? The verse does not command everyone to a battlefield; it commands everyone to honesty about which side of the comparison his own ledger sits on. And it leaves the door of striving open in every age, because the equality it denies — between comfort and commitment — is available, at some price, to every believer in every circumstance.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:20

> **Those who believe and emigrate and strive in the way of God with their wealth and their selves are greater in rank in the Eyes of God. And it is they who are the triumphant.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Verbs That Made a New Aristocracy**

Verse 20 names the top of the ladder verse 19 began to build: *alladhīna āmanū wa-hājarū wa-jāhadū fī sabīli Allāhi bi-amwālihim wa-anfusihim* — "those who believe, and emigrate, and strive in the way of God with their wealth and their selves." Three verbs, each a world. *Āmanū*: they gave their creed first — the foundation without which nothing else ranks (v. 18). *Hājarū*: they emigrated — abandoned house, clan, trade, and homeland to join the Prophet ﷺ in Madinah, the act that gave the Muslim calendar its year one. *Jāhadū bi-amwālihim wa-anfusihim*: they strove with wealth *and* selves — the double currency the sūrah keeps joined (vv. 41, 44, 81, 88, 111). The sequence is the biography of the first Muslim elite: conviction, relocation, and then expenditure of everything the relocation left them. Their rank — *aʿẓamu darajatan ʿinda Allāhi* — is the greatest degree with God, and the verse's refrain-like closing names them *al-fāʾizūn*, the triumphant, the ones who actually *won*.

**The Qur'an's Ladder of Degree**

The doctrine of degrees (*darajāt*) runs through the Qur'an as a whole theology of asymmetrical excellence:

> *"God has preferred in degree those who strive with their wealth and their selves over those who sit back; and to each God has promised the good... but God has preferred the strivers over the sitters with a great reward — degrees from Him, and forgiveness and mercy."* (Qur'an 4:95–96)

> *"They are degrees with God; and God is Seer of what they do."* (Qur'an 3:163)

The idea unsettles every egalitarian sentiment we bring to religion, and the Qur'an is unembarrassed by the discomfort: paradise has structure, nearness has depth, and the currency of the hierarchy is *what a soul actually spent of itself*. Al-Rāzī notes that the ranking is not for the thrill of competition alone but for the pedagogy of aspiration — human beings calibrate their effort to the prizes they believe exist. The Qur'an consistently refuses to flatten those prizes, because a flattened paradise would teach a flattened effort, and the world's repair requires the people on the upper rungs.

**Who Were These, Concretely?**

The verse's categories were not theoretical. The Emigrants (*Muhājirūn*) had left everything twice over — first in the boycott years inside Makkah, then in the Hijrah itself; Abū Bakr arrived in Madinah with five or six thousand dirhams of a former fortune; the Companions of the Bench (*Aṣḥāb al-Ṣuffah*) slept in the mosque for want of homes and learned the Qur'an by heart between hungers. Their striving was literal: Badr, Uḥud, the Trench, Ḥudaybiyah, Khaybar, Makkah, Ḥunayn, Tabūk. When verse 20 says "with their wealth and their selves," it is describing men who had, in the deepest sense, already died — the Hijrah had severed every claim on their future except God's. And the verse's promise of triumph was ratified by their Prophet in the ḥadīth qudsī he conveyed:

> *"God said: I have prepared for My righteous servants what no eye has seen, nor ear heard, nor occurred to the heart of man."* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī (7498) and Muslim (2824) from Abū Hurayrah, on the authority of the Prophet ﷺ from his Lord)

**Triumph Redefined**

The final word — *al-fāʾizūn*, from the root f-w-z, to win, to escape peril into gain, as the winning of a booty or the rescue from drowning — deserves the last reflection. The Qur'an had already used the word for the survivors of the Flood (*"a day... on which the Ark floated with them; so We made it a sign for mankind"* — cf. 29:15) and will use it for the ultimate escape from the Fire. Verse 20 applies it to the strivers *before* any final reckoning: their triumph is already real, already banked, because a life spent at full stakes for God is its own victory whatever the battlefield reports say. The winners of verse 20 are not those who took prizes from the world, but those the world could no longer price. And the verse quietly invites each later believer to locate himself on its three verbs in whatever register his era permits: conviction, movement toward God's community, and expenditure — of money first, and then, at the far end of the ladder, of the self. The ranks are open; the currency has not changed; the Eyes that assign the degrees are the same.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:21

> **Their Lord gives them glad tidings of Mercy from Him, and Contentment, and Gardens wherein they shall have lasting bliss,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**God Himself as the Bearer of Good News**

Verse 21 opens the reward-passage for the strivers of verse 20, and its first surprise is grammatical: *yubashshiruhum rabbuhum* — "their *Lord* gives them glad tidings." In the Qur'an's normal economy, the Prophet is the bashīr, the bearer of good news; here God takes the office upon Himself. The believers of the upper ranks are not sent a message of mercy; the Sender of mercy comes, so to speak, in person. Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī both linger on the tenderness of the construction: it is "their Lord" — the compound of ownership and nurture (rabb) — who stoops to the role of herald for His own servants. The Prophet ﷺ, who conveyed this verse, knew the intimacy it describes from the other side, and he described God's joy at His servant's return in words that let the believer glimpse the Sender's own heart:

> *"God is more joyful at the repentance of one of His servants than one of you who found his camel, laden with his food and drink, after losing it in a barren land... and God rejoices at his repentance more than that man at his camel."* (Recorded by Muslim (2747) from Anas ibn Mālik)

**The Order of the Gifts: Mercy, Contentment, Gardens**

The verse's sequence is a ladder of rewards, and the commentators read its order as doctrine. First *raḥmah* — mercy from Him: the covering and forgiveness that clears the account. Then *riḍwān* — contentment, the divine pleasure itself, which the tradition ranks as the summit of the gifts of the Garden; 3:15 calls it *"greater than the delight"* of paradise's rivers and companions, and verse 72 of this sūrah will say plainly: *"the Contentment from God is greater."* Last come the *jannāt* — the Gardens, *fīhā naʿīm*, "wherein is lasting bliss." The order tells the truth about human desire: first the soul must be safe (mercy), then it must be *approved* (contentment), and only then can it enjoy (bliss). A paradise of pleasures without the smile of the Beloved would be, to the believer of verse 21, a beautifully furnished exile. Al-Ghazālī's later metaphor is already latent in the verse: a child offered gold or a toy chooses the toy, because it loves by its capacity; the mature soul, once it knows the Giver, ranks His pleasure above His gifts.

**Naʿīm: The Texture of the Bliss**

The word *naʿīm* — from a root meaning softness, ease, flourishing pastures — names the *texture* of the Gardens' life: unstrained, unhurried, an enjoyment without the four thorns that pierce every worldly pleasure — guilt, anxiety, boredom, and ending. The Qur'an calls its Paradise *jannāt al-naʿīm* repeatedly (10:9; 22:56; 31:8; 56:12), always with the resonance of rest after exertion — and verse 21 stands immediately after the verse of striving (20) for that reason: the naʿīm is the comma of rest built into the sentence of jihād. The believer who spends his wealth and self is not repaid with a medal but with *softness*: the unhurried ease of a being that no longer needs to defend itself from anything.

**Glad Tidings as a Technology of Endurance**

The deeper function of verse 21 is psychological, and the Qur'an is explicit about it elsewhere: good news is fuel. *"And give glad tidings to those who believe and do righteous deeds that theirs shall be Gardens..."* (2:25) is the refrain after nearly every command of sacrifice. Human beings run on anticipated meaning; a community asked for its wealth and its lives will give both only if it has been shown, repeatedly and vividly, what the expenditure purchases. The Qur'an never apologizes for motivating by reward, because it knows its audience: creatures whose nervous systems are built for expectancy. The mature believer moves gradually from loving the gifts to loving the Giver — the verse's own ladder teaches the transition — but he never despises the gifts, because they are His. And every recitation of verse 21 renews the deposit: mercy from Him, and contentment, and Gardens of bliss — the three-line savings account of everyone whose life verse 20 describes.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:22

> **abiding therein forever. Truly with God is a great reward.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Forever as the Final Commodity**

Verse 22 completes verse 21's promise with the word that transforms everything it touches: *khālidīna fīhā abadan* — "abiding therein forever." The Qur'an's picture of the Garden is extravagant, but every river, spouse, and mansion is denominated in one currency above all: *abad* — endlessness. Worldly pleasure's defining flaw is not its intensity but its duration; the Qur'an's repair is not primarily *more* pleasure but *unending* pleasure — *khuld*, immortality of delight. The word *khālid* (from kh-l-d, to remain, endure) occurs in this sense scores of times; here it is doubled by *abadan* ("forever"), a redundancy the commentators call the Qur'an's grammar of consolation: when the promise is endlessness, no amount of emphasis is wasted. The believer is invited to do the arithmetic the sūrah itself will do in verse 38: the enjoyment of this world is *qalīl* — little, paltry — precisely because it is mortgaged to minutes; the reward with God is *ʿaẓīm* — great — precisely because nothing collects it at the end.

**The Greatness That Grades Rewards**

The closing clause — *inna Allāha ʿindahu ajrun ʿaẓīm* — "truly with God is a great reward" — is a Qur'anic refrain (4:13, 33:31, 48:29 and dozens more), and its placement here is architectural. Verses 20–22 form a single movement: the ranked strivers (20), the Lord's own glad tidings (21), and now the summary valuation (22). The word *ʿinda Allāh* — "with God," "in God's presence" — locates the reward where moth and rust do not corrupt: not merely *from* God but *at* God, in the economy where He Himself is the treasury. The adjective *ʿaẓīm* is one of the Qur'an's grand superlatives — used of God's own name (2:255: *al-ʿaẓīm*), His throne (9:129), the Qur'an itself (15:87, 39:1) — so applying it to the believers' reward is a deliberate magnification: the gift is scaled to the Giver.

**The Certainty Beneath the Comfort**

The verses of reward are not decoration; they are the Qur'an's answer to the investment problem of every serious life. The sūrah is about to command a march that some of its hearers will not survive; before it does, verses 21–22 open the books of the other side. Al-Rāzī observes that the surest motivator is not fear of loss but confidence in gain, and the Qur'an's repeated grammar — *inna* ("truly"), the emphatic particle — is chosen to carry conviction: this is not a slogan but a disclosure. And the tradition anchored the certainty in the Prophet's own conveyance, as in the ḥadīth qudsī already quoted at verse 21: *"no eye has seen, nor ear heard, nor occurred to the heart of man."* The believer's whole risk calculus rests on the credibility of the Messenger — which is why the sūrah, having opened the treasury of the next world (20–22), will now descend into the hard ledger of this one (23–37), commanding the loves and fears that must be re-ranked if the promise is to be acted upon. Faith in forever is the axle on which every chapter of sacrifice turns.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:23

> **O you who believe! Take not your fathers and your brothers as protectors if they prefer disbelief to belief. As for those among you who take them as protectors, it is they who are the wrongdoers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command That Cuts Deepest**

After the rewards of verses 20–22, the sūrah turns to the price of belonging, and it begins with the costliest currency of all: blood. *Lā tattakhidhū ābāʾakum wa-ikhwānakum awliyāʾa* — "take not your fathers and your brothers as protectors" — *in-iṣṭarajū al-kufr ʿalā al-īmān* — "if they prefer disbelief to belief." The address is to believers, and the named temptations are the two relationships around which the entire Arabian — and, honestly, human — world revolved: father and brother, the sources of identity, security, and survival. The verb *iṣṭarā* is instructive: to *purchase in exchange*, to prefer one commodity over another in a transaction — the same commercial metaphor as verse 9. The relatives have priced their allegiance and chosen kufr; the believer is now forbidden to purchase their *walāyah* — their protective alliance, their inner-circle loyalty — at the price of his own creed.

**The Historical Squeeze of the Holdouts**

The commentators link the verse to a concrete human squeeze: the believers still in Makkah after the Hijrah, whose families pressured them to remain — or those in Madinah whose relatives worked, sometimes with tears, sometimes with threats, to pull them back into the old order. Al-Qurṭubī transmits the reports that when the command to emigrate came, some were persuaded by fathers and brothers to stay; others hold the verse a general command for all believers, since verse 25's past-tense account of Ḥunayn suggests a setting after the conquest. Both readings are true at their own level: a specific rescue of the trapped, and a permanent law of allegiance. The Qur'an had already conceded the emotional physics of the situation in a Makkan verse that softens without surrendering:

> *"And if they strive with you to associate with Me that whereof you have no knowledge, obey them not; yet keep company with them in this world kindly."* (Qur'an 31:15)

Verse 23 draws the line that 31:15 keeps kind: *alliance* is severed; *kindness* is not. The believer honors the polytheist parent at every point except the point where the parent demands the soul.

**Walāyah as a Legal and Spiritual Category**

The forbidden thing has a technical name and a technical content. *Walāyah* — from w-l-y, to be near, to have charge of — is the comprehensive bond of patronage: taking someone into one's confidence, depending on their judgment, making them a partner in decisions and destiny. It is more than affection (which cannot be commanded anyway) and more than courtesy; it is the *governance* of one's life-direction. The Qur'an legislates the same boundary repeatedly:

> *"O you who believe, take not My enemy and your enemy as protectors... And whoever of you does that has strayed from the right way."* (Qur'an 60:1)

And it states the positive mirror in the verse that is this sūrah's own axis: God, His Messenger, and the believers are the sanctioned *awliyāʾ* (cf. 9:16, 9:71). The wrongdoer (*ẓālim*) of verse 23's close is therefore not the dutiful son but the soul that has *misplaced* its loyalty — ẓulm again in its root sense of putting things where they do not belong: the father in the seat that belongs to God.

**The Verse in a World of Family Loyalty**

Every culture runs on kinship; the Qur'an respects the institution enough to legislate its limit rather than abolish it — the same Book commands *birr al-wālidayn* (kindness to parents, 17:23–24) in the strongest terms. What verse 23 forbids is the elevation of family from a duty to an *idol*. The modern applications are quieter than Makkah's but just as binding: the believer whose career of compromise is designed to keep a family's approval, whose marriage prospect is chosen against God's limits to please a parent, whose silence about truth is the price of a seat at the tribal table. In each case the walāyah has quietly migrated. And the verse's diagnostic works on the self before it works on the family: it does not say "fathers and brothers are bad"; it says *if they prefer kufr* — the condition is conduct, not kinship. A believing father is a treasure; an idol masquerading as a father is a rival deity, and the Qur'an, which knows how much the severing costs, books the cost in advance: the one who keeps the wrong allegiance is — the verse's own verdict — *al-ẓālimūn*, among the wrongdoers, which is the loneliest category in the Book.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:24

> **Say, "If your fathers, your children, your brothers, your spouses, your tribe, the wealth you have acquired, commerce whose stagnation you fear, and dwellings you find pleasing are more beloved to you than God, and His Messenger, and striving in His way, then wait till God comes with His Command." And God guides not iniquitous people.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Complete Inventory of Human Love**

Verse 24 is the Qur'an's most comprehensive audit of the heart, and its genius lies in the completeness of the list. Eight loves, arranged in an order that reads like a biography: *ābāʾ* (fathers — the past that made you), *abnāʾ* (sons — the future that continues you), *ikhwān* (brothers — the peers who stand beside you), *azwāj* (spouses — the intimates who complete you), *ʿashīrah* (tribe — the belonging that names you), *amwāl* (wealth acquired — your stored labor), *tijārah* (commerce you fear will stagnate — your flowing labor, with the fear attached that names the real object of anxiety), and *masākin* (dwellings you are pleased with — your shelter and taste made architecture). Between them, the eight cover ancestry, posterity, fraternity, intimacy, community, capital, enterprise, and home. There is no ninth. The verse is a mirror with no blind spots: somewhere in this list is the thing you love more than God, unless — the verse's conditional — there is not.

**"More Beloved to You Than God, His Messenger, and Striving"**

The counterweight is a triad: *mina Allāhi wa-rasūlihi wa-jihādin fī sabīlih* — God, His Messenger, and striving in His way. That the love of God is supreme belongs to the Shahādah itself. That the Messenger is named second — and loved *with* God in the same scale-pan — belongs to the architecture of faith, for the Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"None of you believes until I am more beloved to him than his father, his son, and all mankind."* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī (15) and Muslim (70) from Anas ibn Mālik)

And Anas also transmitted the companion teaching that makes the standard measurable:

> *"Three things whoever possesses them has found the sweetness of faith: that God and His Messenger be more beloved to him than anything else; that he love a person only for God's sake; and that he hate to return to disbelief after God has rescued him from it, as he would hate to be thrown into the Fire."* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī (16) and Muslim from Anas)

That *striving* is named third — a deed set alongside God and His Messenger — is the sūrah's signature move: love is proven in expenditure, and the expenditure God has been commanding since verse 13 is the assay of the heart's ranking. The Prophet's love-principle and this verse are two sides of one scale.

**"Wait Till God Comes With His Command"**

The sentence for a failed audit is chilling in its minimalism: *fa-tarabbaṣū ḥattā yaʾtiya Allāhu bi-amrih* — "then wait till God comes with His command." No epithet, no specified punishment — just the announcement that a verdict is in motion. Al-Ṭabarī and others associate the coming command with the conquests and the events of year 9, the season in which the ranking of loves was made brutally practical; but the clause retains its open grammar in every age: God *does* come with His commands — in the form of circumstances that force the ranking every soul has been deferring. The parent who would not choose, the trader who could not choose, is eventually *helped* to choose by the arrival of a day on which the eight loves and the Beloved are suddenly on the same table, and one must go. The verse's "wait" is the interval between a suppressed audit and an administered one.

**The Command Without Crushing the Affections**

Honesty about this verse requires noting what it does *not* command. It does not command the cessation of love for fathers, sons, spouses, or homes; the Qur'an legislates tenderness within every one of those bonds and nowhere treats them as evil. It commands *ranking*, and ranking is only visible when loves compete — which is exactly what the list of eight is: loves in competition with their Maker. Al-Ghazālī would later define the sickness this verse diagnoses as itḥād — the pouring of the heart's infinite capacity for love into vessels too small to hold it; the cure is not emptiness but *order*, loving each creature as a gift of the Giver rather than as a rival to Him. And the closing clause — *wa-Anhā lā yahdī al-qawma al-fāsiqīn* — names the stakes: a heart whose loves are ranked by appetite rather than by God has left the guided road, and guidance is the one commodity that cannot be purchased back with any of the eight. The believer's daily use of verse 24 is the simplest of exams: for each of the eight, ask — *if this and God conflicted today, which would I keep?* — and thank God for every answer that surprises in the right direction, and repair, quickly, every answer that does not.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:25

> **God indeed granted you victory on many a field, and on the Day of Ḥunayn when you were impressed at how numerous you were. But it availed you naught, and the earth, despite its breadth, closed in upon you. Then you turned your backs.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Battle That Taught the Lesson of Numbers**

Verse 25 recalls the single most instructive defeat-turned-victory of the Prophet's career: Ḥunayn, fought in Shawwāl of 8 AH, weeks after the bloodless conquest of Makkah. The tribes of Ḥawāzin and Thaqīf, hardened highlanders of the Ṭāʾif region, had mustered — the sources speak of some twenty thousand, with their families and herds massed behind them — to strike before the new order consolidated. The Prophet ﷺ marched to meet them with the largest army Islām had fielded: twelve thousand by most accounts, Madinans and Makkans together, including recent converts and some Qurashīs not yet Muslim who fought for their city. The verse's confession is the heart of the lesson: *ʿajabatkum kathratukum* — "you were impressed at how numerous you were." Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī record that some in the army said, *"We shall never this day be defeated for lack of numbers"* — a sentence that converted a strength into a theology, and the theology into an idol. The valley of Ḥunayn, with its narrow defiles, was the perfect classroom for the correction: the Hawāzin ambush came down the slopes in the dawn light, the vanguard broke, and twelve thousand discovered that arithmetic is not a lord.

**The Earth That Narrowed**

The verse's psychology is exact: *wa-lam tughni ʿankum shayʾan* — "it availed you naught" — *wa-ḍāqat ʿalaykumu al-arḍu bimā raḥubat* — "and the earth, despite its breadth, narrowed upon you." The same idiom will return in verse 118 for the three left behind: constriction *within* vastness is the Qur'an's signature image for inner collapse. At Ḥunayn the image was physical and psychological at once: a retreating mass compressed into a slope too narrow to hold it, men who could not turn, could not see, could not hear commands above the din. Al-Ṭabarī notes the paradox the verse underlines with *bimā raḥubat* — "despite its breadth": the ground had not shrunk; the souls had. Panic is a narrowing of possibility inside an unchanged world — a truth every anxious person rediscovers. And the final clause, *thumma wallaytum mudbirīn* — "then you turned your backs" — is stated in the second person plural, about the believers themselves: the Qur'an trains its own community's memory on its worst hour, refusing to let victory narratives be written by the victors' flatterers.

**What the Day Proved**

The event proved — and the verse exists to keep proving — that victory was never a function of numbers, wealth, or even the justice of the cause alone; it is given by God, through means He chooses, to hearts He sustains. The Qur'an had taught the doctrine before Ḥunayn:

> *"How many a small company has overcome a great company by God's leave! And God is with the patient."* (Qur'an 2:249; cf. 8:65–66)

Ḥunayn taught it experientially: the same army, disordered by self-confidence, was nothing; regrouped around the Prophet ﷺ (as verse 26 will recount), it was unstoppable. Ibn Kathīr draws the standing rule: reliance on numbers is not planning but shirk in miniature — a covert transfer of trust from the Giver to the gift. Armies, endowments, memberships, and audiences are all Ḥunayn's number: good to have, fatal to trust.

**"Many a Field": The Ledger of Grace**

The verse opens by framing Ḥunayn inside a series — *la-naṣarakumu Allāhu fī mawāḍiʿa kathīrah* — "God indeed granted you victory on many a field" — and the framing is itself doctrine. Badr's three hundred against a thousand; the survival at Uḥud despite the reverse on the mountain; the Trench, where a confederacy of ten thousand dissolved without a full battle; Khaybar's forts; Makkah's gates opened by negotiation and maneuver. The believers are reminded that the pattern of help long preceded Ḥunayn, so that the panic of that morning could not be blamed on God's absence — He had been demonstrably present at every prior field; what was absent, for one terrible hour, was the community's memory. The verse's placement is strategic: it comes as the sūrah prepares to demand the Tabūk march, the most expensive expedition yet, against the world's remaining superpower. Before asking for trust, God files the evidence: you have seen, on many a field, whose the victory is. The believer facing his own Ḥunayns — the moment when the assets fail and the world narrows — holds the same file.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:26

> **Then God sent down His Tranquility upon His Messenger and upon the believers, and sent down hosts whom you saw not, and punished those who disbelieved. And that is the recompense of the disbelievers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Counterattack That Came From Above**

Verse 26 narrates the turning of Ḥunayn, and the first agent of the turn is not a tactic but a descent: *anzala Allāhu sakīnatahu ʿalā rasūlihi wa-ʿalā al-muʾminīn* — "God sent down His tranquility upon His Messenger and upon the believers." *Sakīnah* — from the root s-k-n, stillness, settling, the word behind maskan (dwelling) — is the Qur'an's technical term for the divine calm that descends into hearts at the extremity: promised at Ḥudaybiyah (48:4, 48:18), sent down in the cave of the Hijrah (9:40), and here, on a slope running with broken men. What the rout had scattered was not primarily formation but *composure*; what God restored was composure itself, and the formations followed. The reports — Ibn Kathīr relays them from al-Wāqidī's sources and the sīrah — describe the Prophet ﷺ on his white mule, refusing to retreat, calling the broken units back: *"To me, O servants of God! I am the Messenger of God, no lie — I am the son of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib."* His uncle al-ʿAbbās, a man of legendary voice, planted himself and roared the call to the Anṣār and the Muhājirūn — *"Yā aṣḥāba al-Samurah!"* — until the human wave reversed.

**Hosts That Were Not Seen**

The second descent is military: *wa-anzala junūdan lam tarawhā* — "and He sent down hosts you saw not." The unseen armies are the angels, the auxiliary corps of Badr (3:124–126; 8:9–12), present here again in whatever mode God willed. The tradition debated their function — al-Rāzī, cited in the source commentary, speaks for the majority when he explains that the angels' chief warfare is the *casting of firm inspirations into hearts*; the enemy survivors' own testimony, as al-Rāzī relays, spoke of white-faced men who struck terror long after the battle. The theology is precise and refuses superstition: the believers were not excused from regrouping, calling, and fighting — the human counterattack was real and costly — but the *slope* of events, the terror and the reversal, belonged to Another. As at Badr, the Qur'an's formula holds: *"You did not slay them, but God slew them"* (8:17).

**The Recompense Clause**

The verse closes with judicial restraint: *wa-dhālika jazāʾu al-kāfirīna* — "and that is the recompense of the disbelievers." Not "of the Hawāzin" but of *the disbelievers* — the specific defeat is filed under a universal law: aggression against the community of faith meets, sooner or later, a recompense that its planners did not budget. The Qur'an states the law with the same grammar at 58:5, 59:4? and repeatedly in this sūrah (9:90, 9:101). And the law cuts both ways for its readers: the same God who reversed Ḥunayn in an hour permits His community's losses in other hours for accounts He keeps — as Uḥud had already taught. The believer's confidence is never in the invincibility of his side, but in the *governance* of all sides by the Recompenser.

**Tranquility as a Weapon**

For the modern believer, verse 26's deepest teaching is its first clause: at the hinge of catastrophe, the decisive reinforcement was *stillness*. Every crisis worker, pilot, surgeon, and parent knows the phenomenon in miniature — the moment when panic is the enemy and calm is the counterattack — and the Qur'an names calm's ultimate Source. Sakīnah is not stoic self-hypnosis; it is *sent down*, a gift to those who have done what can be done and stand at the edge of what cannot. The Prophet ﷺ taught his community to ask for it directly in the anxiety prayer of his Sunnah. And the verse's application radiates: to the family in a hospital corridor, the founder in a collapsing market, the believer isolated for his faith in a hostile room. The hosts you do not see are not a metaphor; they are a doctrine — and the tranquility that regroups broken men is still being sent down, onto messengers of patience and onto believers, on every field where the earth narrows and the turning has not yet come.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:27

> **Then thereafter God will relent unto whomsoever He will. And God is Forgiving, Merciful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Mercy Pasted Directly After the Wreckage**

Verse 27 is one sentence, placed with deliberate shock-value: *thumma yatūbu Allāhu min baʿdu dhālika ʿalā man yashāʾ* — "then, after that, God relents unto whomsoever He will." "That" is the punishment of verse 26 — the rout of Ḥawāzin, the dead on the slope, the captives by the thousands. And the very next divine act, before the burial parties have finished, is *tawbah* — turning, the sūrah's name-word, returning as it has returned in verses 3, 11, and 15. The refrain is now unmistakable as a structural principle of the sūrah: judgment, then relenting; judgment, then relenting. Al-Qurṭubī notes the historical fulfillment: the mass of the Hawāzin captives — some six thousand souls with their herds — were released by the Prophet ﷺ at al-Jiʿrānah when their deputation came as Muslims, restored entire to their kin, the Prophet returning even his own fifth-share of the spoils to them; and the Thaqīf of Ṭāʾif, besieged and unbowed that autumn, entered Islām within the year and produced among their sons the conqueror of ʿUmar's Persia and the transmitter of a large share of the ḥadīth corpus — al-Mughīrah ibn Shuʿbah, ʿUrwah ibn al-Zubayr's lineage aside, and ʿUthmān ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ among them. The people of verse 26 became the community of verse 27. The sequence is the sūrah's gospel in two verses.

**"Whomsoever He Will": The Sovereignty of Mercy**

The clause *ʿalā man yashāʾ* — "unto whom He will" — guards the doctrine on both flanks. Against presumption: no survivor, not even a defeated one, may bank on mercy as a wage; relenting is sovereign, and the defeated hearts who met it at al-Jiʿrānah met it as repentant Muslims, not as lucky casualties. Against despair: the door's latch is in no human hand; no priest, no council, no victor's verdict decides whom God may still receive — and the Qur'an names among the received, in this very sūrah, men who built a rival mosque to split the believers (vv. 107–110) and men who stayed behind and lied about it (vv. 117–118), categories every pious opinion of that era had already written off. Al-Rāzī observes that the combination of "after that" (punishment executed) with "whom He will" (mercy retained) is the exact equilibrium the sūrah holds throughout: history's sentences are real, and no sentence is final but God's last one.

**The Two Names That End the Movement**

The verse closes — as verse 5 closed the Sword Verse, as verse 3's invitation closed the proclamation — with *Ghafūrun Raḥīm*: Forgiving, Merciful. The pairing is the Qur'an's most frequent divine signature, and its recurrence at precisely the points of maximum severity is the Book's most eloquent self-interpretation. *Ghafūr* is the One whose forgiveness *covers* (from the same root as the warrior's helmet, mighfar, that covers the head); *Raḥīm* is the One whose mercy is vital, responsive, the womb-rooted raḥmah intensified. Together they teach the reader how to read every hard verse in the passage: the severity is an instrument inside a mercy that surrounds it. The Prophet ﷺ, who received these verses, had already been taught their balance in a divine saying he transmitted:

> *"My mercy precedes My wrath"* — in another wording, *"My mercy prevails over My wrath."* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī (7404) and Muslim (2751) from Abū Hurayrah, in the ḥadīth of the divine night-descent)

**After Punishment, What?**

The pastoral power of verse 27 for the individual believer is in its first word: *thumma* — "then, thereafter." There is an *after*. The defeated, the punished, the author of his own disaster — the verse promises a season on the far side of the wreckage in which relenting is not only possible but divinely initiated ("God relents," not "man repents" — the divine verb comes first, as it will again in verse 118: *"He relented to them that they might repent"*). The believer buried under the consequences of his own Ḥunayn — a ruined reputation, a broken covenant, years of a chosen wrong — reads verse 27 as the sūrah's personal address to him: the account is not closed by the verdict of circumstance; *then, thereafter*, God relents toward whom He will; and He is Forgiving, Merciful. The one thing the verse cannot coexist with is the heart that, offered the turning, keeps its back turned — for that heart verse 28 now waits.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:28

> **O you who believe! The idolaters are surely unclean; so let them not come near the Sacred Mosque after this year of theirs. If you fear poverty, God will enrich you from His Bounty if He will. Truly God is Knowing, Wise.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ritual Purity and the Geography of the Ḥaram**

Verse 28 legislates the custody of the Ḥaram — the sacred precinct of Makkah — and does so with a word that belongs to the technical vocabulary of Islamic ritual law: *innamā al-mushrikūna najasun* — "the idolaters are surely unclean." *Najas* is the term of legal impurity — the state that prevents a person from praying or touching the muṣḥaf until it is removed by the prescribed washing. The commentators are careful with the metaphor: al-Rāzī notes the construction is "an uncleanliness," meaning *characterized by* it, and reads it in the register of creed rather than hygiene — as the source commentary summarizes his position, the idolaters were to be shunned as impure things are shunned, because their false belief is, at the spiritual level, a kind of filth; the spiritual uncleanness is the meaning, the ritual word is the figure. The pre-Islamic pilgrimage had included practices — the circumambulation naked, the idols, the trade fairs of the sacred months — that had made the precinct a place where the House built for the worship of the One had become the headquarters of the many. The verse ends that dispensation: *fa-lā yaqrabū al-masjida al-ḥarāma baʿda ʿāmihim hādhā* — "let them not come near the Sacred Mosque after this year of theirs" — the year 9 AH, when Abū Bakr led the ḥajj and ʿAlī proclaimed the sūrah at Minā.

**The Scope Disputed, the Principle Shared**

The jurists divided over scope. Some — the majority application through the centuries — limited the restriction to the Kaʿbah and its ḥaram; others would bar non-Muslims from any mosque whatsoever, a minority position al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī report without endorsing; and the tradition's practice ran the gamut from prohibition to permission, including the Prophet's own reception of Christian delegations — the people of Najrān — *inside his mosque in Madinah*, where he lodged them and debated them and, at their request, allowed them to pray toward the east in his presence. The distinction most jurists drew is instructive: the Ḥaram is a sanctuary whose custody is creedal (the ground of vv. 17–19), while an ordinary mosque is a house of invitation whose doors open to the seeker (the ground of v. 6). The two policies — custody and invitation — have always lived side by side in the law, and honest commentary records both.

**The Economics of Obedience**

The verse's middle clause is one of the most economically candid moments in the Qur'an: *wa-in khiftum faqlatan* — "if you fear poverty" — *fa-sayughnīkumu Allāhu min faḍlihi in shāʾa* — "God will enrich you from His Bounty if He will." The fear was concrete and rational: the ḥajj economy of Makkah fed the city; the pilgrims of the peninsula came with trade, gifts, and custom, and barring the idolaters seemed, to the merchants of the new order, like sawing off the branch. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī record the concern, and the jurists' discussion of the *jizyah* on the treaty peoples as a compensating revenue; and history delivered its own commentary: within a decade the pilgrimage had grown beyond anything the idolaters' fairs had known, and the lands opened to the community made Makkah's old trade anxieties a historical curiosity. The Qur'an's answer to economic fear is not a lecture on faith but a promise from the Owner of the means: obey first, and the ledger will be supervised by the One who fills it. The pattern recurs through the Book:

> *"And whoever is mindful of God, He will make a way out for him, and provide for him from where he does not reckon."* (Qur'an 65:2–3)

**Knowing, Wise: The Signature on a Costly Rule**

The verse closes with *ʿAlīmun Ḥakīm* — Knowing, Wise — and this time the signature is a direct answer to the anxiety it follows. The rule was economically frightening; the Rule-maker *knows* the fear is real (ʿAlīm) and *governs* the consequences wisely (Ḥakīm). For the modern believer, verse 28's permanent teaching lives in that middle clause. Every generation of believers faces its own version of the ḥajj economy — a income stream, a market access, a career path that seems to require something God has forbidden — and the verse supplies the exact shape of the test: the fear of poverty is *anticipated*, named, and answered with a promise, not dismissed. Trust, in the Qur'an, is never required to be blind; it is required to be *decided* — to act on the arithmetic of the Knowing and the Wise rather than the arithmetic of the fear. And the believer who makes that decision once usually discovers, on the far side of the year, what Makkah's merchants discovered: the Bounty that replaced the trade was never on any ledger they could have drawn.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:29

> **Fight those who believe not in God and in the Last Day, and who do not forbid what God and His Messenger have forbidden, and who follow not the Religion of Truth among those who were given the Book, till they pay the jizyah with a willing hand, being humbled.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse in Its Historical Slot**

Verse 29 is, with verse 5, one of the two most discussed verses of the sūrah, and it must first be located. The context is the Byzantine frontier: the sources and the sūrah's own sequence point to the expedition to Tabūk in Rajab of 9 AH. The Byzantine empire, having just crushed Persia and reclaimed the Levant under Heraclius, was the superpower on the Muslims' northern horizon, and reports reached the Prophet ﷺ that its Arab client-tribes — Ghassān, Lakhm, Judham, ʿĀmilah — were being marshaled against the new polity. The verse addresses the political reality of the People of the Book in that world: communities organized under their own creeds and empires, on the other side of a hardening frontier. Al-Rāzī, who devotes more attention to this verse than any other classical commentator, immediately raises the interpretive problem the modern reader also feels: the Qur'an itself affirms that Jews and Christians *do* claim belief in God and the Last Day —

> *"Among the People of the Book is an upright community who recite God's signs in the watches of the night, prostrating. They believe in God and the Last Day, enjoin right and forbid wrong, and hasten unto good deeds."* (Qur'an 3:113–114)

— and praises the Christians' priests and monks (5:82). His resolution, which the source commentary preserves, is that the verse's description fits those among them who *in practice* reject what God and His Messenger forbade and set their political loyalty against the community, while the legal status it establishes — treaty and tribute — applied as a *category* to the Book-communities as such, without asserting their individual creeds false. The fight, in other words, addresses a political relationship, and the verse itself defines its own off-ramp: not conversion, but *treaty*.

**Jizyah: The Content of the Off-Ramp**

The verse's exit condition — *ḥattā yuʿṭū al-jizyata ʿan yadin wa-hum ṣāghirūn* — "till they pay the jizyah from a willing hand, being humbled" — is the charter of the dhimmah system, and every word of it was litigated by the jurists. *Jizyah* (from j-z-y, to requite, compensate) is a tribute in requital for protection: the non-Muslim treaty-community (*ahl al-dhimmah*, "the people of the covenant" — *dhimmah* being, as verse 8 established, "that which sets up an obligation") paid it in lieu of the two obligations Muslims bore — zakāt and military service — and in exchange received the community's protection of their lives, property, and worship. The amounts were modest and means-tested; the poor, women, children, the elderly, monks and the disabled were exempt; the poor dhimmī was, according to the practice reported of ʿUmar, *supported from the treasury* when old age stranded him — the same ʿUmar who, asked to soften the name, reportedly agreed to call it *ṣadaqah*, charity. The Prophet ﷺ concluded precisely such treaties — with the Christians of Najrān, the Zoroastrians of Bahrain, the Jews of Khaybar — and his letter to the people of Najrān, preserved in the sources, guarantees them protection of their churches, clergy, and customs. On *ʿan yad*, "from a willing hand," the commentators split elegantly: direct payment without intermediary and without delay, or — al-Qurṭubī and al-Zamakhsharī — generously received, since the tribute *averted* war and was therefore itself a kindness. And on *ṣāghirūn*, "humbled," the juristic conscience of the tradition spoke: al-Nawawī records that the Prophet ﷺ and the Rightly-Guided Caliphs never humiliated anyone in its collection, and that it was to be received gently, as a debt is received — the "humbling" being, in al-Rāzī's reading, simply the legal reality of being the protected, non-levied party rather than a prescribed ritual of degradation. Abrogated rudeness is not law; the practice of the Prophet *is*.

**The System Measured by Its Century**

A fair reading requires measuring the dhimmah system against its tenth-century-BC context: a world in which the conquered were enslaved, exiled, or killed as a matter of course, and "toleration" meant the right to pay for survival. Against that baseline, a system that guaranteed worship, internal autonomy, exemption from military service, and protection of life and law — and that the conquered could exit at any time by the creed of the majority — was a genuine innovation, and the Jewish communities that fled Byzantine persecution for Islamic Spain, and the Christian communities that persist to this day across the Middle East, are its historical receipts. Measured against modern citizenship ideals, the system's inequality is real and need not be defended as timeless; the jurists themselves, as the source commentary notes, treated its conditions as circumstances of security in a world of empires, not as articles of creed. What the verse *does* fix as creed is the two principles the sūrah has been building since verse 1: force is governed by treaty-law with defined off-ramps, and the lives of those in covenant are inviolable — the principle the Prophet ﷺ sealed in the harshest terms:

> *"Whoever kills a person under covenant will not smell the fragrance of Paradise, though its fragrance is found from a journey of forty years."* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī (6914) from ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr)

**Reading Verse 29 With the Whole Sūrah**

The verse cannot be responsibly read alone, and the sūrah itself forbids the attempt. Verse 6 ordered protection for the idolater seeking an audience; verse 13 defined the fighting community's enemy by treachery and first-strike; verse 29's own text subordinates the fighting to a *terminable* condition (the treaty), and the verses on either side (28, 30) address creedal disputes in words, not arms. The classical jurists read it exactly so: as the foundation of a *legal regime of peace* with the Book-communities — a regime of obligation, exemption, and protection that made the Islamic world, for most of its history, a shared house of many faiths under one covenant. The modern misuse of verse 29 — by extremists as a license, by polemicists as a proof-text — shares a single methodological flaw: both quote the verse against the system the verse created. The believer's task is the older one: to read the verse as the jurists read it, inside the treaty architecture of the sūrah and the practice of the Prophet, and to draw from it its permanent teaching — that between faith-communities the Qur'an's preferred end-state is not conversion by force (forbidden in the verse's own sibling, 2:256) but *covenant*: obligation, protection, and peace with a receipt.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:30

> **The Jews say that Ezra is the son of God, and the Christians say that the Messiah is the son of God. Those are words from their mouths. They resemble the words of those who disbelieved before. God curse them! How they are perverted!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Claim Attributed, A Claim Explained**

Verse 30 turns from the political relationship with the Book-communities to their creeds, and opens with a report that has required careful commentary in every age: *wa-qālati al-yahūdu ʿUzayrun ibnu Allāhi* — "the Jews say Ezra is the son of God." Al-Ṭabarī and al-Rāzī both address the obvious difficulty — normative Judaism has no such doctrine — with the reports that the formula was the claim of a party among the Jews of Madinah: one account names Finḥāṣ ibn ʿĀzūrāʾ; others say a group held it in imitation of the Christians' "son of God" language, or elevated ʿUzayr (Ezra) because of his unique role in their history — the man credited with restoring the Torah after the exile, so venerated that some later Rabbinic texts compare him to Moses ("the face of Moses shines like the sun, the face of Ezra like the moon," as the tradition the commentators cite puts it). Al-Rāzī adds the speculative but humane reading: honorific titles, hardened by sectarian polemic, become literal claims — exactly the process he supposes happened with the Christians' "son" for Jesus. The Qur'an attributes the claim to speakers in its own audience ("that is their saying with their mouths"), and its critique is aimed at the formula wherever it is actually held, not at every Jew of every century — a distinction the classical commentators made and the modern reader must keep.

**Words of the Mouth Only**

The verse's anatomy of the error is its most incisive clause: *dhālika qawluhum bi-afwāhihim* — "those are words from their mouths." The phrase echoes 48:26's contrast between the lip's confession and the heart's faith, and makes the epistemological point al-Qurṭubī explicates: these are formulas *with no evidential substance*, sounds that do not correspond to any knowable reality — inherited language rather than verified truth. The Qur'an's standard for creed is not sincerity of utterance but correspondence to what has actually been *revealed and demonstrated*, and sonship-claims, it insists, belong to the category of inherited slogans: *yuḍāhiʾūna qawla alladhīna kafarū min qablu* — "they resemble the words of those who disbelieved before" — the deity-families of Egypt and Mesopotamia, the divine sons and daughters of the pre-Islamic Arabs (53:19–23), the demigods of the nations. The Qur'an sees one ancient grammar behind them all: the desire to put a mediator between the creature and the Creator, a son (or daughter) to intercede, to humanize the transcendent, to make God approachable by making Him smaller. It refuses the trade everywhere it finds it.

**"God Curse Them": Understanding the Imprecation**

*Qātalahumu Allāhu* — literally "may God fight them!" — is, as the source commentary notes, an interjection that had hardened in Arabic into an exclamation of astonishment and dismay, not a literal prayer for battle (al-Qurṭubī and al-Zamakhsharī compare its grammaticalization to similar frozen imprecations in every language). The dismay is explained by the closing clause: *annā yuʾfakūn* — "how they are perverted!" — the root ʾ-f-k denotes the twisting of a thing out of its true shape, the word used for the mirage that twists light, for the lie that twists speech. The Qur'an regards the sonship formula not as an innocent metaphor but as a *deformation*: it takes the pure monotheism of the prophets — the doctrine the verse 31 will say they were "commanded only" to hold — and bends it into the old polytheism wearing new names. The grief in the exclamation is audible: these are the heirs of Moses and of Jesus, custodians of great scriptures, and their theology is being graded by the standard of the scripture they inherited. The Qur'an's harshness toward *doctrines* is inseparable from its honor toward *persons* — the same Book calls the Christians "nearest in love to the believers" (5:82) and defends Jesus's mother from slander (4:156) — and the believer who quotes verse 30 must carry both halves of that balance, or he has quoted it dishonestly.

**The Doctrine Restated by Contrast**

Though the verse states the error, its corrective is the Qur'an's entire tawḥīd corpus, nowhere more compactly than in the sūrah of purity the Prophet ﷺ called equal to a third of the Qur'an:

> *"Say: He is God, One; God, the Eternal Refuge; He begets not, nor is He begotten; and none is comparable to Him."* (Qur'an 112:1–4)

For the believer reading verse 30 today, the verse functions less as ammunition against neighbors than as a mirror before inherited formulas of every kind — religious and secular: What do I believe *because it was demonstrated*, and what do I believe *because my circle has always said it*? The Qur'an's critique of "words from mouths" is a standing invitation to every community, Muslim included, to audit its own slogans. And in interreligious settings the verse teaches the believer to disagree about the exact place where the Qur'an disagrees — the definition of God — precisely, without extending the disagreement to the persons God has commanded him to honor and, where covenant allows, to protect.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:31

> **They have taken their rabbis and monks as lords apart from God, as well as the Messiah, son of Mary, though they were only commanded to worship one God. There is no god but He! Glory be to Him above the partners they ascribe.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Ḥadīth That Defines the Verse**

Verse 31's meaning was fixed by the Prophet ﷺ himself, in one of the most quoted exchanges in the tafsīr literature. ʿAdī ibn Ḥātim al-Ṭāʾī — a Christian convert from the tribe of Ṭayy', who came to Madinah wearing his silver cross — heard the verse and objected that his people had never *worshipped* their clergy. The Prophet ﷺ answered:

> *"Did they not forbid what God permitted, so you deemed it forbidden; and permit what God forbade, so you deemed it permitted?"* He said: "Yes." He said: *"That is your worship of them."* (Recorded by al-Tirmidhī (3095) from ʿAdī ibn Ḥātim; also transmitted by al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr in their commentaries; graded ṣaḥīḥ)

The ḥadīth is the master-key to the verse: *al-ḥāʾ*? No — the operative verb *ittakhadhū... arbāban* ("they took as lords") is defined, by the Prophet's own exegesis, not as liturgical adoration but as the *surrender of the moral law to human amendment*. The rabbis and monks became "lords" whenever their rulings overrode God's — whenever ḥalāl and ḥarām migrated from revelation's jurisdiction to the clergy's.

**Lordship as Legislation**

The definition makes the verse one of the Qur'an's most radical statements of tawḥīd, and Ibn Taymiyyah built on it a central argument of his theology: the essence of God's oneness is not only that He alone is prayed to, but that He alone legislates — that the authority to declare things obligatory, forbidden, clean, and unclean belongs to the Owner of the creation, and every transfer of that authority to any creature, priest or parliament or platform, is a species of the shirk this verse condemns. The verse's logic is easy to state: whoever fixes the boundaries of permission is, functionally, the god of the boundary. The Qur'an states the principle in a single rhetorical question elsewhere: *"Do they have partners who have legislated for them religion that God has not permitted?"* (42:21). And the verse's corrective is the creed compacted to seven words: *mā umirū illā li-yaʿbudū ilāhan wāḥidan* — "they were commanded only to worship one God" — followed by the Shahādah's first half itself: *lā ilāha illā Huwa* — "there is no god but He."

**The Balance the Verse Does Not Break**

Honest reading requires the verse's other edge, which the tradition was careful to preserve. The Qur'an elsewhere *commands* the asking of scholars — *"Ask the people of remembrance if you know not"* (16:43) — and the obedience of rightful authority — *"Obey God, and obey the Messenger and those in authority among you"* (4:59) — and it sends believers to learn their religion from those who know it. The sin of verse 31 is not *following* scholars, which the religion could not function without; it is the scholars' amendment of God's law and the laity's ratification of the amendment — the silent upgrade of interpretation into legislation, of exposition into overruling. The Prophet's own Sunnah polices the line he drew. He ﷺ forbade even the veneration of his own person from sliding into the Christian pattern, saying:

> *"Do not exaggerate in me as the Christians exaggerated concerning the son of Mary; I am His servant — so say: the servant of God and His Messenger."* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī from ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb)

**Messengers, Media, and the Modern Baalim**

For the modern reader, verse 31 is less about medieval clergy than about the permanent human instinct it exposes: outsourcing conscience. Every age has its rabbis and monks in the verse's sense — figures whose approval defines right and wrong for their followers: the influencer whose feed replaces the fiqh manual, the party whose platform replaces the ethic, the pundit whose verdicts are never checked against the Book, the corporate culture whose "values" quietly overrule the ten commandments of one's scripture. The test the Prophet ﷺ gave ʿAdī remains perfectly portable: *when they permitted what God forbade, did you deem it permitted?* — and its modern phrasings are uncomfortable to ask. The verse ends with the divine self-absolution: *subḥānahu ʿammā yushrikūn* — "Glory be to Him above what they ascribe" — the tanzīh that closes every discussion of God's nature: He remains what He is, undivided by the partitions men build. The believer's task under verse 31 is the daily re-integration the verse demands: to honor the teachers God sent, and to keep the legislation where the creed put it — with the One there is no god besides.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:32

> **They desire to extinguish the Light of God with their mouths. But God refuses to do aught but complete His Light, though the disbelievers be averse.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Blowing at the Sun**

Verse 32 is the Qur'an's most compact image of futility, and the tradition has always read it with a smile: *yurīdūna an yuṭfiʾū nūra Allāhi bi-afwāhihim* — "they desire to extinguish the Light of God with their mouths." The verb *aṭfaʾa* is what one does to a lamp — the breath-puff that ends a flame. The image gathers the entire campaign of verse 30's inherited formulas and verse 31's human legislations into one picture: people leaning over the lamp of revealed truth and *blowing at it*. What is the Light? The commentators list the layers: the Qur'an itself, the Prophet, the guidance and knowledge of God, the religion of tawḥīd (al-Rāzī compiles the readings). The mouths are the propaganda — the satirical verses, the ridicules, the diplomatic campaigns to strangle the message in its first decade. The mismatch is the meaning: breath against the sun. The classical commentators loved to extend the image: it is as if a man stood at dawn and blew at the rising light to hold back the day.

**God's Refusal Stated in Divine Grammar**

The second half of the verse is grammatically one of the most emphatic constructions in the Qur'an: *wa-yabā Allāhu illā an yutimma nūrahu* — "and God refuses except to complete His Light." The construction *wa-yabā... illā an* — "refuses except that" — is a rhetorical double negative that cancels every alternative: God has *excluded* every outcome except the completion. The verb *atamma* — to bring to fullness, to perfect — promises not merely survival but *consummation*: the light will not just keep burning; it will reach its appointed full extent. And the verse's final concession-clause — *wa-law kariha al-kāfirūn*, "though the disbelievers be averse" — appears again at the head of verse 33, welding the two verses into a single proclamation: history has a destination, and the destination is not neutral.

**The Lamp's Proof of Its Own Nature**

There is an argument inside the image. A light needs no defense against breath; it defends itself by being light. The Qur'an had already made the challenge explicit:

> *"And if you are in doubt concerning what We sent down upon Our servant, then bring a sūrah like it."* (Qur'an 2:23)

The verse's confidence, the commentators stress, is not the bravado of a faction but the self-evidence of a disclosure: the Qur'an's preservation, its inimitability, and the shape of the community that carried it are standing exhibits.
