And history has run the experiment repeatedly: the campaigns to extinguish the light — the boycott, the persecution, the wars, and in later centuries the burnings of manuscripts and the mockeries of every era — have consistently functioned as bellows. ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, who set out sword-in-hand to end the new faith, became its most famous reciter; the letter of the Quraysh to the Negus, seeking extradition of a handful of refugees, ended with a king weeping over a sūrah and sheltering the faith in his kingdom. The verse is not a prediction that Muslims will never suffer; it is a promise about the *light itself* — that no mouth, however mighty, breathes hard enough to finish it.

**What the Verse Asks of the Believer**

For the believer, verse 32 is a doctrine of stamina. Every serious Muslim life encounters its own version of the breath: the mockery of a secular environment, the campaigns of hostile media, the quiet suffocation of family disapproval, the louder temptations of one's own doubts. The verse's teaching is not that the believer must *be* the light's bodyguard — the light has a Guarantor whose refusal is grammatical fact — but that the believer must not mistake the blowing for the outcome. Al-Rāzī notes the pastoral corollary: the community that believes verse 32 does not answer mockery with panic, because panic is the behavior of people whose lamp is actually in danger. The believer's tasks are the ordinary ones — recite, convey, embody, plant — and the completion belongs to God. The same sun has been rising on every century's attempt to blow it out; the averse have changed, the aversion has not, and neither has the Light.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:33

> **He it is Who sent His Messenger with guidance and the Religion of Truth to make it prevail over all religion, though the idolaters be averse.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Sentence Inscribed on History**

Verse 33 is among the most consequential sentences in the Qur'an — so consequential that the Ottomans inscribed it, alongside 61:9, on the mihrab of Ayasofya, and the Tradition counts its repetitions across the Book. *Huwa alladhī arsala rasūlahu bil-hudā wa-dīni al-ḥaqqi li-yuẓhirahu ʿalā al-dīni kullihi* — "He it is Who sent His Messenger with guidance and the Religion of Truth, to make it prevail over all religion." The Prophet ﷺ embodied its confidence in his diplomacy as in his doctrine: the letters to the emperors of the world in the year of Tabūk — Heraclius of Byzantium, Chosroes of Persia, the Muqawqis of Egypt — each opening with the basmalah and closing with the same offer the sūrah's treaty-architecture has taught us to expect. To Heraclius he wrote, as al-Bukhārī records from Abū Sufyān's famous audience before the emperor:

> *"...Accept Islām, and you will be safe; accept Islām, and God will give you a double reward. But if you turn away, then upon you is the sin of the subjects."* (A paraphrase of the letter as recorded by al-Bukhārī (7) from the long ḥadīth of Abū Sufyān ibn Ḥarb)

The claim of verse 33 was thus carried, on parchment, into the courts of the two superpowers within the Prophet's lifetime — a claim not of imminent conquest but of destined manifestation.

**What "Prevail" Means: The Commentators' Debate**

The verb *ẓahara ʿalā* can mean to prevail over, but also simply to manifest, to make known, to bring into the open — and the commentators worked the range carefully, as the source commentary preserves. One school read the verse of victory within the Arabian Peninsula, fulfilled in the Prophet's lifetime — the Peninsula's idols fallen, its sanctuary restored, its tribes covenanting — and noted the modesty of the claim against what the empires still held. Another read *li-yuẓhirahu* as *manifestation*: the religion of truth will be *made known* to all religions — no part of it hidden — which complements the Qur'an's doctrine of the community as *"a middle community, witnesses over mankind"* (2:143). Al-Rāzī, with his philosopher's honesty, raises the strongest form of the problem: if the verse predicts universal political victory, it is — in his own day — visibly unfulfilled; and he refuses to make God's word a failed prophecy. His resolutions point forward: the manifestation reading; the eschatological reading — that the completion arrives at history's end, with the return of Jesus, son of Mary, whom the Qur'an and the Prophet's Sunnah place at the term of the age as a follower of Muḥammad ﷺ; the reading of the pronoun — "to make *him* known," the Prophet, or the religion's proofs, to all peoples. The verse, in other words, was treated by its own classical interpreters with more interpretive discipline than either its fanatical or its hostile modern readers grant it.

**The Mission's Structure: Guidance, Truth, Manifestation**

The verse names the Prophet's payload with precision: *al-hudā* — the guidance, the direction of the road — and *dīn al-ḥaqq* — the religion of truth, the system of ordered life built on what corresponds to reality. The pairing is deliberate: guidance without a social order remains private mysticism; an order without guidance becomes bureaucracy. The Prophet ﷺ delivered both — a Book and a city, a creed and a constitution — and the Qur'an defines the purpose of the whole delivery as *manifestation*: not the secrecy of the mystery-cults, not the ethnic enclosure of the tribal gods, but a religion designed from its first day to be *known* — recited aloud, written down, taught, witnessed, and offered. Al-Qurṭubī draws the practical line: because the religion's destiny is manifestation, the concealment of it is a betrayal of it — the believer is, by employment, a transmitter, and the verse is the job description behind every act of daʿwah from the Prophet's letters to a neighbor's answered question.

**Aversion on Record, Again**

The verse closes where verse 32 closed: *wa-law kariha al-mushrikūn* — "though the idolaters be averse." The repetition is the Qur'an's signature of constancy: the outcome is not hostage to consent. For the believer, verse 33 assigns a posture that is neither triumphalist nor timid: the community that believes its message is destined to be manifest does not despair in the seasons of obscurity — the Makkan years were obscurity itself, and the verse was believed *then* — and does not grasp at coercion, because manifestation by the mouth (v. 32's failed method) is not the divine method; guidance is. The Prophet's commission is defined by the Qur'an itself: *"We have not sent thee save as a bearer of glad tidings and a warner to all mankind"* (34:28). The verse 33 believer works in the daylight of that commission: plant, convey, witness — and leave the rising of the sun to its Owner.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:34

> **O you who believe! Verily many of the rabbis and monks consume the wealth of people falsely, and turn from the way of God. [As for] those who hoard gold and silver and spend it not in the way of God, give them glad tidings of a painful punishment,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Revelation Auditing the Religious Economy**

Verse 34 performs an operation rare in the history of religions: a scripture auditing the religious profession of *other* communities — and, by permanent implication, of its own. *Kathīrun mina al-aḥbāri wa-l-ruhbāni* — "many of the rabbis and monks" — *yaʾkulūna amwāla al-nāsi bil-bāṭil* — "consume the wealth of people falsely." The two offices named are the scholarly and the monastic leaderships of Jews and Christians; the verb *akala*, to eat, is the Qur'an's standard idiom for living off others' money, and the qualifier *bil-bāṭil* — by falsehood, without right — defines the crime: revenue extracted through religious authority rather than earned by religious service. Al-Qurṭubī catalogues the mechanisms the verse covers: dues and indulgences collected in God's name and spent on the collectors; judgments sold; teachings withheld or bent for pay — the whole economy the Gospels themselves thundered against when Jesus overturned the tables. Al-Rāzī, precise as always, notices the quantifier: *kathīrun* — many, not all — the verse itself protects the honest rabbi and the sincere monk from its own indictment, a distinction every reader must carry forward.

**Barred From the Way**

The second charge completes the first: *wa-yaṣuddūna ʿan sabīli Allāh* — "and they bar from the way of God." As at verse 9, the verb ṣadda carries the double sense — they themselves turn away, and they obstruct others — but here the obstruction is *commercial*: a religious economy whose interest lies in keeping the laity dependent, ignorant, and paying. The Qur'an states the underlying principle as a general law of corruption elsewhere: *"O you who believe, indeed many of the rabbis and monks eat the wealth of people in vanity, and bar from the way of God"* — this verse — and its sibling analysis in 2:174 and 3:187, where the People of the Book are rebuked for concealing scripture they were *commanded to make plain*. The logic is economic and obvious: a direct relationship between the soul and the revealed Book is bad for the intermediary's margins. The Qur'an's religion, by contrast, is structurally disintermediated: every believer is commanded to recite the Book himself, and the scholar's fee — in the Prophet's practice and the fiqh built on it — is not a toll on the road but a stipend from the public treasury.

**The Hoarders and the Tax of Light**

The verse's second sentence widens the indictment from clergy to laity: *wa-alladhīna yaknizūna al-dhahaba wa-l-fiḍḍata wa-lā yunfiqūnahā fī sabīli Allāhi* — "those who hoard gold and silver and spend them not in the way of God." The verb *kanaza* — to pile up, to store as treasure — names wealth *withdrawn from circulation*: not property in use but metal in a vault, wealth whose entire function has become its accumulation. The Qur'an does not condemn ownership; it condemns *stagnation* — money that neither feeds, equips, builds, nor frees. The zakāt is the revelation's structural answer: the annual levy of 2.5% that must leave the vault and enter the eight channels of verse 60, a circulatory system designed so that no believer's gold may sit still. The Prophet ﷺ attached the verse's warning to its successor (v. 35) in the starkest terms:

> *"Whoever is given wealth by God and does not pay its zakāt — his wealth will be made on the Day of Resurrection into a bald-headed viper with two black spots over its eyes, which will coil around him and seize his jaws, saying: I am your wealth, I am your treasure."* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī (1403) and Muslim (987) from Abū Hurayrah, in wording close to this)

**The Verse's Twin Mirrors**

For the modern believer, verse 34 holds two mirrors, and honesty requires looking into both. The first faces "them": the historical religious economies that the verse names, and every later version of them. The second faces "us" — because the Qur'an's own community was never exempt from the verse's categories, and the Prophet's warning about the viper was addressed to Muslims. Every religious institution that monetizes access to God, every teacher whose rulings track his donors, every hoard — personal or institutional — that stagnates while the channels of verse 60 run dry, stands inside verse 34's grammar. Al-Rāzī's quantifier (*many*, not all) keeps the indictment from becoming bigotry against an office; the verb (*eat falsely*) keeps it from becoming resentment of wealth as such. The verse draws one clean line, across all communities and centuries: religious authority and accumulated wealth exist to be *spent in the way of God* — and the moment their function becomes their own perpetuation, the "glad tidings of a painful punishment" (the verse's own bitter irony, the bashara of v. 3 returned) are filed in their name.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:35

> **on the Day when it will be heated in the Fire of Hell, and their foreheads, their sides, and their backs will be branded with it. "This is what you hoarded up for yourselves; so taste that which you hoarded."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Metal Returns to Its Maker**

Verse 35 completes verse 34's sentence with one of the Qur'an's most physically precise punishments: *yawma yuḥmā ʿalayhā fī nāri jahannama* — "the day it will be heated in the Fire of Hell" — the *it* being the gold and silver of verse 34. The verb *ḥamiya* is technical: the heating of metal to working temperature, the forge-heat at which branding iron becomes effective. Then the three-point branding: *fa-tukwā bihā jibāhuhum wa-junūbuhum wa-ẓuhūruhum* — foreheads, sides, backs. The imagery answers to the hoarder's biography: the forehead that counted and worried, the side that slept restlessly on the ledger, the back that bowed over the vault and never bowed in prayer. And the voice-over is the verdict's cruelest accuracy: *hādhā mā kanaztum li-anfusikum* — "this is what you hoarded for yourselves" — the treasure they hid from the world arrives on the Day *attached to them*, transformed from their security into their instrument. The theological principle is the Qur'an's doctrine of punished-by-what-you-loved:

> *"Let not those who withhold what God has given them of His bounty think that it is good for them; rather it is bad for them. What they withheld will be hung around their necks on the Day of Resurrection."* (Qur'an 3:180)

**Why the Sensory Detail Matters**

The Qur'an's Hereafter-punishments are not arbitrary cruelties; they are *disclosures* — the true nature of a choice rendered visible and tactile. Al-Qurṭubī, collecting the verses of "tasting" (*dhūqū*, the verse's closing imperative: "taste"), notes that the word always marks *direct experience*: no mediator, no abstraction — the hoarder does not merely learn that hoarding was ruinous; he experiences the ruin in the organ of his hoarding. The brand is also, in the tradition's reading, the great *equalizer* of the fantasy of wealth: metal that commanded armies and bought judges turns out to have one final market price — the flesh of its owner. The same inversion runs through the parable of the two men and the storm-destroyed garden (18:32–42); the pattern is constant: wealth trusted apart from God is a lien against the soul.

**The Prophet's Economy of the Best Wealth**

The Companions heard verses 34–35 and asked the practical question every serious believer asks: *what, then, should we own?* The tradition preserves the Prophet's answer, transmitted in the commentary of al-Tirmidhī and cited by the classical mufassirūn on this verse:

> *"The best wealth is a tongue that remembers God much, and a heart that is thankful, and a believing wife who helps her husband in his religion."* (Recorded by al-Tirmidhī, and cited by the classical commentators on this verse, from the Companions who asked which wealth they should seek after hearing these verses)

The answer relocates wealth entirely: from metal that can be hoarded to capacities that only *circulate* — a tongue that remembers (speech, spent not stored), a heart that thanks (gratitude as continuous expenditure), a partnership that multiplies faith. None of the three can be vaulted; all of them depreciate with disuse — the exact economics of the verse's implicit definition of real wealth.

**Taste, Not Merely Hear**

The verse's last word is its method: *fa-dhūqū mā kuntum taknizūn* — "so taste what you used to hoard." Ibn Kathīr notes the imperfect verb again — a practice, a settled pattern, not an incident; and he notes the grammar of *for yourselves* — the hoarding was self-directed by definition, and the punishment merely completes the sentence the hoarder wrote: you kept it for yourself; now have it, at forge-heat, on the self. The believer's takeaway is not asceticism — the Qur'an elsewhere blesses wealth righteously earned and spent, and the whole zakāt system of this sūrah is its standing proof — but a nightly audit of function: does my money *do* anything in the way of God, or does it merely sit, gathering the temperature of its future? Verse 35's furnace is not a metaphor; it is a deadline. And the eight channels of verse 60, which the sūrah will soon open, are the standing escape route — the revelation's own way of un-branding the believer's balance sheet before the Day does.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:36

> **Truly the number of months in the Eyes of God is twelve months, [laid down] in the Book of God, the day He created the heavens and the earth. Of them four are sacred. That is the upright religion. So wrong not yourselves during them. And fight the idolaters all together, just as they fight you all together. And know that God is with the reverent.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Time as Divine Legislation**

Verse 36 legislates something most moderns never imagine as legislable: the calendar itself. *Inna ʿiddata al-shuhūri ʿinda Allāhi ithnā ʿashara shahran fī kitābi Allāhi* — "truly the number of months with God is twelve, in the Book of God" — *yawma khalaqa al-samāwāti wa-al-arḍa* — "the day He created the heavens and the earth." The claim is not that the Arabs miscounted; it is that the calendar is not a human convention at all. Twelve lunar months is the architecture of time *as created* — the same creation-day language the Qur'an uses for laws it declares built into the cosmos (cf. 41:12, the ordering of the heavens "in two days" of creation-idiom). The month (*shahr*), whose root names the moon's appearance, is a created rhythm; and God, having created the rhythm, retains its legislation. The Prophet ﷺ received the full calendar-text in his Farewell Pilgrmage address, as Abū Bakrah transmitted:

> *"O people! Truly time has come round to the state it was in the day God created the heavens and the earth. The year is twelve months, of which four are sacred: three consecutive — Dhū al-Qaʿdah, Dhū al-Ḥijjah, and Muḥarram — and Rajab of Muḍar, which falls between Jumādā and Shaʿbān."* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī (3197) and Muslim (1679) from Abū Bakrah)

**The Four Sacred Months and the Upright Religion**

The four sacred months — Dhū al-Qaʿdah, Dhū al-Ḥijjah, Muḥarram, Rajab — were a pre-Islamic inheritance the Qur'an confirmed rather than invented: a seasonally distributed ceasefire system in which war ceased, blood-feuds froze, and the pilgrimage and its markets could move in safety across a peninsula without any police power to enforce it. The Qur'an anchors the system in revelation — *dhālika al-dīnu al-qayyimu* — "that is the upright religion" — the same adjective (qayyim, standing straight, unmoving from its appointed course) that the Qur'an uses of its own creed (98:5). Al-Qurṭubī draws the comparison the adjective invites: as tawḥīd is the upright frame of the soul, the sacred calendar is the upright frame of the year; tamper with either and the structure leans. The ethical command follows: *fa-lā taẓlimū fīhinna anfusakum* — "so wrong not yourselves during them" — and the commentators divide, as the source commentary records, over whether *fīhinna* ("in them") means during the four sacred months or across all twelve; the majority who restrict it to the four read with al-Ṭabarī the special sanctity of sacred time: sin in a sanctuary — of place or of season — compounds the crime by the address.

**Collective Defense in Collective Grammar**

The verse's middle command is often skimmed: *wa-qātilū al-mushrikīna kaffatan kamā yuqātilūnakum kaffah* — "and fight the idolaters *all together*, just as they fight you *all together*." The adverb *kaffah* — as one body, in concert — carries the military sociology: the enemy's asset was confederation, the tribal web that assembled coalitions; the believers' answer must be an equal concert of arms. Al-Rāzī reads the clause as a principle of unity in defense; and the jurists built on it the rule that when the community is attacked wholesale, defense is wholesale — the community's obligation rising to the scale of the threat. But the verse's own final clause is the leash that never comes off: *wa-iʿlamū anna Allāha maʿa al-muttaqīn* — "and know that God is with the reverent." Not with the fierce, not with the numerous (Ḥunayn, v. 25, settled that), but with the *muttaqīn* — the word the sūrah has used at verses 4, 7, and 36 to close its every discussion of war. The divine presence rides on ethics, and an army that loses the ethics loses the presence it thought it had.

**A Calendar Nobody Owns**

For the modern reader, verse 36's deepest teaching is its first sentence: time has an Owner. The Qur'an's refusal of intercalation (the point verse 37 will press) means the Islamic year belongs to no state, no market, and no season — the fast and the pilgrimage rotate through the solar year, and no latitude's convenience is privileged, a fairness the source commentary rightly notes: worship is equalized across the globe's long and short days over a lifetime. But the verse's reach is personal as well as astronomical: the believer's year has a revealed shape — months with names, months with sanctity, a rhythm of restraint and release — and the verse's closing question ("wrong not yourselves *in them*") extends to every calendar of a life: the days you treat as ordinary are God's days; the seasons you treat as yours are His seasons. The upright religion is upright in time as in everything else — and the person who learns to keep the sanctity of the sacred months has learned the art the whole calendar teaches: that some hours are set apart, and honoring them is not superstition but the straightening of the year itself.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:37

> **Truly the nasīʾ is but an increase in disbelief, whereby the disbelievers go astray. They make it lawful one year and forbid it another, in order to reconcile it with the number made sacred by God, thus making lawful that which God has forbidden. The evil of their deeds is made to seem fair unto them, but God guides not the disbelieving people.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Manipulation of Sacred Time**

Verse 37 names and condemns a specific technology of corruption: *al-nasīʾ* — from the root n-s-ʾ, to postpone, defer. The practice, as the commentators reconstruct it, was intercalation: the insertion of an extra month to pin the lunar year to the solar seasons — keeping the pilgrimage month, with its markets, in the season convenient for trade. The Arabs had no king and no calendar ministry; the authority to shift the months belonged to a man recognized for the office, and the shifting was politics: a sacred month could be declared "postponed" into profanity when a war or a harvest required it, and a profane month sacred in compensation — *yuḥillūnahu ʿāman wa-yuḥarrimūnahu ʿāman* — "they make it lawful one year and forbid it another." Al-Qurṭubī prefers the version of the practice that inserted whole days and months beyond the twelve — hence, he argues, the verse before this emphasizes that the number is *twelve*, not twelve plus an adjustment. Al-Ṭabarī records the variant: Muḥarram's sanctity postponed onto Ṣafar, or cycled month by month through the years. Whatever the exact mechanics, the crime is constant: **the realm of the sacred was being edited to serve the realm of the convenient.**

**An Increase in Disbelief**

The Qur'an's verdict is deliberately disproportionate to the seeming smallness of a calendar tweak: *inna-mā al-nasīʾu ziyādatun fī al-kufr* — "truly the nasīʾ is but an *increase* in disbelief." Why is moving a month theological murder? Because the sacred calendar is a revelation — a *farīḍah*, an appointed boundary (v. 36 anchored it in the creation itself) — and to move a boundary that God set is to claim the authority of God; it is verse 31's clerical lordship practiced with an almanac. Each postponement is also, in the Qur'an's grammar, a *ziyādah* — an addition: disbelief compounds by arithmetic; every small usurpation of divine legislation trains the usurper and acclimates the audience. And the verse names the engine of the whole economy in its most haunting clause: *zuyyina lahum suʾu aʿmālihim* — "the evil of their deeds was made to seem fair to them." The Qur'an's doctrine of *takhyīl* — the beautification of sin — holds that sustained wrongdoing eventually rebrands itself: the market logic ("the pilgrimage must fall after the harvest") makes the sacrilege look like sense, and the community forgets there was ever a question.

**The Closing of the Loop**

The verse's final clause is its most sobering: *wa-Anhā lā yahdī al-qawma al-kāfirīna* — "and God guides not the disbelieving people." Read carefully, it is not an arbitrary curse but the description of a completed circuit: they edited the guidance they had, so the guidance-system withdrew — *"We shall turn away their hearts and their eyes as they refused to believe in it the first time"* (6:110? cf. 7:146). The Qur'an's psychology of the hardened heart is a feedback loop: choice reshapes perception; perception authorizes the next choice. The nasīʾ-practitioners are the case study — people for whom the sacred had become fully negotiable, and for whom no month, in the end, meant anything but its price. Ibn Kathīr notes the historical seal: at Abū Bakr's pilgrimage in 9 AH, the manipulated months had cycled back to their true places — the Prophet's Ṣaḥīḥ-address words, "time has come round to the state it was in the day God created the heavens and the earth," were simultaneously a fact-report and a verdict: the era of human editing of divine time was over.

**Modern Nasīʾ in Softer Clothing**

The verse's permanent application is wider than calendars, because the technology is permanent: any restructuring of the sacred to serve the market. Every generation re-prices its boundaries — the day of rest that became a shopping day, the marriage covenant renegotiated to the terms of convenience, the moral law amended seasonally by whatever the audience will bear — and each amendment is defended in the same accent the pre-Islamic calendar-men used: *it is only an adjustment; the essence is untouched; everyone benefits.* The Qur'an's diagnosis stands: the first edit is an increase, and the fairest-seeming edits are usually the furthest gone. For the believer, verse 37 is a standing alarm about the place where his own religion quietly becomes negotiable — the ḥalāl that drifts to "circumstantial," the duty that becomes "seasonal" — and the sūrah's remedy is the one God actually gave: a calendar He fixed in the creation, months He named, and a Book whose boundaries no harvest may move.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:38

> **O you who believe! What ails you, that when it is said unto you, "Go forth in the way of God," you sink down heavily to the earth? Are you content with the life of this world over the Hereafter? Yet the enjoyment of the life of this world, compared with the Hereafter, is but a little.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question That Names a Disease**

With verse 38 the sūrah pivots from the nations outside to the hesitations inside, and its opening is a diagnostic exclamation: *mā lakum* — "what ails you?" — the Qur'an's formula for behavior that contradicts a known identity. The behavior described is *iṯṯāqaltum ilā al-arḍ* — "you sink down heavily to the earth": the root th-q-l is weight, heaviness, the sluggishness of a body that has become one with its comfort. Ibn Kathīr and al-Rāzī set the scene: the call is the Tabūk march — Rajab of 9 AH, the hottest season, the date-harvest ripening in the groves, a thousand-kilometer road toward the world's remaining superpower, and a levy of wealth and mounts the community had never before been asked for. Some of the tribes, the commentators record, calculated the heat, the distance, the expense, the enemy — and stayed. The verse does not yet name the hypocrites (their anatomy begins at verse 42); verse 38 addresses *believers* — the address is "O you who believe" — whose faith was real but whose weight had shifted. That distinction carries the whole section: the sūrah's disappointment is reserved, in its strongest form, not for the enemies but for the adherents who would not move.

**The Audit of Contentment**

The verse's second question is the diagnosis inside the diagnosis: *raḍītum bil-ḥayāti al-dunyā mina al-ākhirati* — "are you content with the life of this world rather than the Hereafter?" The word *raḍiya* — content, satisfied — is the exact term the sūrah elsewhere reserves for the believer's destiny (*raḍiyu Allāhu ʿanhum*, "God is content with them," v. 100). The horror of the question is its inversion: contentment, the very signature of paradise, has been claimed by the harvest season. The Qur'an accuses the stayers of a *category error* — mistaking which life is the residence and which is the hotel. And the closing clause supplies the arithmetic: *wa-mā al-ḥayātu al-dunyā fī al-ākhirati illā matāʿ* — the enjoyment (*matāʿ*) of this world, set against the next, is *qalīl* — little, paltry. The same audit runs throughout the Book: *"What is the life of this world but play and diversion, and better is the Abode of the Hereafter for those who are mindful"* (6:32; cf. 13:26; 87:16–17).

**Ḥunayn's Harvest and the Economics of Dates**

The sīrah supplies the concrete texture the verse generalizes. Tabūk was called in the season when the dates of Madinah — the city's wealth — darken on the palms, and the shade is sweetest; some men, reports say, looked at their groves and could not leave them. The Prophet ﷺ saw the physics of it plainly, and the tradition preserves his standing counsel against exactly this drag:

> *"Be in this world as a stranger or a passing traveler."* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī (6416) from Ibn ʿUmar)

The stranger does not renovate the inn. Ibn ʿAjībah, reading verse 38 spiritually, finds in it the anatomy of every soul that has settled: inertia, he says, is not a sin of the limbs but of the love-structure — the self has sunk into the world as into a bed, and the call to move reveals how deep the mattress is. The test of any spiritual state is what happens when the summons comes.

**What Ails Us: The Portable Question**

For the modern believer, verse 38 survives the battlefield it was revealed into, because the structure is permanent: a call goes forth — to serve, to give, to stand witness, to learn, to relocate for God's sake — and the self discovers, at the moment of rising, its true weight. The verse's two questions form a complete self-exam: *What ails me that I sink when called?* and *Which life am I actually content with?* — and the second answers the first, every time. The believer who is honest discovers that his heaviness is not really about heat, distance, or expense: those are the dates; the core is the ranking. Al-Rāzī notes the verse's mercy inside its sting: it does not say "you disbelieved"; it says "you sank" — weight is a condition, not a creed, and conditions can be walked off. The Prophet ﷺ did not curse the heavy believers of Rajab; he marched, and let the road itself re-educate them — and verse 118's story will show what God thought of the three who finally refused the road altogether. Between the sinking of verse 38 and the shunning of verse 118 lies the entire spectrum of human hesitation, and the believer's task in every generation is to locate himself on it honestly — and to rise *before* the call goes forth, carrying the pre-decided heart of the one who already knows which life he is content with.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:39

> **If you go not forth, He will punish you with a painful punishment, and will place another people in your stead, and you will not harm Him in the least. And God is Powerful over all things.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Warning Before the Replacement**

Verse 39 completes verse 38's diagnosis with its consequence, and the consequence has two blades. The first is punishment — *yuʿadhdhibkum ʿadhāban alīman* — "a painful punishment": the commentators divide between readings, as the source commentary notes — al-Qurṭubī records the interpretation of a drought, the withholding of rain from a city that would not march; al-Rāzī and others read the threat eschatologically, of the Hereafter's accounting for a duty abandoned. The second blade is subtler and, to the community's self-love, far more wounding: *wa-yastabdil qawman ghayrakum* — "He will place another people in your stead." The doctrine of replacement is one of the Qur'an's recurring historical laws:

> *"If you turn away, He will replace you with another people, and they will not be like you."* (Qur'an 47:38)

> *"And how many a generation We destroyed before them... and We raised after them another generation."* (Qur'an 6:6; cf. 21:11; 36:81–82)

The principle is not ethnic vengeance; it is organizational theology: God's work does not depend on any particular workforce. The community that declines its assignment is retired while the mission continues — an outcome Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī read historically in the waves of converts, Persian and Abyssinian and Levantine, who would carry the faith the Arab tribes nearly stayed home from.

**You Cannot Harm Him at All**

Between the two blades stands the clause that makes the theology exact: *wa-lā taḍurrūnahu shayʾan* — "and you will not harm Him in the least." The verb ḍarra — to injure, to diminish — is predicated of the stayers and denied absolutely: your defection subtracts nothing from God. The doctrine is the Qur'an's constant answer to every human hubris, from the rebels who think their sin threatens heaven to the reformers who think the truth depends on their tenure:

> *"Whoever strives, strives only for his own soul; truly God is independent of the worlds."* (Qur'an 29:6)

The clause performs a double surgery. On the community's pride: the mission is a privilege, not a necessity — you were invited to the freight, not entrusted with the engine. On the individual's despair: the converse of "you cannot harm Him" is "you cannot help Him either" — and the freedom in that is enormous; the believer serves because service is his healing and his honor, not because God would otherwise be short-handed. Al-Qurṭubī hears in the clause the verse's tenderest irony: the only party harmed by the staying-home is the stayer.

**Power Over All Things: The Signature That Seals Both Blades**

The verse closes: *wa-Anhū ʿalā kulli shayʾin qadīr* — "and God is Powerful over all things." The signature is the guarantee of both threats and both mercies: powerful to punish a staying community, powerful to replace it, powerful also to raise up from the replaced ranks a people whose story no one foresaw. The classical readers of year 9 saw the clause enacted within a decade: the men of Ṭāʾif who had shot arrows at the Prophet's army became reciters of his Book; the Persian empire that was Tabūk's dread became the heartland of his faith; and the Bedouin whom verse 97 will call "most severe in disbelief" became — verse 99 says it plainly — people who spent for nearness to God. Replacement, in the Qur'an, is not merely a threat; it is the mechanism by which God keeps demonstrating that the religion's carrier is chosen by Him, never by pedigree.

**The Verse as an Institution's Mirror**

For the modern believer — and for every institution that thinks itself indispensable to God — verse 39 is the cold shower administered with warmth's intention. Churches of every confession, Islamic organizations, dynasties of scholarship, movements of reform: all are one decision-cycle away from *yastabdil qawman ghayrakum*, and history's graveyards of replaced movements are the verse's receipts. The healthy response is the one the Qur'an itself models: urgency without panic, fidelity without presumption. The community that internalizes "you will not harm Him" stops negotiating its obedience as though God needed the deal — and starts treating every summons as a *gift of participation* with an expiry date. The men of Tabūk's summer, at least those who marched, understood: the boots were theirs, the road was theirs to suffer, but the Light was never theirs to keep — only to carry, for exactly as long as they were willing to walk.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:40

> **If you help him not, yet God has already helped him. Remember when those who disbelieved expelled him, the second of two. Yea, the two were in the cave, when he said to his companion, "Grieve not; truly God is with us." Then God sent down His Tranquility upon him, and supported him with hosts you see not. And He made the word of those who disbelieve to be the lowliest, and the Word of God is the highest. And God is Mighty, Wise.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Foundation Document of the Community's Memory**

Verse 40 is the sūrah's hinge — al-Qurṭubī reports the opinion that it was the *first* verse of the sūrah in order of revelation — and it reaches back from Tabūk's crisis to the founding crisis of Islām: the Hijrah. The opening clause is a rebuke wrapped in a doctrine: *in-tanṣurūhu fa-qad naṣarahu Allāhu* — "if you help him not — God has already helped him." The perfect tense is the point: the help is *already* history. Before any community existed to help the Prophet ﷺ — when the whole of his following could be counted and hunted — God had delivered him; the community that now hesitates to spend on his cause is not the cause's lifeline, only its guests. And then the verse does something the Qur'an rarely does: it names and stages a specific scene, with two speakers, one cave, and one sentence of dialogue that the entire Muslim world knows by heart.

**The Expulsion and the Second of Two**

*Ith yukhrijuhu alladhīna kafarū thānīya ithnayn* — "when the disbelievers expelled him, the second of two." The expulsion is the plot of the Makkan grand council: a chosen man from every clan, one blade each, so that the Banū Hāshim could not collect blood-money from all of Quraysh and would have to accept the killing as fate. On the appointed night the assassins ringed the Prophet's house — and found his bed occupied by ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib, sleeping in his cousin's place, while the Prophet ﷺ, reciting the opening of Yā Sīn, walked through their line unseen and went to the house of the one man who had believed him from the first hour: Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq. The honorific *thānīya ithnayn* — "the second of two" — is understood by the commentators as both description and decoration: of all the titles the Qur'an could have conferred on Abū Bakr, it chose this — the man who was *there*, second in the cave, when Islām was two people and a pursuit. ʿUmar would later weep at the thought that his own Hijrah was daylight and public while Abū Bakr's was in secret and shadow — and say that Abū Bakr's single night outweighed his whole life.

**Inside the Cave: Grief, Fear, and the Sentence That Outlived Them**

The two went south, not north — a counter-intuitive route — to Jabal Thawr, and hid three nights in its cave while the bounty-hunters combed the district. The scene the verse preserves is told by ʿĀʾishah in the Ṣaḥīḥ:

> *"...Then Abū Bakr said: 'O Messenger of God, if one of them were to look down beneath his feet, he would see us.' The Prophet ﷺ said: 'O Abū Bakr, what do you think of two, the third of whom is God?'"* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī (3653) and Muslim (2381) from ʿĀʾishah, from the long account of the Hijrah)

The Qur'an's own record of the moment is *lā taḥzan inna Allāha maʿanā* — "Do not grieve; truly God is with us" — and every word of it is doctrine. *Lā taḥzan*: the prohibition is of *ḥuzn* — grief, the sinking of hope — not of fear; Abū Bakr's fear was love in armor, and the Prophet did not shame it, he steadied it. *Inna Allāha maʿanā*: God is *with us* — the preposition of companionship (*maʿa*) rather than the preposition of aid (*ʿalā*), the grammar of nearness chosen over the grammar of mere rescue. Ibn Kathīr notes that the consolation was given to Abū Bakr — who grieved *for* the Prophet, not for himself — and that the tranquility (sakīnah) was sent down "upon him," the majority reading being the Prophet, some reading Abū Bakr, whose fear was the wound the divine calm came to dress. The cave itself is still shown to pilgrims: a cleft so low one must stoop — the two men who would inherit a third of the known world within a decade, lying still while a spider's web (as the sīrah relates) and nesting doves persuaded the searchers, at arm's length from their quarry, that no one had entered in months.

**The Lowliest Word and the Highest**

The verse's verdict is stated in a construction the commentators savor: *fa-asfalat kalimata alladhīna kafarū* — "and He made the word of those who disbelieve lowliest" — *wa-kalimatu Allāhi hiya al-ʿulyā* — "and God's Word is the Most High." The two "words" are identified by al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī as the creeds themselves: the shirk-confession of the councils versus *lā ilāha illā Allāh*. Within ten years of the cave, the men of that council were dead or defeated, their sanctuary held by the man they had hunted, and their heirs were reciting the Word they had tried to kill. The verse's final signature — *ʿAzīzun Ḥakīm*, Mighty, Wise — closes the file with the two attributes the scene displayed: might that could rescue two men from an army unseen, wisdom that chose to rescue them by a spider's web rather than a legion — because the lesson the community would need, at Tabūk and forever, was not that God *can* save, but that He saves *at the extremity of means*, so that no instrument is ever mistaken for the Savior.

**The Verse Every Believer Carries Into Their Own Cave**

For the modern believer, verse 40 is not a museum piece; it is a portable doctrine with the Prophet's own voice inside it. Every serious life has — or will have — its cave: the night when the plot has closed, the helpers are few or none, and the loyal friend's fear is the only human warmth in the room. The verse's instructions for that night are exact: keep company with your Abū Bakr — the friend whose fear is for you and whose presence is chosen; refuse ḥuzn — the grief that forecasts doom — while accepting the pulse of honest fear; and say the sentence, which has lost none of its tense: *lā taḥzan inna Allāha maʿanā*. The same sentence, the tradition records, was spoken across the generations — Abraham at the fire, and the Prophet at the mustering of the confederates, as Ibn ʿAbbās transmitted of the words *ḥasbunā Allāhu wa-niʿma al-wakīl* (Bukhārī 4563) — and the Qur'an's promise stands over every speaker of it: the tranquility descends, the hosts that no eye sees take their positions, and the Word, however low it lies tonight, is the one that ends highest.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:41

> **Go forth in the way of God, lightly or heavily, and strive with your wealth and yourselves in the way of God. That is better for you, if you but knew.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command With No Exemptions**

Verse 41 converts verse 38's rebuke into a standing order, and its adjective-pair has generated a whole gallery of readings: *infirū khifāfan wa-thiqālan* — "go forth, lightly or heavily." Al-Ṭabarī, collecting the interpretations, offers the range the source commentary preserves: lightly or heavily *armed*; *young or old*; *rich or poor*; *eager or reluctant*; and his own preference — whether it is *easy or hard* for you. The interpretive spread is itself the point: every proposed pairing names a category of excuse, and the verse sweeps them all into a single imperative. Light troops and heavy troops, the unencumbered youth and the burdened elder, the eager volunteer and the reluctant draftee — *infirū*, go forth, all of you. The verb's form (infirū, from the reflexive stem of *nafara*) pictures a body of people *breaking camp* — the community's default state is now mobilized.

**The Double Currency Again**

The verse's second clause repeats the sūrah's formula for total commitment: *wa-jāhidū bi-amwālikum wa-anfusikum fī sabīli Allāhi* — "and strive with your wealth and your selves in the way of God." The order — wealth first, selves second — is deliberate and repeatedly observed (vv. 20, 44, 81, 88, 111), and the commentators note its psychology: wealth is the first and smaller surrender, the warm-up act of the heart; the self is the final one, and the verse trains its soldiers in ascending denomination. A community that will not give its gold will not long give its blood; the man who has learned the *pain* of the smaller gift has begun the curriculum of the larger. And the currency-pair closes the escape hatches on the side of comfort: the one who cannot fight can fund; the one who cannot fund can carry; the verse's economics allow every believer to be *expended* — which is, in the sūrah's theology, the only alternative to being *hoarded* (v. 34–35).

**"Better for You, If You Knew": The Verse's Theory of Motivation**

The verse ends with an epistemic nudge rather than a threat: *dhālika khayrun lakum in kuntum taʿlamūn* — "that is better for you, if you but knew." No punishment is named, no replacement threatened (those were verses 38–39); instead the Qur'an appeals to the listener's mis-calculated self-interest. The going-forth *itself* — its sacrifice, its heat, its cost — is *khayr*, good, benefit, for the one who pays it. This is the Qur'an's consistent theory of motivation at its cleanest: God does not need the march (v. 39 settled that); the march needs the marcher. Every act of service is a transfer *into* the servant's account, and the only ignorance in play is arithmetic: "if you but knew" how the exchange rate actually runs. Al-Rāzī hears in the clause the whole philosophy of the sūrah's sacrifices — that the Believer is never asked to choose between his interest and God's, only between a *real* interest and an imagined one. The believer who grasps this serves with the energy of a man who has read the contract — not the resignation of a man drafted.

**A Permanent Draft Card**

For the modern believer, verse 41 is the standing general order under which every specific duty files. The categories of "light and heavy" remain the honest audit of every congregation: some serve with health and money and few dependents (light); some serve through illness, debt, obligations, and grief (heavy) — and the verse's grammar drafts both. The twin currencies have not changed either: wealth and self remain the only two things a human being actually possesses, and the only two things God asks for. The believer's yearly self-review can be run on verse 41's two axes: *What did I spend this year in the way of God?* and *Where did I myself show up — bodily, personally, uncomfortably?* A year in which neither column has an entry is a year of staying behind — and verse 38's question, *mā lakum*, is still addressed, with the same ache, to "O you who believe."

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:42

> **Were it something ephemeral nearby or an easy journey, they would have followed thee. But the trek was too great for them. And they will swear by God, "If we had been able, we would have gone out with you." They destroy themselves, and God knows that they are liars.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The X-Ray of Convenience-Calibrated Commitment**

Verse 42 is the sūrah's most uncomfortable mirror, and its insight is permanently useful: *law kāna ʿaraḍan qarīban* — "had it been an ephemeral gain nearby" — *wa-safaran qāṭiʿan* — "and an easy journey" — *la-ttabaʿūka* — "they would have followed you." The word *ʿaraḍ* means perishable merchandise — a caravan's goods, a market's profits; *qāṭiʿ* is a journey that "cuts" only briefly across comfort. The verse states the diagnosis before the men themselves could make their excuses: their commitment was calibrated to convenience. A nearby profit or a short trip, and they would have come — with enthusiasm, with cameras, as it were. But Tabūk offered no near loot, only expense; no short trip, only the hard road; and against that ledger their religion turned out to be a fair-weather membership. Al-Rāzī sees the verse as a general law of the two-faced: the hypocrite follows when religion is profitable and vanishes when it is costly, and the *cost curve* is the only honest test of his allegiance. The Qur'an had already run the same X-ray on the stayers of Uḥud:

> *"And a party of them said to their brothers... 'Had they known of fighting, they would not have come out'... Had they known of loot nearby and a short journey, they would have followed you."* (cf. Qur'an 3:167 — the verse's own sibling at Uḥud)

**The Oath They Will Swear**

The verse then fast-forwards: *wa-yahlifūna billāhi* — "they will swear by God" — *law istataʿnā lakharajnā maʿakum* — "if we had been able, we would have gone out with you." The future tense is forensic: God is quoting, in advance, the testimony the stayers will give on the Prophet's return — the same excuse-cycle verses 94, 95, and 96 will dramatize. And the verdict on the oath is absolute: *yuhlikūna anfusahum* — "they destroy themselves" — *wa-Allāhu yaʿlamu annahum lakādhibūn* — "and God knows they are liars." The lying is not in the letter (some were, in fact, unequipped) but in the *representation*: an oath by God that frames inability as the cause, when the cause — as verse 46 will prove — is that they never made preparation. There is a precise psychology here that deserves attention: the false oath is not merely a lie to others; it is the final act of lying to oneself, sealing the excuse into identity. Hence "they destroy themselves" — the verb *ahlaka* is used of their own action upon their own souls; the liar's first casualty has already been invoiced.

**Convenience as the Modern Assay**

Ibn ʿAjībah's spiritual reading extends the verse beyond the battlefield: the near loot and easy journey, he says, are the pleasures and shortcuts the lazy soul will always follow, while the far trek is the journey of the soul toward God — long, hot, and unprofitable to the lower self. The modern believer needs no translation of the assay: every community knows the volunteer who appears when the event is pleasant and vanishes when the work is heavy; every cause knows the supporter whose solidarity lasts exactly until it costs. But the verse's blade turns inward most usefully: *What is my own following calibrated to?* If my religion gets my presence when it is near, pleasant, and profitable — and my absence when it is far, hard, and expensive — then verse 42, which looks like a portrait of the hypocrites of Madinah, is hung on my own wall. The mercy in the diagnosis is its honesty: God does not say the stayers *could not* come; He says they *would not* — and the difference between those two words is the exact width of the door of repentance, which verses 102–118 will show still standing open even for those who stayed.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:43

> **God pardon thee! Why didst thou grant them leave before it became clear to thee who spoke the truth and who the liars were?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Gentle Rebuke From God to His Beloved**

Verse 43 is one of the two or three places in the Qur'an where God addresses the Prophet ﷺ with a correction, and it has been meditated upon for centuries precisely because of its tenderness. *ʿafā Allāhu ʿanka* — "God pardon thee!" — the pardon precedes the correction, as if God wrapped the wound before making it. The classical commentators — Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī — all note the order: the verse opens with forgiveness for a deed that is, at worst, an excess of trust, and the question that follows is not an accusation of sin but a lesson in leadership timing: *lima a'dhintahum* — "why didst thou grant them leave" — *ḥattā yatabayyana laka alladhīna ṣadaqū* — "before it became clear to thee who spoke the truth" — *wa-taʿlama al-kādhibīn* — "and thou knewest the liars." Before the Tabūk march, certain men had asked the Prophet ﷺ for exemption; he granted it; and the sūrah now teaches the principle he himself embodied in later decisions: blanket permissions before a critical mission shield the insincere from exposure. The sequel shows the lesson learned: verse 83 records the Prophet's standing sentence for future requests of this class — *"You shall never go forth with me... sit with those who stay behind"* — and his practice after Tabūk was to withhold the blanket concession that had hidden the insincere.

**Why Allow Permission at All? The Tension With 24:62**

The jurists noticed an apparent tension: this verse seems to censure granting leave, while Sūrat al-Nūr commands it: *"So when they ask thy leave in some matter of theirs, give leave to whomsoever thou wilt"* (24:62). The majority answer, which the source commentary preserves, is that 24:62 abrogated or qualified the practice — or, in al-Rāzī's subtler resolution, that the two verses govern different situations: the *permission of the asker* (24:62) versus the *blanket, unprompted concession* of 9:43, which had the unintended effect of hiding the hypocrites' true colors. The wisdom of the correction is strategic: had no exemption been granted, every stayer would have had to *choose in the open*, and the community would have seen, in one muster, whose faith could carry a pack and whose could not. Exposure, the sūrah is teaching, is a mercy to the community and even to the sinner — the masked believer is the most endangered of men, and a mercy that keeps him masked is no mercy.

**Leadership Lesson: Let Choices Reveal**

Al-Ṭabarī draws from the verse a rule of governance that the modern world would recognize instantly: a leader's job includes *designing the moments in which people's commitments become visible*. The Prophet ﷺ — maʿṣūm, protected from sin — is corrected not for harm done but for a kindness that, in this instance, reduced information: the community marched north not knowing who among its stayers were honest and who were liars, and the sūrah itself had to supply the file (vv. 42 ff.). For every leader since — of families, teams, congregations, nations — the verse sets a standard of *respectful clarity*: generous with persons, but never so generous with process that accountability dissolves. Permission-granting is not always kindness; sometimes the kindest sentence is the question that makes the asker stand behind his own request.

**"God Pardon Thee": The Signature of Prophetic Infallibility Rightly Understood**

The verse is also a standing lesson in how the tradition reads its Prophet. The opening — *ʿafā Allāhu ʿanka* — became the classical proof-text in the discussion of ʿiṣmah (protection from sin): the scholars reasoned that if even this act — a permission given in good faith and mercy — drew a divine "pardon," then the Prophet's other decisions stood on an altogether higher grade; and the Sunni tradition read the verse as honoring him: God corrects His beloved *before* any harm accrues, and teaches the ummah through the correction. The Sufi commentators add the devotional note: if the Master is so trained, what of the student? The believer learns to receive correction as a form of divine attention — *"whatever good befalls thee is from God, and whatever ill befalls thee is from thyself"* (4:79) — and to attach his ego to none of his prior decisions. The man who can hear "why didst thou..." without defensiveness has understood something of the station the verse trains toward: leadership as continuous learning, under a Teacher whose rebukes are themselves a kind of embrace.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:44

> **Those who believe in God and the Last Day ask of thee no leave from striving with their wealth and their selves. And God knows the reverent.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Faith That Does Not Ask Permission**

Verse 44 states a positive diagnostic so clean it functions almost as a law of nature: *lā yastaʾdhinu* — "they ask no leave" — *alladhīna yuʾminūna billāhi wa-l-yawmi al-ākhir* — "those who believe in God and the Last Day" — *an yujāhidū bi-amwālihim wa-anfusihim* — "to be excused from striving with their wealth and their selves." The logic is internal, not imposed: a person who *actually* believes in a Last Day of account does not need to ask whether showing up is required — the question answers itself inside the belief. Leave-asking, in the sūrah's anatomy, is not a neutral administrative act; it is a confession of ambivalence, an announcement that the asker's creed has not yet fully reached the ledger. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī both read the verse as the believer's certificate: eagerness for duty is the *fruit* of the two articles named — belief in God (Who commanded) and in the Last Day (where the command's honor or neglect will be weighed). Where the fruit is absent, the verse quietly asks the tree to check its roots.

**The Verse's Certainty About Motivation**

The closing clause — *wa-Anhū yaʿlamu al-muttaqīn*, "and God knows the reverent" — does double duty. It certifies that God's classification is knowledge, not guesswork: the asker and the non-asker are not sorted by etiquette but by the state of hearts only He sees. And it protects the community from the reverse abuse — the culture in which *never asking for help* becomes a performance. The verse does not condemn the legitimate excuse (verses 91–92 are coming, and they are among the tenderest in the sūrah); it identifies the *category* of those whose asking is a symptom rather than a need. The tradition holds the two truths with one hand each: the Prophet ﷺ granted real excuses generously — the blind, the lame, the penniless were excused by name in revelation itself (48:17) — and the same revelation states flatly that the sincere do not shop for exemptions. A community that loses either half becomes, on one side, a guilt-machine that grinds the legitimately burdened, or, on the other, a permission-culture in which the able-bodied learn the grammar of excuse.

**Wealth and Selves: The Twin Offering One More Time**

Note that even here, in a verse about *not asking*, the sūrah repeats the full currency: *bi-amwālihim wa-anfusihim* — wealth and selves. The repetition across the chapter (vv. 20, 41, 81, 88, 111) is not redundancy; it is the Qur'an refusing to let either denomination be dropped from the account. There have always been believers comfortable giving money precisely so as never to give presence, and believers eager to appear whose giving never reaches the purse. Verse 44's portrait — the believer who does not seek leave from *either* — is the sūrah's composite photograph of ṣiddīqī character: the one whose check and whose boots are both already on the road.

**The Quiet Test of Our Own Requests**

For the modern believer, verse 44's mirror is searching precisely because it is quiet. It does not forbid asking; it observes what asking *means*. Every community runs on volunteers, and every volunteer coordinator knows the two lists: those who ask to be excused from what they were never asked to do — a preemptive diplomacy of low expectation — and those who simply appear, and keep appearing, until they become the load-bearing walls of the place. The verse's internal logic can be self-administered without shame: *when I ask for exemption, is it because of my circumstances or because of my creed's actual ranking?* The honest answer is often mixed — and the sūrah is patient with mixtures (see v. 102's confession) — but the believer who wants the verse's certificate, *God knows the reverent*, knows where the work lies: not in never needing exception, but in never *seeking* it while the legs and the purse still answer the call.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:45

> **Only they ask leave of thee who believe not in God and the Last Day, whose hearts are in doubt, and so they waver in their doubt.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From Symptom to Source: The Anatomy of Wavering**

Verse 45 is verse 44's negative twin, and its severity is carefully constructed: *innamā yastaʾdhinka alladhīna lā yuʾminūna billāhi wa-l-yawmi al-ākhir* — "*only* they ask thy leave who believe not in God and the Last Day." The restrictive particle *innamā* performs the sūrah's signature sorting operation — the same particle that opened verses 18, 28 and 60 — and it must be read through the whole passage's architecture: verse 91 will explicitly exempt the weak, sick, and penniless, and the Prophet ﷺ excused them by revelation. The "only" of verse 45 governs the *unforced* leave-seeker of means and health — the man of verse 42's oath, verse 93's wealthy asker — not the mother of the household, the man with one camel and no sandals. Within that scope, the verse is uncompromising: the unprompted pursuit of exemption, from a duty one is able to bear, is traced to its root — *wa-urtābat qulūbuhum* — "and their hearts wavered in doubt." The root r-y-b is the sūrah's later word for disquiet (v. 110) and the Qur'an's standing word for the doubt that corrodes: the opposite of *lā rayba fīh* — "no doubt in it" — the Book's second-announcement about itself (2:2).

**Doubt as paralysis, Wavering as Its Sign**

The verse's psychology deserves slow reading: doubt is named twice — hearts *in* doubt (*irtābat qulūbuhum*), and then the wavering itself — *fī miryatihim yaʿridūn*, "in their doubt they keep turning away": the verb means to incline aside, to lean away, to present one face and turn another. The doubter's signature is not a settled skepticism but an oscillation: leaning toward the believers when belief pays (v. 42's near loot), leaning away when it costs. Al-Rāzī notes the causal chain the verse draws: weak credence → unstable valuation → situationally recalculated allegiance → the leave-request as the visible tip. The verse thereby teaches a permanent hermeneutic of human behavior: beneath most chronic excuse-making lies not laziness but *arithmetic* — a heart that has never fully priced the Last Day, and so re-prices every duty at the moment of performance. Ibn Kathīr, summarizing the early commentators, identifies the verse's referents concretely: the hypocrites of the Tabūk roll-call, whose requests for exemption the Prophet ﷺ had already granted (v. 43's correction) — the verses now reclassify what the permission had left ambiguous.

**The Verse and the Honest Questioner**

The thoughtful reader will feel the verse's edge and ask the fair question: *is every doubt a mark of hypocrisy?* The tradition's answer distinguishes two experiences that share a name: the *wound* of doubt — the honest believer's dark nights, questions pressed on him by suffering or study — and the *strategy* of doubt — the settled position from which obligations can be perpetually deferred. The Prophet ﷺ received from his Companions the hardest of questions ("God reveals verses about us... will he specify our names?" — cf. v. 64's dread) and answered them patiently; the Qur'an commands believers to *"ask the people of remembrance"* (16:43) and praises those who listen to a word and follow the best of it (39:18). Verse 45 condemns none of this. Its target is the wavering that *selects* doubt whenever doubt is convenient — doubt as an alibi. The believer who interrogates his doubts honestly, in order to settle them, is on the road the Qur'an praises; the one who curates his doubts in order to keep his options open is already standing in verse 45's photograph.

**Certainty as a Discipline, Not a Windfall**

For the modern believer, verses 44–45 together teach that the cure for wavering is not primarily emotional but *structural*: feed the two beliefs the verse names, and the wavering starves. Belief in God is fed by knowledge — study of His Book, attention to His signs in the self and the horizon (41:53); belief in the Last Day is fed by rehearsal — the Prophet ﷺ commanded the frequent remembrance of the destroyer of pleasures, death, not as morbidity but as pricing. The heart that has done this work discovers the verse 44 freedom: duties stop requiring adjudication, because the ranking was settled long before the request could be drafted. And the community whose members mostly live there — past needing permission to serve — becomes what the sūrah is trying to build: a body whose default posture toward its Lord is already on its feet.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:46

> **And had they desired to go forth, they would have made some preparations for it. But God was averse to their being sent forth; so He held them back, and it was said unto them, "Stay back with those who stay back."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Preparation as the Proof of Desire**

Verse 46 delivers one of the Qur'an's most practical tools of moral discernment, hiding in a conditional clause: *wa-law arādū al-khurūja la-aʿaddū lahu ʿuddah* — "had they desired to go forth, they would have made some preparation for it." The principle is behavioral: genuine intention leaks into logistics. The men who swore in verse 42, "if we had been able, we would have gone out with you," are now cross-examined by their own supply chains: where are your packed provisions, your hired mounts, your water-skins counted against the road to Syria? Nobody prepares for a journey he does not intend to take; nobody fails to prepare for one he does. Al-Qurṭubī states the maxim plainly, and it has governed Islamic moral psychology ever since: the liar's excuse can be checked against the ledger of his preparations. The application to religious life generally is immediate — the man who says he *would* pray if he had time has a schedule that can be audited; the one who *would* learn if he could has a shelf that can be inspected. Verse 46 is the Qur'an's methodological charter for taking stated intentions seriously enough to verify them — beginning with our own.

**God Averse: The Theology of Being Held Back**

The verse's middle clause carries a startling theology: *wa-lākin kariha Allāhu anbiʿāthahum* — "but God was averse to their being sent forth" — *fa-thabbatahum* — "so He held them back." The divine aversion is not caprice; the next clause explains it — and verse 47 supplies the reason: their presence in the army would have added nothing but *khubāl* — trouble, confusion, sabotage. God, in the sūrah's teaching, protects His community even through the vices of its weak members: the cowardice that kept the hypocrites home was simultaneously the quarantine that kept them out. Al-Qurṭubī reads the mechanism psychologically — God held them back *by means of* their own fear — and the reading resolves the puzzle of divine and human agency the sūrah keeps raising (cf. v. 16): their choice was theirs, and its outcome was governed. The believer learns a strange comfort here: the community's critical missions are not defenseless against the infiltrated ambivalence of their own ranks; there is a Hand on the roster that the roster does not see.

**"Stay Back With Those Who Stay Back": The Sentence as Sentence**

The verse's closing quotation — *wa-qīla iqʿudū maʿa al-qāʿidīn* — "and it was said: sit with those who sit" — is the sūrah's most devastating line of sarcasm, and the commentators relish its construction. *Qāʿid* — the sitter — is the technical term for those legitimately non-combatant: women, children, the infirm (vv. 83, 93 use it straight). The able-bodied shirkers are now assigned, by a word that reads like an administrative transfer, to the company of those whose sitting is excused: their punishment, in this world, is the category itself. The sentence has two edges, and both draw blood: it brands them with a label they cannot shake (in the Arabic, the rhymed cadence *iqʿudū maʿa al-qāʿidīn* made the phrase proverbial), and it consigns them to permanent minority — the sitting-class of a community defined by its marching. Ibn Kathīr notes that verse 83 will return the sentence at full judicial force; here it is first pronounced as diagnosis: what they wanted — to sit — is granted them as identity.

**The Grace Inside the Sarcasm**

For the modern believer, verse 46 closes with an unexpected pastoral door. The command "sit with those who sit" is, read again, also a *mercy of placement*: the hypocrites were not purged, exposed by name, and punished; they were simply allowed to be what they had chosen, inside a community that would keep teaching beside them. The sūrah's whole treatment of the hidden opposition follows this policy — verse 101 will tell the Prophet, of the hidden hypocrites, *"you know them not; We know them"* — because a community that hunts its members' hearts devours itself; only God can judge the unseen. The believer's task under verse 46 is therefore double: be rigorous about *preparation* as the test of one's own intentions, and be patient with the community's mixed ranks, whose final audit is not ours to run. And let the one who finds himself, honestly, among the sitters for a season, remember the verse's one condition of dignity in sitting: that it be a legitimate *ʿudhr* — excuse of body or means — and not a *karihah* of the heart, which God, sooner or later, always exposes by its logistics.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:47

> **And had they gone forth with thee, it would have increased thee in naught but troubles. And they would have hurried about in your midst, seeking to incite discord among you. And among you are some who hearken to them. And God knows the wrongdoers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Counterfactual That Vindicates the Quarantine**

Verse 47 completes verse 46's theology of the held-back: *wa-law kharajū fīkum mā zādūkum illā khabālan* — "had they gone forth with you, they would have added to you nothing but *khubāl*." The rare noun *khubāl* — the source commentary renders its range: confusion, disquiet, corruption, disunity — is from a root whose primary sense is maiming and madness (the root of *khabūl*, destruction): their presence would not merely have failed to help, it would have *damaged*. The verse is God's vindication of His own quarantine, and its honesty about communal reality is bracing: an army's strength is not the sum of its bodies. A contingent whose hearts are with the enemy is a subtraction wearing a uniform — worse than absence, because it must be fed, trusted, and defended. History's armies learned this in every war the hard way; the Qur'an taught it as doctrine in one clause: *counting heads is not counting strength.*

**The Fifth Column in Motion**

The verse then describes what the stayers would have done had they marched: *wa-la-aḍāʿūna khilālakum* — "they would have pressed to and fro among you" — the verb *īḍāʿ* is the gait of a racing camel, and the image is of men moving constantly through the camp — *yabghūnakumu al-fitnah* — "seeking to stir up discord among you." The verb *baghā* — to seek, with the connotation of covetous pursuit — makes fitnah their *objective*, not their by-product: couriers of rumor between the clans, whispers about the leadership, sown alarm about the Byzantine host. And the verse's most sobering clause follows: *wa-fīkum sammāʿūna lahum* — "and among you are those who listen to them." The camp would not have been evenly resistant; some sincere but weak Muslims were, God says, *listeners* — men through whom the poison could enter the body. Al-Qurṭubī records the variant reading that makes the listeners active informers — passing the Prophet's news to the enemy — but the majority sense is the more haunting one: not traitors, just porous. The verse thereby teaches the community a permanent epidemiology of discord: sedition requires a carrier population, and the carriers are usually not the wicked but the impressionable.

**God Knows the Wrongdoers: The Sorting That Waits**

The verse closes with the clause the sūrah repeats at moments of maximum ambiguity (cf. v. 44, 101, 106): *wa-Anhū yaʿlamu al-ẓālimīn* — "and God knows the wrongdoers." The signature is deliberate at this exact point, because the chapter has just waded into the hardest question a community faces: *what do we do about the mixed loyalties inside our own ranks?* The answer the verse gives is: nothing, yet — God knows; and the revelation's whole policy follows from it. No purge-list is published; no names are read out; the Prophet ﷺ is never commanded to hunt the listeners or the plotters. The community defends its mission (the hypocrites were not taken to Tabūk) and leaves the souls to the Knower — a restraint that preserved Madinah from the witch-hunt logic that destroyed lesser movements, and a standing instruction to every later community tempted to interrogate its members' hearts. Even at Tabūk's return, when the sūrah's sentences fell, they fell on *conduct* — staying behind, building the rival mosque, lying — never on mere inwardness.

**Listening as a Moral Act**

For the modern believer, the verse's most portable teaching is its smallest clause: *among you are some who listen to them.* The believer's ear is a gate, and the Qur'an treats what passes through it as a moral choice. The Companions who "listened" to the sowers of fitnah were not accused of sedition — they were warned about their *listening*. Every generation has its couriers of discord moving at camel-pace through the camp — the group-chat that relays grievance, the platform that monetizes outrage, the circles where leadership is always the joke and unity is always the loser — and every believer stands, hourly, in the position of the *sammāʿ*: the one through whom it either enters the community or dies on his tongue. Verse 47's counsel is the same policy God practiced on the march: keep the sowers at a distance when you can, name the discord-seek no names, and guard the gate that is yours — the ear — knowing that the One who knows the wrongdoers also knows the listeners.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:48

> **And indeed they sought to incite trials earlier, and turned thee over in their minds, until the truth came and the Command of God was manifest, though they were averse.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Record of Plots Prior**

Verse 48 files the Tabūk stayers under a longer criminal record: *la-qadi btaghaw al-fitnata min qablu* — "indeed they sought to stir up fitnah *before*." The commentators, following the early sources, fill the docket: the sabotages at Uḥud — where ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ubayy withdrew a third of the army on the morning of the battle; the machinations around the Banū Qaynuqāʿ and the trench-digging factions; the whisper-campaigns of the mosque's back-benches; and the abortive plot, reported in the sīrah of the return from Tabūk itself, to push the Prophet's camel over a ravine at al-ʿAqabah. The verse's grammar — a perfect verb of seeking (*ibtaghaw*) — states a *practice*: fitnah-seeking was not their response to one unpopular campaign; it was their standing project, resumed at every opportunity. Al-Qurṭubī notes the verse's judicial function: it establishes pattern and intent, the two elements that turn scattered incidents into a character — the same jurisprudential instinct that makes recidivism an aggravating factor in every legal tradition.

**"They Turned Thee Over in Their Minds"**

The verse's second clause is a masterpiece of psychological idiom: *wa-qallabū laka al-umūr* — "they turned matters over for thee." The verb *qallaba* is the tumbler's verb — to flip, invert, turn over and over — the root of the word for the heart (*qalb*), which flips between states, and the classical commentators hear the double exposure: *they* turned the affairs over, and *the heart* turned them over — the plotters brooding on how to flip the Prophet's situation, the heart of every plotter flipping with anxiety over outcomes it cannot control. Ibn Kathīr cites the idiom's ordinary use — pondering a man's case, circling a plan — and al-Rāzī adds the analysis of motive: the plotter must rehearse his plot because it has no footing in truth; reality does not cooperate with him, so he must compensate with endless mental logistics. There is a standing insight here about the psychology of bad faith: sincerity is simple, duplicity is *busy*. The honest man's inner life is a road; the schemer's is a kaleidoscope.

**"Until the Truth Came": The Shelf-Life of Fitnah**

The verse's history has a verdict clause: *ḥattā jāʾa al-ḥaqqu* — "until the truth came" — *wa-ẓahara amru Allāhi* — "and God's command was manifest" — *wa-hum kārihūn* — "though they were averse." The pattern the verse canonizes is the one the Qur'an states as a law in its sibling sūrah: *"They plot, and God plots; and God is the best of plotters"* (8:30). Plot after plot ran its course; in each, the plotters held the advantage of secrecy for a season — and in each, the truth arrived with its inconvenient timing: Uḥud ended in the hypocrites' marginalization; the Trench ended with the confederacy dissolved and their stock at its nadir; Tabūk ended with the community's largest demonstration of solidarity and the sūrah itself reading their private conversations aloud. The verse's comfort is not that the truth arrives quickly — it did not, at Uḥud — but that it arrives *structurally*: fitnah, the Qur'an teaches, is a self-exhausting compound; truth is a self-evidencing one, and time is on its side. Al-Qurṭubī closes his entry with the observation every persecuted believer has carried since: the verse was revealed *to* a community whose plotters were still alive and unpunished — its purpose was not revenge but stamina: keep working; the truth has never once failed to come.

**For the Believer Inside the Plot**

The modern believer usually reads verse 48 from the victim's chair — and it belongs there: everyone who has been "turned over" in a boardroom, a family, or an online campaign knows the vertigo of discovering a plan that predates you. The verse assigns such a believer three postures. First, *documentation of the pattern*: the Qur'an itself names the plotters' record; wisdom keeps its own quiet file rather than reacting to each incident as if it were the first. Second, *refusal of the kaleidoscope*: the believer's answer to being schemed against is not counter-scheming but the simplicity of truth — the one resource the plotters cannot manufacture and cannot, in the end, outlast. Third, *patience calibrated to God's timing*: "until" is the verse's key word — there is an until, and its date is not ours to set. The men of the ʿAqabah plot walked Madinah's streets for years afterward, some to die in their beds — and the Qur'an's own emphasis is not the plotters' end but the community's persistence: the truth came, the command was manifest, and the ṣaḥābah kept marching — from Tabūk, as it happened, directly into the most productive two years in the religion's history.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:49

> **And among them some say, "Grant me leave, and tempt me not." Nay, they have fallen into temptation. And truly Hell encompasses the disbelievers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Man Who Begged Not to Be Tempted — by a Battle**

Verse 49 preserves, in one dramatic quotation, the psychology of pre-emptive excuse at its most self-unaware: *wa-minhum man yaqūlu i'dhan lī wa-lā taftinnī* — "among them is he who says: grant me leave, and do not *tempt* me." The verb *fatana* is the crucible-verb — to test, to refine, to afflict: the word behind the verse 126's trials and the Qur'an's word for the winnowing of faith.
