The speaker's logic, unrolled, is a marvel: *if you take me to Tabūk, I may be tempted — by the Byzantine women, or the comforts of home, or whatever frailty I am confessing to — so leave me here, where I will be safe.* The commentators, Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī transmitting the early reports, identify him as Jadd ibn Qays — a man who, on his own confession recorded in the tradition, feared no temptation more than the women of the Banū al-Aṣfar, the yellow-complexioned Byzantines he might meet on the Syrian road; his request to stay home, wrapped in the language of scrupulousness, was thus an application for distance from his own desire. The Qur'an's reply is instant and surgical: *alā fī al-fitnati saqaṭū* — "have they not fallen into temptation?" — the very safety they sought *was* the fall. Declining the duty, they walked into the trial they thought they were declining: the trial of a heart that has begun negotiating with its God.

**The Inversion That Governs All Escapes**

The verse encodes a law of spiritual physics: the temptation you flee by disobedience is the one you land in. Al-Qurṭubī states the principle with relish — the fitnah of staying behind (exposure, hypocrisy's entrenchment, the community's just anger, and God's account) exceeded every fitnah of the march by an order of magnitude. The modern believer meets the same law weekly in softer dress: the student who avoids the exam lest he fail it, and so fails the course; the believer who avoids the gathering "because his heart isn't ready," whose heart then grows less ready by the month; the professional who dodges the hard assignment of conscience, and finds the dodged thing waiting at home, grown larger, with interest. Avoidance, the verse teaches, is not an exit from testing; it is the *choice of a worse test* — because the test you refuse in the open arrives in the dark, where none of the community's reinforcements can reach you.

**Hell as an Encompassing Reality**

The verse closes with a geographic clause: *wa-inna jahannama la-muḥīṭatun bil-kāfirīn* — "and truly Hell encompasses the disbelievers." The verb *aḥāṭa* is siege-terminology and cartography at once: to surround completely, as a moat surrounds a fortress. Ibn Kathīr and al-Rāzī hear both senses: the disbeliever's end-state is a place he is *in* on every side — and his present state is *enveloped* by it, standing, as it were, already inside the perimeter, with only the deadline unexpired. The clause explains the first clause: the man begging off the march "lest he be tempted" is already deep in a temptation whose circumference he cannot see; the fire's moat is dug around the whole position, and the only door through it is the door he is being asked to march through — obedience, however hot the road.

**Scruple as Disguise: The Permanent Warning**

Verse 49's most durable lesson concerns the religious voice in which refusal so often dresses itself. Jadd ibn Qays did not say *I am afraid*; he said *do not tempt me* — the idiom of piety deployed as the vehicle of escape. Every community since has known the accent: the scrupulous-sounding no, the spiritual vocabulary repurposed to keep the self in its comfort. The sūrah is not mocking genuine scruple — the same Book legislates caution (2:286 binds duty to capacity) — but it teaches leaders and believers to hear the difference between *avoiding sin* and *avoiding the cost of obedience*, which sound identical in the mouth and are opposite in the ledger. The Prophet ﷺ, the reports continue, granted Jadd his leave — and the tradition records that he lived to regret it, becoming a byword among the mufassirūn. And for the believer who has spoken some version of his sentence — *excuse me from this duty; I know myself too well* — the verse's diagnosis stands as both warning and invitation: the self you know is real, the temptation is real, but the geometry is upside down. The duty was the exit. Hell is already encompassing the position; and the only direction that leads out of it is forward.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:50

> **If some good befalls thee, it troubles them. But if some affliction befalls thee, they say, "We already took precautions beforehand," and they turn away, exulting.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Zero-Sum Heart**

Verse 50 is the Qur'an's most concise portrait of envy at full function, and its two clauses are a controlled experiment: run the two stimuli — good and bad — through the same heart and record the outputs. Stimulus one: *in tuṣibka ḥasanah* — "if some good befalls thee" — victory, spoils, the community's advance — output: *sayassaʾūhum* — "it vexes them," the verb denoting sorrow that sours into agitation. Stimulus two: *in tuṣibka muṣībah* — "if an affliction befalls thee" — output: they say *qad akhadhnā amranā min qablu* — "we already took our affair in hand beforehand" — the self-congratulation of the stay-home strategist — *wa-yatawallaw wa-hum yafraḥūn* — "and they turn away, exulting." A normal adversary feels one of these; the envious heart, the verse shows, is calibrated so that the friend's gain registers as loss and the friend's loss as gain — the self has been positioned as the reference frame of all value. The Qur'an had drawn the same double curve at 3:120 and in the Madinan verses on the delayers of Uḥud (4:71–73): *"If a bounty from God befalls thee, it is as if a calamity had befallen them."*

**"We Took Our Affair in Hand": The Pride of the Ungamble**

The hypocrites' recorded sentence deserves a moment of lexical attention. *Qad akhadhnā amranā min qablu* — literally, "we took our affair before" — is the idiom of prudent foresight, the prudent man's phrase; the Qur'an lets them keep the phrase and strips the meaning. What they call foresight was, in the event, the luck of cowardice — the gamble that Tabūk's danger would vindicate their absence — and when the Prophet's return with the community intact made their bet look merely shabby, the phrase aged badly: verse 94 will find them still deploying it. Al-Rāzī notes the diagnostic that never fails: the false sage's foresight is only ever confirmed by outcomes he did not cause, and falsified by every outcome he feared. The believer meets this accent today in every hindsight-warrior — the pundit whose caution is always vindicated by the crash, the colleague whose non-participation was always wisdom once the project limps — and learns the verse's quiet test of counsel: *did he stake anything?* The man who took no risk has earned no "we told you so."

**Exulting While Turning Away: The Body Language of Bad Faith**

The verse's final image is its most damning: they *turn away* (yatawallaw) *while they rejoice* (yafraḥūn). The combination is unnatural — joy is a communal, face-forward emotion; it wants witnesses — and the hypocrites' joy must turn its face away because it cannot bear to be seen. Al-Qurṭubī catches the shame beneath the triumph: even their celebration is furtive, performed in the private quarter of the heart where the believer's grief should live. There is a whole psychology of bad faith in the gesture: the double life does not produce double joy; it produces a joy that cannot look anyone in the eye — not even the self it celebrates. And the Qur'an's framing makes the condition worse than it looks: the verse addresses the Prophet directly — *if good befalls thee* — so the hypocrites' entire emotional economy is indexed to *one man's* fortunes; they have, without noticing, made the Messenger their sun and their eclipse, their weather-system and their grief. Envy, the verse implies, is the sincerest involuntary compliment the enemies of God ever pay His friends.

**The Believer's Immunization**

For the modern believer, verse 50 is both a mirror and a vaccine. As mirror, it asks the question that only honest self-audit can answer: *whose good news grieves me, and whose setback secretly delights me?* The answer, whenever it is not "no one," marks the exact coordinates of the envier's seat inside the heart — and the Prophet ﷺ prescribed the treatment in his warning against hasad: envy consumes good deeds as fire consumes wood (recorded by Abū Dāwūd and others from Abū Hurayrah), and the remedy is the deliberate, spoken blessing of the one envied — for the tongue can retrain the heart. As vaccine, the verse teaches the community's healthy members what to expect and how to interpret it: when your progress quietly upsets people who call themselves your well-wishers, you are not discovering a conspiracy; you are reading verse 50, which was revealed about professionals. The believer's response is the one the next verse will command: re-index the whole emotional economy to God's decree, and the weather-system of other people's hearts stops governing your own.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:51

> **Say, "Naught befalls us, save that which God has decreed for us. He is our Master, and in God let the believers trust."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Re-Indexes Security**

Verse 51 is the answer to verse 50 — a single sentence that dismantles the entire economy of plots, envy, and precaution-wisdom the sūrah has been auditing. *Qul lan yuṣibanā illā mā kataba Allāhu lanā* — "Say: naught befalls us save what God has written for us." The verb *kataba* — wrote — is the Qur'an's decree-verb, the pen-and-tablet language of destiny (cf. 58:21; 85:22, the Preserved Tablet; 54:49: *"We created everything by a measure"*). The theology is the doctrine of *maktūb* at its most pastoral: the believer's harms and gains were drafted before the plotters' great-grandparents were born, and no assembly of hypocrites can edit the text. The logic cuts the nerve that verse 48 exposed — the kaleidoscopic brooding of the schemers and the fear of the schemed-against: if the file is already written, the plotter's plot is a line already in it, and the believer's fear is a proofreading of a manuscript no hand but God's held. The Prophet ﷺ and his Companions recited this verse's doctrine as daily bread — the tradition attaches to its sisters the Prophet's counsel to the young Ibn ʿAbbās:

> *"Know that if the nations gathered to benefit you, they could benefit you only with what God has written for you; and if they gathered to harm you, they could harm you only with what God has written against you; the pens have been lifted and the pages have dried."* (Recorded by al-Tirmidhī (2516) from Ibn ʿAbbās, graded ṣaḥīḥ)

**"He Is Our Master": The Anchor of Trust**

The verse's middle clause names the anchor of the whole surrender: *huwa mawlānā* — "He is our Master." *Mawlā* — patron, protector, lord of the affair — is the walāyah vocabulary the sūrah has been sorting since verse 16 (the forbidden walījah) and verse 23 (the forbidden patrons): every allegiance question in the chapter resolves here, in the discovery that the believer's *mawlā* was never open for election. And the verse's final clause — *wa-ʿalā Allāhi fa-l-yatawakkali al-muʾminūn* — "and in God let the believers trust" — commands the tawakkul that the Qur'an treats as the signature virtue of faith under threat (cf. 3:122, 159–160; 5:11; 14:11–12; 65:3). Tawakkul is not passivity — the same sūrah ordered the march, the spending, the boots — but the ordering of dependence: *work as if the means were real, and rely as if the means were nothing*, because the outcome was drafted by the Writer, not the means.

**The Two Securities**

Set beside verse 50, the verse draws the two rival security-systems of the human heart with perfect clarity. The hypocrites' system is *procedural* — precautions, foresight, taking one's affair in hand — and its outputs are furtive joy and anxious gloating, because procedure has no floor: the next letter from Byzantium, the next muster-roll, can undo it. The believers' system is *doctrinal* — a written decree, a Master, a trust — and its outputs are the ones history records on the Tabūk road: men who marched into the heat singing, who spent without ledgers, whose sleep in the ranks the Qur'an itself praised in the verse of the sleepers before Uḥud (3:154). Al-Rāzī, in the entry the source commentary draws upon, notes the elegant economy: the believer who internalizes verse 51 has simply *outsourced* his security to the only party with no counterparty risk — and the entire apparatus of envy, plots, and precaution-pride, his and everyone else's, becomes weather: real, sometimes hard, and utterly incapable of amending the manuscript.

**What the Verse Does Not Say**

Honesty requires the verse's negative space: it does not say "naught befalls us but good" — the *muṣībah* of verse 50 remains possible; the believer's immunity is not to events but to the *governance* of events by anything less than God. The tradition's handling of the verse therefore pairs it with two disciplines: acceptance (*riḍā*) when the written thing hurts, and action when the written thing commands — the believer recites verse 51 at the grave and at the war-camp with equal calm, because both are the same Writer's work. For the modern reader, verse 51 offers the only anxiety-architecture that has never collapsed: a written decree, a Master who cannot be unseated, and a trust that no market can short. And it offers the community a litmus test older than psychology's term for it: the believer whose calm survives other people's turbulence has, somewhere in his week, been reciting this verse — in Arabic or in its meaning — until the manuscript's Author became, for him, the only news that counts.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:52

> **Say, "Do you anticipate that aught will befall us save one of the two best things? But we anticipate for you that God will afflict you with a punishment from Him, or by our hands. So wait! Truly we are waiting along with you."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Doctrine of the Two Goods**

Verse 52 is the martial mathematics of verse 51 turned into taunt, and its arithmetic has consoled warriors ever since: *hal tarabbaṣūna binā illā iḥdā al-ḥusnayayn* — "do you await for us anything but one of the *two best things*?" The two, as al-Ṭabarī and al-Rāzī define them, are victory or martyrdom: win, and the community advances; die, and the martyr advances — either way the believer's ledger closes in profit. The phrase reframes the entire gamble the hypocrites declined: what looked like a two-outcome risk (survival or death) was, in the believer's accounts, a two-door prize. The Qur'an grounds the doctrine in its tallest theological claim — that life and death are both *with God* and both, for the believer, *toward God*:

> *"Say: naught befalls us save what God has written for us..."* (Qur'an 9:51 — this sūrah's own floor beneath the two goods)

And the Prophet ﷺ made the exchange-rate explicit in the ḥadīth of the martyrs' chapters, recorded from Anas ibn Mālik:

> *"No one who enters Paradise would wish to return to the world, even were he given everything on the earth — save the martyr, who would love to return to the world to be killed ten times over, for the honor he sees."* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim from Anas ibn Mālik)

**The Dignity Psychology of Courage**

Al-Rāzī, as the source commentary highlights, adds an insight that belongs as much to sports psychology as to tafsīr: the courageous live in dignity and self-respect whatever the scoreboard shows, while the coward suffers defeat *twice* — once in outcome and continuously in the self-contempt of the fear itself. The hypocrites who stayed home in the shade were not, on his reading, spared anxiety; they were marinated in it: every rumor from the north, every mustering-roll, every glance of the marching Anṣār taxed them again. The believer awaiting "one of the two best things" had already banked the only good the enemy could not touch: the frame. Modern research on stress describes the same structure — performance anxiety collapses when either outcome of the trial is pre-accepted — but the Qur'an had already stated it as creed, and had already noticed its corollary: *the taunt is optional.* Verse 52's rhetorical confidence is the outward accent of an inward settlement; the believer is not performing bravado, he is reporting his accounting.

**"Punishment From Him or at Our Hands": The Twin Arsenals**

The verse's second movement turns the tables: the believers, in turn, await for the enemy one of two outcomes — *ʿadhābun min ʿindihi* — punishment from God (drought, panic, the sword of circumstance: verse 39's drought reading, Uḥud's aftermath) — *aw bi-aydīnā* — or at the believers' hands, the conventional battle. The pairing is doctrine, not bravado: the community's arms are one instrument inside a portfolio whose majority is hidden; God has, as the sūrah keeps showing (vv. 26, 40), a full arsenal of means no staff-officer budgets. And the verse's final exchange — *fa-tarabbaṣū innā maʿakum mutarabbaṣūn*, "so wait; we are waiting with you" — delivers the coldest line in the sūrah: the challenge to a waiting-contest, from the side that has already pre-accepted both its outcomes. Ibn Kathīr hears in it the grammar of men unburdened by fear of men — the same register as the Prophet's ﷺ letter to Heraclius: accept Islām and be safe; refuse, and the sin of the subjects is on you — the community that can make offers because its nerve-endings no longer terminate in the enemy's opinion.

**The Two Goods Off the Battlefield**

For the modern believer, verse 52's structure survives every battlefield: the "two best things" generalize to *any* trial undertaken for God — succeed, and the work advances; fail visibly while faithful, and the reward banked is the martyr's currency by whatever name the era gives it. The believer who internalizes the frame discovers what the ṣaḥābah discovered on the Tabūk road: the dread was the worst of the campaign, and the dread was optional. The verse's one hard requirement is honesty about the ranking: the "two goods" math works only for the cause that is actually God's — the believer cannot baptize his career and call every setback a martyrdom; verse 24's audit stands behind verse 52 as its license. But where the cause is His, and the heart has settled its accounts, the verse offers a fearlessness that no threat-actor, online or on any horizon, has ever learned to touch: we are awaiting one of two best things — and while you decide which, we will simply keep working.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:53

> **Say, "Spend willingly or unwillingly; it shall never be accepted from you. Truly you are an iniquitous people."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command That Announces Its Own Refusal**

Verse 53 is one of the Qur'an's most startling constructions: an imperative joined to a negation — *anfiqū ṭawʿan aw karhan* — "spend, willingly or unwillingly" — *lan yutqabbala minkum* — "it will never be accepted from you." The syntax does violence to our sense of purpose: why command a payment that has just been pre-declined? The commentators unfold the rhetoric. On one reading (al-Rāzī records it), the verse is addressed to the hypocrites who had offered, or would offer, contributions to buy proximity or pardon without allegiance — spend if you wish, the verse says, but know the spiritual destination: nothing. Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī connect it to the men who stayed behind from Tabūk and then proposed to fund the next campaign — the wallet offered in place of the self. The refusal of the wallet is thus a teaching about what the community's treasury actually collects: not value transfers but *allegiance made visible*. The deeper logic is the Qur'an's theology of acceptance, stated at its most uncompromising in the story of the two sons of Adam:

> *"God accepts only from the reverent."* (Qur'an 5:27)

**Willingly or Unwillingly: The Two Faces of Hollow Giving**

The verse's pair — *ṭawʿan aw karhan* — maps the two modes in which insincere giving occurs. *Ṭawʿan*, willingly: the gift given gladly — but gladly for the wrong currency: reputation, access, the purchase of a name on the donors' wall, the management of the Prophet's opinion. *Karhan*, unwillingly: the gift squeezed out by embarrassment or the fear of exposure — the alms of the trapped. The verse's refusal covers both because the defect is shared: in neither case does the gift arise from faith — the heart's actual turning toward God — which is the only substance the divine treasury registers. Al-Qurṭubī notes the verse's implicit mercy toward the sincere poor: their tiny, honest gifts outrank the treasury-filling of the unwilling rich — the same doctrine the sūrah will dramatize in verse 79's two-dirham donor and in the Prophet's ﷺ saying:

> *"One dirham outstripped a hundred thousand dirhams."* They said: "How, O Messenger of God?" He said: *"A man had two dirhams and gave one in charity, and a man of great wealth took a hundred thousand from his plenty."* (Recorded in the Sunan collections — Abū Dāwūd and al-Nasāʾī — from Abū Hurayrah; graded ṣaḥīḥ by the tradition's authorities)

**"An Iniquitous People": The Diagnosis Behind the Refusal**

The verse closes with the classification that grounds the refusal: *innakum qawmun fāsiqūn* — "truly you are a transgressing people." *Fisq*, as the sūrah has used it since verse 8, is the exit from obedience's jurisdiction — the root of the date's splitting from its skin. The hypocrites' spending could not be accepted because the spenders had *exited* the covenant the spending pretended to honor: one cannot fund a treaty one is actively violating. The verse thereby fixes a distinction every charity administrator and every believer must hold: the difference between *the gift* and *the giver's account*. Islam accepts gifts from sinners — all givers are sinners — but not from those whose giving is a *continuation* of a settled enmity, a costume worn over the very rebellion it claims to serve.

**The Verse's Awkward Modern Question**

For the modern believer, verse 53's most piercing use is self-directed: it forces the question of what, exactly, my giving is *for*. The same bank transfer can be ṭawʿan in the flesh and karhan in the heart — the automatic donation that buys freedom from guilt, the visible generosity timed for the announcement, the religious giving that functions as the subscription fee of a life otherwise unconverted. The verse does not discourage such giving's *worldly* effects — the poor eat either way, and the fiqh accepts it for public purposes — but it strips it of the one effect the giver secretly wants credited: acceptance. And it holds out, by contrast, the standard of the accepted gift, which the sūrah will paint in verse 92's weepers and verse 104's reassurance: the gift of the believer — flawed, small, but honestly indexed to God — of which the Prophet ﷺ said that it *lands in God's hand before the giver's hand is asked*, as Ibn Masʿūd's report in the commentary of verse 104 will say. Between the two economies the believer chooses daily; and verse 53 stands at the gate of the wrong one, warning — with the accent of a God who declines a fortune and receives two dirhams — that the treasury's only currency is the heart.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:54

> **And naught prevented their spending from being accepted from them, save that they disbelieved in God and in His Messenger, and only come to the prayer lazily, and only spend reluctantly.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Four-Part Autopsy of a Rejected Gift**

Verse 54 performs the autopsy that verse 53 pronounced: *wa-mā manaʿahum an tuqbala minhum nafaqātuhum* — "and naught prevented their spending from being accepted" — and the causes are itemized like findings on a chart. First: *illā annahum kafarū billāhi wa-bi-rasūlih* — "they disbelieved in God and His Messenger" — the root condition, the failed axis. Second: the prayer is approached *wa-hum kisālā* — lazily, the plural of *kasal*, the heaviness of practiced reluctance. Third: their spending is only *wa-hum kārihūn* — with averted hearts, under protest. The three findings state one pathology at three depths: creed (what they believe), worship (how they meet God), and wealth (what they release) — and the order is the diagnosis: the defective creed leaks into the prayer, and the defective prayer leaks into the purse. Al-Qurṭubī notes the forensic elegance: had only the creed been corrupt, the spending might still have been externally useful; but the verse shows the corruption *propagating* — disbelief has a metabolism, and it digests worship and charity into their hollow forms.

**The Anatomy of Lazy Prayer**

The second finding deserves its own anatomy, because the Qur'an returns to it with the precision of a physician tracking a symptom. The hypocrites' prayer is described in Sūrat al-Nisāʾ in the verse this one echoes:

> *"And when they rise for prayer, they rise lazily, showing off to men, and remembering God but little."* (Qur'an 4:142)

And the Prophet ﷺ located the symptom where his community would actually feel it:

> *"The most burdensome prayers for the hypocrites are the night prayer and the dawn prayer; and were they to know what is in them, they would come even if crawling."* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī (657) and Muslim (651) from Abū Hurayrah)

Laziness in ṣalāh is not a scheduling problem; it is a *barometer*. The prayer is the daily meeting of the covenant, and its felt weight discloses, with almost embarrassing accuracy, where the heart's valuation actually sits. The believer who experiences his prayer as cost has, in the very experience, verse 54's diagnosis running live — which is why the tradition treats dullness in worship not primarily as an etiquette failure but as an early-warning system, to be answered not with self-flagellation but with the cure the sūrah will prescribe in verses 112 and 119: repentance, truthfulness, and company.

**Do the Deeds of the Disbeliever Profit at All?**

The verse pressed on the tradition one of its most-debated theological questions: do the apparently good deeds of those outside faith — kindness, generosity, the feeding of the poor — profit nothing in the Hereafter? The verse, with 25:23 and 18:105, anchors the majority answer of the Sunni theologians: deeds are accepted only upon an axis of faith, because the deed's *value* is in its address; a magnificent gift sent to no address returns undelivered. Al-Qurṭubī, as the source commentary records, raises the fair counter-text — does not God reward whomever He wills in this world (17:18)? — and resolves the two registers: the world's rewards (a good reputation, functioning trust, prosperity's machinery) are granted to whatever works; the Hereafter's rewards are a relationship, and relationships are not dispensed to unaddressed envelopes. The tradition also transmits the Prophet's ﷺ answer to ʿĀʾishah's question about a generous man of bad creed — *"his charity will not profit him; not a single day did he say: My Lord, forgive me my sin on the Day of Judgment"* — the tradition cites it in the commentary on this verse: the missing line was not the giving but the *asking*, and the asking would have re-addressed the gift.

**The Verse as a Warning With the Door Still Open**

For the modern believer, verse 54 is the sūrah's most exacting self-audit, and its three findings convert directly into three standing questions: *What do I actually believe about the One I address in prayer?* — the creed finding. *What does my body do when the adhān sounds — rise, negotiate, delay?* — the prayer finding. *What is the temperature of my giving — release or extraction?* — the spending finding. The findings are humbling because they are *correlated*: each leaks into the next, and the believer will discover, over a lifetime, that repair also propagates — a mended creed warms the prayer, and a warmed prayer loosens the hand. The verse's severity, like the sūrah's every severity, is diagnostic rather than condemnatory: verse 102's confessors and verse 118's three will show that every finding on this chart — even its worst — could still, that year, be walked into the clinic of repentance that God had built at the sūrah's center.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:55

> **And let not their wealth or their children impress thee. God desires but to punish them thereby in the life of this world, and that their souls should depart while they are disbelieving.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Do Not Be Impressed: The Command Against Awe of Assets**

Verse 55 addresses the Prophet ﷺ — and through him every believer — with an imperative about perception itself: *wa-lā tuʿjibka amwāluhum wa-lā awlāduhum* — "let not their wealth or their children impress thee." The verb *aʿjaba* means to amaze, to strike with wonder; the command is a veto on a specific *feeling*: the awe that rises involuntarily at the sight of flourishing adversaries — the sleek herds, the armed retainers, the sons like saplings around the tribal tent. The Qur'an knows the feeling's pull because it mapped it in the enemy's own psychology: *"Their wealth and their children will not avail them anything against God"* (58:17; cf. 3:10). The verse then supplies the reframe that dissolves the awe: *innamā yurīdu Allāhu an yuʿadhdhibahum bihā fī al-ḥayāti al-dunyā* — "God desires only to punish them *thereby* in the life of this world" — the very assets that impress are the instruments of their trial.

**Prosperity as Punishment: The Doctrine of Istidrāj**

This is the doctrine the tradition named *istidrāj* — the leading-on by ease: blessings that function, for the ungrateful heart, not as reward but as accumulation of evidence. The Qur'an states the mechanism with its own terrible patience:

> *"And those who cry lies to Our signs — We shall lead them by degrees, whence they know not; and I grant them respite — for My scheme is firm."* (Qur'an 7:182–183)

The commentators read verse 55's "punish them thereby" along two coordinated lines, which the source commentary preserves: the *al-Qurṭubī/Ṭabarī* reading — that the wealth itself becomes the punishment's instrument (the zakāt extracted as levy, the losses, the anxieties of possession, the children as grief and rebellion); and the *Rāzī* reading — deeper and more psychological: attachment to abundance is itself suffering; the possessor who does not acknowledge the Giver lives in the permanent fear of the loss of what he struggled to gain, and that fear is the punishment, served daily. Both readings converge on the verse's cruelest clause: *wa-an tazāgha anfusuhum wa-hum kāfirūn* — "and that their souls depart while they are disbelieving" — the ending that no worldly column can offset. Al-Rāzī notes the doctrinal boundary the verse draws around all worldly success: since the soul's final state is the only terminal value, prosperity that prolongs and deepens bad faith is, on the last audit, a sentence — the most efficient punishment ever devised, because the punished clings to the instrument.

**Wealth and Children: The Twin Attachments**

The pairing — *amwāl* and *awlād* — is the Qur'an's standing binomial for the two deepest terrestrial loves (18:46), the two *fitnah* of 8:28: *"Your wealth and your children are only a trial."* Verse 55's application to the enemies is the negative of its application to the believers: for the grateful, the same binomial is worship's raw material and paradise's investments; for the ingrate, it is the sedative of the soul and the weight of the drowning (63:9: *"Let not your wealth nor your children divert you from the remembrance of God"*). The verse's realism about children is startling to modern sentiment: it does not say children are bad; it says they are *leverage* — the most powerful lever on the heart's priorities, operable in either direction. The parent who compromises creed for the children's comfort has discovered the lever's use by the world; the parent who raises, spends, and prays for them as trust-holding has discovered its use by God.

**The Verse's Twin in Verse 85**

Verse 55 will return, word for word, at verse 85, and al-Rāzī — cited in the source commentary — explains the repetition with the commentator's ear: the attractions of wealth and children are the strongest of this world's magnets, and the sūrah repeats the reframe because the awe it cures regenerates; each audience — those of verse 55's context and the parties of verse 85 — needed it separately. For the modern believer, the verse's use is precise and unglamorous: it is the corrective lens to be fitted whenever the believer catches himself measuring God's favor by visible column — the immoral colleague's promotion, the cruel institution's growth, the scoffing dynasty's ease. The lens shows the column running under a different headline: *accumulating*, not *winning*. And it returns the believer to his own ledger's only terminal line — *the state of the soul at departure* — the line on which, the sūrah insists, all the shimmering totals either closed in loss or, by repentance, can still close in the one gain that swallows the rest.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:56

> **And they swear by God that truly they are of you, but they are not of you. They are a people afraid.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Oath of Belonging**

Verse 56 captures the hypocrites' public posture in a single formula: *wa-yahlifūna billāhi innahum la-minkum* — "they swear by God that they are indeed of you." The oath of membership — by God, no less — is the counterfeit of the covenant the believers actually hold. Al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr read the historical setting plainly: after the community's return from Tabūk, the stayers needed their belonging on record, and the cheapest notarization available was the divine name. The Qur'an had already catalogued this diplomacy of oaths (v. 42, and the coming verses 62, 74, 95, 107 — the sūrah's running exhibit of perjury as social strategy), and the Prophet ﷺ had received its doctrine in a divine warning that separates the accent from the fact:

> *"They swear by God that they are of you, but they are not of you — rather, they are a people afraid."* (Qur'an 9:56 — this verse; cf. the Qur'an's own analysis at 63:1–2 of the munāfiqūn who swear *"we bear witness that you are the Messenger of God,"* and God answers: *"God knows that you are indeed His Messenger, and God bears witness that the hypocrites are liars."*)

**Of You, and Not of You**

The verse's negation — *wa-mā hum minkum* — is the sūrah's severest taxonomy: membership in the community of faith is not an identity that survives the death of its content. The Qur'an does not say they are *bad* members; it denies the membership's existence at the level that matters. The ground is then given in three words of devastating simplicity: *qawmun yafraqūn* — "a people afraid." The root f-r-q is the verb of parting and splitting (the root of *furqān*, the separator) — what fear does to them: it *splits* them from the community whenever the community becomes dangerous, and stitches them back whenever the community becomes powerful. Their belonging is a function of the fear's direction, which is why it can be sworn so sincerely in the season of safety and betrayed so smoothly in the season of risk. Al-Ṭabarī records the reading "afraid of you" — the believers' political strength was the glue of their loyalty — and the modern reader will recognize in an instant the figure the verse photographs: the situational ally, the fair-weather member, the institutional colleague whose conviction tracks the org chart.

**Fear as the Basis of False Belonging**

The verse's psychology deserves respectful attention, because fear — unlike malice — is a half-honest motive. The hypocrites of Madinah were not, mostly, the religion's enemies by conviction; they were its neighbors by cowardice, and their crime was the decision, renewed daily, to let fear mint their identity. The Qur'an's critique is thus aimed less at the fear (which the believers also felt, cf. 3:154) than at the fear's *office*: they promoted fear from a feeling to a policy — the policy of permanent hedging, the insurance portfolio of the soul, held with every party at once. And the verse's diagnosis explains the entire economics of hypocrisy that the sūrah has been auditing: the false oath (this verse), the convenient staying-home (v. 42), the precaution-boast (v. 50) — every entry is an installment of the same premium: the coward's tax, paid in integrity, to every power that might someday matter.

**The Question the Verse Asks the Believer**

For the modern believer, verse 56 functions first as a caution about the community's porous edge — the sūrah's own policy (v. 101's *"you know them not; We know them"*) forbids the inquisition that some eras have attempted against suspected half-members; the verse's knowledge belongs to God, and the community judges conduct, not the interiors the oaths pretend to certify. But the verse's second office is the mirror: *what is my belonging made of?* Every believer's membership is a mixture — love of God, love of the people, habit, and (be honest) a certain amount of fear of being outside — and the verse invites the believer to audit the mixture's ratios and to move, deliberately, the fear's weight onto the only fear that never splits: *ittaqū Allāha haqqa tuqātih* (3:102) — the fear of God, which verse 13 has already ranked above the fear of men. The one whose belonging is built on that fear has a membership that no season of danger can void — because the only party he feared is the party who wrote the covenant.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:57

> **Were they to find a refuge, or caves, or a place to enter, they would turn toward it defiantly.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fantasy of Exit**

Verse 57 sketches, in one conditional sentence, the interior of the coward's life: *law yajidūna maljaʾan aw maghārāt aw muddakhalan* — "were they to find a refuge, or caves, or a place to enter" — *la-wallaw ilayhi wa-hum yajmaḥūn* — "they would bolt toward it, headlong." The three nouns grade the fantasy's desperation: *maljaʾ*, a refuge, a fortress of shelter; *maghārāt*, caves — the fugitive's shelter, and al-Zamakhsharī hears in the root (from the "slipping away" of a wild animal into its den) the specific instinct of prey; *muddakhal*, a bore-hole, a well-shaft, the low opening a man enters on all fours — Ibn Kathīr glosses it as the underground chamber, the last address of the cornered. And the verb of their turning, *yajmaḥūn*, is the verse's cruel masterpiece: the j-m-ḥ root describes the horse that has thrown its rider and runs blind — the ungoverned bolt of an animal that has abandoned steering; some commentators add the ship that no crew can turn. Their departure from the community, in other words, is not an exit but an *escape from their own control* — they do not leave; they flee, and the fleeing is happening already, in the heart, continuously; only the physical exits are missing.

**Why There Is No Cave**

The verse's conditional — *law yajidūna*, "were they to find" — carries an implicit verdict: they do not find it, and cannot. Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī read the clause as the Qur'an's disclosure of their permanent condition: a man who wants out of a covenant with God has not found the world's geography cooperative — Madinah's society, the community's law, the Prophet's patience, and God's Book form a *moral* environment with no border posts; the hypocrite can bolt in his heart all he wants, but he wakes every morning still *inside* — inside the ummah's protection, its prayer-lines, its social web, its zakāt, its peace. The verse thus names, centuries before the term existed, the spiritual truth the tradition would phrase as: *there is no refuge from God except in God* — the sentence this sūrah itself will canonize at verse 118 for the three penitents: *"they deemed there was no refuge from God except to Him."* The hypocrite of verse 57 and the penitent of verse 118 share the same discovery from opposite sides: the geography of God has no outside; the only question is whether one's being cornered ends in flight or in surrender.

**The Claustrophobia of the Double Life**

Read psychologically, the verse is one of the Qur'an's sharpest studies of the divided self's suffering. The hypocrite's project — belonging outwardly, exiting inwardly — requires an *inside* that can be exited; and verse 57's bolt for the caves is the fantasy of the self trying to escape its own awareness: from the community's gaze, from the Book that keeps reading him aloud (v. 64), from the Prophet's presence that keeps knowing (v. 127's glances), from the God who hears the najwā (v. 78). Al-Rāzī, in the entry the source commentary preserves, notes the poignancy: their whole condition is *constriction* — ḍayq, the sūrah's word (vv. 25, 118) for the narrowing that has no physical cause — and the fantasy of the cave is simply constriction dreaming of an exit. The believer who has lived any season of serious hypocrisy — two lives, two ledgers, the exhausting arithmetic of being seen — will recognize the photograph with an uncomfortable familiarity; the Qur'an is describing, with the compassion of a physician rather than the relish of a prosecutor, the *cost* of the arrangement to the one inside it.

**From Bolt to Turn**

For the modern believer, verse 57's pastoral use lies in its quiet contrast with the sūrah's other movements. Every soul in this sūrah is, at some point, cornered: the idolater at the four-month deadline (v. 2), the believer at Ḥunayn's narrow slope (v. 25), the three penitents in the fifty days (v. 118) — and the hypocrites, cornered perpetually, dreaming of caves. The difference among them is never the cornering; it is the *direction of the turn*. The verb *wallaw* — they turned away — is the sūrah's negative of *tāba* — he turned back. Both verbs are turns; one turns away to nowhere, one turns back to the Only One. The verse's final lesson is thus an invitation disguised as a portrait: when the constriction comes, and the heart starts hunting for caves — the distraction, the move, the silence, the screen — the believer can recognize the bolt for what it is, and turn the turn around. There is no cave. There was never a cave. There is only the Refuge the verse 118 found by surrendering the search.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:58

> **And among them some reproach thee over the charitable offerings; if they are given thereof, they are content, but if they are not given thereof, behold, they are angry.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Charity Distribution Politics of Year Nine**

Verse 58 opens the sūrah's zakāt-justice sequence with a case study in donor-capture politics: *wa-minhum man yalmazuka fī al-ṣadaqāt* — "among them are those who reproach thee over the charitable offerings." The verb *lamaza* is to find fault, to taunt with words — the sharpened register of public complaint. The ṣadaqāt in question are the zakāt funds the Prophet ﷺ distributed after Tabūk, and the complainants' logic, as the verse's conditional exposes, is nakedly transactional: *fa-in uʿṭaw minhā raḍū* — "if they are given thereof, they are content" — *wa-in lam yuʿṭaw minhā idhā hum yaskhaṭūn* — "and if they are not given thereof, behold, they are angry." Contentment and rage as the two outputs of a single input variable: allocation to self. Al-Rāzī draws the diagnostic line the source commentary preserves: their happiness is indexed to receipt, not to justice — a criterion that reveals, with one test, that the *cause* they plead (fairness) is not the *cause* they serve (share).

**The Incident Behind the Verse**

The exegetical reports, transmitted by al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī on this verse, preserve the scene: as the Prophet ﷺ was distributing spoils and alms, a man — the accounts vary in naming him; some reports speak of a Bedouin of dubious allegiance — said to him: *"Be just, O Messenger of God!"* — an accusation wrapped in a compliment's clothing. The Prophet's reply has rung through the tradition ever since:

> *"Woe to you! Who will be just if I am not just? You have failed and lost if I am not just."* (Transmitted in the Ṣaḥīḥ collections; al-Bukhārī records the related wording from Jābir ibn ʿAbd Allāh)

The related ḥadīth in the Ṣaḥīḥayn carries the Prophet's rebuke of the man who told him *"fear God, Muhammad"*: *"Who will obey God if I disobey Him?"* The two incidents bracket the same doctrine: the Prophet's justice was above the community's audit precisely because it was under God's.

**Justice From the Distributor's Side**

Verse 58 speaks to the darker half of a universal problem — the complainant's — but the incident it anchors speaks equally to the distributor's side, and the two together form the Qur'an's complete politics of charity. On the leader's side: the Prophet ﷺ took the accusation without retaliation, answered it with argument, and left the man unharmed — because a state that punishes complaint teaches silence, not contentment, and the verses that follow (60, and the Mukhaṭṭam? no — v. 60's eight categories) are his real answer: distribution by revealed rule, not by discretion. On the complainant's side: the verse's conditional x-ray — content if given, furious if not — is the standing self-test of every applicant, every grantee, every community member who has ever dressed appetite in the language of equity. The believer who can say, honestly, *"I would have accepted the other allocation without rage"* has passed; the one whose justice-theory always computes his own share upward is standing in the verse, being read.

**Zakāt Entitlement as Creed, Not Grievance**

The deeper theology of the passage, which verses 59–60 will complete, is the relocation of entitlement: the funds belong to God and His Messenger's *rule*, and the believer's stance toward his non-receipt is itself an act of worship — verse 59's *"God suffices us; God will give us of His bounty."* The Prophet ﷺ had modeled the stance in his own distribution: the sīrah's firm datum is that he gave to some and not to others, by strategy, and called on God as witness. For the modern believer — in an age when every allocation, from inheritances to institutional budgets, mints its complainers — verse 58 offers the clean old test before the grievance is filed: *am I upset because a wrong was done, or because the wrong was done to my line-item?* The verse does not forbid the first (Islamic law built an entire apparatus of complaint and audit); it exposes the second, and leaves the angry unreceived applicant standing where the verse leaves him — at the door of verse 59, where the cure for the anger is not a bigger share but a bigger Sufficer.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:59

> **If only they had been content with what God and His Messenger gave them, and said, "God suffices us. God will give unto us from His Bounty, as will the Messenger. Truly our desire is for God."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Counter-Portrait: Contentment as Creed**

Verse 59 hangs beside verse 58 like a photograph of the road not taken: *wa-law annahum raḍū mā ātāhumu Allāhu wa-rasūluhu* — "if only they had been content with what God and His Messenger gave them." The syntax is the Qur'an's sigh — *wa-law*, the conditional of lost opportunity — and what follows is not a scolding but an instruction in the grammar the complainants never learned: *qālū ḥasbunā Allāhu* — "they would have said: God suffices us" — *sa-yuʾtīnā Allāhu min faḍlihi wa-rasūluhu* — "God will give us of His bounty, and His Messenger" — *innā ilā Allāhi rāghibūn* — "truly to God is our desire." The three clauses are a complete spiritual technology for the moment of non-receipt: a sufficiency-clause (God is enough), a provision-clause (He will give, from bounty, through means including the Messenger), and a desire-clause (the destination of the wanting is God Himself). Al-Rāzī, in the entry the source commentary preserves, reads the verse as the correct ordering of worldly desire: the believer may desire the bounty — the verse does not forbid it — but the desire must run *through* means to the Giver, indexed to Him, so that every allocation, received or not, terminates in the same Address.

**Ḥasbunā Allāh: The Sentence With a Pedigree**

The middle clause's key phrase — *ḥasbunā Allāh* — is one of the great sentences of the tradition, and Ibn ʿAbbās transmitted its biography at the Ṣaḥīḥ's level of authority:

> *"Ḥasbunā Allāhu wa-niʿma al-wakīl — 'God suffices us; how excellent a Guardian' — Ibrāhīm said when he was cast into the fire, and Muḥammad said it when they said: 'The people have gathered against you, so fear them' — and it increased them in faith, and they said: 'God suffices us; how excellent a Guardian.'"* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī (4563) from Ibn ʿAbbās, on 3:173)

The pedigree teaches the phrase's physics: it is not the mantra of those who lack provision but the confession of those who have re-located it. Ibrāhīm in the fire and the Prophet at the mustering of the confederates — the phrase belongs to extremities precisely because its logic is total: if the Sufficer is real, the fire and the army are both just weather.

**Contentment With the Allocation, Not With the Lot**

Precision matters: the verse commands contentment with *what God and His Messenger gave them* — the allocation, the share, the decision — not contentment with the condition of needing. Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī both preserve the balance: the believer works for improvement, asks God for bounty, and even petitions the distributor — but the *inner settlement* is done in advance: whatever the answer, the Sufficer suffices. The distinction cures the two caricatures of riḍā: the passivity that calls resignation contentment, and the grasping that calls ambition faith. The verse's person — the one the sūrah wishes the complainants had been — is fully human: he wanted the share, he asked, he perhaps even appealed; but his peace was never on the table, because it was never in the allocation to begin with.

**The Cure for Allocation Rage**

For the modern believer, verse 59 is the practical prescription for the exact syndrome verse 58 diagnosed, and its three clauses convert directly into a protocol for every season of non-receipt — the salary not granted, the seat not given, the bequest cut short, the grant rejected. First, speak the sufficiency: *ḥasbunā Allāh* — aloud, in Arabic or in its meaning, until the sentence outranks the sting. Second, keep the provision-channel open in expectation: *sa-yuʾtīnā* — He *will* give — from a bounty the current allocator does not administer; the refusal that stung was one nozzle on a pipe whose pressure has not dropped. Third, re-file the desire: *ilā Allāhi rāghibūn* — the wanting itself is re-addressed, and the thing wanted becomes a waypoint on a road whose destination survived the news. The believer who runs this protocol discovers what the ṣaḥābah discovered at the confederates' mustering (3:173): the sentence does not merely console — it *increases faith* — because every non-receipt, handled this way, becomes fresh evidence that the Sufficer holds, and the heart walks away from the closed door richer than it walked in.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:60

> **The charitable offerings are only for the poor, and the indigent, and those working with them, and those whose hearts are [to be] reconciled, and for [ransoming] slaves and for debtors, and in the way of God, and for the traveler: a duty from God. And God is Knowing, Wise.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Revelation's Budget Charter**

Verse 60 is the Qur'an's entire fiscal constitution for the alms in one sentence, and its opening particle — *innamā*, "only" — is restrictive: the ṣadaqāt are *for* these eight categories and no others. The occasion is verse 58's quarrel, and the answer is institutional: distribution disputes are not resolved by better complainants or thicker-skinned distributors but by *revealed rule* — a budget that no governor, including the Prophet ﷺ himself, may edit. Al-Rāzī, as the source commentary notes, argues from the verse's restrictive grammar that the ṣadaqāt here are the obligatory zakāt (voluntary charity remains open to mosques, schools, bridges, and every good work): the eight categories are a *tax code*, not a ceiling on generosity. The closing signature — *farīḍatan mina Allāhi*, "a duty from God... Knowing, Wise" — stamps the ledger with divine authorship, the only signature that ends the argument about whose favorite cause deserves the line-item. And the Prophet ﷺ carried the charter into the field as his instruction to Muʿādh ibn Jabal on the mission to Yemen:

> *"...Teach them that God has prescribed charity upon them: to be taken from their rich and given to their poor."* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī (1395) and Muslim from Ibn ʿAbbās, on the Prophet's dispatch of Muʿādh)

**The Eight Categories, Weighed One by One**

The classical jurists built the whole law of zakāt distribution on this verse's word-order, and each item carries a principle. **The poor (*fuqarāʾ*) and the indigent (*masākīn*)**: two words, one puzzle — the schools debated their difference for centuries. Some held the *faqīr* has partial means and the *miskīn* has nothing; some the reverse; some made one the needy Muslim and the other the needy non-Muslim under protection. The dispute's very existence teaches the verse's first principle: *the poor are a plurality* — poverty has taxonomies, and a serious alms-system knows them. **Those working with them (*al-ʿāmilīna ʿalayhā*)**: the collectors, scribes, and auditors paid from the fund — revelation building a civil service before the community had a bureaucracy, and sanctifying in the process the wages of honest administration. **Those whose hearts are to be reconciled (*muʾallafati qulūbuhum*)**: the contested category — new Muslims consolidating faith, notables whose allegiance steadied their tribes, wavering polytheists drawn toward the community; the jurists of the strong-state era debated whether it lapsed with Islam's strength — al-Qurṭubī records both sides — and the category's survival in the text keeps open the principle that *hearts are infrastructure*, fundable, and that the budget may invest in peace. **For [ransoming] slaves (*fī al-riqāb*)**: the fund buys freedom — a line-item for the dismantling of an institution the Qur'an was strangling by every legal means. **The debtors (*al-ghārimīn)**: those crushed by obligations not of their profligacy — al-Ṭabarī's example is the man whose livelihood burned or whose calamity swallowed him; the Prophet's ﷺ practice, recorded in the tradition the source commentary cites, was to say *"Give him charity"* of the debt-swamped man, and when the donations fell short, to tell the creditors: *"Take what you have found; you have no more than that"* — divine bankruptcy law in one sentence. **In the way of God (*fī sabīli Allāhi*)**: the classic scope is the public defense and the pilgrimage of the stranded — the public goods no household can carry alone. **The traveler (*ibn al-sabīl*)**: the wayfarer cut off from his means — entitled to relief *even if he is rich at home* (Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī), because need, not net worth on paper, is the criterion of the hour.

**What the List Reveals About God's Priorities**

Read as a whole, the verse is a map of the divine social conscience. Of the eight categories, only one (arguably) serves the religious institution itself; the rest are the poor, the enslaved, the indebted, the stranded, the fragile of heart, and the community's defense. The zakāt is not a temple-tax; it is a *circulatory system* — the Qur'an's answer to verse 34's stagnation: gold that sits is branded; gold that moves through these eight channels is transformed into freedom, relief, and social peace. And the categories' *order* has been read by the jurists as deliberate: relief of need first, twice over (poor, indigent), before any institutional claim. A community's budget is its creed made arithmetic, and verse 60 wrote the creed's fiscal clause: the wealth of the rich, flowing on schedule, to the eight doors where God has chosen to be found.

**Farīḍah Min Allāh: The Signature That Ends the Argument**

The verse's final words — *a duty from God; and God is Knowing, Wise* — are not decoration; they are the clause that makes the charter work. The quarrel of verse 58 was, at bottom, a quarrel about authority: who says what the money is for? The verse answers with a signature no appeal can reach. For the modern believer, verse 60 is therefore two things at once. Historically, it is the root of fourteen centuries of Islamic public finance — the waqf endowments, the treasury of the caliphate, the relief codes of half the world's civilizations. Personally, it is a standing audit of one's own private "budget": the believer who itemizes his giving against the eight doors will discover, usually, a portfolio of preferences where revelation had drawn a map of needs — and the verse's quiet claim, standing behind the audit, is that the Giver of the wealth knows, better than the holder, where it was always meant to go. Ibn ʿAjībah's spiritual reading of the verse completes the circle: even the soul's distributions have their eight doors — the poor in spirit, the indebted by sin, the travelers toward God — and the alms of the heart, like the alms of the purse, are *only* for those whom the Owner lists.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:61

> **And among them are those who torment the Prophet, and say, "He is an ear." Say, "An ear that is good for you. He believes in God and he has faith in the believers, and he is a mercy to those among you who believe." And [as for] those who torment the Messenger of God, theirs shall be a painful punishment.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Insult Decoded: "He Is All Ear"**

Verse 61 preserves the hypocrites' nickname for the Prophet ﷺ with clinical accuracy: *huwa udhun* — "he is an ear." Al-Rāzī's gloss, which the source commentary preserves, decodes it: the taunt means *he is all hearing and no judgment* — a man who accepts whatever he is told, swayed by every talebearer and last speaker. Being called an "ear," he observes, is like calling a spy an "eye" — one faculty made into the whole man, and the one faculty a leader must not over-trust. Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī record the speakers' boast behind it: *"We say what we wish, then come to him and swear to it, and he believes us"* — the Prophet's credulity, as they saw it, was a vulnerability to be farmed. The insult is thus a psychological operation: strip the leader's listening — his patience with every complainant, his refusal to prejudge — of its dignity, recast it as weakness, and thereby license the whisperers to keep whispering.

**God's Reversal: The Ear That Is Good for You**

The Qur'an's reply is one of the Book's great inversions: *qul udhun khayrun lakum* — "say: an ear that is good for you." God does not deny the listening; He certifies it — the ear in question hears *what is good*, not what is evil, as al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī read the elliptical phrase: he listens in order to believe the best, to verify before he judges, to leave every person the room to be honest. Then the verse states the listening's foundations in two clauses of the verb āmana: *yuʾminu billāhi* — "he believes in God" (the vertical trust) — *wa-yuʾminu lil-muʾminīn* — "he has faith in the believers" — the second clause's preposition (*li-* rather than *bi-*) is read by Ṭabarī and Rāzī as: *he believes the believers* — when a believer speaks, he takes him at his word. The Prophet's trust was not naivety; it was *policy*: a community whose leader assumes lying will become a community that lies to him; a community whose leader extends credit of belief becomes, measurably, more worth it. The verse's third clause crowns the portrait: *raḥmatun lil-muʾminīn* — "a mercy to those of you who believe" — the same clause that will seal the sūrah at verse 128, where the mercy is named again. The sūrah thus frames itself with the Prophet's mercy: near the beginning, as the answer to mockery; at the end, as the final memory.

**Tormenting the Prophet as a Listed Crime**

The verse's closing warning — *alladhīna yuʾdhūna rasūla Allāhi lahum ʿadhābun alīm* — "those who torment the Messenger of God, theirs is a painful punishment" — places personal harm to the Prophet among the gravest of charges, and the tradition weighed it with the same care it weighed verse 12's vilification: ʿĀʾishah reported the operating rule of his own household and court:

> *"The Messenger of God ﷺ never took revenge for himself in any matter unless the sanctity of God was violated; when God's sanctity was violated, he never held back."* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī (3560) and Muslim (2327) from ʿĀʾishah)

The taunters of verse 61 were, therefore, not struck down; they were answered by revelation and left to the accounting of God — the Prophet ﷺ enduring, as he had always endured, the mockery that the Qur'an itself records his enemies saying of him. The danger of *yuʾdhūna* is real — the verb runs from minor annoyance to grave injury — but its court, in this world, was the Prophet's patience, and its sentence, in the next, is God's alone.

**Listening as Strength: The Leadership Lesson**

For the modern believer, verse 61 is a complete seminar on the much-mocked virtue of listening. Every organization knows the taunt in its modern dialect: the accessible leader is "a pushover," the manager who hears every side is "indecisive," the parent who listens is "soft." The verse counters with the distinction the mockers always miss: there is an ear that is *khayrun lakum* — good *for you* — an ear joined to faith in God, credit-of-belief for the honest, and mercy for the community; and there is, admittedly, an ear that is merely suggestible — the difference lies not in the hearing but in the *anchoring*. The Prophet ﷺ listened without being governed by the listening: his rulings followed revelation and justice, not the last lobby; but no one left his audience unheard. The believer who leads anything — a family, a team, a congregation — can take the verse as a job description and its insult as a badge: to be called "an ear" by people who wish you would listen less is, on the verse's own terms, to be described accurately and complimented spiritually; and the punishment clause stands, quietly, over the entire industry of making good listeners ashamed of their gift.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:62

> **They swear by God to you, to please you. But God and His Messenger are worthier of being pleased by them, if they are believers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Oaths Aimed at the Wrong Audience**

Verse 62 completes the scene verse 61 opened: the taunters, having been answered by revelation, pivot to damage control — *yahlifūna billāhi lakum* — "they swear by God *to you*" — *li-yurḍūkum* — "in order to please you." The second-person plural, the commentators note, is deliberate: the oaths were addressed not only to the Prophet but to the body of believers — the community whose opinion, now that the sūrah had read their secrets aloud, had become cold. And the Qur'an's correction is a single redirect: *fa-innā Allāha wa-rasūlahu aḥaqqu an yurḍūhu* — "God and His Messenger are worthier of being pleased by them, if they are believers." The comparative (*aḥaqqu*, worthier) is the verse's whole doctrine in one declension: *someone is always being pleased*; the only question under audit is the ranking of the audience.

**The Hierarchy of Audiences**

The verse is the Qur'an's most compact statement of what the tradition would call *ikhlāṣ* applied socially: the believer's conduct is performed, ultimately, before one Audience, and all human audiences are secondary courts whose verdicts matter only inside the primary one. The Prophet ﷺ drew the same line for the community in the ḥadīth of intention, and its framing of social approval is exact:

> *"Whoever seeks the pleasure of God by the anger of men, God is pleased with him and suffices him; and whoever seeks the pleasure of men by the anger of God, God is angry with him and makes men angry with him."* (Transmitted in the ḥadīth collections from Abū Hurayrah with a ḥasan chain; the wording is close to this)

Verse 62 states the doctrine from the community's side: the hypocrites worked the inverse economy — God's displeasure purchased at the price of men's comfort — and the verse prices the trade in one clause: it is not worth it, *if they are believers*.

**The Conditional That Doubles as an Invitation**

The verse ends — like verses 44's and 23's conditionals — with a trailing test: *in kānū muʾminīn* — "if they are believers." The conditional is not a sentence passed; it is a mirror offered. The hypocrites claimed the creed; the verse quietly defines the creed's observable signature: *whose displeasure do you actually fear, and whose pleasure do you actually labor for?* Al-Qurṭubī notes the verse's mercy inside its rebuke: it does not say "they are not believers"; it says the believers' priority is knowable — and the door of re-prioritization stands open for anyone, including an oath-swearing crowd-pleaser, willing to walk through it. The sūrah's later verses will show exactly such walks: verse 102's confessional party, verse 118's three — men who had spent years pleasing the wrong audience and then, in one season, re-addressed their entire account.

**Pleasing People Without Worshiping Them**

The believer's modern use of verse 62 requires its careful boundary, because the verse does not command rudeness toward human approval. The Prophet ﷺ pleased people constantly — with gifts, generosity, gentleness, and argument; the Qur'an itself commands *kindness to parents* even in disagreement (17:23) and winning hearts (ṣadaqah's fourth category, v. 60). What the verse polices is the *address of the self*: courtesy performed for God can absorb human displeasure when the two collide; courtesy performed *for men* collapses at the first collision, because it has no reserve account. The professional who tells the truth and loses the promotion, the student who declines the shortcut and loses the crowd, the parent who holds the line and loses the tantrum's applause — each has run verse 62's arithmetic and chosen the worthier audience. And the verse's quiet promise, echoed by the ḥadīth above, is the only solvency guarantee in the social economy: the pleasure of God, once held, *suffices* — and its compound interest is paid in a currency no human court can print or devalue.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:63

> **Do they know not that whosoever opposes God and His Messenger, surely for him shall be the Fire of Hell, to abide therein? That is the great disgrace.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question That Restores Gravity**

Verse 63 follows the crowd-pleasing oaths of verse 62 with a question of almost physical weight: *a-wa-lam yaʿlamū* — "do they not *know*?" The hypocrites have been managing perceptions — nicknames, oaths, audiences — as though the entire conflict were social; the verse interrupts the theater with the metaphysical fact: *anna man yuḥāddu Allāha wa-rasūlahu* — "that whosoever *opposes* God and His Messenger" — *fa-inna lahu nāra jahannama khālidan fīhā* — "for him is the Fire of Hell, abiding therein." The verb *muḥāddah* (from ḥ-d-d, the boundary) is the Qur'an's strongest term of enmity: to set oneself at the *limit* against someone, to take up a position on the boundary-line as an adversary — not the drift of lukewarm faith (the sūrah has other words for that), but deliberate contraposition. The Qur'an uses the same verb where the stakes are stated plainly: *"Those who oppose God and His Messenger — they are among the most humiliated"* (58:20). Verse 63's question, in context, is thus the bill arriving for the mockery of verse 61 and the perjury of verse 62: do the performers not know what stage they are actually standing on?

**Fire and Disgrace: The Two Registers of Hell**

The verse's closing distinction deserves the attention the tradition gave it: *dhālika huwa al-khizyu al-ʿaẓīm* — "that is the great disgrace." Why add disgrace to fire? Al-Qurṭubī, as the source commentary preserves, distinguishes the two registers of the punishment: the Fire is the *bodily* penalty; the khizy is the *moral* one — the public unraveling of every persona. The hypocrite's whole project, from verse 56 onward, was face: oaths to keep it, audiences to win it, mockery to spend it. The khizy is the face's final audit — the day the mask is lifted, before God and the assembled worlds, by the Owner of the records (v. 94: *"God has informed us"*). The Qur'an's psychology of shame is precise: shame is the social self's currency, and hell's disgrace is the discovery that the only social self that ever mattered was the one standing before God — and it was bankrupt. Modern readers who find the imagery harsh should notice what it replaces: in the Qur'an, the exposure is *true*, not arbitrary — the disgrace consists entirely of accurate information finally becoming public, the double life revealed as single, the audience-pleasing revealed as God-opposing.

**Opposing the Messenger as Opposing God**

The verse's construction — "whoever opposes God *and His Messenger*" — encodes the doctrine the sūrah has been building all along: the two opponents are one case. To work against the Messenger is not to work against a man; it is to work against the One who sent him — the same merger the Qur'an states in the pledge verse (48:10) and in the severity of 4:65, where belief itself is tested by submission to the Prophet's judgment. The theology is not personality-cult; it is *transmission*: the Messenger is the address through which God's claim reaches the world, and the address cannot be attacked without the Sender receiving the message. For the believer, the doctrine runs in the positive: to honor, obey, and love the Prophet ﷺ (v. 24's ranking; v. 61's defense) is simply the practical form of honoring God — there is no separate corridor.

**Knowing as the Standard**

The verse's framing question — "do they not *know*?" — sets the standard of accountability and, paradoxically, the standard of hope. The Fire of Hell is for the one who *knew* and set himself against; the ignorance that excuses is real, and the Qur'an guards it (4:165: *"We never punish until We send a messenger"*; cf. 17:15). The hypocrites of the sūrah occupied the most dangerous position in the moral universe: not ignorance of the truth, but knowledge of it, managed — the heard revelation, the witnessed miracles, the recited secrets (v. 64's dread of exposure) — all filed under reputation-management. Verse 63's question is therefore also its warning to every later believer: the heart that *knows* and performs otherwise is not halfway between belief and unbelief; it is in muḥāddah's waiting room, one decision at a time, and the exit sign on that room reads the same in every century: repentance — the turning the sūrah keeps opening at every door.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:64

> **The hypocrites dread lest a sūrah be sent down against them, informing them of that which is in their hearts. Say, "Go on mocking. Truly God will bring forth what you dread."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Surveillance They Feared Was Their Own Conscience**

Verse 64 is the psychological key to the entire hypocrite-cycle: *yaḥdharu al-munāfiqūna an tunazzala ʿalayhim sūratun tunabbiʾuhum bimā fī qulūbihim* — "the hypocrites dread lest a sūrah be sent down against them, informing them of what is in their hearts." The dread is specific and well-founded: it had happened — repeatedly. The Qur'an had quoted their private sayings (v. 49's "do not tempt me," v. 61's "he is an ear"), their oaths, their jokes; and each revelation of a secret tightened the circle of the possible: either the Prophet ﷺ was informed by God — in which case the entire religious claim was true and their position was madness — or someone among them was informing — in which case no gathering, no whisper, was safe. Al-Rāzī, in the analysis the source commentary preserves, shows the trap from inside: some hypocrites did possess a flicker of conviction that he was truly a prophet, but jealousy and obstinacy kept them from yielding; others, watching secret after secret surface, simply lived in surveillance-state dread. Either way, the verse discloses the cost of their arrangement that no external audit could: the double life is *its own* occupation — no rest on either side of the mask.

**"Go On Mocking": The Coldest Sentence in the Sūrah**

God's instruction to the Prophet ﷺ is stark: *sāhirū* — "go on mocking" (the imperative of s-h-r in its sense of idle sport) — *inna Allāha mukhrijun mā taḥdharūn* — "God will bring out what you dread." The imperative is not permission but *resignation with a deadline*: proceed with the entertainment; the exposure you fear is already scheduled. The verse's grammar of dread-to-fulfillment is the Qur'an's doctrine of exposure, stated elsewhere as law: *"Or do those in whose hearts is a disease think that God will not bring their malice to light?"* (47:29–30). And the fulfillment, in the event, took the exact form the dread imagined: verse 65 will quote the joke itself; verses 74, 95, and 101 will read the hearts' contents; and verse 101 will add the sentence that completed the surveillance: *"You know them not; We know them."* The community never got the dossier — God kept it — but the *hypocrites* got everything they feared: the knowledge that it existed.

**Mockery as the Hypocrite's Native Language**

The verse's command ("go on mocking") also documents, incidentally, the hypocrites' characteristic *mode* — and the Qur'an's catalogue of it is extensive (2:14–15; 4:140; 5:57–58; 6:5; 31:6; 45:9). Mockery is the mode of the half-believer by structural necessity: the sincere either believe or argue; the double-lifer can neither openly deny (the audience forbids it) nor honestly affirm (the heart forbids it), so he inhabits the third register — the sneer, the wink, the joke that can be disowned. Verse 64's insight is that the mockery is not incidental to their condition; it is its exhaust — the sound a heart makes when it knows too much to deny and wants too much to affirm. And the Qur'an's single-sentence rebuttal stands over the industry forever: mock on; the God you joke about is the God who runs the archive.

**The Verse as a Mirror for the Secret-Keeper in Everyone**

For the modern believer, verse 64's use is not — must not be — a warrant for paranoid readings of one's neighbors ("their hearts must be hiding something"); verse 101 explicitly withholds the community's jurisdiction over interiors. Its proper address is the self. Everyone with an unwitnessed life — the browser history, the private ledger, the resentments rehearsed in the car — knows the small dread the verse magnifies: *what if it were read aloud?* The believer's tradition made a discipline of exactly that rehearsal: the Prophet ﷺ spoke of the prayer of the night — *"the most excellent prayer after the prescribed ones is the prayer of the middle of the night"* — and the tradition's settled counsel is the ḥadīth qudsī of the seven shaded, among them *"a man who remembers God in private and his eyes overflow"* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī (660) and Muslim (1031) from Abū Hurayrah) — the opposite of verse 64's dread: the secret life that *longs* for its eventual publicity, lived already before God. The difference between the two secret-keepers is the whole difference the verse draws: the hypocrite's hidden room dreads the light; the believer's hidden room was built for it.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:65

> **And if thou askest them, they will surely say, "We were only engaging [in vain talk] and playing." Say, "Is it God, His signs, and His Messenger you were mocking?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Joke That Made Revelation**

Verse 65 preserves an incident so specific it reads like a transcript, and the exegetical reports — al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī transmit them, as the source commentary summarizes — set the scene on the road to Tabūk, among the riders of the campaign. In a moment of ease, one of the party — the reports name a man of the munāfiqūn — let the mask slip in verse:

> *"I have seen no people with greater appetite, falser tongues, or more cowardice in the field than these reciters of ours."*

A Companion heard it and carried it to the Prophet ﷺ — only to find that the revelation had arrived before him. The joker, summoned, mounted his defense in the words verse 65 quotes: *innamā naḥnu nakhūḍu wa-nalʿab* — "we were only plunging into talk and playing" — the idiom of idle banter, the "just kidding" of the seventh century. And the Prophet ﷺ recited the answer that had already descended, and — the reports note with awe — *said and did no more*: no punishment, no confiscation, no tribunal; the verse itself was the sentence, and its question the verdict that mattered.

**The Qur'an's Doctrine of the "Just Kidding" Defense**

The verse's interrogative — *a-bi-llāhi wa-āyātihi wa-rasūlihi kuntum tazharūn* — "is it God, His signs, and His Messenger you were mocking?" — dismantles the defense in one syntactic move. The Qur'an does not dispute that it was play; it establishes what was the *object* of the play. Mockery, unlike murder, leaves no corpus delicti on the ground; its entire substance is its target — and the target here is named in the accusative: God, His signs (the verses being recited around the camp), His Messenger. The three objects close the loopholes in order: not the men mocked for their appetite and cowardice — the sneer at the *reciters* was a sneer at the recited; not the community's manners — its creed. Verse 66 will complete the legal finding: the classification of the act, once its object is established, is not "bad manners" but *disbelief after belief*. The tradition built on these verses its settled ethics of religious satire: jest that targets God, revelation, or the Prophet is not located in the jurisdiction of humor at all — because humor's license has never extended to the object of worship; and the Prophet's own practice, rich in laughter and play within lawful bounds, marks the boundary from the other side: he raced ʿĀʾishah and let her win, then win again on a later day; he answered the elderly woman who asked to be carried to Paradise with the gentle jest that no old woman would enter it — meaning, by its intent, that she would enter it young; his jokes, the collections record, were true and gentle, never at the expense of the sacred and never of the weak.

**The Slip as Confession**

Al-Rāzī's analysis of the incident — preserved in the source commentary — deserves its own meditation: the joke was the confession. However much the joker would have preferred, in the cold light of the summons, to have it back, the words had come out of a place in him where they were *stored*; mockery that had never lived in the heart does not surface in a relaxed moment on a hard road. This is the Qur'an's deep anthropology of speech: *"What fills the vessel spills from it"* is the idiom the tradition built on the like of verse 65 — the loose tongue as the overflow-gauge of the sealed heart. Modern psychology calls it the slip; the Qur'an simply reads it: the campfire is the confession booth of the unguarded. And the believer's lesson runs both ways: guard the storage, and you will not need to manage the spills — the man whose private speech about God and His Messenger is reverent has no dangerous material in the tank when the road gets long and the tongue gets loose.

**Why Only a Question Was the Punishment**

The sequel's restraint is doctrine, not neglect.
