The Prophet ﷺ recited the verses and let the man go — because the sūrah's entire policy toward the hypocrites (vv. 46, 101, 106) is exposure-of-record, not purge-of-persons: the community's unity, its legal order, and God's exclusive knowledge of hearts were all better served by a public text than a public execution. Verse 66 will even announce the split verdict to come — some pardoned, some punished — and history records the incident's man among the repentant of the second category. For the modern believer, verse 65's lasting office is its portrait of how seriously God takes the *tone* of a soul: creeds are written in jokes as much as in testaments, and the believer who audits his own humor — the group-chat's targets, the stand-up's subjects, the memes that get shared at 1 a.m. — is performing the verse's question on himself before revelation needs to: *what, exactly, is being mocked here?* The question, asked early enough, is a mercy; asked only when the recording is played back, it is verse 65's exact scene — the defense of play, and the divine correction of the record.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:66

> **Make no excuses. You disbelieved after having believed. If We pardon a group of you, We shall punish another group for having been guilty.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verdict on the "Play"**

Verse 66 delivers the juridical finding on the campfire mockery in two imperatives that permit no reply: *lā taʿtadhirū* — "make no excuses" — *qad kafartum baʿda īmānikum* — "you have disbelieved after your belief." The classification is shocking by design: what began as banter ends in the technical register of apostasy's grammar. The commentators, as the source commentary records, read the sentence with careful discrimination. Al-Rāzī and those with him: mockery of God, His signs, and His Messenger is itself — in a settled, deliberate form — disbelief, because it positions the mocker at the boundary-line against (muḥāddah, v. 63) the mocked; the sneer at the object of worship is the worship's negation wearing a smile. The verb sequence — disbelieved *after* believing — also settles the verse's range: it addresses men whose Islam had been real enough to be left (the "play" was the sound of its leaving), not the never-convinced. Ibn Kathīr notes the Qur'an's repeated use of this exact grammar — its sibling is the verse *"Do you believe in part of the Book and disbelieve in part?"* (2:85) — the category of the *partial* believer, whose creed holds until the night it is joked about.

**The Two Groups: The Split Verdict**

The verse's second sentence softens the first with a mystery: *in naʿfu ʿan ṭāʾifatin minkum nuʿadhdhib ṭāʾifah* — "if We pardon a group of you, We shall punish another group, for they were criminals." The sentence ascribes to God — not to the Prophet, not to the court — the sorting of the mockers, and the commentators, as the source commentary preserves, offer the two main readings of the split. One school: the two groups are the *initiators* of the mockery and the *listeners who laughed* — guilt graded by role, an audacious doctrine in any century (the audience as accomplice). The other, preferred by al-Ṭabarī and consistent with the sūrah's whole economy: the groups are the *repentant* and the *obstinate* — pardon for the ones who would turn (and verse 102's confessors and verse 118's three are the walk-ons of this reading), punishment for the ones who would manage, excuse, and re-offend. The two readings share the operative principle: within a single shared act, the hearts are not single — and God's verdicts, unlike human courts', can split the crowd.

**"Make No Excuses": The Theology of a Closed File**

The opening imperative deserves its own meditation, because it announces a state of the soul the Qur'an elsewhere describes with the same finality: the moment when management ends. Excuses — ʿudhr in the language of the jurists — are honored throughout the Qur'an (v. 91's sincere excused; 24:62's leave-seekers heard); but there is a point in the anatomy of wrongdoing where the excuse is not a bridge back but a *continuation of the crime in the grammar of innocence* — and verse 66 announces that the mockers had crossed it. Al-Qurṭubī notes the pastoral danger of the moment: the first lie after exposure ("we were only playing") had already been told; a second would have hardened the file permanently. The verse's harshness is thus a mercy engineered at the last possible second: close the excuse-file *now*, and the door of verse 102 is still open; keep the file open, and the excuses will consume the years that repentance needed. The tradition's formulation of the principle — *the believer's repentance is his judge's best witness* — is latent in the verse's structure: those pardoned will be the ones who stop explaining.

**The Modern Border of the Joke**

For the believer today, verses 65–66 together draw a border that no generation has found comfortable, least of all this one, whose media economy runs on transgression-as-comedy. The border is not "no laughter" — the Prophet's ﷺ Sunnah is full of it — but *object and intent*: what is being denied in the laugh, and does the laugher mean the denial? An honest application requires the same discrimination the verse models: the parody of corrupt clerics is not the parody of prophecy; the joke about one's own weakness in prayer is not the joke about prayer; satire that punctures hypocrisy shares God's own register (v. 65's "mock on" to the mockers; 2:15), while satire that punctures the sacred is verse 65's crime. The believer who cannot tell which side of the line his laughter stands on can run the verse's own test — ask *of what is this the denial?* — and if the honest answer is God, His signs, or His Messenger, then verse 66's file is open, its verdict written in the past tense, and its pardon, as ever in this sūrah, waits on exactly one thing: the closing of the excuse-file and the turning back of the ones who had, for one campfire's laughter, walked out of the covenant they were riding under.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:67

> **The hypocrites, men and women, are like unto one another, enjoining wrong, forbidding right, clutching their hands shut. They forgot God; so He forgot them. Truly the hypocrites are iniquitous.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Photographic Negative of the Believer**

Verse 67 is the anatomical chart of hypocrisy rendered in a single frame, and the sūrah has engineered it deliberately: verse 71 will photograph the believers in the same grammar — men and women, enjoining and forbidding, the paired duties — so that the two verses can be laid over each other like a photograph and its negative. *Al-munāfiqūna wa-l-munāfiqātu baʿḍuhum min baʿḍ* — "the hypocrite men and hypocrite women are of one another" — the idiom of kinship and kind: al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī read it as "they are of their own sort," a species that recognizes itself, breeds true, and circulates its own values. Then the inversion, in the exact terms of the Qur'an's greatest social command: *yaʾmurūna bil-munkari wa-yanhawna ʿani al-maʿrūfi* — "they enjoin wrong and forbid right" — the sacred formula of *al-amr bil-maʿrūf wa-l-nahy ʿan al-munkar* (3:104, 110, 114; 22:41) turned inside out. The hypocrite is not the absence of moral energy; he is its *inversion*: he campaigns — for the wrong, quietly normalizing it among his set, and against the right, mocking those who practice it. The believing community's signature activity appears in the hypocrite's body, running in reverse.

**The Clutched Hands and the Forgotten God**

The third trait is gestural: *wa-yaqbiḍūna aydiyahum* — "and they clutch their hands shut" — the fist pressed to the chest, the physical signature of refusal to spend. The commentators read the gesture in both directions, as the source commentary records: the hand kept from *fighting* and from *giving* — the two expenditures (anfus, amwāl) the sūrah has joined since verse 20 — withdrawn in one motion. Then comes the verse's heart, the most chilling reciprocity in the Book: *nasū Allāha fa-ansāhum* — "they forgot God, so He forgot them." Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī unfold the idiom's mercy-bracket: God does not forget as creatures forget — *"My Lord never errs, nor forgets"* (20:52) — the "forgetting" is the Qur'an's permitted anthropomorphism of *abandonment*: they omitted God from their remembering, so He omitted them from His care, guidance, and gentleness. The doctrine is stated at its most explicit in the verse the commentators cite: *"This Day We forget you as you forgot the meeting of this your Day"* (45:34). The reciprocity is exact to the atom: the measure of God's presence in a life is the measure of the life's remembrance — nothing withheld on either side but what was withheld on the other.

**A Society That Runs on Inverted Values**

The verse's sociology deserves slow reading, because it describes something more systematic than individual vice: *an environment*. Enjoining wrong is a social act — it requires an audience; forbidding right is influence-work — it requires trusted status. The hypocrites of Madinah constituted what modern sociology would call a counter-community: their seating in the mosque's back rows, their salon of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ubayy, their economy of excuses and mockery (vv. 61–66) — a parallel moral order in which cowardice was prudence, stinginess was sense, and the believers' sacrifices were material for jokes. Every generation since has known the type of space: the group chat where integrity is cringe, the professional culture where the honest are naive and the crooked are clever, the family where prayer is mocked and compromise admired. Verse 67 is its charter of incorporation — and its articles are always the same three: normalize the wrong, marginalize the right, and keep the wallet shut against the community's needs.

**The Gendered Pairing That Carries a Doctrine**

Note — before verse 71 makes it explicit — that the verse names *men and women* in parallel: hypocrisy, like faith, is not gendered. The women of the hypocrite class appear throughout the sūrah's events (the lamp-on-the-stick signaling of the confederates' sympathizers; the mosque-of-Ḍirār donors; verse 67's own class), and the Qur'an's decision to legislate them into the same sentence as the men establishes, from the divine side, the principle verse 71 will complete: moral agency — for virtue or vice — is identically structured in both halves of humanity. The believer's takeaway from verse 67 is finally diagnostic: *which system am I helping to normalize?* — because every member of any community is, at every hour, either enforcing its maʿrūf or marketing its munkar, and the fist — closed or open — is the tell. The one who has forgotten God is not awaiting punishment; he is, by the verse's terrible grammar, already living inside it: unremembered by the only Remembrance that ever held a heart together.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:68

> **Verily God has promised the hypocrites, men and women, and the disbelievers the Fire of Hell, to abide therein. It shall suffice them. God curses them, and theirs shall be a lasting punishment.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Promise From the Other Side**

Verse 68 completes verse 67's anatomy with its prognosis, and the two verses mirror, clause for clause, the promise made to the believers at verse 72 — gardens, contentment, abiding — rendered in negative. *Waʿada Allāhu al-munāfiqīna wa-l-munāfiqāti wa-l-kāfirīna nāra jahannama* — "God has promised the hypocrites, men and women, and the disbelievers, the Fire of Hell" — and the verb is the covenant-verb, *waʿada*, the same word that carries the promises of Paradise. The Qur'an is deliberate about the symmetry: God's word binds in both directions; the promise of mercy and the promise of justice are one speech-act, guaranteed by one Speaker. Verse 67's men-and-women pairing persists — both sexes filed under one promise — and the hypocrites are now classed with the open disbelievers, the sūrah having completed, in verses 65–66, the demonstration of why: the concealed and the declared rejection are one creed with two publicists.

**"It Shall Suffice Them": The Arithmetic of Sufficiency**

The verse's second clause has the ring of a dark echo across the sūrah: *hiya ḥasbuhum* — "it suffices them." The believer's sufficiency is God (ḥasbunā Allāhu, v. 59); the hypocrite's sufficiency is the Fire — the thing itself, entire, needing no supplement. Al-Qurṭubī hears in the clause both the adequacy of the punishment (no further torment required; the Fire is complete) and the *replacement* it performs: those who refused to make God their sufficiency receive a sufficiency of another order — the destiny they chose, delivered without remainder. The Qur'an uses the construction at 4:55 (*"Hell suffices as a blaze"*) and 58:8 (*"Hell suffices them, burning therein — what an evil journey's end"*), and the latter's idiom — the journey's end, *masīr* — is the quiet horror of the whole doctrine: the hypocrites thought they were managing a residence; they were on a road, and the road had a destination all along, and the destination is *enough*.

**The Curse as Distance**

*Wa-laʿanahumu Allāhu* — "and God curses them" — is the verse's third clause, and the tradition is careful with the verb: laʿn in the Qur'an's usage is not a magical imprecation but the decree of removal — to be banished from the mercy's radius, expelled from the circle of blessing. Al-Qurṭubī, on the verses where God curses (2:159, 161), defines it as *al-ibʿād ʿan al-raḥmah* — the being-far-from-mercy. The definition reconciles the clause with everything else the sūrah says of God's mercy (v. 27's relenting; v. 104's acceptance of repentance): the curse is not a temper; it is a location — the far place where the persistently self-expelled finally, irrevocably, are. That is why verse 68's promise falls on the class *as finally constituted*: those who died upon the file — the unrepentant managers, the excuse-makers to the end — and why the sūrah can hold verse 68 beside verse 102's *"it may be God will relent to them"* without contradiction: the promise names the end-state of the settled; the door names everyone short of it.

**The Lasting Punishment and the Lesson of Its Duration**

The verse closes: *wa-lahum ʿadhābun muqīm* — "theirs is a lasting punishment" — *muqīm*, from the verb to reside, to remain in place: the punishment itself has settled; it has moved in. Ibn Kathīr notes the adjective's terror for people whose entire career was motion — the oscillation of verse 45 (*fī miryatihim yaʿridūn*), the bolting of verse 57 (*yajmaḥūn*), the hastening to and fro of verse 47 — the hypocrites' signature was restlessness between two addresses, and their sentence is the one thing they never tried: to stay. For the believer, verse 68's pastoral office is its *contrast-function*: read immediately after verse 67's forgetting-God, it shows what the reciprocal forgetting, pursued to its term, finally means — and it retroactively prices every hour of remembrance, every open hand, every ounce of courage in the maʿrūf: those are the coins of the only sufficiency that will not, on the Day, be described as *enough* in the Fire's accent. The God who promises is the God who relents (v. 104) — and the two facts together leave the reader of verses 67–68 exactly where the sūrah wants him: choosing, while the choice is still his, which promise to be living toward.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:69

> **[Remember] those who were before you. They were mightier than you in power, and greater in wealth and children. So they enjoyed their share, and you enjoyed your share, just as those before you enjoyed their share. And you have engaged [in vain talk] as they engaged [in vain talk]. It is they whose deeds came to naught in this world and in the Hereafter, and it is they who are the losers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Warning in the Comparative**

Verse 69 turns from the verdict (v. 68) to the lesson beneath it: *ka-alladhīna min qablikum* — "like those before you" — and the comparison is engineered to wound the pride that might resist it: they were *mightier in power* (*ashadda baʾs*), *greater in wealth and children* (*aktharu amwālan wa-awlād*) — everything, in short, that the Arabs of the year 9 counted as security, possessed in superlative degree by the nations already destroyed. The Qur'an argues from the archaeological record: if ḥubṭ — the voiding of deeds — is what became of mightier hands and fuller treasuries, on what ground do the current imitators stand? The argument recurs through the Book as a standing refutation of civilizational complacency:

> *"Yet never did a messenger come to them but they mocked him. So We destroyed those mightier than them in prowess, and the example of the ancients has passed away."* (Qur'an 43:7–8; cf. 28:78; 30:9; 40:21; 50:36)

**The Prophet's Ḥadīth of the Lizard Hole**

The verse's diagnosis of imitation — *"you have plunged as they plunged"* — received from the Prophet ﷺ its most famous commentary, in the ḥadīth of the lizard:

> *"By Him in Whose Hand is my soul, you will follow the ways of those who were before you, span by span, cubit by cubit — so much so that were one of them to enter the hole of a lizard, you would enter it too."* They said: "O Messenger of God, the Jews and the Christians?" He said: *"Who, then?"* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī (7320) and Muslim (2669) from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī)

The ḥadīth completes the verse's anthropology: imitation of the predecessors is not a risk the community might avoid; it is the default current of the human heart, and only deliberate counter-pressure — revelation, remembrance, the maʿrūf-enjoining of verse 71 — keeps a community from floating into the predecessors' channel and, with them, over the predecessors' falls. The lizard-hole image has its own doctrine: the absurdity is the point — the imitator will follow his model even where reason screams, because the imitation has become identity.

**Three Deeds, Three Shares, One Ending**

The verse's structure is a ledger in three columns. Column one: *fa-istamtaʿū bi-nasībihim* — "they enjoyed their share" — the language of allotted portion, the Qur'an's neutral term for the worldly experience of every creature, believer or not. Column two: *fastamtaʿtum bi-nasībikum* — "and you enjoyed your share" — the hypocrites of Madinah had their seasons too: the trade, the shade, the sitting-room conversations of verse 65. Column three: the ending: *fa-ḥabiṭat aʿmāluhum fī al-dunyā wa-al-ākhirah* — "their deeds came to naught in this world and the Hereafter." The double voiding is the verse's distinctive severity: in the Hereafter, the deeds are rejected (v. 53's refusal); in this world, they are *hollow even as they happen* — the enjoyment contains no blessing, the achievements build nothing that outlasts the season, the whole share is consumed like a loan whose repayment schedule starts at death. Al-Rāzī hears in the double clause the Qur'an's economics of deeds: an act can fail in either ledger — wasted in this world though accepted there (the believer's unrequited sacrifices), or profitable here though void there (the hypocrite's enjoyed portions) — and the losers, *al-khāsirūn*, are those whose acts fail in both: the only unreserved bankruptcy.

**Our Own Lizard Holes**

For the modern believer, verse 69 is uncomfortably easy to apply, because the imitation it diagnoses is no longer subtle: the community's calendars, metrics of success, entertainment, debt structures, and even its vices have been adopted, in many places, wholesale from civilizations whose own accountants of meaning have long since declared the model failing. The verse does not forbid learning from others — the Prophet ﷺ adopted Persian trench-craft for Madinah's defense, and stood watching the Abyssinians' spear-play in his own mosque, telling those who would stop it to leave them be — he adopted, on revelation's watch, the practices of others where they served the maʿrūf; what verse 69 polices is *imitation as identity*: the span-by-span crawl into another's hole, especially when the hole's owner has already published the voiding of his deeds. The believer's diagnostic questions are the verse's own: *Which of my enjoyments are my share, and which are my script? Whose ways am I following in this decision — and did theirs end well?* The verse's last word — *al-khāsirūn*, the losers — is a coin the Qur'an mints for one category only: those who traded both worlds for a season of someone else's share; and its mercy, as always in this sūrah, is the door it leaves beside the warning: the imitation can be stopped mid-crawl, and the One Who relents (v. 104) is the One Who reported the lizard hole in the first place — so that no one could say he was not told.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:70

> **Has not the account come to them of those who before them, the people of Noah, ʿĀd, and Thamūd, and the people of Abraham, the inhabitants of Midian, and the overthrown cities? Their messengers brought them clear proofs. God wronged them not, but themselves did they wrong.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Archive of Six Destructions**

Verse 70 opens the file of sacred history like a prosecutor laying out six prior cases: *alam yaʾtihim nabaʾu alladhīna min qablihim* — "has the *account* not come to them of those before them?" — and the roll-call is deliberate and comprehensive. **The people of Noah** — the archetypal case: a messenger of 950 years of ministry, a flood that archived a whole civilization. **ʿĀd** — the mighty builders of the sands to whom Hūd was sent: *"as for ʿĀd, they were arrogant in the land without right, and said: who is mightier than us in power?"* (41:15) — their ruins, which the tradition identifies with the "Iram of the pillars" of 89:6, were the Arabs' own heritage of hubris. **Thamūd** — the rock-carvers of the Ḥijr to whom Ṣāliḥ came, the people of the she-camel whose town lay on the pilgrimage road — the destruction the Arabs could *walk through*. **The people of Abraham** — the fire that did not burn, and the overturning that followed their rejection of him. **The inhabitants of Midian (Madyan)** — Shuʿayb's people, the dishonest merchants, destroyed by the day of overshadowing. **The overthrown cities (al-muʾtafikāt)** — Lot's people, the cities literally flipped — al-Qurṭubī records also the broader reading, "every overturned people," but the tradition's center of gravity is the Dead Sea plain. Six nations, six prophets, six mechanisms of ruin — water, wind, earthquake, fire, and brimstone — but one finding, which the verse states with juridical restraint.

**"Clear Proofs": The Standard of Accountability**

The common factor in the six cases is stated before the verdict: *jāʾathum rusuluhum bil-bayyināt* — "their messengers came to them with clear proofs." The bayyināt — manifest evidences, arguments that leave no darkness of ambiguity — establish the case's threshold: destruction never fell on ignorance. The Qur'an's doctrine of prophetic due process is absolute: *"We never punish until We send a messenger"* (17:15); *"God would never wrong them, but themselves they wronged"* — this verse's own verdict. The theodicy is stated in one clause: *mā ẓalamahumu Allāhu wa-lākin kānū anfusahum yaẓlamūn* — "God wronged them not, but themselves they wronged" — the reflexive grammar of the Qur'an's doctrine of sin: the wrong returns upon the wronger; God's acts in the file are responses, in strict proportion, to the soul's self-addressed harm. For any reader troubled by the destruction-narratives, verse 70 supplies the tradition's own frame: ruin is not arbitrary weather; it is the terminal stage of a self-harm that clear proofs had interrupted, and that the community chose to resume.

**History as the Believer's Laboratory**

The Qur'an's use of the six cases is not antiquarian; it is *operational*. The nations were destroyed — the verse implies — for the same configuration the sūrah has been auditing: rejection after evidence, mockery of the messenger (43:7), hoarded wealth (v. 34's clergy; the ancient file of Pharaoh), the inversion of the moral order (v. 67). The historian's discipline the verse commands — *has the account not come to you?* — assumes a reader who will actually read the record, and the Qur'an elsewhere marvels at those who do not: *"Have they not traveled in the land, and seen the end of those before them?"* (30:9; cf. 35:44; 47:10). The ruins were, for the Arabs, within a week's ride; for the modern believer, within a library's reach. And the lesson the archive teaches is not "civilizations fall" — a banality — but the *shape* of their falling: prosperity → arrogance → the silencing of conscience → the mockery of the warner → the end. The sūrah's hypocrites had reached stage four (v. 65); verse 70 is the archive opened at precisely that page.

**The Mirror Finished Before the Warning Ends**

Note where the verse sits: between the verdict on the hypocrites (vv. 67–68) and the portrait of the believers (v. 71) — the two communities of the sūrah's climax. The placement is the argument: before showing what the believers *are*, God shows what everyone was *warned about becoming* — and the six-case file ends, as it began, in the grammar of reflexive wrong: *themselves they wronged*. For the modern believer, verse 70's use is double. As doctrine, it grounds the hardest comfort in religion: nothing that befalls a soul — of nation or of person — is injustice from God; the universe's Judge is the file's cleanest defendant. As discipline, it commands the study of ends — the biographies of dead complacencies, personal and civilizational — as a regular act of worship: the believer who reads the account has, by the verse's own logic, joined the jury; the one who declines the reading has chosen the defendant's table. And for the one who finds himself, honestly, mid-pattern — prosperity, drift, a mockery problem of his own — verse 70 stands open at exactly his page, with the next verse ready to show him the exit the six never took: *but the believing men and believing women...*

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:71

> **But the believing men and believing women are protectors of one another, enjoining right and forbidding wrong, performing the prayer, giving the alms, and obeying God and His Messenger. They are those upon whom God will have Mercy. Truly God is Mighty, Wise.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Positive Photograph, Finally Developed**

Verse 71 is one of the great summits of the Qur'an's social teaching, and its force is architectural: it is verse 67's negative, printed positive. Where the hypocrite men and women were "of one another" in enjoining wrong, the *believing* men and women are *awliyāʾ baʿḍuhum li-baʿḍ* — "protectors, allies, friends of one another" — the walāyah vocabulary of the sūrah (vv. 16, 23) now given its true object: the believers themselves, to one another, across every boundary the old world held sacred. Then the four-fold activity of the protected community: *yaʾmurūna bil-maʿrūfi wa-yanhawna ʿani al-munkari* — enjoining right, forbidding wrong — the community's moral immune system, restored to its rightful polarity; *wa-yuqīmūna al-ṣalāta wa-yuʾtūna al-zakāta* — establishing the prayer and giving the alms — the vertical and horizontal pillars, the same pair that has defined covenant membership since verse 11; *wa-yuṭīʿūna Allāha wa-rasūlahu* — obeying God and His Messenger — the allegiance beneath all the rest. The verse is the constitution of the ummah in one sentence: mutual guardianship, moral agency, worship, economic solidarity, obedience — and then the outcome, which is not conquest or prosperity but something quieter and larger: *sa-yarḥamuhumu Allāh* — "God will have mercy upon them."

**Women Named as Full Moral Citizens**

The verse's most consequential phrase, for its own age and for the argument of every age since, is its second word: *wa-l-muʾminātu* — "and the believing women." The Qur'an legislates women into the community's core mandate — the *amr bil-maʿrūf* — as agents, not adjuncts: women who enjoin, forbid, establish, give, and obey are not the *supported* of the community but its *load-bearing* members. Al-Qurṭubī, reading the verse's pairing of 67 and 71, draws the doctrinal conclusion the tradition canonized: the obligations and dignities of moral authority fall identically on both sexes — the same teaching the Qur'an states at length in 33:35, its own litany of the paired participles (the submitting men and the submitting women... the enjoining men and the enjoining women...). The history of Muslim women's scholarship, teaching, market-inspection, and public moral voice — from ʿĀʾishah, the ḥadīth-transmitter and teacher of the tābiʿūn, to the women jurists and muftīs the biographical dictionaries record across the centuries — stands on this verse's platform, and every later curtailment of it is answerable to its text.

**Walāyah as Mutual Protection, Not Mutual Flattery**

The word translated "protectors" — *awliyāʾ*, plural of *walī* — deserves its full weight in a verse that also commands *forbidding wrong*. The believers' mutual walāyah is not the loyalty of the gang (everything the friend does is defended); it is the loyalty of the *guardian* (everything the friend is, is defended — including from his own wrongdoing). Al-Rāzī notes the built-in tension the verse resolves: how can a community both protect its members and correct them? Only by locating the protection *above* the correction: the believer who forbids his brother's wrong is executing the walāyah, not violating it — the guardianship extends to the soul's interests, not merely its preferences. The Prophet ﷺ drew the exact line in his famous similes of the community:

> *"The believer to the believer is like a building, each part strengthening the other"* — and he interlaced his fingers. (Recorded by al-Bukhārī (481) and Muslim (2585) from Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī)

> *"The parable of the believers in their mutual love, mercy, and affection is that of a body: when one limb suffers, the whole body responds with sleeplessness and fever."* (Recorded by Muslim (2586) from al-Nuʿmān ibn Bashīr)

**The Mercy Outcome and the Mighty-Wise Signature**

The verse's promise — *"God will have mercy upon them"* — answers verse 68's promise with perfect symmetry: the Fire suffices the hypocrites; mercy clothes the believers. And the closing names — *ʿAzīzun Ḥakīm*, Mighty, Wise — are the guarantee of the whole constitution: the mutual protection the verse commands is not a survival strategy the community invented, but an order the Mighty One enforces and the Wise One designed; its power to hold societies together is not a hypothesis but a decree. For the modern believer, verse 71 is the chapter's cleanest self-portrait to hold against the mirror: *Am I, to the people of my community, a walī in this verse's sense — one whose presence increases the maʿrūf and decreases the munkar, whose prayer and alms are live, whose obedience is first to God?* And the community-scale version of the question is the one every generation must answer anew: a congregation, an organization, a whole civilization of believing men and women who *do* verse 71 is promised, on divine authority, the one outcome no policy paper can deliver — the mercy of God, arriving from the direction its Mighty, Wise Author chose.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:72

> **God has promised the believing men and the believing women Gardens with rivers running below, to abide therein, and goodly dwellings in the Gardens of Eden. But Contentment from God is greater; that is the great triumph!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Promise That Answers Verse 68**

Verse 72 stands to verse 71 as verse 68 stood to verse 67: the anatomy is followed by the destiny, and the destinies are symmetrical to the clause. *Waʿada Allāhu al-muʾminīna wa-l-muʾmināti* — "God has promised the believing men and the believing women" — the covenant-verb again (v. 68's waʿada in the negative), the gendered pair again, and the estate again: *jannātin tajrī min taḥtihā al-anhāru* — "Gardens with rivers running beneath." The image, the Qur'an's most repeated description of Paradise (2:25 and scores of siblings), is agricultural before it is architectural: the garden of Arabia is the walled orchard watered by running channels — shade, water, fruit, enclosure — and the promise is pitched deliberately to a people for whom the garden was the end of every desert. To abide therein — *khālidīna fīhā* — the forever of verse 22; and then the promise adds a second, more interior estate: *masākin ṭayyibah* — "goodly dwellings" — *fī jannāti ʿAdn* — "in Gardens of Eden."

**ʿAdn: The Heart of the Garden**

The term *ʿAdn* (Eden — the root of delight, pleasantness, the dwelling-place of settled felicity) was located by the commentators with loving precision, as the source commentary preserves: the center of the Garden; its highest level; a city whose palace is the Divine Throne; a palace of gold reserved for prophets, martyrs, and just rulers; or — the reading that pleases the grammarians — not a name at all but an adjective: "gardens of dwelling," gardens whose essence is to be *inhabited*, permanent residences rather than resorts. The choice between geography and grammar matters little devotionally: either way the verse promises not merely pleasures but *home* — the thing for which the whole human pilgrimage, from the first expulsion, has been homesick. And the dwellings are *ṭayyibah* — good, wholesome, pure — the adjective of the goodly word of God (14:24) and of lawful provision: the believer's eternal address is described in the vocabulary of *ṭayyib*, because the tradition's deepest instinct about Paradise is that it is the finally-unbroken continuity of the pure life, not its contradiction.

**"But the Contentment From God Is Greater"**

Then the verse performs the move that lifts it above every merely material paradise: *wa-riḍwānun mina Allāhi akbar* — "and Contentment from God is *greater*." Greater than the gardens, the rivers, the dwellings, the forever. The tradition — al-Qurṭubī among its clearest exponents — savors the implication: the supreme reward of the Garden is God Himself — His pleasure experienced, His face beheld (the doctrine of the ruʾyah the Qur'an seals elsewhere: *"Faces on that Day will be radiant, looking upon their Lord"* — 75:22–23). Many, as the source commentary notes, went further and read *riḍwān* as the *name* of the highest Paradise: the summit-region of the Garden where the reward is nothing but the Giver's unclouded approval. The psychology is exact and available to every human observer: the child who has done well wants the parent's face more than the promised toy; the soldier wants the commander's "well done" more than the decoration; the lover wants the beloved's pleasure more than the beloved's gifts. The Qur'an canonizes the hierarchy for eternity: the gifts are real, the rivers run — but the *akbar*, the greater, is the smile of the One who gives them.

**The Great Triumph, Correctly Priced**

The verse closes: *dhālika huwa al-fawzu al-ʿaẓīm* — "that is the great triumph" — the same seal as verses 89 and 100, and the word *fawz* (winning, deliverance, escape-into-gain) now fully defined: triumph is not the Garden-plus-rivers package; it is the package *plus the pleased Face* — and of the two, the Face is the greater line-item. For the modern believer, verse 72 is the completion of the sūrah's entire economics: it began with sales (v. 9's paltry price), ran through purchases (v. 111's divine bargain), and closes its portrait of the believers with the one acquisition that retroactively prices them all. The believer's practical use of the verse is its hierarchy-test: when imagining or praying for the Hereafter, what does the heart list first — the rivers, the reunions, the restorations — or the riḍwān? The verse does not shame the first list (God made the rivers, and mentions them first); it simply prices the second above it, and promises that those who spend their lives for God (v. 111) will one day discover, in the garden's first hour, which line-item was the treasure — and that it was, all along, greater.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:73

> **O Prophet! Strive against the disbelievers and the hypocrites, and be harsh with them. Their refuge is Hell. What an evil journey's end!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Two-Front Command**

Verse 73 is addressed to the Prophet ﷺ by title, not community — *yā ayyuhā al-nabiyyu* — and its command has two objects, the two adversaries the sūrah has been treating in separate files until now: *jāhidi al-kuffāra wa-l-munāfiqīna* — "strive against the disbelievers and the hypocrites." The pairing is doctrinally deliberate: unbelief outside and hypocrisy inside are, in the Qur'an's military theology, one encirclement in two segments, and the Prophet is commanded to conduct a single jihād against both. The verb is not qātala (fight) but *jāhada* — strive, exert — a word wide enough to hold the sword and everything beside it, and the commentators divided the labor accordingly: al-Ṭabarī transmits the classic distribution — *the sword against the disbelievers, and the tongue and argument against the hypocrites* — for the hypocrites, resident inside the covenant, were not an army to be met but a party to be answered, exposed by the sūrah itself, restrained by law, and left, finally, to God's audit. Al-Rāzī, as the source commentary records, refuses the hard split on lexical grounds: nothing in jihād restricts it to any instrument; the believer employs, in each theater, the means the situation warrants.

**Ghilaẓ: Harshness With a License, Not a Character**

The third command is the verse's hardest word for gentle ears: *wa-ghluẓ ʿalayhim* — "and be harsh with them." The root gh-l-ẓ means thickness, density, stiffness — the quality the Qur'an will command of the whole community at this sūrah's verse 123: *"let them find harshness (ghilẓah) in you"* — and pair, at 48:29, with its mirror: *"stern against the disbelievers, merciful among themselves."* Al-Rāzī's gloss, preserved in the source commentary, is the necessary one: the command presupposes a man in whom gentleness and mercy are the settled character — the Prophet's own, of whom God said *"By God's mercy you were gentle with them; had you been harsh and hard-hearted they would have scattered from about you"* (3:159) — and it licenses, for the specific theater of open aggression and organized treachery, a deliberate *stiffness* that such a character can deploy without being corrupted by it. The doctrine has a built-in governor: only the genuinely gentle can be trusted with harshness, because for them it is an instrument, not an appetite. The Qur'an's own balance-sheet of the Prophet — who wept for his enemies' sons, forgave the conquerors of Makkah, and prayed for the tribe that stoned him — is the standing proof that verse 73's ghilaẓ was an instrument stored, rarely and precisely, beside a character of mercy.

**The Evil Destination Clause**

The verse's closing sentence — *wa-maʾwāhum jahannam* — "their refuge is Hell" — *wa-biʾsa al-maṣīr* — "what an evil journey's end" — returns to the road-idiom of verse 68: both adversaries, whatever their different roads — the open highway of kufr, the double track of nifāq — arrive at the same destination, and the verse files them under one address. The pastoral logic of naming Hell an *evil maṣīr* rather than cataloguing its torments is the Qur'an's recurring tact: the reader is not frightened with detail but with direction — a *destination* is something one is, even now, traveling toward, and the verse's grammar makes every hearer a traveler asked to check his own map.

**The Believer's Interior Two Fronts**

Ibn ʿAjībah, in the spiritual reading the source commentary preserves, turns the verse inward where every serious reader must finally take it: the disbeliever and the hypocrite are also *components of a single self* — the open lower self that has never pretended, and the religiously-costumed lower self that prays, fasts, and serves its own image; against both the believer is commanded to strive, and against the second the struggle is subtler, because it lives in the house. The Sufis made this verse's interior application a pillar of the spiritual curriculum: the jihād against the overt sin is the easier war; the jihād against religiosity-as-identity is the lifelong one, demanding the ghilaẓ of honest self-audit and the sword of sincerity. For the modern believer, verse 73 offers both theaters honestly named: the external — a community's measured firmness against aggression and organized treachery, held inside the treaty-law the sūrah has built; and the internal — the daily campaign against the unbeliever and the hypocrite in the mirror, of whom the second is always the harder war.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:74

> **They swear by God that they said it not, but indeed they spoke the word of disbelief, and disbelieved after having submitted [to God]. And they had ambitions that they did not achieve, and they were vengeful only because God and His Messenger enriched them from His Bounty. If they repent, it would be better for them. But if they turn away, God will punish them with a painful punishment in this world and in the Hereafter, and on earth they shall have neither protector nor helper.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Word They Swore They Never Spoke**

Verse 74 is the sūrah's densest legal file on the hypocrites, and it opens with perjury under oath: *yahlifūna billāhi mā qālū* — "they swear by God that they said it not" — while *wa-la-qad qālū kalimata al-kufr* — "indeed they spoke the word of disbelief." The reports, transmitted by al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī, identify the word variously: one stream has the mockers saying, *"If Muhammad is truthful, then we are worse than the donkeys we ride"* — a sentence whose very structure concedes the truth it denies; another connects the verse to ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ubayy's famous remark after Uḥud — "feed your dog and he will eat you... if we return to Madinah, the mightier will expel the lowlier" — which Zayd ibn Arqam overheard and carried to the Prophet ﷺ, and which Ibn Ubayy then denied on oath, with his confederates swearing beside him; a third, harsher report, which al-Rāzī relays, reads the "ambition they did not achieve" as a literal plot to kill the Prophet ﷺ at the ʿAqabah pass on the return from Tabūk. The verse does not choose between the reports, and does not need to: it files the *pattern* — the word spoken, the oath denying it, the scheme beneath it.

**Enriched and Resentful: The Anatomy of Ingratitude**

The verse's most surgically personal clause is its psychology of motive: *wa-mā naqamū illā an aghnāhumu Allāhu wa-rasūluhu min faḍlih* — "they were vengeful only because God and His Messenger enriched them of His Bounty." The offense taken by the hypocrites was, at bottom, *prosperity* — the prosperity Madinah's new order brought them, which made their own pretense feel cheap. Al-Qurṭubī cites the proverb the Arabs coined for the type: *"Beware the evil of one to whom you have done good"* — and the tradition's account of the concrete case: the Prophet ﷺ had even paid blood-money on behalf of one of them, ransoming him from embarrassment — and the ransom became the grievance. Here is the Qur'an at its most clinically honest about a recognizable human economy: the beneficiary of a kindness who turns hostile because the kindness humiliated him — the favor's receipt reminding him, daily, of his dependence on the man he needs to despise. Modern psychology has names for the reflex; the Qur'an had already described it and, in the same clause, refused to let it pass as grievance: *what they resented was God's bounty through His Messenger* — their spite was, technically, theological.

**The Door Held Open Inside the Sentence**

And then, in the middle of the harshest dossier in the sūrah, the grammar turns: *in yatūbū yakun khayran lahum* — "if they repent, it would be better for them." The same offer made to the idolaters at verse 3, to the disbelievers at verse 27, is made here to perjuring schemers — because the offer, as the sūrah has shown at every turn, is not a grade of the audience's merit but a property of the Offerer's mercy. The conditional that follows — *wa-in yatawallaw* — returns to the sentence structure of verse 38–39: double punishment, this world and the next; and a new closing clause that lands with special weight on men of property and clan: *wa-mā lahum fī al-arḍi min waliyyin wa-lā naṣīr* — "and they have on earth neither protector nor helper." Ibn Kathīr notes the terror of the clause for its first audience: a people whose entire security-architecture was *walī* — the patron-protector of the tribal order — are told that the architecture's Owner has voided their policy; the earth itself, whose breadth they might flee across (v. 57), holds no party answerable for them.

**The Verse's Standing Warning**

For the modern believer, verse 74's durable teachings are three. First, the oath does not launder the act: perjury is not a defense but a second offense — the Qur'an treats the sworn denial as part of the crime's anatomy, and every legal system that later criminalized false testimony under oath was codifying this verse's instinct. Second, resentment of one's benefactor is a *spiritual diagnosis*, not a relationship problem: the hypocrites' hostility to the Prophet ﷺ was fed by his generosity, and the believer who notices himself growing cold toward people who have done him good — a parent, a teacher, a community — has caught, at its earliest stage, the disease the verse names, and its cure is gratitude made explicit. Third, the door's position is the sūrah's gospel in miniature: it stands open *inside the indictment*, before the sentence finishes — "if they repent, it would be better for them" is grammatically prior to "if they turn away" — and the tradition's record that some of verse 74's own addresseces walked through it (verse 102's confessors were of this generation) proves the offer was not rhetoric. The worst file in the sūrah still ends, as the sūrah itself will end, with a turn of hope — for whoever will take it before the turning is no longer his.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:75

> **And among them are those who make a pact with God [saying], "If He gives unto us from His Bounty, we will surely spend in charity, and we will surely be among the righteous."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Bargain Made in the Heart's Hard Season**

Verse 75 opens a two-verse case study — verses 75–76 — in what the tradition came to call promissory religion: *wa-minhum man ʿāhada Allāha* — "among them are those who made a covenant with God" — *la-in ātānā min faḍlih* — "if He gives us of His Bounty" — *la-naṣṣaddaqanna* — "we will most surely give charity" — *wa-la-nakūnanna mina al-ṣāliḥīn* — "and we will most surely be among the righteous." The intensified Arabic forms (the doubling of the oath-particles) show how solemn the inner vow was: in the lean season, the soul promises God anything. The commentators, as the source commentary records, connect the verses with a named case — Thaʿlabah ibn Ḥāṭib, a man of the Anṣār who came to the Prophet ﷺ asking him to pray for his enrichment, swearing that whatever came his way would be well spent; the Prophet's counsel was the wisdom of the whole passage — the reports give his warning that he feared for Thaʿlabah the seduction of wealth, and his counsel that a moderate provision with gratitude is better than plenty that overwhelms; Thaʿlabah insisted, and the Prophet prayed. The sūrah's verdict on the type is delivered by the next verse — but verse 75's own anatomy deserves the attention first, because the vow's *content* is perfectly orthodox: spend in charity, join the righteous. The defect was not in the resolution but in the *consideration* — the "if": faith priced as a contract for services.

**The "If" That Outsourced Trust**

The grammar of the vow is its exposure: *la-in ātānā* — "if He gives us." The covenant was conditional on the Benefactor's performance, which is to say it was not covenant at all — it was a contingent order. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī both hear the flaw: the sincere believer gives *because* God has already given — life, breath, the ninety-nine mercies beneath every need — whereas the promissory believer withholds the soul's loyalty until a further payment clears. The Qur'an had already legislated the healthy form of the impulse: *"And of whatever We have given them, they spend"* (2:3) — spending as the fruit of reception, not the price of it. And the sūrah itself will shortly command the healthy form as the community's standing discipline: *khudh min amwālihim ṣadaqah* — "take of their wealth a charity" (v. 103) — an unconditional levy, not a pledged remainder. Verse 75's promissory religion, by contrast, treats God as a counterparty who must perform first — and the next verse will show what happens to such contracts when the consideration is paid: the counterparty discovers he never intended to close.

**The Universal Vow of the Tight Season**

The believer who has never made Thaʿlabah's bargain has lived a charmed life. The vow of the tight season is one of humanity's most standard prayers — the examination-time piety, the hospital-bed reformation, the market-crash covenant: *if You get me through this, I will change.* The tradition's counsel on such vows is more nuanced than the verse's verdict on the hypocrites: the Prophet ﷺ discouraged vows as obligations (*"Do not make vows, for a vow neither hastens anything nor deflects it; it only extracts from the stingy"* (recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim from Ibn ʿUmar), yet he honored the *repentance* that often rides on them, and the Qur'an praises those who *"fulfill their covenant with God"* after vowing (cf. 13:20; 76:7). The dividing line, which verse 75 exists to draw, is the vow's *function*: a vow as the heart's rising toward God in its hour of need — real, though frail; a vow as commerce, the soul's IOU to be redeemed or repudiated at settlement — the disease. Thaʿlabah's case, the reports say, was heard out by the Prophet ﷺ with a warning, not a rebuke — because the Commander of the faithful was also the physician of hearts, and he knew that the difference between the two kinds of vow is often invisible until the money arrives. Which is precisely what the next verse records.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:76

> **But when He gave unto them from His Bounty, they were miserly with it, and turned away in rejection.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Settlement Date of the Soul's Contract**

Verse 76 is verse 75's verdict, delivered in one line of terrible economy: *fa-lammā ātāhum min faḍlih* — "but when He gave them of His Bounty" — *ba-khilū bih* — "they were miserly with it" — *wa-tawallaw wa-hum muʿriḍūn* — "and turned away, averting." Everything the intensified oath-particles of verse 75 promised — the *most surely* of the giving and the righteousness — evaporates at the moment of performance; the same verb *tawallaw* that described the averted hearts of verses 50 and 57 now describes the wallet-shirkers: the turning-away is one verb for all of them. Al-Rāzī observes the terrifying arithmetic of the case: the covenant contained *two* pledges — the spending and the righteousness — and the default contained *two* failures: the hoarding and the averted face — the contract and its breach are symmetrical to the clause, because the heart that made the vow whole is the heart that breaks it whole. Al-Qurṭubī records the Qur'an's own name for the two verses' subject — those whom *"God heard... say... then when He gave them of His Bounty they were miserly with it"* (the theme is restated at 57:24) — the type of those who *"conceal and turn away"* — and the tradition's grief is that the default is not exotic: it is the statistically normal outcome of promissory religion, which is why the verse, though brief, closes a case the whole Qur'an keeps re-opening.

**Thaʿlabah's Descent**

The sīrah-based reports of the named case — Thaʿlabah ibn Ḥāṭib — supply the verse's narrative flesh, as the source commentary preserves: the wealth came (from the spoils the Prophet ﷺ assigned him), and it grew; he moved out of Madinah to a nearby valley to tend it; his congregational prayers thinned from five to some, then to Friday alone, then not even that; and when the zakāt-collectors of the Prophet's dispatch reached him after the revelation of verse 103's levy, he received them with the quarrel the tradition preserves in his own words: *this is nothing but the tribute-tax (jizyah)* — the levy of a king, not the due of a God he had vowed to serve. He refused; he appealed to the Prophet ﷺ; and the reports close with the Prophet's broken sentence — *"Woe to you, Thaʿlabah!"* — and, in the fuller accounts, with the man's later ruin and penury under Abū Bakr's caliphate, hauling his scattered herds back to Madinah, and the tradition's verdict that the verses had named him. The case is a complete parable of 75–76: the vow, the wealth, the default, the categorizing of God's due as the state's extortion — each stage fully documented, each stage the sūrah had already written.

**Miserliness as Apostasy in Miniature**

The verse's word for the failure — *bakhilū* — belongs in Islamic theology to a category larger than thrift: Bukhl is, in the tradition's taxonomy, the *shadow of shirk*, because the miser's creed-in-practice is that his survival is stored in the wealth rather than in its Giver; he hoards as an atheist hordes — as though there were no re-supply. Al-Qurṭubī draws the line the Qur'an draws: the believer gives *from* the wealth, not *out of* it — the treasury remains God's, and the hand is a pipe, not a dam. And the verse's second failure — *they turned away, averting* — shows that the hoarding was never merely financial: the misered wealth was the *symptom* of a turned face; the closing fist and the averted heart are one posture, anatomically continuous. The Prophet ﷺ connected the anatomy explicitly: *"Beware of avarice, for avarice destroyed those before you: it commanded them to cut off kin, and they cut off; to shed blood, and they shed; to violate what God made sacred, and they violated it."* (Transmitted by Aḥmad and Abū Dāwūd from ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr)

**The Test That Must Be Prayed For Gently**

For the modern believer, verses 75–76 form a single examination paper with one question — *what did I do when the money finally came?* — and the tradition's wise handling of it is the Prophet's handling of Thaʿlabah: pray for provision with the caveat of the weak, not the confidence of the strong; ask God for the *middle* with gratitude rather than the plenty with risk; and when plenty comes anyway, execute the standing levies (verse 103 is coming, and verse 60's eight channels with it) *before* the heart has time to draft its exceptions. The believer who has already broken a verse-75 vow should read the pair to its last implication: the verdict of 76 is on the *unrepented* default — the sūrah's next verses (77–78) will show the door still standing for those who confess — and the difference between Thaʿlabah's end and verse 102's confessors' end was never the size of the failure; it was the presence, at settlement date, of a heart willing to be wrong in the open.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:77

> **So He requited them with hypocrisy in their hearts, till the Day they will meet Him, for having failed in that which they promised Him and for having lied.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Sin Selecting Its Own Punishment**

Verse 77 states, in one clause, one of the Qur'an's most profound laws of the inner life: *fa-aʿqabahum nifāqun fī qulūbihim* — "so He requited them with hypocrisy in their hearts." The grammar is causal and clinical: the broken vow (v. 76) was not merely *followed by* hypocrisy as a judge's sentence follows a crime — it *produced* it, the verb ʿaqaba (to follow in sequence, to requite) carrying the sense of consequence built into the act itself. The commentators divide, as the source commentary records, over the subject of the verb — most read God as the Requiter (He requited them); al-Qurṭubī transmits the alternative reading in which the verse's opening means "so their miserliness requited them with hypocrisy" — and the division is really a convergence: in the Qur'an's moral physics, God's requital and the deed's own fruit are one process, the Judge ruling through the causality He built. The principle deserves slow attention: *the punishment for defaulting on a covenant with God is a heart that defaults more easily* — hypocrisy (nifāq) itself as the sentence. Sin against sincerity produces insincerity; the breach widens the channel it flowed through.

**"Till the Day They Meet Him": The Chronic Sentence**

The duration clause is the verse's severest word: *ilā yawmi yalqawnahu* — "until the Day they meet Him." The hypocrisy is not an episode but a settled climate of the heart, scheduled to run until the appointment it has been avoiding. Al-Rāzī reads the clause as both sentence and diagnosis: the condition is *chronic* because it is self-maintaining — each new occasion for honesty presents itself as a new occasion for the now-practiced dishonesty, and the habit compounds. Ibn Kathīr hears the echo of the covenant-vocabulary: they failed (nakathū) the promise they had solemnly made (the ʿahd of v. 75), and the failing has a lifespan. The tradition's formulation of the law — that sins are the seeds of the punished self — stands behind the whole verse: *"Whatever affliction befalls you is from what your hands have earned, and He pardons much"* (42:30); and the Prophet ﷺ taught the anatomy in his four-sign portrait of the hypocrite, the ḥadīth the commentators cite on this verse:

> *"The signs of the hypocrite are four: when he is entrusted, he betrays; when he speaks, he lies; when he promises, he breaks; and when he disputes, he behaves with wickedness."* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī (33) and Muslim (59) from Abū Hurayrah)

Verse 77 is the etiology of that portrait: the signs are not four separate vices but one compounded condition — a heart that met a covenant with God and defaulted is a heart now trained to default everywhere.

**The Two Causes Itemized**

The verse closes its indictment with two findings, and their order is doctrine: *bimā akhlafū Allāha mā waʿadūhu* — "for having failed in that which they promised Him" — *wa-bimā kānū yakdhibūn* — "and for having lied." The perfect (they broke, once, the specific covenant of v. 75) is followed by the imperfect (they used to lie — the continuous practice); the single breach and the settled habit are named as joint causes, because in the Qur'an's accounting the solitary default draws its strength from the accumulated small ones, and the accumulated small ones reach their term in the solitary default. There is a whole theory of moral formation here that modern habit-science has merely confirmed: character is the residue of repeated acts, and the residue, past a threshold, begins to *author* the acts — the liar eventually lies because he is, now, in fact, a liar. Verse 77's mercy, as ever in this sūrah, is its placement: the law is stated *before* the threshold is crossed for any reader who is still on the near side of it — and the confession of verse 102 will show that even some who crossed it were pulled back by the hand that authored the law.

**The Verse as a Daily Warning Read Slowly**

For the modern believer, verse 77 is best read at the end of any day that contained a promise. The tradition's practical counsel follows the verse's own structure: audit the specific covenant (what did I solemnly intend at dawn — with God, with family, with my own soul?) and audit the practice of truth (the small, easy lies of the afternoon — the "soon," the "I tried," the "I forgot"). The believer who keeps both findings clean has kept his heart's climate clean; the one who lets them slide should read the verse's middle clause again — the hypocrisy is not waiting at the end of the road as a verdict; it is *installing itself in the heart* as the road's own pavement, one broken promise at a time, until the Day it is all finally, and too literally, met.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:78

> **Know they not that God knows their secret and their private discourse, and that God is Knower of things unseen?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Three Layers of the Hidden**

Verse 78 asks the question that stands above every strategy the sūrah has exposed — the oaths, the cave-fantasies, the campfire mockery, the broken vows: *a-wa-lam yaʿlamū anna Allāha yaʿlamu sirrahum wa-najwāhum* — "do they not know that God knows their secret and their private discourse?" The verse's two nouns map the two chambers of the hidden life with technical precision, as the source commentary preserves. *Sirr* — the secret: the thing kept from others, and also the innermost stratum of a thing — the intention beneath the act beneath the word. *Najwā* — the private discourse: the whispered conversation of confidants (the root n-j-w describes the leaning-together of heads), the conspiracy's medium — in this context, as al-Ṭabarī specifies, the exchanges the hypocrites held about the Prophet ﷺ and the believers. Between the two nouns the verse covers the whole hidden estate: what you conceal alone (sirr) and what you conceal together (najwā) — and then the closing Name covers everything else: *wa-anna Allāha ʿallāmu al-ghuyūb* — "God is the Knower of the unseen" — the intensive form (*ʿallām*, Knower upon Knower) asserting not merely that He knows the hidden, but that knowing-the-hidden is His expertise, His standing attribute, the very definition of His omniscience's edge over every creature's privacy.

**The Qur'an's Own Demonstrations**

The hypocrites of the sūrah had not merely been told this; they had *watched it happen* — and verse 78's question ("do they not know?") is almost rhetorical, because the demonstrations were on the public record: verse 64's dread of the sūrah that would "inform them of what is in their hearts" had been fulfilled in verse 65's verbatim quotation of the campfire joke; verse 74's "word of disbelief" had been quoted before it was denied; and the sīrah records the pattern's most public proof — the ḥadīth, transmitted in the collections, in which the Prophet ﷺ stood among his Companions and said, *"What is the matter with people who..."* — describing a secret act or conversation exactly as it had occurred, until one present said aloud what the sūrah had been teaching for years: the Messenger speaks of things no man told him. The Qur'an generalizes the demonstration into doctrine at 58:6–7, verses the commentators attach here: *"On the Day God resurrects them all and informs them of what they did — God has enumerated it, while they have forgotten it... Do you not see that God knows what is in the heavens and what is in the earth? Not three confer in secret but He is their fourth... nor less than that nor more, but He is with them wherever they are."*

**Omniscience as Moral Infrastructure**

The verse's practical function in the believer's life is infrastructure, not ornament. Human moral systems rely on enforcement's *probability* — the likelihood of being seen — and entire industries, from camera contracts to audit regimes, exist to raise it. Verse 78 installs the permanent camera: the awareness that *sirr* and *najwā* are, to one Observer, public record. The tradition named the resulting state *iḥsān* and made it the religion's highest station of practice — the Prophet ﷺ defined it, in the ḥadīth of Gabriel, in exactly the verse's terms:

> *"(Excellence is) to worship God as though you see Him; for if you do not see Him, He sees you."* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī (50) and Muslim (8) from Abū Hurayrah, the ḥadīth of Jibrīl)

And the difference between God's omniscience and every human surveillance must be said with equal clarity, because the verse is a comfort as much as an audit: the Seer of the secrets is also the *Coverer* of them (al-Sattār, in the tradition's Names) and the Accepter of repentance (v. 104's Tawwāb) — He sees the najwā but does not sell it, publish it, or weaponize it; He waits, through decades of knowing, for the one conversation that turns it all around. Al-Rāzī observes that the sincere are the hypocrites' exact inverse in the verse's own chambers: their sirr is worship no one witnesses, their najwā is counsel toward good — and Ibn ʿAjībah's gloss, preserved in the source commentary, completes the picture: the spiritually serious are *satisfied with what God gives them and worship when no one can see* — the hidden life lived as an appointment with its only Observer.

**The Whisper Census**

For the modern believer, verse 78 lands on a world that has industrialized the najwā — the group chat, the private channel, the locked tab — while celebrating the death of privacy in everyone else's case. The verse's question sorts the whole economy into two kinds of whisper: the ones you would repeat if God's name were spoken in the room, and the ones you would not — and God's name, the verse quietly announces, is *always* in the room; the room was always His. The believer's discipline is ancient and simple: before sending, ask the verse's question in the first person — *do I not know that God knows this?* — and if the answer embarrasses, the sūrah's center is still open: the One who knows the sirr is the One who relented, in this same sūrah, upon three men whose secret He had exposed to their whole city — which is, when all is said, the difference between an omniscience that terrifies and an omniscience that loves: both see everything; only one stayed to say so.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:79

> **As for they who reproach those believers who give freely and those who have naught but their keep [to give], and ridicule them—God ridicules them, and theirs shall be a painful punishment.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Scene at the Collection**

Verse 79 preserves one of the most human scenes in the sūrah, and the exegetical reports — al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī transmit them, as the source commentary summarizes — reconstruct it fully. At the Tabūk encampment, the Prophet ﷺ called for donations to equip the army. The generous responded at every scale: ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān equipped a third of the army, and his giving became proverbial; ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf arrived with four thousand dirhams of an eight-thousand fortune; Abū Bakr brought everything he had. Then a man of the Anṣār — the reports name Abū ʿAqīl — came with a ṣāʿ of dates he had earned by laboring through the night: he poured half into the collection and kept half for his family's food. And the hypocrites, watching, did what verse 79 records: they *yalmazūna al-muṭawwiʿīn* — reproached the volunteers — mocking the great givers for showing off and the small giver for his poverty: *"God has no need of this man's ṣāʿ"* — by their account, the poor man gave to be seen among the donors, and the rich gave to be seen giving. The verse's word for the small gift is *juhd* — effort, what a person strains to earn; the man had nothing but his keep, and he gave from its half.

**The Sin of Class Inside the Community**

The verse's two objects are named with precision: *al-muṭawwiʿīn* — "those who volunteer freely," giving above duty — and *alladhīna lā yajidūna illā juhdahum* — "those who find nothing to give but their effort" — and together they define the full spectrum of legitimate giving, and the mockers' innovation was to attack *both ends at once*: generosity ridiculed as vanity, poverty ridiculed as worthlessness. Al-Qurṭubī sees the deeper poison: the mockery was aimed not at the givers but at the *giving itself* — if abundance is show and smallness is shame, then no one may give at all, and the whole channel of verse 60's eight categories is discredited at the point of collection. Every charitable ecosystem since has known the voice: the sneer at the check that is "just PR," the sneer at the coins that are "pointless" — two sneers, one purpose, which is to make giving ridiculous at every denomination. And the Qur'an's reply uses the same verb reversed: *sakhira Allāhu minhum* — "God ridicules them" — the mirror-judgment of 2:15: the mockers mocked; God mocks the mockers — the idiom, the tradition is careful to say, is the Qur'an's permitted anthropomorphism of requital-in-kind: their laughter at the sincere becomes, in the divine ledger, the laughable absurdity of their position — men of no generosity ridiculing the generosity of God's servants before God.

**God's Accounting of the Two Dirhams**

The verse's doctrine — that the ṣāʿ and the fortune may stand equal, or the ṣāʿ above — is stated in the ḥadīth of the surpassing dirham transmitted in the Sunan collections: a man with two dirhams who gave one outstripped a man of vast wealth who gave a hundred thousand — because the first gave from his all, the second from his edge. And the Prophet ﷺ attached the same exchange-rate to the smallest lawful gift:

> *"Charity equal to a single date, given from what is earned honestly — God accepts it, and grows it for its giver as one of you grows his foal, until it becomes like a mountain."* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī (1410) from Abū Hurayrah, in wording close to this)

The theology answers the mockers exactly: the worth of the gift is set by the Giver's accounting — capacity, honesty, and intention — not by the spectators' market for spectacle; and the man with the half-ṣāʿ held, in the divine ledger, a position the mockers' entire estates did not touch.

**The Standing Protection of the Small Giver**

For the modern believer, verse 79 is the Qur'an's charter for the dignity of small donations — and its indictment of the culture that ridicules them. The believer who gives five when five is the week's margin, the volunteer whose "small" hours are dismissed beside the philanthropist's gift, the child whose coins are smiled at: all stand inside this verse's protection, and the smile at their expense is, by the verse's plain grammar, aimed upward — the ridicule of the sincere is the ridicule of the One who receives them. The verse also polices the believer's own mouth in the age of the hot take: the reflexive cynicism about every giver's motive — *he's doing it for the optics; she's doing it for the brand* — is verse 79's sin wearing modern dress; motives are God's audit, not the crowd's sport, and the Qur'an's rule is the reverse of the sneer: *publicize* the good (the sūrah itself publicized the Tabūk donors) and *conceal* the doubt. The believer who keeps that rule has joined the verse's blessed party; the one who cannot stop scoffing at givers should read the verse's last clause again — *lahum ʿadhābun alīm* — and remember that the pain it promises was incurred, in the first instance, by nothing worse than a joke about a man's dates.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:80

> **Seek forgiveness for them, or seek not forgiveness for them. If thou seekest forgiveness for them seventy times, God will not forgive them. That is because they disbelieved in God and His Messenger. And God guides not iniquitous people.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prophet's Prayer and Its Limit**

Verse 80 descends into one of the most painful episodes of the Madinan years: the death of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ubayy, the arch-hypocrite of the sūrah, in the year 9 AH. The reports — al-Bukhārī preserves the full scene from ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb's narration — describe the Prophet ﷺ at the graveside, asked by the dying man's son (a sincere believer, ʿAbd Allāh, one of the sūrah's own exemplary figures) for his shirt as a shroud and his prayer at the burial; and ʿUmar, appalled, gripping the Prophet's garment and appealing to everything revealed about the man's party — and the Prophet ﷺ answering with the mercy that was his signature:

> *"God gave me the choice, and I have chosen; were I to seek forgiveness for him more than seventy times, God would still not forgive him."* — and the Prophet prayed over him. Then was revealed: *"Seek forgiveness for them, or seek not... if thou seekest forgiveness for them seventy times, God will not forgive them."* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī in the ḥadīth of the funeral of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ubayy, from ʿUmar; and al-Bukhārī transmits the Prophet's follow-up: *"If I knew that exceeding seventy would bring forgiveness, I would exceed it."*)

The sūrah then closed the office for the future: verse 84 will forbid the funeral prayer over the died-in-hypocrisy altogether — and the reports differ, as the source commentary notes, over whether verse 84 came before the Prophet's prayer over Ibn Ubayy or after it. The sequence — a mercy offered, a limit revealed — is itself the doctrine.

**Seventy as a Number, and Mercy as a Ceiling Named**

The number seventy, as al-Qurṭubī notes, is the Arabic idiom for a vast, indefinite count — the point is not the arithmetic but the closure: *lan yaghfira Allāhu lahum* — God will not forgive them — repeated with the oath-like future negative (*lan*): the file of the died-in-obstinacy is closed, not because God's mercy ran out, but because the recipients did: the verse's own stated cause is their settled disbelief in God and His Messenger. Ibn Kathīr and the tradition pair the verse with its sibling revealed in the same season:

> *"It is the same for them whether thou seekest forgiveness for them or seekest forgiveness for them not: God will never forgive them. Truly God guides not the iniquitous people."* (Qur'an 63:6)

The two verses together define the doctrine of intercession's *jurisdiction*: the Prophet's prayer — the most beloved of intercessors, whose intercession on the Day of Resurrection is the greatest of its kind (al-Bukhārī and Muslim transmit the ḥadīth of the station of praise) — operates only within God's permission and the recipient's capacity; the shafāʿah, as the Qur'an states at 2:255, belongs to none *"save by His permission."*

**Why the Prophet Was Told to Keep Praying**

The verse's deep poignancy — and the commentators dwell on it — is the window it opens on mercy's own education. God does not need the Prophet's prayer for Ibn Ubayy, nor for anyone; He commands and permits prayers for reasons in the pray-er and the community, not in Himself. The Prophet's willingness to stand at the grave of his chief slanderer, shirt in hand, was the Sunnah's permanent demonstration of the verse 128 heart — *raʾūfun raḥīm* — and the revelation's permission-then-limit was the permanent demonstration of mercy's boundary: the Prophet may love whom he will; God forgives whom He wills, and the two lines cross only where the recipient lets them. Al-Qurṭubī hears the balance as the sūrah's own: verses 113–114 will generalize the ruling (no forgiveness-seeking for the died-in-shirk, even kin) and will resolve it with Ibrāhīm's precedent — but the pastoral note is struck here first: the Prophet was not rebuked for praying; the prayer was a mercy offered to the end of the door, and the door's closing was God's alone to announce.

**Intercession Rightly Understood, Grief Rightly Carried**

For the modern believer, verse 80 disciplines two very modern confusions. The first is sentimental universalism — the assumption that love, sincerely intense, must eventually oblige heaven; the verse's grammar refuses: the closed file was closed by its owner's lifetime of choices, and the believer's grief does not hold jurisdiction over God's justice — nor, the tradition quickly adds, does the believer ever *know* which files are closed: the verse named its men; the believer prays for his dead within the revealed limits (the Muslim dead: unrestricted; the non-Muslim dead: not — but their *living* counterparts: kindness without ceasing, and the call without despair), and leaves the ledgers to the Ledger-Keeper. The second confusion is the harshness that masquerades as orthodoxy — the believer who quotes this verse to bludgeon the grief of others; the Prophet ﷺ stood at the graveside of an enemy with his shirt in hand, and the sūrah that recorded the limit also recorded, in the same year, the repentance of three stayers (v. 118) and the acceptance of confessional sinners (v. 102). The verse 80 believer holds both halves: he does not pray where God has closed the office — and he never, ever closes it in anyone's face himself, having watched the Prophet ﷺ leave even that announcement to the One who owns it.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:81

> **Those who were left behind exulted in staying back, opposing the Messenger of God, and were averse to striving with their wealth and with their selves in the way of God. And they said, "Go not forth in the heat." Say, "The Fire of Hell is of a heat more intense," if they but understood.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Rejoicing That Damns**

Verse 81 opens the post-Tabūk verdicts with a paradox that is the key to its pathology: *fariḥa al-mukhallafūna bimaqʿadihim khilāfa rasūli Allāhi* — "those who were left behind *rejoiced* at sitting still, *in opposition* to the Messenger of God." The rejoicing is the damning detail. A believer who misses a duty grieves his missing; the ṣaḥābah who lagged behind with valid excuse wept (v. 92) at their absence; but these men *celebrated* theirs — because their objective, the reports make clear, was never mere comfort: it was the Prophet's *failure*. Sitting was instrumentally enjoyable only as khilāf — opposition; they wanted the campaign to collapse, the Messenger humiliated, the movement ended; their picnic in the shade was a political statement. Al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr both press the point: the verse's syntax convicts them not of laziness but of enmity — the aversion (*kariha*) was to striving *with their wealth and their selves*, the double currency again, and the verse's finding is that they wanted both currencies safe *and* the cause dead, which is the precise definition of the internal enemy.

**"Go Not Forth in the Heat": The Slogan of Comfort**

The verse preserves their actual counsel to one another — *lā tanfirū fī al-ḥarr* — "go not forth in the heat" — the only direct quotation of hypocrite domestic propaganda in the sūrah, and a perfect specimen of the genre: reasonable, solicitous, temperature-based. The sīrah adds the context that makes the slogan's cynicism visible: the march to Tabūk was called in Rajab — the dead of summer, the season of ripening dates, when the shade of Madinah's orchards was sweetest and the road to Syria was an oven; the Prophet ﷺ announced the destination openly (the distance demanded open preparation, and the sūrah's verses 41–46 show the community equipping itself), and the heat was the stayers' last presentable excuse. Ibn Kathīr notes that they said it among themselves — a whisper-campaign, not a petition: they did not plead the heat to the Prophet; they marketed it to the marginal, to peel the wavering away from the muster.

**The Fire's Reply: The Verse's Thermal Arithmetic**

God's answer is one sentence of perfect symmetry: *qul nāru jahannama ashaddu ḥarā* — "Say: the Fire of Hell is of a heat more intense." The logic is a scale with two pans: if heat is the principle on which duties are declined, then heat must also be the measure of destinies — and on that scale the ḥarr of the Syrian road, which ends, is trivial against the ḥarār of the Fire, which does not.
