Al-Rāzī reads the clause as exposing the excuse's incoherence: the man who spares himself sixty days of heat has, by his own stated principle, purchased an unending one — the calculus is not cruel but *consequential*, the excuse weighed in its own units. And the verse's final conditional — *in kānū yafqahūn* — "if they but understood" — uses fiqh's verb: not *if they knew* but *if they had the comprehension that processes implications*; the deficiency is not information (they had watched Badr, Uḥud, the Trench) but the deep understanding that connects principles across domains. The hypocrite's mind, the verse implies, is not uninformed; it is un-integrating — able to feel the sun on one road, unable to feel the fire in one sentence.

**The Modern Heat and the Higher Fire**

For the modern believer, verse 81 survives every battlefield, because its structure — *comfort declined into, destiny priced in its own units* — is permanent. The duty that comes in the wrong season (the course offered only at dawn, the service shift only in winter), the giving that would bite in a lean quarter, the stand that would cost a comfortable seat: each arrives with its heat, and each invites the slogan — *not now, not in this season, not at this temperature.* The verse's reply transfers without modification: whatever comfort is protected by the refusal, the refusal itself is the down-payment on a hotter account, and the believer who *integrates* — who lets the principle cross domains — feels the arithmetic before the Day itemizes it. The verse's last mercy is its address: it is spoken, throughout, as a *Say* to men still alive, still in the season of choosing — and the same sūrah, within forty verses, will open the ledger of three men who finally chose the heat of honesty over the shade of excuses, and found the fire of regret cooler than they feared.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:82

> **So let them laugh little and weep much, as a recompense for that which they used to earn.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Imperatives About Tears**

Verse 82 is two commands and a cause: *falyaḍḥakū qalīlan wa-yabkū kathīran* — "so let them laugh little and weep much" — *jazāʾan bimā kānū yaksibūn* — "as a recompense for what they used to earn." The imperatives are future-concessive: not advice but sentence — the laughter of the shades is rationed, and the tears are scheduled; the verse's grammar converts their chosen pleasures into arrears. The commentators, as the source commentary preserves, read the verse at both ends of time: the immediate — the gloating of verse 50 and the rejoicing of verse 81 turned to grief as the campaigns succeeded and the sūrah's exposures continued; and the ultimate — the Day when, as al-Qurṭubī says, the laughing is done and the account begins. The Qur'an had already staged the Day's version of the scene:

> *"So let them laugh a little and weep much, as a recompense for what they used to earn"* (this verse) — with its eschatological twin: *"And He will say: Today We forget you as you forgot the meeting of this your Day; and your refuge is the Fire..."* (cf. 45:34–35, the verse the tradition reads beside it)

And the Prophet ﷺ compressed the whole doctrine into the warning the Companions carried:

> *"If you knew what I know, you would laugh little and weep much."* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim from Abū Hurayrah and others, in wording close to this)

**The Two Weepings of the Qur'an**

The verse draws the sharpest line in the sūrah between two identical behaviors: the hypocrites are *made* to weep — punished with tears — while the believers of verse 92 *choose* them — rewarded for tears that overflow from longing to serve. The Qur'an's tears-portfolio is exact: condemned is the weeping of regret-that-refuses (and the laughter of heedlessness); praised is the weeping from God's remembrance — *"they fall upon their faces weeping, and it increases them in humility"* (17:109); *"the eyes overflow"* of those who could not find mounts (9:92); the weeper in the night-prayer among the seven shaded (the ḥadīth of al-Bukhārī and Muslim); the Prophet ﷺ weeping at Ibrāhīm's grave with the words that made grief lawful and bounded:

> *"The eye weeps, and the heart grieves, and we say only what pleases our Lord; and we are grieved at your parting, Ibrāhīm."* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī (1303) from Anas ibn Mālik)

The tradition's verdict on tears as such is thus neither sentimental nor stoic: tears are a language, and the verse judges only what they say.

**The Laughter Question**

The verse's first clause — *laugh little* — has been read by some ascetic ages as a general ration on joy; the Sunnah's record resists the reading: the Prophet ﷺ laughed, the reports say, until his molars showed; he raced, he joked, he received his Companions smiling; and his community's scholars distinguished carefully: the condemned laughter is the *kathīr* kind — the constant entertainment of a heart avoiding its audit — the laughter that functions, as one early master said, as the hypnosis of the heedless. Al-Qurṭubī balances the scales with the sūrah's own data: the believers' emotion-economy in chapter nine runs on grief for God and joy in God (*hum yastabshirūn*, "they rejoice," v. 124) — both currencies strong, both addressed upward; the hypocrites' economy runs on glee at the believers' losses and comfort in the shade — both addressed sideways, both therefore rationed.

**The Audit of an Emotion Portfolio**

For the modern believer, verse 82 is best read as a portfolio review, not a prohibition of laughter: the Qur'an asks not *how much* a man laughs but *at what* and *instead of what* — the laughter that fills the space where seriousness should have lived, the feed that arrives at 1 a.m. precisely so that no question has to be felt; and, symmetrically, the weeping that never comes — the stone-dry years through which the believer passes without one tear for himself, his state, or his meeting. The verse's sentence on that portfolio is simply the future arriving: the ration is already in force; the Day merely publishes the ledger. And the believer who wants his tears re-addressed has the sūrah's own method: not the self-whipping of manufactured sadness, but the placing of the self in the rooms where the verse's subjects wept — the night-prayer, the recitation of the Book, the gathering of the truthful (v. 119's command) — where tears, as the ṣaḥābah discovered, arrive on their own, and arrive, unlike the hypocrites', as a recompense rather than as one.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:83

> **And if God returns thee to a group of them, and they seek leave of thee to go forth, say, "You shall not go forth with me ever; nor shall you fight with me against any enemy. You were content to stay back the first time; so stay back with those who remain behind."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Standing Sentence**

Verse 83 delivers, with judicial finality, the sentence verse 43's correction had foreshadowed: *fa-in raddaka Allāhu ilā ṭāʾifatin minhum* — "if God returns thee to a group of them" — the scenario being the Prophet's return from Tabūk to Madinah, where the stayers remained — *fa-staʾdhinūka lil-khurūj* — "and they seek thy leave to go forth" — on the *next* campaign, whenever it should come. Their calculation, Ibn Kathīr explains, was reputational: the first absence had aged badly, and the next muster offered a chance to rebuild the public ledger — service performed, at last, where it could be seen. The Prophet's scripted answer ends the option permanently: *lan takhūjū maʿiya abadan* — "you shall not go forth with me *ever*" — *wa-lā tuqātilū maʿī ʿaduwwan* — "nor fight with me against any enemy." The sentence is not a fine but a discharge — and verse 47 has already explained its wisdom: *had they gone forth with you, they would have added to you nothing but troubles* — the camp of a critical campaign is no place for men whose hearts are with the enemy; their presence is a subtraction wearing a uniform.

**Quarantine as Protection of the Mission**

The jurists and commentators, reading verses 47 and 83 together, draw the doctrine the source commentary preserves: the community's exclusion of the unreliable from its critical operations is not vengeance but *immunology*. Al-Rāzī notes the precision of the harm avoided — their going forth would have meant *khabāl*: confusion in the ranks, courier-work between the camp and the enemy, morale-work among the weak (v. 47's sammāʿūn), the negotiation of surrender at the worst moment; better a hole in the line than a fifth column inside it. And the sentence's final clause returns the sūrah's signature sarcasm with full weight: *fa-qʿudū maʿa al-qāʿidīn* — "so sit with those who sit" — the second deployment of the verse 46 formula, now not as divine aside but as the Prophet's own standing rule: the identity they chose — the sitters — is granted to them as a permanent rank. Al-Qurṭubī hears in the sentence's grammar an almost legislative mercy: no names are published, no property seized, no punishment beyond the loss of a seat they never wanted; the community simply *organizes itself around* the unreliable, and lets the unreliable organize itself around the bench.

**"You Were Content the First Time": The Verse's Theory of Precedent**

The sentence's stated ground deserves attention: *fa-qad raḍītum bil-quoʿūdi al-marrah al-ūlā* — "you were *content* to sit back the first time." The word is raḍiya — contentment — the same word the sūrah uses for God's pleasure (v. 100) and the believer's sufficiency (v. 59): the emotion is honored even in its misdirection; what convicts the stayers is not weakness but *settled satisfaction* with the wrong arrangement. Al-Ṭabarī draws the principle courts still use: the first act, performed under duress, might be excused; the first act, performed with contentment, *establishes character*, and character is the basis of standing. The believer who wants a second chance at trust must show, in the interval, the dis-content of repentance — the itch of the wrong choice — which is exactly what the three of verse 118 will show and be received for; the men of verse 83, by contrast, showed the contentment of men waiting for better optics, and the door of the army closed on them not in anger but in arithmetic.

**Exclusion and the Modern Community**

For the modern believer, verse 83 is the hardest of the sūrah's communal rules and the most necessary: *reliability is a precondition of responsibility.* The congregation that keeps handing its critical work to the intermittently committed — because they are charming, fundable, or simply present — will keep discovering verse 47's khubāl; and the verse licenses, gently but firmly, the exit of the unreliable from the load-bearing roles: no humiliation, no purge, just a reassignment to the bench they have already chosen, and — the verse's quiet hope — the long reflection the bench provides. And for the one who finds himself benched: the sūrah's own testimony is that benches are where some of its greatest figures sat for fifty days (v. 118) and rose from them into the divine pleasure; the difference between the bench as sentence and the bench as mercy is made, entirely, by what the sitter does with the sitting — whether he waits for optics, or lets the quiet finish what the muster began.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:84

> **And never pray over one of them who dies, nor stand by his grave. Truly they disbelieved in God and His Messenger and died iniquitous.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Rite That Defines the Community's Boundary**

Verse 84 converts verse 80's verdict into liturgical law: *wa-lā tuṣalli ʿalā aḥadin minhum abadan* — "and never pray over any of them who dies" — *wa-lā taqum ʿalā qabrih* — "nor stand by his grave." The two prohibited acts are the two stations of the Muslim funeral: the janāzah prayer — the communal supplication offered over the shrouded dead, a standing prayer without bowing or prostration, four takbīrs wrapped around forgiveness-requests — and the graveside presence at the lowering and burial. Al-Qurṭubī, precise as ever about the rite, notes its theology: the funeral prayer is, in its essence, an *intercession* — the community standing as one to ask mercy for its member — and the verse withdraws the rite from the died-in-open-hypocrisy because the intercession's jurisdiction (v. 80's seventy-times limit) had already been ruled closed. The verse thus functions as the community's most solemn boundary-marker: not at nationality, not at clan, but at the covenant of faith, lived and died in.

**The Case of Ibn Ubayy, Decided Twice**

The occasion is the one verse 80 narrated: the death of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ubayy — and the reports, as the source commentary preserves, differ on the sequencing. In the Ṣaḥīḥ's account from ʿUmar, the Prophet ﷺ *did* pray over him and gave his shirt for the shroud, and verse 84 came after; other reports hold the verse revealed *before* the prayer, with the Prophet answering his own scruple — "God gave me the choice" — and proceeding under the choice's license. Ibn Kathīr reconciles: the verses were coming down in that season's sequence, and the Prophet's mercy ran ahead of the final ruling, which then fixed the law for all who died thereafter in the like of Ibn Ubayy's state. The tradition's takeaway is in the tension itself: the Prophet's ﷺ personal mercy was so vast it moved at the very edge of the permissive — and the revelation's boundary, when it came, did not retroactively condemn the mercy; it simply closed the office. Ibn ʿAjībah's reflection, which the source commentary preserves, widens the lens: when a sincere servant dies, all beings pray for him — angels, the earth, the believing community; and when a hypocrite dies, the whole liturgy of creation is withdrawn — the rite the believers are forbidden is one that heaven had already declined to attend.

**"Nor Stand by His Grave": The Standing's Meaning**

The second prohibition — the graveside standing — extended the boundary from the prayer to the presence: not even the honor of company at the lowering. The jurists derived from the verse and the Prophet's practice the settled law: the funeral rites belong to those who died upon the covenant; and they balanced it, from the same Prophet's Sunnah, with the standing duties of kindness — he ﷺ visited his mother's grave (a polytheist's) and wept and said, *"I sought my Lord's leave to seek forgiveness for her, and He did not grant it; and I asked leave to visit her grave, and He granted it — so visit the graves, for they remind of death"* (recorded by Muslim from Abū Hurayrah). The distinction is exact and humane: *rites* belong to the covenant; *memory, grief, and the reminder of death* belong to humanity — and the believer who has lost a non-Muslim parent or friend holds both truths at once, weeping at the grave as the Prophet wept, praying only for the living.

**A Hard Verse Pastorally Handled**

For the modern believer, verse 84 is among the sūrah's most painful rulings — the believer with a lapsed father, an estranged unbelieving mother, a friend who died far from the faith — and honesty requires letting the verse say what it says while the Sunnah supplies its boundaries: the prohibition is addressed to the Prophet ﷺ and the community's *liturgy*, not to the grief of hearts, which God never forbids; it names a *state* of death (dying in opposition to God and His Messenger — the verse's own stated ground) that only God's own sūrah-time exposures rendered certifiable, which is why the jurists held that the funeral prayer is withheld, in later ages, only from the one whose died-in-disbelief is public and manifest — the concealed heart is never the community's to judge (v. 101). And the believer who stands, weeping, at such a grave stands inside the Prophet's own precedent at his mother's — with the reminder the Prophet commanded standing there to collect: the grave's lesson is not the dead man's account, which is closed, but the standing man's, which is not.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:85

> **And let not their wealth or their children impress thee. God desires only to punish them thereby in the life of this world, and that their souls should depart while they are disbelieving.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse Repeated: Why the Sūrah Says It Twice**

Verse 85 is, word for word, verse 55 — one of the Qur'an's rare exact repetitions — and al-Rāzī, in the observation the source commentary preserves, explains the sūrah's design: the magnetism of wealth and children is the strongest this world exerts, and the cure the verse prescribes regenerates as fast as the disease, so the revelation administers the corrective twice, at two different points in the exposure-cycle, to two different audiences of the same type. The first repetition fell among the verses of the stayers' excuses; the second falls here, among the verses of their sentence — and the placement is the sermon: at the moment when the community might most be tempted to envy the men of shade and orchards (the ones, perhaps, whose funerals verse 84 had just discussed), the Qur'an resets the lens one final time: *wa-lā tuʿjibka amwāluhum wa-lā awlāduhum* — "let not their wealth or their children impress thee."

**Prosperity's Double Function**

The verse's middle clause restates the doctrine of istidrāj with the sūrah's characteristic economy: *innamā yurīdu Allāhu an yuʿadhdhibahum bihā fī al-ḥayāti al-dunyā* — "God desires only to punish them *thereby* in the life of this world" — the *thereby* is the doctrine: the assets themselves as the instrument of the trial — the wealth that must now be defended, the children whose future feeds the compromises, the estate whose management consumes the years that worship needed; the blessing, unaccompanied by gratitude, converted into a slow tax on the soul. And the verse's final clause — *wa-an tazāgha anfusuhum wa-hum kāfirūn* — "and that their souls depart while they are disbelieving" — names the terminal outcome the tradition counts the worst of all stations: *sūʾ al-khātimah*, the evil ending — the dying upon the misdirection that the prosperity financed. The Qur'an's two verses on this death are among its most sobering:

> *"O you who believe, let not your wealth nor your children divert you from the remembrance of God; and whosoever does that — those are the losers."* (Qur'an 63:9)

> *"And spend of that whereof He has made you trustees..."* (Qur'an 57:7 — the trust-framing of all possessions)

**The Two Readings of "Punish Them Thereby"**

The commentators, as the source commentary records, offer two coordinated readings of the punishment clause, and both deserve statement because each corrects a modern error. The first — al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī's — reads the punishment as *eventual extraction*: the alms-levy on the wealth, the losses, the griefs that children bring to the ingrate — prosperity under curse, its enjoyments amortized by afflictions. The second — al-Rāzī's psychological reading — is subtler and more universal: the punishment is *the attachment itself* — the possessor who does not acknowledge the Giver lives in the standing fear of losing what he struggled to gain, and that fear, compounding daily, is the sentence served in installments; the anxiety-saturated enjoyment of the godless rich is, on this reading, not the prelude to punishment but its first phase. Both readings converge on the verse's last clause: the soul that spent its one life clutching, departs clutching — *while they are disbelieving* — the hands full and the account empty.

**The Final Reset Before the Confessions**

For the modern believer, verse 85's office is the one its repetition performs inside the sūrah: a *reset button* for the envy that exposure-sequences always regenerate. The believer who has watched the uncompromising prosper — again — reads verse 85 as the sūrah's last word before it turns to its most tender pages: verses 88–92's portraits of the striving Messenger and the weeping poor, verses 100–118's roll-call of the honored and the repented. The structure itself teaches: when tempted to envy the estate of the iniquent, do not argue with the temptation — *change the channel* to the ledger the sūrah is about to open: the Messenger who marched in the heat, the ṣādiqūn who wept for a mount, the sābiqūn with whom God was pleased, the three whose earth narrowed and whose Lord turned to them. The wealth-and-children channel will always broadcast; the verse's counsel is not to be impressed by its programming — because the network, the sūrah quietly reminds twice, is running the advertisement department of a fire.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:86

> **And when a sūrah is sent down, saying, "Believe in God and strive alongside His Messenger," those of wealth among them ask leave of thee and say, "Let us stay with those who stay behind."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Revelation That Auditions Its Hearers**

Verse 86 gives the sūrah's diagnostic technology in its most elegant form: *wa-idhā unzilat sūratun an āminū billāhi wa-jāhidū maʿa rasūlih* — "when a sūrah is sent down, saying: believe in God and strive alongside His Messenger." The sūrah is described generically — *a* sūrah, any sūrah — and its content is compressed to two imperatives: believe, strive; and the particle of the report is *idhā* — "whenever": a standing experimental condition. The exegetes, as the source commentary preserves, identify the specific occasion (a verse of the Tabūk season calling to the campaign), but the verse's grammar universalizes it: *every* revelation that pairs faith with costly action functions as an audition — the same words landing on the whole assembly and sorting it by inner composition, exactly as 2:265's rain lands on gardens and rock-strewn ground and reveals what each was made of. The Qur'an states the sorting mechanism plainly:

> *"And We sent down of the Qur'an that which is a healing and a mercy for the believers; and it increases the wrongdoers in nothing but loss."* (Qur'an 17:82)

**The Wealthy Request a Waiver**

The verse's second half is the audition's results, reported in the register of near-comedy: *istaʾdhanaka ashiddāʾu minkum riddan* — "the men of substance among them ask thy leave" — *wa-qālū dharnā nakun maʿa al-qāʿidīn* — "and say: let us stay with those who stay." Two details carry the condemnation. First, the askers are the *wealthy* — the adjective the sūrah has already problematized (v. 85's wealth-that-punishes): the very men whose resources made the striving of verse 41 mandatory at the *highest* personal cost-tier — wealth *and* self — ask instead for the lowest, which is neither. Second, their formula is a community-request: "let *us* be with the ones who stay" — they ask not to be exceptions but members of a class, the sitting-class, requesting in effect recognition of sitting as their battalion. Ibn Kathīr notes the phrase's later iconic status: the tradition remembered *al-qāʿidūn* — "the stayers-behind" — as the standing name of the choice; and the sūrah's verdict on the class had already been written in verse 46: *sitting is stamped upon them* — the joke, if it is one, is that they ask permission for what has already been declared their permanent condition.

**The Root of the Waiver Request**

Al-Qurṭubī reads the request with a psychologist's care: it is not cowardice in the simple sense (some of the wealthy stayers had fought at Uḥud) but *the specific allergy of property to risk* — wealth, once accumulated, generates its own risk-calculus in which the portfolio's survival outranks every other good; the sūrah's two imperatives (believe, strive) present the audit that capital cannot pass without converting itself into provision and mounts. Al-Rāzī sharpens the point with the verse's deeper structure: faith (*īmān*) and striving (*jihād*) are named together because, in the Madinan revelation, they are a *single package* — belief verified by costly obedience; the request "leave us the faith without the striving" is thus, whether the requesters know it or not, a request to divide what God has joined — and the sūrah's answer, in verse 88's next breath, is that the division does not exist: the Messenger and those with him believe *and* strive, with both currencies.

**Every Congregation's Standing Moment**

For the modern believer, verse 86 is a mirror hung at the door of every sermon, class, and appeal that names a duty with a price tag. The pattern repeats with perfect fidelity: the announcement lands on all — a cause needs tutors, a family needs reconciliation at the cost of pride, a community's vulnerable need visitors, a truth needs saying in a room where it will cost — and the wealthy-and-able request, politely, to be counted with the seated. The verse's diagnostic gift is its naming of the *mechanism*: the request is not random; it comes from the exact quarter where the duty's cost is highest — and its honest name is not prudence but a divided loyalty, the property requesting exemption from the faith it was meant to serve. The believer's self-audit question writes itself: when the sūrah-sayings of my own week name their two imperatives, which class do I petition to join — and what, in my case, is the portfolio making the petition?

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:87

> **They were content to be with those who stay behind, and a seal was set upon their hearts, so that they understand not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Contentment That Becomes Character**

Verse 87 completes verse 86's sentence with its cause, and the first clause is the sūrah's verdict on the will: *raḍū bil-quoʿūdi maʿa al-qāʿidīn* — "they were content to sit with the sitters." The verb — raḍiya, to be well-pleased — is the same verb that carries the sūrah's highest blessing (v. 100: *raḍiya Allāhu ʿanhum* — God well-pleased with them); the vocabulary is never accidental: the verse locates the tragedy not in the sitting (a circumstance) but in the *contentment* (a verdict of the soul upon its circumstance) — they looked at the sitting, the shade, the preserved portfolio, and pronounced it *good*; the one emotion that should have been impossible for a believer at the hour of the Messenger's march became their settled satisfaction. Al-Qurṭubī's reflection, which the source commentary preserves, deserves its full weight: contentment is the self's signature on its situation; wherever a soul's contentment lies, there is its religion — and these souls signed for the bench.

**The Seal: Divine Reciprocity and Its History**

The verse's second clause states the consequence in the Qur'an's most sober idiom: *fa-tubiʿa ʿalā qulūbihim* — "and a seal was set upon their hearts, so that they understand not" (or, in the source commentary's noted variant reading, *tutbiʿa* — passive: "it was sealed") — the root ṭ-b-ʿ (to seal, to stamp) continuing the dossier of heart-fates the sūrah has been compiling: the disease of v. 125, the sealing of v. 93, the sealing-verb of v. 77. The doctrine of the seal is stated in the Qur'an with unfailing causal order, and 2:7 gives it as a formula:

> *"As for the disbelievers, whether thou warnest them or warnest them not, it is the same for them: they believe not. God has sealed their hearts and their hearing, and over their eyes is a covering; and theirs shall be a great punishment."* (Qur'an 2:7)

The order, as the commentators insist, is never: God seals, therefore they cannot believe; but: they chose disbelief until belief's organ atrophied, and the atrophy is described from God's side as His seal — 40:28's Pharaoh's courtier states the human-side of the same process: *"Ye kill a man merely because he says: my Lord is God?"* and the tradition's rule is Ibn ʿAjībah's: the attributes of rigidity in the Book follow upon rigidity in the creature — the sun hardens clay and softens wax by the same shining. Verse 87's sealed men had sat through Badr, Uḥud, Hudaybiyah, Khaybar, and the sūrah's own exposures; each event found the heart softer or harder, and the repeated choices of the bench had finished the heart's conversion into its final constituency.

**"So That They Understand Not": The Loss That Guards Itself**

The seal's stated consequence — *fa-hum lā yafqahūn* — "so that they understand not" — repeats verse 81's closing diagnosis (*in kānū yafqahūn*), and the repetition is the sūrah's grimmest statistic: the failure is specifically of *fiqh* — comprehension that processes implications, not raw information; they heard "believe and strive" and computed "excuse and sit"; they watched the campaigns succeed and computed luck; the understanding-faculty had been co-opted so long as the portfolio's lawyer that it could no longer serve as truth's judge even in its own defense. Ibn Taymiyyah's dictum fits the verse like a glove: the punishment of sin is further sin — the misunderstanding that follows the mischoosing, guarding the sinner from the very comprehension that would have freed him; and the Qur'an names the final self-protection of the sealed class in 2:18's parable: deaf, dumb, blind — *and they will not return*.

**The Present-Tense Mercy of a Past-Tense Verdict**

For the modern believer, verse 87 must be read with the sūrah's own chronology in view, or it will be misread as fatalism. The verse describes a *reached* state, the far end of a long choosing — but the believer reading it is, by that very reading, still in the choosing: the fact that a passage can still feel like a warning is the evidence that the seal has not closed; the sealed heart reads warnings as noise. The tradition's counsel is thus diagnostic and immediate: treat every fresh tug toward costly obedience as the organ still alive — and answer it in the season of its tugging, because contentment compounds: each sitting accepted with satisfaction makes the next sitting more comfortable and the standing more alien, until the seat has grown around the sitter like the proverbial bark. The verse's bitter phrase — *content to be with those who stay behind* — is, read rightly, the most motivating sentence in the sūrah: it is the photograph of a finished man, posted at the trailhead for everyone still on the path to see exactly where the comfortable fork delivers.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:88

> **But the Messenger and those who believe with him strive with their wealth and with their selves. And theirs are the good things of the Hereafter. And it is they who are the prospering.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse of Contrast and Its Twin Currencies**

Verse 88 is the sūrah's hinge from verdict to portrait: after the waivers of verse 86 and the seals of verse 87, one *lākin* — "but" — turns the camera: *lakinna al-rasūla wa-alladhīna āmanū maʿahu jāhadū bi-amwālihim wa-anfusihim* — "but the Messenger and those who believe with him strive with their wealth and their selves." The formula is the sūrah's exact standard of exchange, now seen from the credit side: the two currencies (māl and nafs) that verses 41, 44, and 81 demanded and the stayers withheld, the Messenger and his people spent — and the verb is the intensive jāhadū, the root j-h-d that names effort at the edge of capacity. The verse's symmetry is its sermon: the sūrah has spent eighty-seven verses itemizing what the averted withheld; verse 88 simply presents the account of those who did not, and lets the two ledgers lie side by side — the sitting-class with its sealed receipts, and the striving-class with *al-ḥasanāt* — "the good things" — of the Hereafter, the plural of beauty that the Qur'an elsewhere defines:

> *"God has promised the believers, men and women, gardens beneath which rivers flow, to abide therein, and goodly dwelling-places in the Gardens of Eden; and a pleasure from God greater than that; that is the supreme triumph."* (Qur'an 9:72 — the promise verse 88 compresses)

And the verse's closing participle — *wa-ulāʾika hum al-mufliḥūn* — "and it is they who are the prospering" — takes the Qur'an's commercial verb (falāḥ: to till successfully, to harvest, to thrive) and awards it, definitively, to the spenders: the ones who parted with both currencies are the ones whose farm succeeded.

**The Class Register: "Those with Him"**

The verse's phrase *alladhīna āmanū maʿahu* — "those who believe *with him*" — deserves notice, because it names the community by its direction rather than its pedigree: not "those who believe in what he brought" only, but those whose belief placed them physically at his side — the Muhājirūn who left Makkah, the Anṣār who opened Madinah, and the ranks of Tabūk's marchers. And the Qur'an will close the sūrah's historical register with the same class, named and honored, in verse 100: *al-sābiqūna al-awwalūna* — the first forerunners — *"God is well-pleased with them, and they are well-pleased with Him."* The Prophet ﷺ fixed the register's order of honor in the counsel he gave to the community's latecomers:

> *"Do not revile my Companions; for by Him in whose hand is my soul, were one of you to spend the like of Mount Uḥud in gold, he would not attain the measure of one of them, nor its half."* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī (3673) and Muslim (2540), from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī)

The ḥadīth is verse 88's arithmetic made explicit: the two-currency spending of the first generation was made at the world's highest price (everything, on a promise unseen) and bought what later gold cannot.

**Spending as Belief Made Visible**

The exegetes draw from the verse the doctrine the sūrah has been assembling since verse 44: *jihād* here is faith's visibility — the believer's inner conviction arriving at the point of expenditure, wealth and self both convertible into obedience. Ibn Taymiyyah's principle, which the tradition summarizes, reads verse 88 as the definition of the community's leadership-class: whoever's belief stays interior while his currencies stay home stands, whatever his labels, with the sealed class of verse 87; whoever's belief reaches the spending stands with the Messenger — the taxonomy is behavioral, and it runs on the two ledgers only. Al-Qurṭubī adds the verse's quiet mercy: the sentence is a description, not a caste — the door of the striving-class was open to the sūrah's very next subjects, the confessional sinners of verse 102 and the three suspended men of verse 118, who crossed from the bench to the muster not by birth but by repentance, and found verse 88's *mufliḥūn* enlarged to hold them.

**The Modern Translation of Two Currencies**

For the modern believer, verse 88 translates without loss: the currencies have not changed, only the fronts. Wealth still converts — into the funded school, the settled debt of a struggling family, the institution that outlives its donor; the self still converts — into the years given to service, the risks taken for the truth's public standing, the comfort spent on the community's hard assignments. The verse's audit-question remains binary and portable: what did my belief cost my *māl* this year, and what did it cost my *nafs*? The honest answers place each believer, for that year, in one of the sūrah's two classes, which are the only two classes the sūrah recognizes. The promise attached to the spending class — *the good things of the Hereafter* and the harvest-name *mufliḥūn* — is the Qur'an's standing rebuttal of the arithmetic of loss: nothing spent on God's path is spent; it is sown, and the sūrah's last word on such sowing is the one the Prophet ﷺ gave at the campaign's muster — *the spending man's shade is his charity on the Day there is no shade* (recorded in the Sunan), or as the Book itself closes the account: *"Whatever good you send ahead for your souls, you will find it with God, better and greater in reward."* (73:20; cf. 2:110)

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:89

> **God has prepared for them Gardens beneath which rivers flow, wherein they will abide forever. That is the supreme triumph.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prepared Place**

Verse 89 answers verse 88's striving with the tense of its own: *aʿadda Allāhu lahum* — "God has *prepared* for them" — the perfect tense, the work already done. Al-Rāzī lingers on the grammar's comfort: the believers march in the heat of this world while their reward stands *completed* in the next — the destination not merely promised but furnished; the verb ʿadda is the Qur'an's word for a host prepared for a guest (as 12:31's banquet-table *uʿiddat* — "was prepared" — for Yūsuf's arrivals), and the image beneath the doctrine is hospitality: the striving travelers are not going to a prize to be won but to a table already set. And the place's own name is given with the Qur'an's fixed geography: *jannātin tajrī min taḥtihā al-anhār* — "Gardens beneath which rivers flow" — the archetype of the desert imagination's impossibility: shade, water, and green without labor, the exact inversion of the Tabūk road.

**Abiding: The Reward's Real Name**

The verse's middle clause carries the reward's true weight: *khālidīna fīhā abadan* — "abiding therein forever." The tradition's masters, surveying the Qur'an's descriptions of Paradise, note that its goods are named in local currencies (rivers, gardens, companions, silk) precisely because the reader must be paid in a currency he can value — but the reward's *name* is not any of the goods: it is the *abiding*. Every pleasure this world offers arrives already depreciating — the meal ends, the youth ages, the estate changes hands; the essential poverty of the creature is not pain but *perishing*, and the Qur'an's supreme promise is the end of perishing: *khālidīna* — staying, remaining, the verb of what does not run out. The Prophet ﷺ taught the community to reach for this exact promise in its simplest daily transaction:

> *"Whoever says: there is no god but God alone, without partner... God will admit him through one of the eight gates of Paradise, whichever of them he wills."* — and in the Ṣaḥīḥ's wording: he will be delivered from the Fire (recorded by Muslim and al-Bukhārī in variants from Abū Hurayrah, on the word of pure declaration)

**"The Supreme Triumph": The Sūrah's Accounting Term**

The verse's final word — *dhālika huwa al-fawzu al-ʿaẓīm* — "that is the supreme triumph" — is the Qur'an's ledger-language: fawz, gain, profit, the winning of a sought objective. The sūrah has been conducting a seminar on profit since verse 52's "two good things" and verse 81's thermal arithmetic; verse 89 closes the account: the world's entire portfolio — its wealth (v. 85's punishing asset), its comforts (v. 86's bench), its laughter (v. 82's ration) — is a *trading position* whose term expires at the grave; and the two currencies spent on the path of God (v. 88) are the only holdings that transfer. The Qur'an states the exchange-rate elsewhere in the commercial idiom verse 89 assumes:

> *"O you who believe, shall I direct you to a commerce that will save you from a painful punishment? That you believe in God and His Messenger, and strive with your wealth and your selves in the way of God — that is better for you, if you but know."* (Qur'an 61:10–11)

**Forever as Discipline, Not Wallpaper**

For the modern believer, verse 89's eternity is easily repeated and slowly believed, and the sūrah's own method is to *cost* it: the verse stands immediately after the two-currency verse, and its grammar says the preparation is for *them* — the strivers just defined; forever is not the believer's default but his destination, and the sūrah spends its middle chapters mapping which roads deliver. The believer's practical discipline is the verse's tense: *prepared already* — the work is finished, the rooms are furnished, the rivers run now; and the day's duty, viewed from verse 89, is not the earning of Paradise but the *not straying from the road that leads to what is already ours* — a reframe the ṣaḥābah carried into battle and the modern believer can carry into Monday: the appointments are made; the traveler has only to keep walking.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:90

> **And some of the Bedouins came to seek exemption, and those who lied to God and His Messenger stayed back. A painful punishment shall befall those who disbelieved among them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Responses to One Muster**

Verse 90 opens the sūrah's final human gallery — the Bedouin periphery of the community, reacting to the Tabūk summons — and the verse sorts them, with the space of half a line, into the truthful and the false: *wa-jāʾa al-muʿadhdhirūna min al-aʿrābi* — "the excusers of the Bedouins came" — "of the Bedouins" — *li-yuʾdhan lahum* — "that leave might be granted them" — and the exegetical reports, which al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr preserve, name the scene: delegations arrived with reasons, and the reasons had to be weighed. The verse's second clause renders the verdict on the ones who did not trouble to come: *wa-taqallada alladhīna kadhabū Allāha wa-rasūlahu* — "and those who lied to God and His Messenger stayed back" — the verb taqallada, from the root q-l-d, is the verse's brilliant lexical choice: literally, to wear something hanging loose around the neck — a necklace, a garland, a yoke — here, to settle at ease in staying; the stayers *wore* their default like an ornament, comfortable and displayed. And the verse's third movement announces the penalty that closes the whole series: *sa-yuṣību alladhīna kafarū minhum ʿadhābun alīm* — "a painful punishment shall befall those of them who disbelieved" — the qualifier *minhum* ("among them") doing decisive legal work: not all the Bedouin, but the disbelieving among them — the sūrah's signal that the next verses will begin sorting this population with the finest grain the revelation has yet used.

**The Excuse Economy of the Periphery**

The Bedouin hold a distinct position in the sūrah's sociology, and verse 90 presupposes what verse 97 will state: the desert populations were newer to the covenant, harder to reach with instruction, and more subject to the season's logic (date-harvest, water, grazing) than the settled community of Madinah. The excusers who came were, by the mere fact of coming, a middle class — their reasons might be true or false, and the Prophet ﷺ heard them: this is the Sunnah's settled practice from the campaign — he accepted the excuses of the manifestly burdened (v. 91's blameless classes) and turned a knowing face from the convenient ones, without extracting confessions. Al-Qurṭubī draws the pastoral doctrine the tradition kept: the community's leadership is obliged to *provide a door* for the truthful burdened (the leave-system of verses 91–92) precisely so that the door itself may separate the honest from the theatrical — an economy of excuses runs on trust until disclosure arrives, and the sūrah's disclosures (vv. 93–96, 101) are always God's work, not the leader's surveillance.

**Lying to God: The Verse's Theology of the False Excuse**

The clause that convicts the absent is startling in its addressee: *alladhīna kadhabū Allāha wa-rasūlahu* — "those who *lied to God and His Messenger*." A man who stays home from a march has lied, at most, to a courier — but the verse's grammar makes the excuse-economy vertical: the summons was God's, the leave-system God's, the Commander God's deputy; to game the system is to attempt a fraud upon the Court itself. Al-Rāzī reads the clause as the sūrah's reductio of the hypocrite's whole theory: he imagines he is managing human opinion (the Prophet's, the neighbors', history's), and the verse prices his maneuver at its true addressee — an attempted deception of the One who, verse 78 has just declared, knows the sirr and the najwā; the absurdity is the point, and the sūrah's idiom for the hypocrite's fate will complete it in verse 77's seal.

**The Sort Has Begun: Reading On**

For the modern believer, verse 90 is best understood as the overture of a movement (verses 90–101) that the tradition reads as the revelation's most delicate sociology: God sorting a mixed population — excuse-makers, liars, blameless disabled, weeping poor, comfort-sitters, secretly sincere desert believers — into the ledger-lines each has chosen. The verse's own first lesson lands before the music starts: *come and be examined* — the excusers who traveled to the Prophet to state their case are, whatever their truth-content, in a categorically better position than the ornamental stayers who wore their default at home; the community's door, in every age, is where the sorting begins, and the believer who brings his real reasons into the real room — the imam's study, the board meeting, the family table — has at least kept his fraud small enough to be corrected, which is the beginning of verse 103's purification.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:91

> **There is no blame upon the weak, nor upon the sick, nor upon those who find nothing to spend, if they are sincere to God and His Messenger. There is no way against the virtuous. And God is Forgiving, Merciful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Great Charter of Excuse**

If verse 90 is the sorting's overture, verse 91 is its charter of mercy, and the tradition has always read it so: *laysa ʿalā al-ḍuʿafāʾi* — "there is no blame upon the weak" — *wa-lā ʿalā al-marḍā* — "nor upon the sick" — *wa-lā ʿalā alladhīna lā yajidūna mā yunfiqūna* — "nor upon those who find nothing to spend" — three classes, three nouns naming three different poverties of capacity: bodily weakness (the old, the frail, ʿĀmir ibn Buḥayr-type reports of the Madinan aged), sickness (the chronically ill, whose participation would have been a burden to the army and a destruction to themselves), and the destitute (the verse 92 class — those who came weeping because there was nothing in the purse at all). And over all three stands the verse's one non-negotiable condition — *iḍā naṣaḥū Allāha wa-rasūlahu* — "if they are sincere to God and His Messenger" — the verb naṣaḥa, whose root n-ṣ-ḥ names the purity of honey freed of wax: sincerity as the absence of admixture, the heart single in its loyalty even when the body cannot march.

**"No Way Against the Virtuous": The Legal Maxims**

The verse's fourth clause — *mā ʿalā al-muḥsinīna min sabīl* — "there is no way against the virtuous" — became, in the tradition's hands, a legal maxim of the first order, cited across the fiqh manuals as the charter of the excused: the muḥsin (the doer of iḥsān) is legally unreachable — no censure, no suspicion, no duty-demand beyond capacity; Ibn Kathīr states its use plainly: the verse is the proof that the blind, the lame, and the chronically ill of verse 92's world bear no campaign-duty at all, and the Sunnah confirmed it in the Prophet's ﷺ practice — he took the excuses of the manifestly burdened and named no names. And the condition-clause carries the verse's balance: the exemption is filed under sincerity, not under the disability itself — a bitter heart in a broken body gains nothing from the charter, and a sincere heart in a broken body loses nothing by the brokenness; the ledger of duty, as the sūrah keeps insisting, runs on the two currencies each soul actually holds.

**The Weeping Poor of Verse 92, Announced Here**

The exegetes link verse 91 to the scene the very next verse records — the ṣaḥābah who came to the Prophet ﷺ asking transport to Tabūk, and, told there was none, *turned away, their eyes overflowing with tears* (v. 92) — and al-Qurṭubī's note has entered the tradition permanently: the weeping of the able-but-unmounted at their exclusion from the march was, in the divine accounting, a *striving* — their grief itself was credited as the deed their poverty forbade; the Prophet ﷺ is reported to have said of the likes of them that they are *partners in the campaign's reward* though they stayed (the reports of the Ṣaḥīḥ collections state the principle: the one who dies still intending the deed shares its reward). The doctrine completes verse 91's mercy with its deepest layer: God audits not only capacity but *the will at capacity's edge* — the wish that outran the wallet is seen, and entered in the book.

**Sincerity as the Modern Gateway**

For the modern believer, verse 91 is the standing corrective to two chronic errors. The first is the community's: the congregation that quietly demotes its weak, sick, and poor from full membership — seating them outside the decision-making, the serving, the honored — reads verse 91 as its constitutional text: the excluded-by-capacity are *not excluded*, and the sūrah will honor its own charter by letting verse 92's weepers stand, in the revelation itself, as the equal portrait of the marchers. The second error is the individual's: the believer who reads his own limitations (health, means, circumstance) as a verdict of second-class standing in God's service — verse 91 hands him the charter countersigned with two Names: *wa-Allāhu Ghafūrun Raḥīm* — Forgiving, Merciful — the exemptions administered by the Judge who is glad to grant them; and the verse's one condition names the believer's entire remaining duty, available at every income and every diagnosis: *naṣāḥ* — the unmixed heart, loyal to God and His Messenger in the small room where no one audits it, which is the only room the verse requires, and the only room God, who knows the sirr, ever audits anyway.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:92

> **Nor is there blame upon those who, when they came to thee that thou mightest mount them, and thou saidst, "I can find nothing whereon to mount you," went back, their eyes overflowing with tears, grieving that they could find nothing to spend.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Portrait the Revelation Painted and Kept**

Verse 92 is the sūrah's most tender portrait, and the tradition — al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, and the sīrah literature — preserves its occasion by name: the musters of Tabūk, when the poor of Madinah, the likes of ʿImrān ibn Ḥuṣayn and Abū Laylā and the seven of the Banū ʿAbd ibn ʿAwf (the reports of the Sunan collections transmit the scene in several lines), came to the Prophet ﷺ saying: *ḥamilnā* — "carry us" — that we may march; and he had to answer with the truth: *lā ajidu mā aḥmilukum ʿalayhi* — "I find nothing whereon to mount you" — the treasury empty, the camels spent; and they turned back — *rajaʿū* — with eyes streaming, *gharḍā* — the word's root gh-r-ḍ naming the eye inflamed and brimming from grief — because they had no *infāq* — nothing to spend — and would miss the march. The verse's structure is its verdict: the entire legal treatment of these men is one clause — *laysa ʿalā al-sabīl* — no way, no blame, nothing chargeable — and then the portrait stands in the Qur'an forever: the only place in the Book where weeping is the heroism.

**Sincerity's Perfect Image**

The exegetes read the verse as the completion of verse 91's condition (*iḍā naṣaḥū*) with a photographic case: here is naṣāḥ made visible — the men's grief was not for reputation (they were publicly excused, publicly poor), not for share in the spoils (their tears flowed at the news of *no mounts*, before any battle), but for the lost *communion of the duty itself* — they wept as lovers weep at a missed meeting; and the Qur'an honored the grief by fixing it in recitation until the Day. Al-Rāzī draws the doctrine: the deed's worth is set by the will's intent at the point of obstruction — these men were stopped only by God's own distribution of resources (they lacked what He did not give), and the heart that resents nothing of His distribution but grieves only its distance from His command has already performed the obedience it could not ride to. The Prophet ﷺ stated the accounting in the ḥadīth that governed all such cases:

> *"Whoever intends a good deed and does not do it, God writes it with Him as a complete good deed."* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī (6491) and Muslim (131) from Ibn ʿAbbās)

**The Community's Empty Treasury and the Prophet's Truth**

The scene teaches a second lesson the tradition has not let die: the Prophet ﷺ, asked for mounts, said *I find nothing* — the leader of a community, at the hour of its greatest campaign, telling the poorest of his followers the exact truth about the treasury; no theater of abundance, no borrowed credit, no shame-faced spin — and the truth, spoken plainly, did not break the bonds; the men wept and *turned back*, still his, still the sūrah's honored. Ibn Kathīr's implication stands for every community's leadership: the trust of the poor is kept not by pretending capacity but by truthful poverty honored with full dignity — and the Qur'an's own response (engraving the weepers in the Book) shows what heaven thinks of leaders whose treasuries are honest and whose poor are beloved.

**Tears as the Modern Measure**

For the modern believer, verse 92 supplies the sūrah's most personal audit-question: *what was the last thing I wept at missing?* The question sorts the inner life with one probe: the man who weeps at lost investment opportunities but not at lost chances to serve; the woman who grieves a canceled vacation but not a canceled ḥajj; the community that mourns a defunded program only when the photo-op dies — verse 92 stands as the portrait of the inverted scale, tears at the *right* losses. And the verse guards the believer's theology of circumstance at its most painful joint: when capacity genuinely fails — the health that will not carry the pilgrimage, the wallet that will not carry the project, the era that will not permit the service — the verse's weepers model the only response that keeps the failure holy: turn back, keep the longing, and let the longing be the deed; God's pen, the Prophet ﷺ promised, was already writing it complete — and the sūrah itself, by including them, has published their reward in advance.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:93

> **The way is only against those who seek exemption of thee while they are rich. They are content to be with those who stay behind. And God has sealed their hearts, so that they know not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Symmetry Completed: Rich Askers, Poor Weepers**

Verse 93 completes the sūrah's diptych with a formal precision the commentators love to display: verse 91 opened *laysa ʿalā al-ḍuʿafāʾi* — "no blame upon the weak"; verse 93 opens *innamā al-sabīlu* — "the way is *only* against" — the same legal instrument (sabīl, "way of proceeding, grounds of action") pointed in the opposite direction; and the class it targets is defined by a single devastating qualifier: *alladhīna yastaʾdhanūnaka wa-hum aghniyāʾ* — "those who seek leave of thee *while they are rich*." The poor who came weeping for want of mounts (v. 92) and the rich who came working the leave-system (v. 86) are set in one frame, and the difference between them is not the request (both asked to stay) and not the staying (both stayed) — it is the *capacity*: the one had nothing to bring and grieved it; the other had everything to bring and managed it. Ibn Kathīr's rule states the sūrah's jurisprudence in a line: the exemption-charter is means-tested by God, who audits in the currency the applicant actually holds.

**The Contentment Clause, Repeated for Cause**

The verse's second clause repeats verse 87's verdict verbatim — *raḍū bil-quoʿūdi maʿa al-qāʿidīn* — "they were content to be with those who stay behind" — and the repetition is load-bearing: the first statement convicted the will; the second, placed after the rich-asker clause, convicts the *economics* of the will — this is contentment *as a financial strategy*: the wealth that could have funded the march (v. 88's two-currency standard) is retained precisely by the sitting, and the sitting is chosen precisely to retain it; the heart's satisfaction is a portfolio position. Al-Qurṭubī's ethical gloss turns the verse into a mirror for every age: ask of any man's contentment, *what does it cost him annually?* — for the poor believer's contentment with God costs him only patience, while the rich defaulter's contentment with the bench pays him dividends, and the Qur'an does not treat the two contentments as the same emotion merely because they wear the same word.

**Sealed "So That They Know Not": The Cognition Clause**

The verse's final clause — *fa-ṭababa Allāhu ʿalā qulūbihim fa-hum lā yaʿlamūn* — "and God has sealed their hearts, so that they know not" — deploys the seal of verses 87 and 77 with a new consequence-verb: not *understand not* (lā yafqahūn) but *know not* (lā yaʿlamūn) — and the descent from understanding to knowing is itself diagnostic, al-Rāzī observes: the rich askers had already lost comprehension of implications (v. 87); what remains to lose is the plain knowledge of their own case — the self-awareness that they are, in fact, the verse's subjects: men who can recite the exemption-verses and not recognize the mirror; who hear "those who seek exemption while they are rich" and picture someone else. The seal's final mercy-turned-judgment is exactly this anesthesia: the condition that spares them embarrassment in the short term costs them the addressability — the capacity to *know* — on which every repentance (v. 102, v. 118) depends; and the sūrah's rescue-arithmetic is therefore urgent by design: the sealed man of verse 93 is invited, in the verses still to come, to break the anesthesia *from the outside* — by act rather than insight: give (v. 103), confess (v. 102), associate with the truthful (v. 119) — because for a heart that can no longer *know* its state, the deed comes first and the knowing follows it home.

**The Means-Test the Believer Administers to Himself**

For the modern believer, verse 93's sabīl runs directly to the self, and its single qualifier — *while they are rich* — is the whole of the probe. The believer negotiating his exemptions (time, talent, treasure) must ask, with verse 92's weepers as the control group: is my request made at the edge of my genuine capacity (then the charter of 91 covers me, and God is Forgiving, Merciful), or is it made from within my comfort, to protect my comfort? The honest answer is obtainable, the verse implies, by one test the rich asker never ran: *the grief test* — when I could not serve, did my eyes run? The poor wept at their exclusion; the rich calculated theirs; and between those two responses, sharper than any means-test the community could draft, the Qur'an has drawn — for every generation, every income bracket, and every excuse — the sūrah's real dividing line.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:94

> **They will make excuses to you when you return to them. Say, "Do not make excuses; we do not believe you. God has informed us of your news. And God and His Messenger will see your deeds. Then you will be returned to the Knower of the unseen and the seen, and He will inform you of what you used to do."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Scripted Apology**

Verse 94 places the community in the moment after the march: *yaʿtadhirūna ilaykum idhā rajaʿtum ilayhim* — "they will make excuses to you when you return to them" — the imperfect of certainty: the apologies were, at the verse's descent, already in rehearsal; the sīrah preserves the scene of the Prophet's ﷺ return to Madinah, when the stayers — the reports name the chiefs of the evasion, Jadd ibn Qays among them — came in one by one with their hands on the Prophet's pulpit-rail, swearing and explaining, and he accepted their surfaces with the words, *as for him who has lied, God will deal with him* — the reports of the Ṣaḥīḥ collections transmit his ﷺ practice: he took their oaths and left their hearts to God. The verse's scripted reply — *qul lā taʿtadhirū* — "say: do not make excuses" — with its stated ground, *qad nabbaʾanā Allāhu min akhbārikum* — "God has informed us of your news" — announces the sūrah's regime-change in the community's information-order: the Leader's knowledge is no longer merely observational; it is revealed; the excuse-market has lost its buyer, because the one Party to every negotiation already disclosed the file.

**The End of the Alibi Economy**

The verse's middle clause — *wa-sayarā Allāhu ʿamalakum wa-rasūluhu* — "and God will see your deeds, and His Messenger" — is often read as threat, and it is; but al-Qurṭubī reads its full architecture as the closing of three escape-doors at once: the door of the alibi (closed by revelation — God has informed us), the door of the private deed (closed by surveillance — God and His Messenger will *see*), and the door of the final audit's surprises (closed by the return-clause — *thumma turaddūna ilā ʿālimi al-ghaybi wa-l-shahādati fa-yunabbiʾukum bimā kuntum taʿmalūn* — "then you will be returned to the Knower of the unseen and the seen, and He will inform you of what you used to do"). The last clause completes verse 78's omniscience-doctrine eschatologically: the God who knew the sirr and the najwā in this life will, at the return, *publish* — the hidden counsel quoted back, verbatim, by its only true witness; and the sūrah's earlier dread (v. 64: "that the sūrah should inform them of what is in their hearts") is revealed as the miniatures of the Day's full exhibition.

**Deeds as the Only Remaining Language**

Al-Rāzī draws the verse's pastoral logic, which the tradition adopted as a rule of spiritual direction: when words have lost their standing (perjury established, alibis revealed), God does not close the case — He changes the admissible evidence: *God and His Messenger will see your deeds.* The hypocrite's tragedy is that he has spent his capital on testimony (oaths, excuses, performances of grief) while the new regime trades only in deeds; and the sūrah's sequence is deliberately open-doored: verse 102's confessional sinners will be received, not for eloquence, but for the visible act — the damaged charity repaired, the confession made without alibi — and the three of verse 118 for the plain truthfulness of silence. The verse thus functions, within the sūrah, as the signpost over the road from theater to reality: *stop explaining; start doing* — the counsel the modern believer may recognize as the one thing every serious moral tradition has independently rediscovered, and the Qur'an states here as a divine protocol.

**The Return to the Two-Knowing King**

The verse's final title repays slow reading: *ʿālim al-ghaybi wa-l-shahāda* — "the Knower of the unseen *and* the seen" — the two domains of verses 78 and 94 joined in one Name; He knew what was hidden while it happened and what was shown; nothing crosses from one domain to the other except through Him — the secret becomes seen (at the informing), and the seen is judged with the secret it carried. For the modern believer, verse 94 is the standing refusal of the alibi-life: the curated profile, the polished narrative, the explanation always ready — the verse's *say* levels all of it with five words — *God has informed us of your news* — and then offers, in the same breath, the only durable freedom: a life whose news needs no managing, because its deeds were done for the Two Watchers the verse names — God and His Messenger — and are ready, as the return-clause promises, to be *informed of* without shame: which is, when all is said, the whole difference between the two men of the sūrah's final gallery — the one whose file was read to him in verse 94's dread, and the one of verse 111 who *rejoices in the bargain he has made*.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:95

> **They will swear to you by God, when you return to them, that you may turn aside from them. So turn aside from them; truly they are unclean. And Hell is their refuge, as a recompense for what they used to earn.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Oath's Undeclared Purpose**

Verse 95 continues the return-scene with the anatomy of the apology's engine: *sa-yahlifūna billāhi idhā rajaʿtum ilayhim* — "they will swear to you by God when you return to them" — *li-tuʿriḍū ʿanhum* — "that you may turn aside from them." The oath's purpose-clause is the verse's exposure: the swearing is not (as oaths are meant to be) testimony in the service of truth, but *instrument* — a tool for producing an outcome: the community's aversion turned aside (*aʿraḍa*, to turn the face away from), the matter dropped, the social peace restored on the hypocrites' terms. The sūrah has documented this oath-technology from its opening movement (vv. 56, 62, 74: they swear by God that they are of you; they swear to please you; they swear they said nothing) — verse 95 names its purpose for the first time outright, and the purpose is the purchase of avoidance: *make them stop looking at us.*

**The Divine Counter-Command**

Then comes the verse's great surprise, and the tradition's commentators from al-Ṭabarī onward have paused on it: *fa-aʿriḍū ʿanhum* — "so turn aside from them" — God commands the very thing the oaths were engineered to obtain; the hypocrites will get what they swore for. Al-Qurṭubī resolves the paradox that has troubled readers: the two aʿraḍ-s are not the same act — the hypocrites sought the believers' *aversion from their case* (investigation, exposure, the social reckoning their conduct had earned), and God grants instead the believers' *aversion from their company* — the cold, deliberate withdrawal of fellowship that the sūrah elsewhere prescribes for unrepentant corruption; the verse is not the hypocrites' victory but their sentence: you wanted the believers to look away? They will look away — at you, and for good; and what they look away *to* is stated in the verse's next clauses. Al-Rāzī reads the command's mercy-forward face: the community is spared the endless litigation of unreconcilable hearts — the Prophet's ﷺ energy is not to be consumed adjudicating perjuries that God has already adjudicated; the file is closed above; close it below.

**"They Are Unclean": Ritual Language as Moral Verdict**

The verse's ground-clause — *innahum rijsun* — "truly they are unclean" — borrows the ritual vocabulary (rijs: the defilement that bars approach to prayer and Qur'an) and applies it to persons as such, and the exegetes note the deliberate shock: the hypocrites are scrupulous in the visible purities (wudūʾ, food, dress) — their defilement is entirely interior, the najāsah that no water reaches, the 2:10 state: *"in their hearts is a disease."* The verse thus completes the sūrah's inside-out inversion: the campfire-mockers (v. 65) were ritually pure men with filthy hearts; the weeping poor (v. 92) were materially destitute men with the purest interior the Book records — and the final assessments — *rijs* here, *mufliḥ* there — run on the interior axis alone. And the destiny-clause closes the accounting with the sūrah's standard signature — *wa-maʾwāhum jahannamu jazāʾan bimā kānū yaksibūn* — "and Hell is their refuge, as a recompense for what they used to earn" — the refuge-metaphor (maʾwā: dwelling, shelter) inverted into horror: the home the escapers ran to, when they ran from every duty, has been prepared to receive them — the terminal address of a lifetime of avoidance.

**Turning Aside in the Modern Community**

For the modern believer, verse 95 states a hard and useful communal law: *fellowship is conditional on the case a heart makes of itself.* The believer is not the community's detective (the Prophet ﷺ accepted surfaces and left hearts to God — that practice stands); but when conduct has been exposed and answered with perjury, the verse prescribes neither rage nor reform-campaign — it prescribes withdrawal: the deliberate, dignified, unhostile turning-away that lets the unclean case cool outside the fellowship's warmth, with the door left open at exactly the width God left it open in this same sūrah — through verse 102's confession and verse 118's repentance, which the withdrawn warmth is, in part, designed to make longed-for. The believer who turns aside as verse 95 commands does so without gloating, having read the verse's last word on himself: that all sons of Adam are candidates for rijs, that his own cleanliness is a loan from the same Merciful, and that his turning-away is itself an act of worship only so long as it is done *for God* — not for the pleasure of looking down.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:96

> **They will swear to you that you may be pleased with them. But if you are pleased with them, truly God is not pleased with iniquitous people.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Pleasure Market and Its Real Currency**

Verse 96 is the oath-series' final entry and its most compressed: *sa-yahlifūna lakum li-tarḍaw ʿanhum* — "they will swear to you that you may be pleased with them" — and the stated goal is the exposure: not truth, not reconciliation, not repentance — *pleasure*; the commodity the hypocrite understands and trades in is the approval of the person whose power he fears, and the oath is his instrument for buying it. The sūrah has traced the same market through its whole length: they swore "that you may be pleased with them" is the culmination of *they swear by God that they are of you* (v. 56), *they swear to you that you may be pleased with them* (v. 62), *they swear by God that they said nothing* (v. 74) — oaths addressed, always, to the human auditor, because the human auditor is the one whose displeasure costs them position, and the divine auditor is the one they have decided not to believe in. Verse 94 removed the alibi's value; verse 96 removes the oath's: the buyer is bankrupt.

**The Prophet's Pleasure and God's Disjunction**

The verse's conditional carries the whole doctrine in two clauses: *fa-in tarḍaw ʿanhum* — "but if you are pleased with them" — *fa-inna Allāha lā yarḍā ʿan al-qawmi al-fāsiqīn* — "truly God is not pleased with iniquitous people." The grammar sets the two pleasures in explicit disjunction: the community's approval may be obtained by performance; God's cannot; and between the two, if they diverge, the verse has already ruled — in verse 96 as in the sūrah's whole economy — which one is the asset: the Prophet's ﷺ pleasure, in this life, was worth having (the believers coveted it, the weepers grieved at losing it); but it is a *derivative* good, valuable because it tracked God's; severed from that tracking — purchased by perjury for the perverse — it becomes a liability on both sides of the transaction: the swearer spends his hereafter acquiring a Muslim's smile, and the smiler learns his smile was quoted in a transaction God declined to honor. Al-Qurṭubī's formulation became the tradition's rule: created pleasure is contested ground; the Creator's pleasure is the standard — and whoever audits his life only against human approval has, whatever his market success, sold the standard for the sentiment.

**Pleasure Theology: The Verse's Positive Space**

The verse's negative clause — *God is not pleased with iniquitous people* — opens onto the Qur'an's entire pleasure-theology by contrast, and the sūrah itself will state the positive within ten verses (v. 100): *raḍiya Allāhu ʿanhum wa-raḍū ʿanhu* — "God is well-pleased with them, and they are well-pleased with Him" — the double pleasure that is the sūrah's (and arguably the Book's) happiest sentence. The asymmetry deserves the believer's attention: God's pleasure is not for sale, but it is *attainable* — by the road the sūrah has been mapping (sincerity, spending, striving, confession, truthfulness); the market the hypocrites mistook it for was their error's root: they believed all approval was purchasable performance, because that was the only approval they had ever sought; the verse's conditional informs them, too late but in time to be read by later readers, that the one Audience whose pleasure matters is the one Audience that reads hearts — and reads them, verse 78 has established, even in their najwā.

**The Modern Approval Economy**

For the modern believer, verse 96 was written, with suspicious precision, about the present: the economy of followers, endorsements, and the performative apology; the metric-driven self that checks its standing the way the hypocrites checked the Prophet's face; the institutional life in which pleasing the room has quietly replaced pleasing God as the operating objective, with the oath-of-the-algorithm — the optimized statement, the timed contrition — as its instrument. The verse's disjunction still cuts: *if the room is pleased and God is not, the room's pleasure is the loss* — and the Prophet ﷺ fixed the polarity in the warning the tradition memorized:

> *"Whoever seeks God's pleasure at the cost of people's anger, God will be pleased with him and suffice him against the people; and whoever seeks people's pleasure at the cost of God's anger, God will be angry with him and appoint over him the people."* (Transmitted by al-Tirmidhī from ʿĀʾishah, who said the Prophet ﷺ said it regarding the alteration of the Qur'an's wording under pressure — the principle is the ḥadīth's standing doctrine)

The believer's daily discipline follows the verse's own structure: before the performance (the post, the apology, the concession), ask the verse's single question in the first person — *whose pleasure, exactly, is this sentence engineered to obtain?* — and let the honest answer, as in all the sūrah's audits, place the deed in one of the two ledgers, which the sūrah will spend its final verses describing with a precision that makes the choice, at last, easy: the ledger of the pleased God, prepared before the mountains, or the ledger of verse 96's silent close — the market where the swearer's certificates were never legal tender.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:97

> **The Bedouins are more entrenched in disbelief and hypocrisy, and more likely not to know the limits of what God has sent down upon His Messenger. And God is Knower, Wise.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sociology of Distance**

Verse 97 opens the sūrah's final population-study — the Bedouin (*al-aʿrāb*) of the community's desert periphery — with a diagnostic whose severity startles: *al-aʿrābu ashaddu kufran wa-nifāqan* — "the Bedouins are more entrenched in disbelief and hypocrisy" — the comparative *ashadd* (harder, tougher, more rooted) naming not a categorical verdict but a *statistical gradient*: the desert populations, at the sūrah's moment, stood further from the covenant's center than the settled community — and the verse's second clause states the mechanism with the precision of social science: *wa-ajbaru an lā yaʿlamū ḥudūda mā anzala Allāhu ʿalā rasūlih* — "and more likely not to know the limits of what God has sent down upon His Messenger." The distance is *epistemic before it is moral*: the revelation's instruction happened in Madinah — its mosque-school, its recitation circles, the Prophet's ﷺ own teaching; the Bedouin's seasonal orbit touched the center and left it; the *ḥudūd* — the boundaries the revelation draws (duties, prohibitions, penalties) — reach a man only as fast as the man reaches them, and geography is a variable of grace. Ibn ʿAbbās, as the tradition transmits, read the verse as explanation rather than condemnation: the desert simply *knew less*, and knowing less, was more exposed to the old leverage — the tribal calculus, the caravan pragmatism, the auction of loyalties the desert had always run.

**"More Likely": The Qur'an's Refusal of Essentialism**

The verse's grammar refuses what its surface seems to invite: a stereotype. The comparative and the probability-clause (*ajbar an lā yaʿlamū* — "more disposed not to know") construct a *distribution*, not an essence — and the sūrah itself, in verses 98–99, will immediately publish the distribution's other tail: Bedouin who believe in God and the Last Day and count their spending as *investments with God* (v. 99's blessed ledger). The Qur'an's method — gradient now, exceptions within twenty verses — became the tradition's method for every demographic generalization it ever faced: describe the measurable tendency, refuse the categorical verdict, honor the individual case; and the verse's closing divine signature — *wa-Allāhu ʿAlīmun Ḥakīm* — "God is Knower, Wise" — seals the method: the Knower who states the statistic and the Wise who legislates around it are the same God who, three verses later, names the exception blessed. Al-Rāzī's gloss keeps the balance for every age: the verse grades *conditions* (distance from instruction, hardness of life) that any population may occupy — the modern Bedouin of the verse being every community's untaught margin, whatever its postcode.

**The Faith of the Margin, Correctly Priced**

The verse's practical office in the sūrah's economy is calibration: the community's leadership is told, before the final verses' absolutions and accusations, that the periphery's faith arrives *unevenly* — much of it sincere but shallow-rooted (the verse 123 campaigns would expose the loyalties), some of it opportunistic (verse 98's tally: some count their spending a loss and await your reverses), and some of it pure gold (v. 99). The calibration governed the Prophet's ﷺ own dealing with the tribes — patient instruction, graduated obligation, zakāt-collectors sent *out* to close the epistemic gap the verse names — and it stands as the revelation's standing instruction to every daʿwah: the margin's ignorance is a *logistics problem* before it is a character problem; the limits must be *delivered* (the verb of instruction) before they can be blamed for being unknown. For the modern believer, verse 97 is thus two mirrors at once: a warning against the essentialism that writes off whole populations (the verse's own next verses forbid it), and a warning against the margin's own temptation — the comfortable assumption that distance from the community of instruction is a neutral fact; the verse prices the distance in the only currency that matters, *knowledge of what God sent down*, and then signs the invoice with two Names that make the excuse of ignorance a deadline rather than an alibi.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:98

> **And among the Bedouins are those who take whatever they spend as a loss, and wait for the reverses to befall you. May the evil reverses befall them! And God is Hearing, Knowing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Ledger of the Reluctant Donor**

Verse 98 completes the sūrah's portrait-gallery of the mixed middle — after verse 97's gradient, the first specimen: *wa-min al-aʿrābi man yattakhidhu mā yunfiqu maghraman* — "among the Bedouins are those who take what they spend as a *maghram*" — a loss, a fine, an imposition extracted by force — the word's root gh-r-m naming a liability imposed from outside (a court-levied fine, a debt of honor), the exact opposite of a gift. The pathology is in the bookkeeping: the same coins, given by verse 99's Bedouin as an *investment* with God, are entered here as *tribute exacted by an occupying power* — because the giver's model of the community is not covenant but extortion; he pays the zakāt-collector the way a merchant pays protection money, in proportion to his fear, with an inner ledger of resentment. Ibn Kathīr's examples come from the collection-seasons after Tabūk: the tribes that paid, and muttered, and waited.

**Waiting for Your Reverses**

The verse's second clause exposes what the first only implied: *wa-yatarabbaṣu bikum al-dawāʾir* — "and they wait for the *dawāʾir* to befall you" — the word is the verse's masterstroke: literally "the circles" — the wheeling of fortune, the turns of fate that bring an empire down as they brought it up; the donor-resentful is revealed as a *spectator of the community's fortunes with money on its fall* — his alms were not generosity but hedge, premiums paid against the day the believers' star completes its circle and he can collect his true allegiance back.
