The Qur'an's verdict on the posture is the verse's third clause — a curse in the optative mood that echoes the hypocrites' own idiom back at them: *ʿalayhim dāʾiratu al-sawʾ* — "upon *them* be the circle of evil" — let the very wheel they watched for turn, and let it turn on them. And the verse's closing signature — *wa-Allāhu Samīʿun ʿAlīm* — "God is Hearing, Knowing" — seals the exposure: the mutter at the collector's departure, the tent-counsel about the believers' coming collapse — heard and known; the omniscience-doctrine of verses 78 and 94, applied now to the periphery's private ledgers.

**The Two Ledgers, Set Side by Side**

The tradition, reading verses 98 and 99 as a designed pair (the sūrah's final diptych), displays the Qur'an's method at its most surgical: two Bedouins, same line-items, opposite accounting-systems. The verse 98 man *spends and calls it a fine*; the verse 99 man *spends and calls it a loan with God* (the Qur'an's own idiom: *yudhīfūna ʿinda Allāhi qarḍan ḥasanan* — 64:17 and 5:12); the difference is not in the coins but in the *narrative the heart tells about the coins* — and the sūrah's doctrine, built from verse 34's wealth-as-fitnah through verse 103's wealth-as-purifier, is that the narrative is the whole moral content of the transaction: the same dirham is a maghram or an investment, a spiritual liability or a spiritual asset, according to nothing but the giver's understanding of whom he is dealing with. Al-Rāzī states the rule the tradition kept: God taxes nothing but the heart's pricing of what it gives — the miser's dirham and the lover's dirham are one metal and two currencies.

**Resentful Giving in the Modern Ledger**

For the modern believer, verse 98 is uncomfortably contemporary, because resentful giving survived the zakāt-collector by many centuries: the believer who pays his zakāt as one pays a parking ticket — late, minimized, with the silent grievance that the money was *his*; the volunteer who serves with an inner ledger of how unappreciated the service is; the parent who provides for the family and prices the providing as *maghram*, a fine levied by dependents. The verse's diagnostic question is simply the ledger-question: *what did I write next to my last act of spending-for-God — gift, or exaction?* — and its correction is the verse 99 ledger, available at the same price: the recalculation of the same coins as advances on the only account that cannot fail. And the verse's middle clause audits the spectatorship that resentment always breeds: the half-hoping watch for the community's stumble, the flick of satisfaction at the believer's scandal, the news-consumption of the ummah's troubles with something that is not grief — the verse names the posture, prices it (the optative falls on it), and hands the reader thirty seconds to check his own heart's news-feed before the sūrah turns, in verse 99, to the ledger he could have kept all along.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:99

> **And among the Bedouins are those who believe in God and the Last Day, and take what they spend as means of nearness to God and the prayers of the Messenger. Truly, it is a means of nearness for them. God will cause them to enter into His Mercy. Truly, God is Forgiving, Merciful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Ledger: Giving as Approach**

Verse 99 answers verse 98's maghram-ledger with its opposite number, and the tradition reads the pair as the sūrah's closing benediction on the desert: *wa-min al-aʿrābi man yuʾminu billāhi wa-l-yawmi al-ākhir* — "among the Bedouins are those who believe in God and the Last Day" — the two article creed (Lord and accounting-day) that the Qur'an treats as faith's load-bearing pair; *wa-yattakhidhūna mā yunfiqūna qurubātin* — "and they take what they spend as *qurubāt*" — the word is the verse's gift to the tradition: from the root q-r-b (to be near), the plural noun naming *means of approach* — the gifts a petitioner brings to draw near a great one; the believer's spending, in this ledger, is not payment, not hedge, not fine, but *approach* — the outward movement of a heart that wants to be closer, coin by coin, to God. The reports name the class from the Tabūk season — the reports of the collections transmit the likes of the tribesmen who brought what they had and asked the Prophet's ﷺ prayer over it — and the verse's second object is their exact motive: *wa-ṣalawāti al-rasūli* — "and the prayers of the Messenger" — they valued their gift as the occasion of the Prophet's ﷺ supplication, the poor man's gold; the prayer over the donor was, in the Sunnah, a standing institution:

> *"O God, bless the family of so-and-so" — the Prophet ﷺ would pray over the household that had given him charity.* (The Ṣaḥīḥ collections transmit his practice, from Anas ibn Mālik: when given charity he would pray, *O God, bless them*; and when given a gift, *bless the household*)

**The Divine Receipt**

Then comes the verse's receipt, and it is one of the Qur'an's most tender confirmations: *a-lā innahā qurbatun lahum* — "truly, it is a means of nearness *for them*" — God countersigns the ledger: the approach they intended *will be delivered*; the spending, entered by them as approach, is ratified by Him as approach — and then the verse pays the dividend in the language of destination: *sa-yudkhiluhumu Allāhu fī raḥmatih* — "God will cause them to enter into His Mercy" — the *fī* (into) naming not a waiting-room but an interior: mercy as a country with an entering. Al-Qurṭubī hears the syntax of reassurance: the Bedouin believer, far from the mosque-schools of Madinah, is promised the same address as the sābiqūn of verse 100 — the mercy that God *is pleased* (raḍiya) to give; and the verse's closing Names — *Ghafūrun Raḥīm* — are the sūrah's last absolution-signatures before its final movement. The nearness-doctrine stands as the Qur'an's settled economics:

> *"Whatever good you send ahead for your souls, you will find it with God, better and greater in reward."* (Qur'an 73:20)

**Correcting the Center's Condescension**

The tradition draws from verses 97–99, read together, a doctrine aimed at the community's *center*, and it has lost none of its edge: the sūrah's closing population-study refuses to let the settled believer condescend to the margin — verse 97's gradient is real (distance costs knowledge), but verse 99's exception is *co-named with the Book's own economics*: the desert donor's qurbah is the same qurbah the ḥadīth literature will build its whole theory of giving upon; the periphery's sincere are not junior believers but *full shareholders* whose ledger God countersigns in the same ink as Anṣār and Muhājirūn. The Prophet's ﷺ practice fixed the community's etiquette: he honored the tribe's gift at its giver's intention, prayed over it publicly, and let the margin's gold enter the common treasury without a scent of the center's superiority — the Sunnah's permanent rebuke to every urban congregation that smiles at the provincial accent of its own donors.

**Nearness as the Final Reframe of Wealth**

For the modern believer, verse 99 completes the sūrah's economics seminar with the only reframe that makes generosity durable: not duty (the duty-frame holds, but duty alone grinds), not fine (verse 98's ledger), not even investment alone (though the Qur'an licenses the metaphor) — but *approach*: giving as the physical act of a heart moving toward God, each transfer of wealth a step on a road whose terminus is *entry into His Mercy*. The believer who re-ledgers his spending this way discovers, the tradition testifies, that the categories of reluctant and joyful giving were never about the amounts at all: the same standing-order becomes a fine or an approach according to the story it is told inside — and verse 99 supplies the story, countersigned above: *it is truly a means of nearness for them* — the receipt already issued, the entry already promised, the Mercy already open at the door the coins were knocking on.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:100

> **As for the first forerunners of the Emigrants and the Helpers, and those who follow them in virtue, God is well-pleased with them, and they are well-pleased with Him. And He has prepared for them Gardens beneath which rivers flow, wherein they will abide forever. That is the supreme triumph.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Roll of Honor**

Verse 100 is the sūrah's hinge of glory — after ninety-nine verses of exposure and sorting, the revelation opens the ledger of the pleased: *al-sābiqūna al-awwalūna min al-muhājirīna wa-l-anṣāri* — "the first forerunners, of the Emigrants and the Helpers." Every word is a rank. *Sābiqūn* — forerunners, from s-b-q, the verb of the race: those who got there first, not by geography but by decision — the first to believe, the first to bleed, the first to spend; *al-awwalūn* — the earliest, the seniority of the covenant's dawn. Then the two wings, each named by its deed: *al-muhājirūn* — the Emigrants, who abandoned Makkah's securities for God — and *al-anṣār* — the Helpers, who opened Madinah's homes and date-orchards to the abandoned; the tradition's reports preserve the tiers of even this honored class (the first of the first: the early Muslims of Dār al-Arqam; the Badr veterans — the Prophet ﷺ said of Badr's three hundred and odd, in the report of the Ṣaḥīḥ collections, *"God looked upon the people of Badr and said: do what you wish, for I have forgiven you"* — words the scholars spent careers explaining, so that no license be drawn from them, but whose plain sense stands: the rank of Badr is a rank God Himself certified); and the verse's third term generalizes the roll beyond the first generation: *wa-alladhīna ittabaʿūhum bi-iḥsān* — "and those who follow them in virtue" — the door of the ledger left open for every later believer who joins the *way* of the forerunners with the same excellence — the clause by which, al-Ṭabarī and the tradition hold, the verse belongs to the ummah's whole future, and by which the modern reader stands, addressed, at the verse's edge.

**The Double Pleasure: The Qur'an's Happiest Sentence**

Then the verse's center, which the tradition has always read as the summit of what a creature can be told: *raḍiya Allāhu ʿanhum wa-raḍū ʿanhu* — "God is well-pleased with them, and they are well-pleased with Him." The two pleasures are asymmetric in kind and perfectly matched in fact. God's pleasure — raḍā — is not the absence of anger but a positive *delight of the Lord in His servants*: the Qur'an binds it to His *beautiful pleasure* (the verse 72 *riḍwānun min Allāhi akbar* — "a pleasure from God greater" than the Garden itself) and the tradition transmits that on the Day, God will *say* it, so those near may hear, to those far (the ḥadīth of the veil of light, recorded by Muslim from Abū Saʿīd: He will call, and the believers will answer, and He will say: *I am pleased with you*); and the servants' pleasure — *raḍū ʿanhu* — is the completion of verse 59's training: *wa-law annahum raḍū mā atāhumu Allāhu wa-rasūluh* — "had they been content with what God and His Messenger gave them" — the contentment the sūrah prescribed as the cure for the always-wanting; the ledger's two entries meet: He is pleased to give, they are pleased to receive, and the circle of pleasing closes with nothing outside it — which is why the tradition named this mutual raḍā the *essence of the religion's promise*: not garden-plus (though garden comes), but *God pleased and the soul at last unreservedly pleased with God* — the endpoint of every verse 92 tear and every verse 88 coin.

**The Prepared Return and the Named Triumph**

The verse's closing clauses repeat verse 89's benediction to the letter — *jannāt tajrī min taḥtihā al-anhāru khālidīna fīhā abadan* — Gardens, rivers, forever — and *dhālika huwa al-fawzu al-ʿaẓīm*, the supreme triumph — and the repetition is deliberate completion: verse 89 blessed the marchers of Tabūk; verse 100 certifies the class of every marcher, the two-currency spenders' whole lineage, and files it as the sūrah's standing definition of success against which every other ledger in the chapter (the sealed, the fined, the circle-waiters) is the contrast. Ibn Kathīr's summation became the Sunnah's flagship citation on the generation question — the verse is the proof-text behind the Prophet's ﷺ *do not revile my Companions* (al-Bukhārī and Muslim, from Abū Saʿīd, on the Uḥud-gold saying), and behind the community's settled adab: the honor of the first generations is not nostalgia; it is *revealed accounting* — God said He is pleased, and the believer who insinuates otherwise is arguing with the Book.

**Following in Virtue: The Verse's Personal Address**

For the modern believer, verse 100's third clause is his own name read aloud: *those who follow them in virtue* — the forerunners' way is documented (their spending, their patience, their truthfulness, their contentment), and the follower's path is the same way at a later mile-marker. The tradition's masters distilled the following into components the verse's own sūrah supplies: love of what they loved (the Messenger first), spending as they spent (from the edge, not the surplus), truth when truth is expensive (the v. 119 inheritance of verse 118), and contentment as the baseline temperament (v. 59). And the verse's promise should be read at its full face-value, because the sūrah has spent one hundred verses teaching the believer to distrust exactly the wrong ledgers: the double pleasure is not the mystics' private rumor — it is the publicly recited, contractually stated destination of *whoever follows them in iḥsān*; the believer's task, morning by morning, is the small arithmetical question the whole sūrah has taught him to ask — *is today's spending, today's contentment, today's truth, being done in the manner of those with whom God is pleased?* — and the verse's last two words answer what such a life adds up to, in the end: *al-fawzu al-ʿaẓīm*.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:101

> **And among those around you of the Bedouins are hypocrites, and among the people of Madinah. They are obstinate in hypocrisy. Thou knowest them not; We know them. We shall punish them twice; then they shall be returned to a great punishment.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Infiltration Map**

Verse 101 returns the sūrah to its dark register after verse 100's glory, and the transition is itself doctrine: the same community that contains the pleased-of-God contains, in its very fabric, the double-lifers — *wa-mimman ḥawlakum min al-aʿrābi munāfiqūn* — "among those around you of the Bedouins are hypocrites" — *wa-min ahli al-madīnah* — "and of the people of Madinah" — the two rings of the Prophet's ﷺ world, desert and city, both infected; and the city's clause adds the clinical finding: *maradū ʿalā al-nifāq* — "they are *sick upon* hypocrisy," the root m-r-d (rebellion, sick obstinacy) naming a settled disease: not the lapse of the wavering but the chronic condition of the committed — hypocrisy as their *posture*, defended like a diagnosis they identify with. The verse thus completes the sūrah's epidemiology: the earlier chapters of the disease (vv. 64–66, 74–78) sampled the Madinan cases; verses 97–99 mapped the desert's gradients; verse 101 files the terminal strains of both populations together — and the reader is meant to feel the pairing with verse 100: one sūrah, one community, two ledgers running to the end of time.

**"Thou Knowest Them Not; We Know Them": The Prophet's Limits**

Then the verse delivers the sentence that governed the entire doctrine of the invisible hypocrite: *lā taʿlamuhum naḥnu naʿlamuhum* — "thou knowest them not; We know them." The Prophet ﷺ, upon whom the sīrah records dozens of divinely-disclosed identities (the verse 50 and 64 exposures, the wall-of-Banū ʿAwf scene, the ʿAmr ibn ʿAwf envoy), is here formally informed of his epistemic limit: *this* class — the deep-strain, city-and-desert infiltrators — is not disclosed to him; and the jurists built the community's whole legal philosophy of the hidden heart on the phrase: the Prophet's ﷺ governance proceeded on the *visible* (his own Sunnah-ruling: *I have not been commanded to pierce the hearts of the people* — he accepted the Bedouin's word at face value, and the community's judges inherited exactly that rule); concealed nifāq is God's jurisdiction, and the community's blood, property, and membership are protected by the declaration *ẓāhir* — outward word — however the interior runs. Ibn Taymiyyah's summary is the tradition's standing maxim: whoever shows Islam, we treat with him by Islam's law, and whoever's heart God knows otherwise, God is the Judge of hearts.

**"We Shall Punish Them Twice"**

The verse's middle clause — *sa-nuʿadhdhibuhum marratayni* — "We shall punish them twice" — is read by the exegetes, as the source commentary records, along two main lines: punishment in this world and punishment in the grave (the barzakh's trials — the tradition's reports of the deceased's questioning, established in the Ṣaḥīḥ collections); or the double of scandal and separation in this life plus the Hereafter's reckoning; al-Qurṭubī adds the readings' shared core: a measured installment-plan of pain before the *ʿadhābun ʿaẓīm* — the great punishment — of the return; the theology is istidrāj's dark twin: the believer's trials are purgatives, the hypocrite's are installments — the same pain, opposite ledgers — and the verse's final clause (*thumma yuraddūna ilā*) closes the file with the sūrah's signature verb of return: delivered, at last, to the audit they spent two lifetimes declining.

**The Believer's Peace Under the Map**

For the modern believer, verse 101 performs two opposite offices at once, and both are needed. First, the sobering one: the pure community never existed — the Prophet's ﷺ own Madinah, with the Prophet alive in it and the revelation descending upon it, contained an unrevealed fifth column; the believer who expects the modern congregation to be certifiably pure is expecting better than the Prophet's ﷺ own city received, and the expectation itself — the purist's disillusionment cycle — is refuted by the verse's calm cartography. Second, the liberating one: *thou knowest them not* — the believer is not commissioned to know them; the community is governed by the visible; the witch-hunt is not zeal but insubordination against the verse's own division of labor. The believer's job description, in a mixed community, is the sūrah's: work your own ledger (v. 100's following-in-virtue), take the declaration as given, and leave the interiors to the One who ended the verse by naming Himself their only competent Auditor — *naḥnu naʿlamuhum* — We know them — the sentence both the community's peace and the hidden man's hope, since the One who knows him also knows, as verse 102 is about to demonstrate, exactly how to receive him if he ever walks out of the double life into confession.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:102

> **And others have confessed their sins. They mixed a righteous deed with another that was evil. It may be that God will relent unto them. Truly God is Forgiving, Merciful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Confessors of the Middle**

Verse 102 is the sūrah's great hinge of mercy, and the tradition preserves its occasion with the detail of a case-file: *wa-ākharūna iʿtarafū bidhunūbihim* — "and *others* have confessed their sins" — the word *ākharūn* placing them as a third class between the pure (v. 100) and the terminally obstinate (v. 101): the reports — al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, and the sīrah literature — name the season: after Tabūk, among the stayers, a party came to the Prophet ﷺ and bound their case to the truth: *khalāṭū ʿamalan ṣāliḥan wa-ākhara sayyiʾan* — "they mixed a righteous deed with another that was evil" — the confession's content is the verse's own pathology-of-the-middle: belief intact, prayers kept, charity given — and the duty abandoned when it cost the dearest; not the hypocrite's double life (their Islam and their failure share one public face — that is the confession's point) but the believer's *mixture*: ṣāliḥ and sayyiʾ in one ledger, the sūrah's own vv. 117–118 subjects will be the extreme case of the class; these confessors are its ordinary. And the Prophet ﷺ received them precisely as the verse receives them — the reports state that the verse *itself* descended upon their sitting: they had linked their case to God's judgment, and God answered by name, delaying their sentence (*ʿasā Allāhu an yatūba ʿalayhim* — "it may be that God will relent upon them") — the ʿasā of hope, which the scholars note is, from God, near-promise: discipline administered inside mercy, the sentence suspended so that the *work* of repentance could be done — verse 103's command (take from their wealth) descends as its protocol.

**The Confession's Anatomy**

The exegetes display the verse's grammar of recovery as a sequence the tradition turned into the fiqh of tawbah itself. First, *iʿtarafū* — they confessed: the facts conceded before God and His Messenger, no alibi (the verse 94 door), no oath (the v. 96 market) — the sūrah's two failed technologies replaced by the one that works. Second, the honest accounting of the mixture: no inflation of the righteous deed, no minimization of the evil — the ledger read as it runs, which is the only reading the Auditor accepts, since He keeps the original. Third, the binding of the outcome to God's judgment: in the reports, they submitted their case entirely to God's judgment — the posture the Qur'an names elsewhere as the mark of the servants who are *hurried to good deeds, and they call upon Us in hope and fear* (cf. 21:90). Al-Rāzī's summary reads the verse as the Book's standing charter for the mixed believer: between the purity of verse 100 and the obstinacy of verse 101 lies the inhabited middle of the human condition, and the verse installs its door: *confession, not management* — the difference, the tradition's masters said, between the man who brings his wound to the physician and the man who bandages it to preserve the picture of health.

**"It May Be That God Will Relent"**

The verse's hope-clause is deliberately unpunctuated by certainty, and the commentators mine its pedagogy: immediate absolution would have priced confession cheaply; the suspended ʿasā held the confessors in the posture of the hopeful-and-afraid long enough for the repentance to consolidate into character — and then verse 103's *khudh min amwālihim* — "take from their wealth" — supplied the deed that would settle it: a purifying charity; and verses 106–108 of the same sūrah will complete the class-portrait (some deferred to God's command, trusting His judgment alone). The two Names that close verse 102 — *inna Allāha Ghafūrun Raḥīm* — are the same absolution-pair of verses 91 and 99; the sūrah's mercy is not a different mercy for the sinner than for the sick and the sincere — it is the same Forgiving, the same Merciful, with the same jurisdiction over every class in the chapter.

**The Middle-Class Believer's Verse**

For the modern believer, verse 102 is, of the whole sūrah, the verse most likely to carry his own name: the believer of the mixture — faithful in much, failed in the costly thing, praying and paying while the one duty went abandoned — who reads chapter nine's exposures with alternating fear and relief, recognizing himself at the edges of every portrait. The verse's instruction is exactly fitted: *stop the management and make the confession* — to God directly, and where human amends are owed, to the humans themselves; keep the accounting honest on both sides of the mixture; and receive the ʿasā as it was received — as a door held open, not a verdict delivered — with verse 103's practical sequel close behind: the concrete act of repair (the charity, the restitution, the returned trust) that converts the confession from theater's cousin into the physician's case. The verse's last word is its charter: God is Forgiving, Merciful — the Names under which the middle class of every generation has walked out of the sorted files of chapter nine's first half and into the ledger of verse 100, which was, all along, holding a line open for the *followers in virtue* — including the follower who starts from his knees.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:103

> **Take from their wealth a charity, that thou mayest purify them and cause them to grow thereby; and pray for them; truly thy prayer is tranquility for them. And God is Hearing, Knowing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prescription Written to the Physician**

Verse 103 is the sūrah's most pastoral command, and its grammar casts the Prophet ﷺ in the role the whole tradition has memorized: *khudh min amwālihim ṣadaqatan* — "take from their wealth a charity" — addressed to the Messenger, about the confessors of verse 102, at the exact moment their case hung in the ʿasā-of-hope. The command's stated purposes are three, and they are the verse's gift to the theology of giving. First: *tuṭahhiruhum* — "that thou mayest *purify* them" — the root ṭ-h-r, ritual purity: the charity as an agent of cleansing — the tradition's masters said the wealth left in the mixed ledger carried the smudge of its mixed earning, and the giving washes it. Second: *wa-tuzakkīhim bihā* — "and cause them to *grow* thereby" — the root z-k-w, growth and increase (as the desert herbage's zaky-welling rain): the charity not only cleanses the past but fertilizes the soul's future — character, the verse implies, is grown, and giving is its cultivation. Third: *wa-ṣalli ʿalayhim* — "and pray over them" — the Prophet's ﷺ supplication as the seal of the process: *inna ṣalātaka sakanun lahum* — "truly thy prayer is *tranquility* (sakan) for them" — the word of 48:4's *sakanahu fī qulūb al-muʾminīn* — the calm that settles on a heart when its case has, at last, been *placed* with God.

**Zakāt as Therapy, Not Tax**

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī, followed by the tradition's jurists and its spiritual masters alike, built the verse's doctrine into the very definition of zakāt: the obligation is not a revenue-system that happens to be religious — it is a *therapy*, prescribed to the giver, that happens to fund the poor. The two verbal nouns make the case in miniature — *taṭhīr* (purification) and *tazkiya* (growth): the divine Physician orders the giving *for the patient's own blood-work*, so to speak — to purge the attachment (the v. 77 sealing agent, the v. 98 maghram-mentality) and to grow the opposite organ, the v. 99 qurbah-heart that experiences giving as approach. The Prophet ﷺ framed the whole sūrah's wealth-teaching in the image the collections preserve: charity *extinguishes sin as water extinguishes fire*; and the sūrah's own sequence — Thaʿlabah's wallet-sealing (v. 75–77) followed by this prescription — is the case-study followed by the cure: what killed Thaʿlabah's soul was not his wealth but his wealth's *unspent* state; verse 103 orders the spending that keeps the possession livable.

**The Prayer Over the Gift: The Seal of Acceptance**

The verse's closing movement — the Prophet's ﷺ prayer as *sakan* — carries the Sunnah's standing institution, which the Ṣaḥīḥ reports preserve from Anas: when a household brought charity, the Prophet ﷺ prayed, *"O God, bless the family of so-and-so"* — and Anas adds the application: the prayer over the donors was sought as the gift's receipt; verse 103 reveals why the receipt matters to the giver's psychology: the act of giving, sealed by the beloved's supplication, *settles* — the sakan of a transaction witnessed and countersigned; and the verse's final Names — *Allāhu Samīʿun ʿAlīm* — countersign from above what the Prophet ﷺ signed from earth: the Hearing who received the confession (v. 102), the Knowing who weighs the mixture, have both been in the room the whole time. The verse is thus the sūrah's quietest miracle of reception: the men of verse 102 who *submitted their case* receive, within one verse, a *treatment plan, a growth program, and a prayer* — the file closed not with a verdict but with a physician's appointment.

**The Believer's Own Prescription**

For the modern believer, verse 103 is best read as what it grammatically is — a *prescription* — and its therapeutic logic transfers whole: the believer of the mixed ledger (which is every believer who has read verse 102 honestly) is not told to *feel* purified, nor to await a verdict, but to *give* — concretely, from the very wealth in question, in an amount that touches (the scholars note the prescription's given-at-the-Prophet's-discretion dosage) — and to attach to the giving the prayer of the righteous (to be prayed *for* him by whoever is prayed through — the imam, the parent, the shaykh — or by himself, in the sūrah's expanded courtesy: the community's prayer over the donor is the receipt the verse institutes). The growth-clause is the long-term contract: giving as the soul's agriculture, season after season, until the wallet's reflex — the v. 75 instinct to clutch — has been cultivated into the v. 99 reflex to approach; and the sakan-clause names the proof of the whole treatment: when the believer's giving has become the occasion of quiet — a settled, witnessed, countersigned calm — the therapy is working; the ledger is being purified; the growth is in the soil.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:104

> **Know they not that God is He Who accepts repentance from His servants, and receives their acts of charity, and that God is He Who is the Relenting, the Merciful?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse of the Accepting Names**

Verse 104 is the sūrah's doxological pause — the confession-and-purification sequence (vv. 102–103) lifted, for one verse, into the doctrine of *who receives it all*: *a-wa-lam yaʿlamū anna Allāha huwa yaqbalu al-tawwata ʿan ʿibādihi* — "know they not that God — He it is — accepts repentance from His servants" — the structure's emphasis (God, *He*, the pronoun of exclusivity) makes the point the tradition never tired of: repentance's entire economy is divine property; no priest, no intermediary, no fee — the penitent's confession goes where the confessors of verse 102 sent it, *directly*, and the verse names the Officer of Reception by Name: *al-Tawwāb* — the Relenting, the intensive form of the root t-w-b: not merely the One who *once* accepted a return, but the One whose *characteristic operation* is accepting returns — again and again, by nature, forever. And the second clause extends the reception-desk to the giving: *wa-yaʾkhadhu al-ṣadaqāti* — "and receives (takes) the charities" — the verb akhadha, to *take into hand*: the Qur'an's most tactile image of acceptance — as the Book states elsewhere in its own idiom: *God takes the souls at death* (39:42) — here applied to the widow's mite and the confessors' purifying gift: God *takes* them, personally, as one takes a thing one wants.

**The Repentance Doctrine the Sūrah Has Been Assembling**

Verse 104 gathers the sūrah's scattered repentance-materials into one Name. The chapter has held the door of tawbah open from its first movement — *except those who repent...* (v. 3, 5, 11, 15, 27, 102) — through the exposure-sequences, always conditional, always available; and the tradition's scholars read verse 104 as the sūrah's own commentary on why: because *acceptance is not an exception God makes but an attribute God has* — the Tawwāb of the verse, paired with the Raḥīm — and the Qur'an seals the doctrine in the Book's most comprehensive repentance-verse:

> *"Say: O My servants who have transgressed against themselves, despair not of God's Mercy; truly God forgives all sins; truly He is the Forgiving, the Merciful."* (Qur'an 39:53)

The Prophet ﷺ gave the doctrine its frequency-teaching in the ḥadīth the collections transmit on God's own self-report: *God stretches out His hand by night to accept the repentance of the day's sinner, and by day to accept the night's sinner, until the sun rises from its setting place* (recorded by Muslim from Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī) — the Tawwāb's reception-desk open every hour the world stands.

**"From His Servants": The Acceptance's Address**

The verse's qualifier — *ʿan ʿibādihi* — "from His servants" — carries the pastoral emphasis the exegetes draw out: the repentance-acceptance is addressed to *servants* — the title of the believer's highest dignity (the Prophet ﷺ is named *ʿabd* in the isrāʾ-verse before he is named anything else) — and the verse thus refuses the penitent's despair from the acceptance's own side: the One receiving the confession is not a distant magistrate but *your* Lord, receiving *His* servant's return, and the tradition's reports of the divine rejoicing over the returner (the ḥadīth of the lost camel, al-Bukhārī and Muslim from Anas: God is *more joyful* at the repentance of His servant than one of you who finds his camel laden with his provision after losing it in the desert) fix the reception's *tone*: acceptance with gladness — the Tawwāb not merely processing the return but celebrating it.

**The Question's Address: Everyone in the Room**

The verse's opening rhetorical question — *a-wa-lam yaʿlamū* — "do they not know?" — is formally addressed to the confessors and their class, but the tradition reads its true audience as everyone the sūrah has sorted: the sealed askers of v. 93 (who *know not* their own case), the managed reputations of v. 96, the maghram-donors of v. 98 — every ledger in the chapter is malfunctioning on the same broken assumption, that God's reception is grudging, rationed, or mythical; and the verse corrects the assumption at its root with two Names (Tawwāb, Raḥīm) that make the reception *character*. For the modern believer, verse 104 is the verse to read at the exact moment before any confession — the moment the middle-class soul hovers between management and truth: the reception-desk is real, it is open, it is *glad*; the only unknown in the whole transaction is on the petitioner's side of the desk — and the sūrah's next verse (105) will complete the protocol: having repented and given, *work* — for God will see your work, and His Messenger, and the believers: the verse 104 believer walks out of the confession-room not with a verdict but with a commission.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:105

> **And say: Work; and God will see your work, and His Messenger, and the believers. And you will be returned to the Knower of the unseen and the seen; and He will inform you of what you used to do.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Commission to the Repentant**

Verse 105 follows verse 104's reception-doctrine with its necessary consequence, cast as a command to be *said* to the confessors: *wa-quliʿmalū* — the rendering: "and say: *work*" — the verb ʿamila opening an imperative that the tradition has always read as the tawbah-protocol's final stage: the penitent's case, received (v. 104) and treated (v. 103), is closed not with a certificate but with a *job* — *work*, in the imperfect's open-ended aspect: keep working, work on — the believer's answer to a sorted past being an unsorted future of deeds. And the verse's middle clause installs the workplace's supervision with a triple audience: *fa-sayarā Allāhu ʿamalakum wa-rasūluhu wa-l-muʾminūn* — "and God will see your work, and His Messenger, and the believers" — the verse 94 formula (God and His Messenger will see) *expanded* by one watcher: the believers — the community itself, the witnesses of the confessors' rehabilitation, whose restored confidence was both the repentance's evidence and its reward; the exegetes note the order's courtesy: the public dimension of repentance (amends, restitution, rebuilt trust) is real — deeds must *show* — but it stands third in the list, behind the two Auditors whose seeing does not depend on optics.

**Deed-Theology: The Sūrah's Running Thread Completed**

The verse gathers the sūrah's whole doctrine of the ʿamal. From verse 34's warning through verse 88's strivers to verse 102's *mixed* deed, chapter nine has treated works as the self's real biography — the thing actually filed, whatever the words were doing (vv. 94–96's failed testimonies); and verse 105 states the filing-system in full: deeds are seen — by God (directly, at commission), by His Messenger (the Sunnah's standard as the measure), by the believers (the community's testimony) — and then *returned* — *thumma turaddūna ilā ʿālimi al-ghaybi wa-l-shahāda* — to the Knower of the unseen and the seen — where the triple seeing is archived in the single Knowing, and *informed* — *fa-yunabbiʾukum bimā kuntum taʿmalūn* — "and He will inform you of what you used to do" — the verse 94 exhibition, now addressed to the *workers* rather than the excuse-makers: the same last-day briefing, experienced as either vindication or exposure, purely according to what the work was. The Qur'an's parallel commission-verse elsewhere confirms the protocol's shape:

> *"So whoever hopes for the meeting with his Lord, let him work righteousness, and associate none in the worship of his Lord."* (Qur'an 18:110)

**The Believers as Witnesses: A Communal Doctrine**

The clause *and the believers* carries a legal and social doctrine the jurists developed carefully: the community's *ʿadālah* system — character-testimony, the witness-tradition of Islamic law — is built on the premise this verse states: the believers are an audience whose seeing matters, because the community's life runs on warranted trust; and the verse balances the two failure-modes the sūrah has already condemned: the hypocrite's theater (deeds done *for* the believers' seeing — v. 94's management) and the gnostic's contempt (deeds done *against* the community's seeing — the secret "purity" that owes nothing to the congregation); the verse's worker works *before* all three watchers at once — God as the end, the Messenger as the measure, the believers as the trust-partners — with no performance and no secrecy, which is the entire Sunnah's ethic of the deed in one clause.

**Work as the Modern Believer's Standing Answer**

For the modern believer, verse 105 is the sūrah's answer to its own hardest question — *what does one do after being sorted?* — and the answer's grammar is the teaching: an imperative, addressed to everyone who has just read his own file in the chapter's ledgers (the mixed, the manager, the afraid), and the imperative is not *feel*, not *explain*, not *self-forgive* — it is *work*; the rehabilitated self is built in the doing: the prayed prayer, the paid debt, the served poor, the told truth — deeds that can be *seen* by the three audiences because they exist. And the verse's final clause converts the modern believer's most common spiritual anxiety — the unresolved question of where one really stands — into a settled administrative fact: the standing will be *informed* at the return; the Knower of both domains has the file complete; and the worker's whole task, between this morning and that announcement, is contained in the verse's single opening imperative, which the tradition has recommended reciting to itself at the start of every day the sūrah has sorted: *iʿmalū* — get to work.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:106

> **And others are deferred to the command of God, whether He punish them or relent toward them. And God is Knower, Wise.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Estate: The Deferred**

Verse 106 completes the sūrah's post-Tabūk taxonomy with its most theologically interesting class: *wa-ākharūna murjawna li-amri Allāhi* — "and others are *deferred* to the command of God" — the root r-j-w naming both hope and postponement: their case is *suspended* — the reports, which al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr transmit, identify them as a party of the stayers who neither confessed (like v. 102's men) nor manufactured excuses (like vv. 93–94's), and were simply *left* — the Prophet ﷺ made no ruling on them, and the revelation made none: their file held open between the two closures. The verse's own suspension-clause states the range: *immā yuʿadhdhibuhum wa-immā yatūbu ʿalayhim* — "whether He punish them or relent toward them" — and the tradition's commentators divide over which class of stayers these were — the sincere-but-paralyzed (Kaʿb ibn Mālik's report, in the Ṣaḥīḥ collections, mentions exactly this three-way sorting of the Madinan stayers: the truthful three, the confessional party whose repentance the Prophet accepted, and *others* whose case God deferred) — with the weight of the reports reading verse 106's men as eventually encompassed in the relief of verses 117–118's season: the deferred file resolved in mercy.

**The Theology of the Unresolved Case**

The verse's doctrine deserves full statement, because it touches every believer's life at some hour: *there are legitimate suspensions.* The sūrah's whole first movement ran on disclosure — files opened, verdicts read (vv. 64–66, 74, 93–96, 101); but verse 106 establishes, inside the very same sūrah, a class of *withheld verdicts* — and the closing Names govern the withholding: *wa-Allāhu ʿAlīmun Ḥakīm* — God is *Knower, Wise*: the suspension is not ignorance (He knows) nor indecision (He is Wise) but *pedagogy* — the unresolved case as the exact medicine the resolved case would have spoiled: the confessor of v. 102 needed work to do (v. 103's prescription); the deferred man needed *waiting* — the long, unclassified interval in which the soul, unable to close its own file, learns the posture the Qur'an names as the believer's core discipline: *"O you who believe, be patient, and vie in patience, and stand firm, and be mindful of God, that you may prosper."* (3:200) The deferred class is patience's curriculum.

**The Suspended Believer's Inner Weather**

Al-Qurṭubī's pastoral note, drawn from the reports of the season, describes the deferred men's condition with the vividness of eyewitness: they were *the most troubled of the people* — neither cleared (so no relief) nor condemned (so no despair) — living in the verse's own grammar, *between the two clauses* (immā... wa-immā), suspended between punishment and relenting, and the suspension itself worked the purification that neither closure could: every prayer of those weeks was prayed *at the edge*; every report of the Prophet's ﷺ expression was read for a signal; the scholars observe that the state itself — hovering hope (rajāʾ, the verse's own root) over fear — is the spiritual condition the tradition names *bayn al-khawfi wa-l-rajāʾ*, between fear and hope, which the masters declared the balanced believer's permanent weather; the deferred class was enrolled in it involuntarily and found it, as the reports' resolution confirms, salvific.

**The Open Files of the Modern Believer**

For the modern believer, verse 106 names the state that every serious life eventually contains: the prayer neither clearly answered nor clearly refused; the sin repented-of but not yet *felt* clean; the relationship neither reconciled nor closed; the era in which God is *silent about you* — no verdict, no clarity, only the standing command of verse 105 (work) and the standing Names of verse 106 (Knower, Wise). The verse's counsel is its grammar: the file is *with God*, and its very openness is His management, not His neglect; the believer's task in suspension is not to force a verdict (self-absolution) or write one (self-condemnation) but to *inhabit the interval* as the deferred men did — working, hoping, fearing, and letting the closing Names carry the classification; and the sūrah's own sequel is the believer's hope's warrant: within twelve verses, God will *turn* (v. 117) — and the turn's first named beneficiaries will include, by the reports' consensus, the very suspended case this verse left open: the deferred file resolved, in the end, by the same merciful Officer to whom it was deferred.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:107

> **And as for those who have taken a mosque for harm and disbelief and to cause division among the believers, and as a refuge for one who warred against God and His Messenger before—they will surely swear, "We desired nothing but good." And God bears witness that they are liars.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Building That Was a Weapon**

Verse 107 opens the sūrah's final great case-file, and the tradition — al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Wāḥidī's asbāb collections, and the sīrah literature — transmits its occasion with unusual completeness: in the Tabūk season, while the Prophet ﷺ was away, a party of Madinan men — a dozen-odd, led in the project by Abū ʿĀmir al-Rāhib ("the Monk," so called for his ascetic dress; a Madinan who had fought the Muslims at Uḥud and then fled to the Byzantines, and who had *sent ahead* instructions for the mosque) — built, on the edge of Qubāʾ, a rival prayer-house: *wa-alladhīna ittakhadhū masjidan ḍirāran* — "those who took a mosque for *ḍarar*" — harm. The verse's four prepositional phrases are the building's architectural drawings, and the exegetes read them as the structure's true load-bearing purposes, each a betrayal of what a mosque is: *ḍirāran* — for harm (against the Qubāʾ congregation, the faithful's gathering-place); *wa-kufran* — and disbelief (a construction whose *function* was creedal, built by and for the mission of unbelief); *wa-tafrīqan bayna al-muʾminīn* — and to cause division among the believers (splitting the community's worship-geography at the precise week the community's unity was most strained — the Prophet away at war); *wa-irṣādan li-man ḥāraba Allāha wa-rasūlahu min qablu* — and as a refuge (*irṣād*: a way-station, a supply-base) for him who warred against God and His Messenger before — Abū ʿĀmir himself: the mosque as the advance-quarters of the enemy commander, a safe-house awaiting its asset's return.

**The Weaponization of the Sacred Form**

The case's doctrinal gravity comes from its form: everything about the project was *religiously correct* — a building, called a mosque (masjid, a place of prostration), presumably with a prayer-niche and an imam; the men, when it was done, invited the Prophet ﷺ (by letter, at Tabūk) to come and *pray in it and inaugurate it* — the petition of pious clients. Al-Rāzī's analysis, which the tradition adopted, exposes the mechanism the verse teaches every generation to detect: the sanctity of the *form* is hijackable — a religious institution can be founded, financed, and architected for purposes that are the form's exact inversion (harm, disbelief, division, enemy-supply), and the form's sanctity functions then as *camouflage*: who can object to a mosque? The verse's answer: God can, and did — the reveal arriving not by espionage but by revelation; and the sūrah's ruling (v. 108: *never stand in it*) established the community's standing criterion: institutions are judged by their foundations (*taʾsīs*, the word of v. 110) and functions, not their letterheads; a "mosque" whose ḍarar-architecture is real is, in the divine taxonomy, not a mosque at all but a *masjid ḍirār* — the compound noun the Islamic vocabulary minted from this verse and has used ever since for every pious-looking instrument of harm.

**"We Desired Nothing but Good": The Oath Inherited**

The verse's closing movement is the sūrah's compressed signature — the founders' prepared defense: *la-yahlifunna in aradnā illā al-ḥusnā* — "they will surely swear: we desired nothing but good" — the future-emphatic oath (the same oath-technology of vv. 56, 62, 74, 95–96) deployed here at its most audacious, over a building whose blueprint the verse has just published; and the verdict over the oath is the divine countersignature: *wa-Allāhu yashhadu innahum la-kādhibūn* — "and God bears witness that they are liars" — the omniscience-doctrine of vv. 78 and 94 arriving as courtroom testimony: the community may be unable to prove the blueprint; God can, and does — the witness-clause (yashhadu) being the Qur'an's strongest possible evidentiary register, God Himself entering the record.

**The Qubāʾ Mirror: The Sūrah's Built-in Contrast**

The exegetes universally read verses 107–110 against the mosque they were built to defend: Qubāʾ, the first mosque of Islam, founded by the Prophet ﷺ in the first week of the Hijrah — and the Qur'an itself supplies the contrast-verse, embedded in the sūrah's own sequel (v. 108): *a mosque founded upon taqwā from its first day* — the two buildings standing, in the revelation's presentation, as the two *types* of every religious institution: the one founded on God-consciousness and therefore a witness to truth; the other founded on harm and therefore — whatever its mimicry — an instrument of the war it serves. The Prophet's ﷺ Sunnah sealed the typology's honor-side permanently: he visited Qubāʾ (riding or walking) every Saturday, praying in it — the reports of al-Bukhārī and Muslim preserve the standing practice — so that the community's memory holds both buildings forever: the one blessed by forty years of Prophetic and then communal prayer; the other — on the verse's descent — *ordered demolished* (the reports state the Prophet ﷺ sent word from Tabūk — the command carried by Mālik ibn al-Dukhshūm and two companions — and upon his return the structure was torn down and burned, its timbers returning to the world as ordinary wood, which is, the tradition observes, exactly what they had always been).

**The Verse in Every Generation**

For the modern believer, verse 107 is not archaeology: the masjid ḍirār is a *type*, and the type recurs wherever the sacred form is founded for the unsacred function — the charity that launders, the academy that indoctrinates hatred, the platform that performs religion while engineering division, the "interfaith" or "reform" body whose funding-trail and private minutes read like the verse's four clauses (harm, disbelief, division, refuge). The verse's training of the believer's eye is its four-item checklist — *founded by whom? funded by whom? functioning for what? and who profits when the believers split?* — and its training of the believer's humility is the checklist's other edge: the same four questions, honestly run, on the institutions one's own side loves; because the verse's deepest warning is not that enemies build weapons — it is that weapons can be built in the vocabulary of prayer, sworn over as *good*, and published as piety, and that the detection-office belongs not to the credulous crowd but to the One who bore witness from the first day the foundation was dug: *and God bears witness that they are liars.*

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:108

> **Never stand therein. A mosque founded upon reverence for God from its first day is more worthy of thy standing therein. In it are men who love to purify themselves; and God loves those who purify themselves.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sentence on the Building**

Verse 108 opens with the Qur'an's categorical liturgical ruling: *lā taqum fīhi abadan* — "never stand therein — *ever*" — the abadan closing, with the force of the eternal decree, the ḍirār project's career as a house of prayer: the Prophet ﷺ, petitioned at Tabūk to inaugurate it, is commanded never to enter it at all. The exegetes note the ruling's economy: not a demolition-order from heaven (the demolition was the Prophet's ﷺ practical implementation, on return, of what the verse had already ruled) but the *definitional* verdict — the building is removed from the category *mosque* by the only Authority who holds the category; standing (qiyām, the standing of prayer) is reserved for what is founded on what it claims. And the verse's instrument of contrast is then named: *lamasjidun ussira ʿalā al-taqwā min awwali yawmin* — "a mosque founded upon taqwā from its first day" — Qubāʾ: the exegetes' reports on its founding are theologically rich — the Prophet ﷺ laid its first stone with his own hands (the reports of the Sunan detail his cloak-brick), the Companions built it amid their poverty, each pair of men sharing the load of one brick-basket; the Prophet ﷺ, as the reports of al-Bukhārī and Muslim transmit, was asked which mosque was founded on taqwā and answered: *"It is this mosque of mine"* — on the majority reading, pointing to his Madinan mosque; and the scholars reconcile the two buildings' claims (the Medinan mosque as the Prophet's ﷺ own foundation; Qubāʾ as the first community-foundation of the Hijrah) by the shared property the verse names: taqwā at the *first day* — the foundation's moral composition, laid in with the first stones.

**"In It Are Men Who Love to Purify Themselves"**

The verse's second half names the true mosque's other foundation — its *people*: *fīhi rijālun yuḥibbūna an yataṭahharū* — "in it are men who love to purify themselves" — and the clause carries the sūrah's sweetest minor ḥadīth-occasion, transmitted by al-Bukhārī and Muslim from ʿUmar's narration of the verse's descent: the Prophet ﷺ asked the Ansārī Abū Ayyūb al-Anṣārī (the reports vary among Companions — Abū Ayyūb, Muʿādh, or ʿAmr ibn al-Jamūḥ, with the majority line on Abū Ayyūb) about the men of Qubāʾ, and the answer came with the desert's candor: *we are Anṣār; our houses are near the water, our women and neighbors come and go — and we cleanse ourselves thoroughly with water after relieving ourselves.* The Prophet ﷺ said: *keep to that.* So the verse's yataṭahharū refers, in the first layer of its occasion, to their scrupulous water-cleansing — the humblest imaginable purity — and the verse raises it into the register of divine love — and God's love-clause closes the file: *wa-Allāhu yuḥibbu al-mutaṭahharīn* — "and God loves those who purify themselves." The doctrine is the sūrah's in miniature: the ḍirār founders *swore* to good and were called liars; the Qubāʾ men *confessed* their constraint (no water for the full washing) and were loved for the humble purity they *did* practice — the two mosques' difference distilled into the two answers: performance versus confession, again.

**Taqwā's Architecture: Foundations and Occupants**

Al-Rāzī draws the verse's institutional doctrine together, and the tradition kept it as the philosophy of the sacred building: a true house of God is founded on two purities — the *foundation's* (taqwā at the first day: the purpose, the funding, the builders' integrity) and the *occupants'* (a congregation whose defining love is purification) — and the two are mutually attracting: pure foundations gather purifying people, which is why the verse's grammar moves so easily from the building's founding to the men within it; the scholars note that the converse is the verse's warning by implication: a congregation that *stops loving purification* hollows its building's foundation from within, whatever the stones' pedigree — taqwā is not an inheritance but a maintenance-schedule. Ibn Kathīr's cross-reference completes the frame with the Qur'an's other foundation-verse: *"Is one who founded his building upon reverence for God and good pleasure better, or one who founded his building on the brink of a crumbling bank...?"* (9:109 — the next verse) — the two builders of the parable standing, respectively, at Qubāʾ's stones and the ḍirār's.

**Purity as the Personal Application**

For the modern believer, verse 108's address is double. Institutionally, it is the criterion-verse: the believer's presence, prayer, and platform-signature are forms of *standing* — and the verse teaches that where one stands is itself a confession; a mosque is more worthy of standing-in when its first-day foundation and its people's loves are what this verse names, and the believer's loyalty belongs to that composition, not to the building's mere claim of category. Personally, the verse hands the believer the smallest and most practicable of the sūrah's credentials: *to be among those who love to purify themselves* — the Qur'an's phrase is not "who are pure" but *who love to purify* — yuḥibbūna *an* yataṭahharū: the love of the process, the insistence on the water, the scruple about what touches the body before it touches the prayer; and the verse's last clause states the return on that love in six words the tradition has slept and risen on: *wa-Allāhu yuḥibbu al-mutaṭahharīn* — God loves those who purify themselves — the Object of the love being, Himself, the love's author, so that the whole economy closes: He taught the love, He receives the loving, and He loves the lover back.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:109

> **Is one who founded his building upon reverence for God and good pleasure better, or one who founded his building upon the brink of a crumbling bank that topples with him into the Fire of Hell? And God guides not iniquitous people.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Parable of the Two Builders**

Verse 109 converts the two mosques of verses 107–108 into a universal parable, and does it with one of the Qur'an's most haunting images: *a-fa-man assasa bunyānahu ʿalā taqwā min Allāhi wa-riḍwānin* — "is one who founded his building upon reverence for God and good pleasure better" — *am man assasa bunyānahu ʿalā shafā jurufin hārin* — "or one who founded his building upon the brink (shafā) of a crumbling bank (juruf) that is collapsing (hār)" — the juruf being the wadi's edge: the vertical wall of packed earth that the flood has undercut — standing complete to the eye, hollow at the base, one season of water from catastrophe. The image's precision is the parable's terror: the ḍirār-builder's work *looked* finished — walls, roof, mimbar; the parable reveals the substrate: the whole structure standing on an undermined edge, and the collapse coming *with him in it* — *fa-anhāra bihī fī nāri jahannama* — "so it toppled with him into the Fire of Hell" — the builder falling, in the image, inside his own building, his foundation delivering him to the address it was always headed for.

**Foundation as Destiny**

The exegetes read the verse's two buildings with deliberate width. In the occasion's first layer, they are Qubāʾ (or the Prophet's Madinan mosque) and the masjid ḍirār; but the parable's terms — building, foundation, bank, flood — open onto everything the foundation-metaphor can carry: al-Ṭabarī reads every *bunyān* — every constructed life: creed, family, institution, career, civilization — with its ʿaqīdah as its foundation; al-Rāzī adds the psychological reading: the man who builds his inner life on the approval of men (the v. 96 currency) has founded on the juruf, because men's pleasure is precisely the substrate that *undercuts* — it shifts with the season that withdraws attention; and the tradition's Sufi masters took the parable as the map of the *nafs* itself: every sin retained is a flood-channel dug under the wall of the soul, and the verse's verb *inhāra* (to collapse utterly) names the terminal event the Qur'an promises the self-compromised life at both its ends — the public collapse in this world (the ḍirār's demolition was its miniature) and the fall into the Fire at the return. The verse's stated pair of foundations — *taqwā min Allāhi wa-riḍwān* — deserves the believer's slow reading: reverence *and* good pleasure: the fear-side and the love-side of the divine relationship laid together in one footing, since the scholars note that taqwā alone might build a fortress, but taqwā *plus riḍwān* builds a home.

**The Geology of the Wadi-Edge**

The image rewards a moment of geological attention, because the Qur'an chose it from the Arabs' lived map: the juruf is the Nile-of-the-desert problem — the wadi that is bone-dry eleven months of the year and a killer in the twelfth; the man who builds on its *brink* (shafā — the lip, the rim) is not the fool who builds in the channel; he is the subtler fool who builds *beside* disaster, close enough to use the view and near enough to fall — and the Arabs' oral law had already banned the juruf-build for exactly this reason: the Qur'an takes the settled Bedouin engineering-prohibition and universalizes it into eschatology. Al-Qurṭubī's note completes the engineering: the hār-participle (crumbling, in the active process of collapse) means the foundation was *already failing at the building's dedication* — the ḍirār's harm, disbelief, division, and enemy-refuge were the visible cracks from the first day; the flood only publishes what the footing always was. The parable's dread-logic lands on the verse's last clause: *wa-Allāhu lā yahdī al-qawma al-ẓālimīn* — "and God guides not the wrongdoing people" — the ẓālim, in the Qur'an's root-metaphor, is he who *places things out of place* — and the builder who placed his mosque upon harm has, in the same act, placed himself upon the juruf: the misplacement is one act, and it is total.

**The Question the Believer Brings to His Own Plans**

For the modern believer, verse 109 is the Qur'an's standing question over every groundbreaking — literal and figurative: the marriage founded on what (God's pleasure, or the bank's)? the venture, the ministry, the platform, the migration, the curriculum? The verse's diagnostic refuses the dedication-ceremony's testimony (the ḍirār would have held a beautiful one) in favor of the *geology*: what is under the first row of stones? The believer's audit-questions write themselves from the verse's own vocabulary: Is there anything, in this foundation, that the flood will find — a compromised income-stream, an unrepented wrong done to a person, a purpose that whispers ḍarar or tafrīq when the lights are low? And the verse's mercy is the parable's open first half: the taqwā-foundation is *buildable at any hour* — the verse's first builder is described not by his structure's grandeur but by his footing's material, and footing-material is chosen before each stone is laid, which means the parable, read at the right moment, is not a sentence but a *choice*: the believer standing today on a crumbling lip may still, before the flood-season, refuse the view, walk back from the edge, and begin again — on the ground the whole sūrah has been describing: *a mosque founded upon taqwā from its first day.*

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:110

> **The building they have built will not cease to be a cause of doubt in their hearts, unless their hearts are torn to pieces. And God is Knower, Wise.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Building That Outlives Its Purpose**

Verse 110 closes the masjid ḍirār file with a psychological sentence of extraordinary precision: *lā yazālu bunyānuhum alladhī banaw ribatan fī qulūbihim* — "the building they have built will not cease to be a *ribah* in their hearts" — and the word ribah is the verse's terrible gift: in the Qur'an's usage (as at 9:45 and 8:46), ribah is *doubt* — the specific tremor of the unsettled heart; some of the exegetes, as the source commentary notes, read it here as *ruybah* — disturbance, unease — and the two readings converge: the building they built for division becomes, permanently, the *symptom of their division*: every glance at it (or at its demolished lot — the sentence outlives the demolition) re-opens the doubt it was built to weaponize; the weapon's recoil is total and permanent — *illā an tataqaṭṭaʿa qulūbuhum* — "unless their hearts are torn to pieces." The exception-clause is the tradition's debate-piece, and the readings the commentators preserve all deserve statement: that the doubt persists *until* their hearts shatter (in death and hell — al-Ṭabarī's and Ibn ʿAbbās's line: the building's doubt clinging to them into the Fire); or *unless* their hearts are torn *by grief over the building* (the minority line — remorse as the tear that releases the ribah); al-Rāzī holds the two together as the verse's full range: the misbuilt structure guarantees the inner tremor, and only a shattering — whether of the heart in impenitence or of the heart *in repentance* — ends it.

**The Doctrine of Built-In Consequences**

The verse states, in the vocabulary of architecture, the same law verse 77 stated in the vocabulary of vows: *the deed outlives the doing, and lives on in the doer.* The ḍirār founders imagined a building as a tool — erected, used, forgotten; the verse corrects the metaphysics: what a man builds for harm becomes a *permanent tenant of his own chest* — the doubt that motivated the building (the v. 45 ribah of those whose hearts were diseased, the v. 44 wavering) is *housed* by the building, given a fixture, an address, a permanence; and the tradition's masters draw the general law that the Qur'an states elsewhere in its monument-verse: *"Whosoever intercedes for a good cause shall have a share thereof; and whosoever intercedes for an evil cause shall have its burden"* (4:85) — deeds compound; founders inherit. Ibn Kathīr's application is unsparing: the ḍarār-party's hearts were *never* at rest after their mosque — the reports of their after-histories (scattered into the ḍirār's rubble, some to persist in hypocrisy to death, at least one — the reports of the sīrah season mention the party's remnant — to resurface in the apostasy-wars) read like the verse's clause played out in case-files.

**Doubt as the Signature of the Misplaced Foundation**

Read with verse 109, verse 110 completes the parable's psychology: the juruf-builder does not merely *fall* in the end — he *doubts* the whole time; instability at the foundation transmits itself upward as tremor in the occupant. And the converse, which the sūrah has been teaching since verse 40's *lā taḥzan inna Allāha maʿanā*, is the believer's inheritance: the taqwā-foundation's signature is *sakīnah* — the settling-calm that descended in the cave — as the ribah is the ḍirār-foundation's; the tradition's diagnostic has stood ever since: certainty and tranquility track the foundation, not the façade; the man of tremor need not examine his feelings first — he needs to examine his footing; the feelings are the seismograph, and the verse has just explained what the needles' tremble means. The closing Names — *wa-Allāhu ʿAlīmun Ḥakīm* — seal the file with the same pair that closed verse 106's suspension: the Knower who read the blueprint before the foundation-trench was dug; the Wise whose sentence on the building was fitted, with exact economy, to the builders' own chosen instrument — they built a doubt-house, and they were given its rooms to live in.

**The Modern Tenant**

For the modern believer, verse 110 names a state much discussed in the language of anxiety and little discussed in the language of foundations: the *chronic unsettlement that attends a knowingly compromised project* — the venture whose seed-money still whispers, the platform built on a wound given and never healed, the reputation maintained by a lie that must be remembered daily to be repeated daily. The verse's claim is that such structures are not neutral assets with a guilty appendix: the guilt *is* the building's ribah, load-bearing and permanent; the tenant cannot renovate around it, and the sentence stands — no rest, unless the hearts be torn. And here the verse's minority reading (the tear of remorse) becomes the believer's hope's technicality: the exception-clause, read across the sūrah's whole mercy-arc (vv. 102–106, 117–118), suggests that the shattering God will honor as release is the *repentant* one — the demolition of the project *and* the confession over its rubble; the ḍirār's lot, in the reports, became a ground where the people stored their firewood and stubble: a use honest as the ground's history — which is, perhaps, the verse's quiet last word on what every torn-down misbuilding's site should become: a place of common use, tended by a community whose own foundations, by then, have been moved — stone by stone — to the only ground the sūrah certifies.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:111

> **Truly God has purchased from the believers their lives and their wealth for the price that theirs shall be the Garden. They fight in the way of God, and they slay and are slain—a promise binding upon Him, in the Torah, the Gospel, and the Qur'an. And who is truer to his covenant than God? Rejoice then in the bargain you have made; and that is the supreme triumph.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Deed of Sale**

Verse 111 is the sūrah's commercial summit — the whole chapter's trading-vocabulary (commerce in 61:10's idiom, the two currencies of v. 88, the ledgers of vv. 98–99) gathered into one of the Qur'an's most majestic sentences: *inna Allāha ishtarā mina al-muʾminīna anfusahum wa-amwālahum* — "truly God has *purchased* from the believers their selves and their wealth" — the verb shtarā (to buy) announced in the emphatic construction of a legal instrument: a deed of sale, drafted in heaven, its parties named, its consideration stated, its signatures binding. The merchandise: the two currencies the sūrah has priced throughout — *anfusahum wa-amwālahum* — their selves and their wealth; the buyer: God; the price: *anna lahumu al-jannah* — "that theirs shall be the Garden" — and the tradition's jurists savor the deed's inversion of the market: in every human commerce, the buyer pays currency and receives goods; here the Buyer receives the servants and pays *the Garden* — the wealth flows down the contract's grace-side, the Only Rich One purchasing from the only poor party in the transaction, and paying, from His own estate, the only asset that never depreciates. Al-Qurṭubī's note has entered the tradition: the believers sold what they could not keep and were paid what they cannot lose.

**Slaying and Being Slain: The Deed's Performance Clause**

The contract's middle clause is its performance-specification: *yujāhilūna fī sabīli Allāhi fa-yaqtulūna wa-yuqtalūn* — "they fight in the way of God, and they slay and are slain" — the paired verbs, active and passive, in the Qur'an's mirrored grammar: the sold self does both, in both directions, until the contract completes; and the specification is the sūrah's sober crown on the whole Tabūk sequence: the believers who marched in the heat (v. 81's ḥarr) were not adventurers but *vendors executing a deed* — their presence at the front, their wealth on the requisition-lists, their lives under the spears, all of it *delivery of the merchandise*, and the Garden its consideration. The Prophet ﷺ read the verse's economy over the Uḥud dead in the report the collections transmit: *no servant wounded in God's way but that he comes on the Day of Resurrection with his wound flowing, its color the color of blood, its scent the scent of musk*; and and the consideration's first installment — the fallen vendor's lodging while the contract completes — is described in the report from Ibn Masʿūd:

> *"The souls of the martyrs are in the bellies of green birds, which go wherever they wish in Paradise, then return to lanterns suspended beneath the Throne. Your Lord cast a glance at them and said: what do you wish for? They said: nothing — we have been given what You have given no other of Your creation. He said: shall I not give you what is better? They said: what is better than this? He said: that I return you to the world, that you may be killed again."* (Recorded by Muslim (1887) from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd)

**"Binding Upon Him, in the Torah, the Gospel, and the Qur'an"**

The deed's notarization-clause is unique in the Qur'an: *waʿdan ʿalayhi ḥaqqan fī al-tawrāti wa-l-injīli wa-l-qurʾān* — "a promise binding upon Him, in the Torah, the Gospel, and the Qur'an" — the three Books cited as the contract's prior registry: the covenant of the sold life is as old as revelation itself. The exegetes display the precedents: from the Torah's idiom, the covenant of the whole people's consecration to God (*they will fight for Me*, in the prophets' wars); from the Gospel, the verse the Qur'an itself quotes as its own echo-confirmation:

> *"O you who believe, shall I direct you to a commerce that will save you from a painful punishment? That you believe in God and His Messenger, and strive with your wealth and your selves in the way of God..."* (Qur'an 61:10–11 — the sūrah's own sister-verse on the bargain)

— and the Gospel's own standing offer, in the tradition's reading, of the life exchanged for the Kingdom. Then the notary's question seals the instrument: *wa-man aṣdaqu min Allāhi mīthāqan* — "and who is truer than God in covenant?" — the rhetorical question the Qur'an asks over its promises (cf. 4:122), guaranteeing, from the Buyer's own attribute of truthfulness, the consideration's delivery in full.

**Rejoice in the Bargain**

The verse's closing address is the only place in the Qur'an where God commands a commercial emotion: *fa-stabshirū bi-bayʿikum alladhī bāyaʿtum bih* — "rejoice then in the bargain you have made" — the verb of good news's own family (bashara), applied to a contract; and the finale: *wa-dhālika huwa al-fawzu al-ʿaẓīm* — "and that is the supreme triumph" — verse 89's and verse 100's crown-phrase completing the set: the marchers (v. 89), the pleased (v. 100), and now the sold (v. 111) all filed under the same triumph. For the modern believer, the verse's office is the re-pricing of everything: the believer's life and wealth were *never* his own unencumbered property — the deed exists, the sale has closed, and the only live question is the vendor's conduct: whether he lives as a delivered man or an embezzler of the sold merchandise; and the verse's commanded emotion is the tell of honest performance — the believer who has genuinely handed over the goods *rejoices* at the price, finds the spending light and the risk luminous, and regards every unbought life (his own, had he not sold) with the quiet pity of a man who knows what the market never learned: that the only gainful trade ever offered to human beings is the one this verse notarizes — selves and wealth for the Garden, countersigned in three Books, guaranteed by the only party to any contract who cannot default: *and who is truer to his covenant than God?*

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:112

> **Those who repent, those who worship, those who praise, those who journey, those who bow, those who prostrate, those who enjoin right and forbid wrong, and those who observe the limits of God. And give glad tidings to the believers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Ten Epithets: The Community's Portrait**

Verse 112 is the sūrah's caption for the people of the bargain — the verse 111 contract's human fine-print: who, exactly, are the sold? The Qur'an answers with its great participial chain, ten (by the majority count of the exegetes, who combine the opening verbs) epithets, each an intensive participle (the Arabic ism al-fāʿil in its habitual sense): *al-tāʾibūn* — the repenters, those whose turn back to God is a life-posture, not an event; *al-ʿābidūn* — the worshippers, whose ʿibādah is the constant occupation; *al-ḥāmidūn* — the praisers, who narrate God's goodness in every state; *al-sāʾiḥūn* — the journeymen, whose s-a-ḥ root names the desert-wanderers — read by the tradition in two registers that reinforce: the literal travelers for God (the emigrants, the campaigners, the pilgrims) and the spiritual itinerants (the early Ṣufīs claimed the verse's term for their station: *al-siyāḥah* — the itinerancy of the heart through the stations of nearness, with God as its only rest); *al-rākiʿūn* — the bowing; *al-sājidūn* — the prostrating — the two postures named as characters of persons: bodies trained, by ten thousand repetitions, into the grammar of surrender; *al-āmirūna bil-maʿrūfi wa-l-nāhūna ʿani al-munkar* — the commanders of right and forbidders of wrong — the public office: the community's moral maintenance-corps, whose duty the sūrah's own verses 67 and 71 have defined as the line between the believing and the hypocritical community; *wa-l-ḥāfiẓūna li-ḥudūdi Allāh* — and the keepers of God's limits — the ḥudūd of verse 97's epistemology now kept: the boundaries learned, honored, patrolled in the soul. And over the whole portrait, the sūrah's one-line commission: *wa-bashshiri al-muʾminīn* — "and give glad tidings to the believers."

**The List as a Ladder and as a Map**

The tradition has read the ten epithets with systematic care, and two readings organize the commentaries. The first reads the list as a *ladder* — al-Qurṭubī and others trace an order of grace: it opens with tawbah (the gate: nothing begins without the turn) and closes with ḥifẓ al-ḥudūd (the summit: the keeping of what was learned), with worship, praise, journey, and posture as the ladder's rungs between; and the glad-tidings clause as the ladder's topmost announcement. The second reads the list as a *map of the person*: the epithets distribute across the whole territory of the sold life — interior (repentance), vertical (worship, praise), liturgical (bowing, prostration), geographic (journeying), social (commanding and forbidding), and legal-observant (keeping the limits) — and the map's teaching is completeness: the bargain of verse 111 was for *selves and wealth* — total merchandise — and its community is correspondingly total: no compartment unsold, no province of the life outside the contract; the ḥadīth collections confirm the portrait's own self-description in the Prophet's ﷺ summary of his community's best:

> *"The best of you are those who, when they are seen, God is remembered."* — the tradition transmits the sense; and the Ṣaḥīḥ's formulation from the Companion-report: *"A people among the servants of God, neither prophets nor martyrs, whom the prophets and martyrs will envy on the Day of Resurrection for their seats with God... they are the people of the gatherings of remembrance."* (Transmitted in the Sunan collections from Anas, in wording close to this)

**Commanding Right as the List's Public Face**

The seventh-eighth epithets deserve the sūrah's own emphasis, because chapter nine has spent its whole length on the two communities' difference along precisely this axis: verse 67's hypocrites — *they enjoin what is wrong and forbid what is right* — the photographic negative; verse 71's believers — *they enjoin what is right and forbid what is wrong* — the positive; verse 112's chain includes the same pair as the sold community's *public duty*: the believer's purchase includes, at no extra charge and no exemption, the office of moral witness — the tradition's jurists built upon the epithet the entire doctrine of al-amr bil-maʿrūf: its gradations (the hand, the tongue, the heart, as the famous triad from the Muslim corpus has it: *"whoever of you sees a wrong, let him change it with his hand; if unable, with his tongue; if unable, then with his heart — and that is the weakest of faith"* — recorded by Muslim from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī); and its manners — knowledge before speech, sincerity before rebuke, gentleness as the mode (the sūrah's own 3:159 rule, cited already at verse 73).

**Glad Tidings as the Closing Genre**

The verse's final clause — *give glad tidings to the believers* — is genre-announcement: after one hundred eleven verses whose dominant register has been exposure, warning, and sorting, the sūrah's portrait-gallery closes with bashārah — the verb bashshir (to bring good news) that the Qur'an uses for its deepest mercies (*bashshir al-muʾminīn* — the refrain of the Book); and the tradition notes the placement's courtesy: the ten-epithet life is demanding — the map admits no unsold compartment — and the demand is served, at the list's end, not with a threat but with a herald.
