For the modern believer, verse 112 is thus the sūrah's *self-portrait offered for adoption*: the believer reading the ten epithets is meant to do with them what the tradition's spiritual masters taught their students to do — read the list slowly, once per epithet, asking the adoption-question: *is this me yet — and if not yet, what is its next smallest step?* — repentance's next turning, praise's next sentence, the prayer's next prostration, the journey's next mile, the witness's next honest word; and the verse's last clause answers the anxiety such an audit always generates, with the sūrah's own final genre: not a grade, but a glad tiding — the believer is not being scored against the ten, but *summoned into them*, by a Seller who has already paid.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:113

> **It is not for the Prophet and those who believe to seek forgiveness for the polytheists, even if they be kinsmen, after it has become clear to them that they are companions of the Blaze.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The General Rule from the Private Wound**

Verse 113 states the ruling that verse 80's episode pressed toward, and its occasion — the occasion all the exegetes cite — is the Prophet's ﷺ own father-crisis: the reports (al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, and the ḥadīth corpus) transmit that the Prophet ﷺ said he would keep asking forgiveness for his mother — or, in the strong line, for his parents — until God forbade it, and this verse descended: *mā kāna lil-nabiyyi wa-alladhīna āmanū an yastaghfirū lil-mushrikīn* — "it is not for the Prophet and those who believe to seek forgiveness for the polytheists — *wa-law kānū ulī al-qurbā* — even if they be kinsmen — *min baʿdi mā tabayyana lahum annahum aṣḥābu al-jaḥīm* — after it has become clear to them that they are companions of the Blaze." The verse's ground-clause does the legal work, and the tradition's jurists read it with precision: the prohibition attaches *after clarity* — after the person died in manifest shirk, the file's classification settled in the only Court that matters; the believer's prayer cannot follow a soul into a verdict already rendered by the Judge of the verdict.

**The Deeper Ground: Covenant's Grammar**

Ibn Kathīr anchors the verse in the Book's own theology of the two covenants: forgiveness-seeking is an *intra-covenant* act — the believer asks the Judge to apply mercy to one who died owing the Judge the one debt the Judge has declared He will not forgive being unrepented: the association of partners (4:48's ruling in its context); and the Qur'an had already given the ruling's case-law in Ibrāhīm, the proto-covenant father, whose file the next verse (114) opens. The prohibition is thus not a limit on the believer's love (which God never commands) but on the believer's *liturgy*: love may grieve (the Prophet ﷺ wept at his mother's grave, Muslim's report transmits — and was *granted* the visitation he was denied the forgiveness-prayer); love may serve the living kinsman without limit (the ṣilah-duties stand); what love may not do is attempt to reopen the settled case — because the attempt itself would confess a theology the believer does not hold: that God's clear rulings await our amendment.

**The Prophet's Own Case as the Verse's Seal**

The verse's pathos, which the tradition never bypasses, is in its addressee's biography: the Prophet ﷺ — the intercessor whose intercession the Book and the Ṣaḥīḥ collections celebrate (the ḥadīth of the Station of Praise) — is told, here, that his office does not extend past the covenant's edge, *even for the parents who gave him to the world*; and the reports state his submission in the very terms of the occasion: he had persisted in seeking forgiveness for them, and when the verse descended he stopped — as al-Bukhārī and Muslim transmit in the related line from Abū Hurayrah, he sought his Lord's leave regarding his mother, and it was not granted (the grave-visitation he was granted is the v. 84 section's subject above). The verse and the Sunnah together draw the exact boundary the tradition kept: liturgy inside the covenant, humanity everywhere.

**The Rule in the Believer's Hands**

For the modern believer, verse 113 is a boundary-marker of affections, and it must be carried with both its edges. Its prohibition-edge guards the theology: the believer does not negotiate the afterlife of the manifest denier with God — no matter the blood-tie — because the request itself would litigate against God's own clarity; and its tenderness-edge, without which the verse is not being read but wielded: the prohibition concerns the *dead in manifest shirk* — it does not license contempt for the living unbeliever (whose rights, kindness, and call the sūrah's 6 and 60:8 verses command), nor verdicts on the *hidden* (v. 101's rule), nor the grief of hearts, which God has never once in the Book condemned. The believer who has lost a non-Muslim parent or friend stands, on this verse, exactly where the Prophet ﷺ stood at his mother's grave: denied a prayer, granted a visitation — and the tradition's counsel to such a believer is the Prophet's own: go, weep, remember death, be kind to the living, and leave the dead's file — unopened by you, perfectly known to Him — with the only One who ever had the authority to open it.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:114

> **And the seeking of forgiveness by Abraham for his father was only because of a promise he had made to him. But when it became clear to him that he was an enemy of God, he declared himself innocent of him. Truly Abraham was tender-hearted, clement.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Case That Set the Precedent**

Verse 114 opens the file of Ibrāhīm — the occasion's logic being that the ruling of verse 113 (no forgiveness-seeking for the died-in-shirk) had already been set in revelation's first father: *wa-mā kāna istighfāru Ibrāhīma li-abīhi illā ʿan mawʿidatin waʿadahā iyyāh* — "and the seeking of forgiveness by Abraham for his father was only because of a promise he had made to him." The Book's own dossier supplies the promise: at Ibrāhīm's parting from his father — the Qur'an's tenderest leave-taking — he had said, *sawfa astaghfiru laka rabbī* — *"I shall ask forgiveness for you from my Lord"* (19:47; cf. 14:41, the prayer *"forgive my father — he was of the astray"*); and he kept the promise's letter through the interval of hope: *falammā tabayyana lahu annahu ʿaduwwun lillāhi tabarraʾa minhu* — "but when it became clear to him that he was an enemy of God, he declared himself innocent of him" — the verb tabarraʾa (to wash one's hands, to declare barāʾah — the sūrah's own title-word, its opening announcement of dissociation) marking the moment the filial hope died and the covenant's priority settled the account. The tradition's chronology-note (Ibn Kathīr's, from the exegetes): the clarity came with the father's death in his shirk — or, in al-Ṭabarī's line, with the manifest persistence that made the classification clear — and Ibrāhīm, informed by the clarity, did what verse 113 now commanded the last Prophet: stopped the prayer.

**The Ethics of Promises and the Priority of Clarity**

The exegetes mine the verse's legal psychology: a promise made (*I shall seek forgiveness*) binds its maker until its performance becomes *disobedience* — and the verse's doctrine is that clarity dissolves the obligation: the promise was made in the season of hope (before the father's settled state was known); when the facts cleared, the promise's ground cleared with them. Al-Qurṭubī generalizes the rule the jurists kept: promises bind *except* where performance would transgress a revealed limit — and the believer's exit from such a promise is not breach but *barāʾah*: the honest, publicized realignment with the higher claim. And the verse's application to the believer's own entanglements is immediate: the friendship promised to a course, the loyalty promised to a project, the family peace promised to a silence — each promise holds until clarity (the manifest enemy-of-God fact, in the verse's own terms) converts its keeping into disobedience; then the Ibrāhīmic move is required: dissociation, declared — the opposite of the modern instinct, which keeps the promise and quietly amends the creed.

**"Tender-Hearted, Clement": The Portrait's Necessity**

The verse's closing description — *inna Ibrāhīma la-awwāhun ḥalīm* — "truly Abraham was tender-hearted (awwāh), clement (ḥalīm)" — is the tradition's favorite clause, because it forbids the reading the verse would otherwise invite: that the dissociation was easy, cold, or glad. *Awwāh* is the intensive of sighing — the man of frequent *ah*, of the freely-moved heart; *ḥalīm* is the patient, forbearing one who does not act on first provocation; the portrait insists that the man who washed his hands of his father did it with the softest heart in revelation's gallery — the Qur'an's ruling issued *through* maximum tenderness, to establish forever that the covenant's priority is not hardness but *rank order*: love of God above love of father, executed by a man who loved his father with an awwāh's whole capacity. The Prophet ﷺ certified the ranking himself, in the ḥadīth the collections transmit:

> *"None of you believes until I am more beloved to him than his father, his son, and all people."* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī (15) and Muslim (70) from Anas)

— with 9:24 of this sūrah stating the same trial in its severest form; and Ibrāhīm stands in verse 114 as the ranking's demonstrated case: the tenderest son, the highest creed.

**The Model for the Modern Believer's Hardest Dissociations**

For the modern believer, verse 114 is the Sunnah's template for the era's commonest covenant-crisis: the believing child of an unbelieving (or apostatizing) parent; the convert whose family reads the baptism — or the shahādah — as betrayal; the believer whose kinship-circle demands, as the price of belonging, the company of what God has clearly forbidden. The verse's template has three components, and none may be dropped: the *prior kindness* (Ibrāhīm's years of pleading, promise, and prayer — the 19:42–47 record — model the pre-clarity conduct: gentleness, argument, invitation); the *clarity test* (the dissociation triggers not on difference, disagreement, or even hostility, but on the *manifest enemy-of-God* fact — a bar the tradition set deliberately high, forbidding the teenager's weaponized barāʾah against a merely disappointing parent); and the *tender execution* (the awwāh's manner — grief without cruelty, distance without insult, the door of the father's repentance, on the traditional reading of 14:41's continuing hope for the *believing* among them, held open to the last hour it was open). The believer who holds all three has done, in the modern family's minefield, exactly what the verse's subject did at his father's death-bed's edge: kept the rank order, kept the tenderness, and left the account — as Ibrāhīm left Āzar's — in the hands of the One who receives both the dissociation and the *ah* that followed it.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:115

> **And God would never lead a people astray after having guided them, until He makes clear to them that from which they should protect themselves. Truly God is Knower of all things.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse of Fair Procedure**

Verse 115 interrupts the dissociation-sequence with a doctrinal statement the tradition counts among the Qur'an's principles of divine justice — the occasion being, as the reports and the context establish, the three suspended men of verse 118's story, whose followers wavered (were they condemned? had God led them astray?): *wa-mā kāna Allāhu li-yuḍilla qawman baʿda idh hadāhum* — "and God would never lead a people astray after having guided them — *ḥattā yubayyina lahum mā yattaqūna* — until He makes clear to them that from which they should protect themselves" — the verb yubayyina (to make clear, to explain) naming God's procedural rule: misguidance as *judgment* never precedes clarity; the astray-as-verdict (iḍlāl in the judicial sense) falls only after the ḥudūd are delivered — the limits published, the warning given, the protection-target identified — and the tradition's theologians read the verse as the Book's own answer to the predestination puzzle the era's debates would raise: God's leading-astray (in verses like 2:6–7's sealing) is never the *cause* of anyone's loss; it is the judicial consequence of loss already chosen, and verse 115 states the court's rule of procedure that guarantees it: *no condemnation without prior clarification.*

**The Guidance-Astray Axis in the Qur'an's Grammar**

Al-Rāzī and the tradition's systematicians organize the verse's vocabulary with care: the Qur'an uses iḍlāl (leading astray) in two registers — the *creational* (God's providence permitting, in His wisdom, the loser's chosen loss — the idiom of 16:93: *"He leads astray whom He wills and guides whom He wills"*, which the scholars read with 76:3's *"We guided him to the path, whether grateful or ungrateful"*) and the *judicial* (the withdrawal of grace after clarity refused) — and verse 115 rules on the second: the judicial iḍlāl *never precedes* the bayān; the fair-provision clause stands between the guided state and the strayed state, an absolute bar. Al-Qurṭubī's illustration is the sūrah's own material: the men of verses 93 and 101 — their sealing (the judicial register) followed the delivered limits (the Tabūk summons, the two-currency command) and their refusal of the clarity; the clarity-courier in every case being, as the tradition summarizes, the messenger and the Book — *"And We never punish until We send a messenger"* (17:15).

**The Three Men's Case Under the Rule**

The verse's occasion-note — the three suspended men — supplies its most tender application: their followers feared that the community's boycott (v. 118's fifty days) meant God had *led them* astray — had they not been guided, then dropped? — and the verse answers before the question fully forms: impossible — *until clarity*; no soul is dropped whose warning was not complete; and the reports complete the application: the three men's clarity *was* complete (they knew the command, admitted the truth of their failure — their case file, in Kaʿb ibn Mālik's famous narration, contains no claimed excuse at all), and precisely that completeness — no loophole, no ambiguity — became, with the suspension and its resolution (v. 118's relief), the structure of their repentance's perfection: nothing left to appeal but God's mercy, which was the appeal that succeeded.

**Fairness as the Believer's Copy of the Rule**

For the modern believer, verse 115 is both a creed-statement and a management manual. As creed: it settles the anxious loop that reads every difficulty as abandonment — God does not drop the guided; the *felt* distance after guidance has, on this verse's procedure, one of two causes: unaddressed clarity (something made clear that the soul has not yet obeyed — the verse's implicit invitation to an audit: *what bayān has recently arrived?*) or trial-by-love (the ṣadr-widening of 94:5–6's *with hardship comes ease* tradition) — never arbitrary misguidance. As manual: the believer entrusted with others (parent, teacher, leader) inherits the rule as a *sunnah of governance*: no condemnation of the instructed before the instruction was complete — the child not punished for the rule never taught, the employee not blamed for the standard never stated, the community not charged with the clarity never delivered; and the verse's closing signature — *inna Allāha bi-kulli shayʾin ʿAlīm* — "truly God is Knower of all things" — administers both halves: the One who knows whether the clarity reached you, and the One who knows, in every case you will ever judge, whether it reached *them*.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:116

> **Truly unto God belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth. He gives life and causes death. And you have no guardian or helper besides God.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse of Sole Dominion**

Verse 116 stands in the sūrah's final movement like a keystone: *inna lillāhi mulku al-samāwāti wa-l-arḍi* — "truly unto God belongs the kingdom (mulk) of the heavens and the earth" — the mulk being not territory merely but *sovereignty*: the active, exercised, unconditional right to command, give, withhold, and judge; and the tradition's exegetes read the verse's placement as the sūrah's doctrinal summary — after seventeen pages of communities sorted, campaigns commanded, ledgers opened and closed, the revelation states the ground of it all: the Sorter owns the sorted. The verse's second clause states the sovereignty's two extremities: *yuḥyī wa-yumītu* — "He gives life and causes death" — the two acts that bracket every creature's whole story, and whose ownership is the Qur'an's standing refutation of every rival claimant:

> *"Say: O God, Master of the Kingdom, Thou givest the kingdom to whomsoever Thou wilt, and seizest the kingdom from whomsoever Thou wilt; Thou exaltest whom Thou wilt and abasest whom Thou wilt; in Thy hand is good; truly Thou art powerful over all things."* (Qur'an 3:26)

And the verse's closing clause delivers the personal corollary: *wa-mā lakum min dūni Allāhi min waliyyin wa-lā naṣīr* — "and you have no guardian (waliyy) or helper (naṣīr) besides God" — the same two nouns the sūrah deployed at its center (v. 74: *mā lihum min waliyyin wa-lā naṣīrin* — the perjurers' verdict), now returned as universal doctrine over all humanity: the guardianship and the help are God's alone — a fact that cuts both ways, dread for those who made God their adversary (no protector against Him), and rest for those who made Him their mawlā (v. 51's *ḥasbunā Allāhu* — He suffices — needs no second).

**Ownership as the Sūrah's Own Conclusion**

The commentators note how exactly the verse answers the sūrah's running temptations. Against the wealth-worship (vv. 34–35, 55, 85, 98): the wealth was always His — the mulk's inventory, not the hoarder's. Against the fear of men that produced the excuses (vv. 56–57, 86): the men they feared own nothing — no waliyy, no naṣīr; the fear was misallocated sovereignty. Against the mosque-builders and their patron (vv. 107–110): the ground they built on was the King's ground, and the King ruled on the lease. And against the believer's own residual paganism — the quiet belief that outcomes are held by systems, markets, powers, and persons: the verse performs, in one sentence, the tawḥīd-cure the tradition names *tawakkul's intellectual foundation*: trust in God is not a mood but a *jurisdictional fact* — the Kingdom is His; the case is His; there is no other court. Ibn ʿAjībah's gloss, in the spirit of the verse, summarizes: he who knows the King needs no other patron; he who knows the Kingdom's extent fears no other ruler; and he who knows life and death are His two hands spends both currencies (the sūrah's māl and nafs, one last time) at the only counter that never closes.

**The Two Extremities and the Believer's Peace**

The life-death clause carries the verse's personal weight, and the tradition reads it at the bedside as much as in the seminary: *yuḥyī wa-yumītu* — the One who began your story without consulting your means (the mulk's grant of life) will end it without consulting your fears (the mulk's recall); and between the two extremities, every intermediate sovereignty — the physician's, the employer's, the state's — is delegated, derivative, and terminable: the believer honors the delegates (the Sunnah's whole ethic of means) while refusing them the *heart's* dependence, which the verse's last clause reserves: no waliyy, no naṣīr, *besides God*. The Qur'an's most serene citizen is made by exactly this grammar — active in means, unowned by them — as the Prophet ﷺ taught ʿAbbād ibn Bishr's generation and every generation since in the counsel the collections transmit: *tie the camel and trust in God* (recorded in the Sunan from Anas, in wording the tradition has made proverbial).

**No Other Court**

For the modern believer, verse 116 is the sūrah's closing door shut firmly on the escape-architecture the whole chapter has been dismantling: the believer who has followed the sūrah's ledgers this far has watched every alternative guardian fail on the page — wealth (punishing), kin and patrons (perjured), the sitting-class (sealed), the ḍirār network (demolished), even the Prophet's ﷺ own intercession (bounded at v. 80 and 113) — and the verse states what the demonstrations proved: *there was never any other court.* The verse's daily office is the re-filing of anxieties: each fear that arrives naming another holder of outcomes (the diagnosis, the contract, the verdict, the border) is to be carried, by the verse's grammar, back to the only File-Owner — whose kingdom includes the heavens (the scale of the fear's subject), the earth (the address of the fear's object), life and death (the worst and the best case), and who has told the believer, in this same sūrah, what the Kingdom's disposition toward the repentant is: *He turned upon them* (v. 117, the next verse) — the King's own first move, recorded, for the reassurance of every subject who reads His rulings to the end.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:117

> **God has turned in mercy to the Prophet, and the Emigrants and the Helpers who followed him in the hour of hardship, after the hearts of a group of them had almost deviated. Then He turned unto them. Truly He is Kind, Merciful toward them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Divine Turn**

Verse 117 is the sūrah's mercy-crest — the verb *tāba* (to turn, return, relent) now applied *from God's side*: *la-qad tāba Allāhu ʿalā al-nabiyyi* — "God has turned in mercy upon the Prophet" — the ʿalā (upon) marking the direction of the divine motion: downward, toward, *first*; and the beneficiaries are named in the sūrah's own order of honor: the Prophet ﷺ; *wa-l-muhājirīna wa-l-anṣāri alladhīna ittabaʿūhu fī sāʿati al-ʿusrati* — "and the Emigrants and the Helpers who followed him in the hour of hardship" — the ʿusrah (hardness, straitness) naming Tabūk's season: the heat, the distance, the empty treasury, the march under whose conditions the whole sūrah has lived; and the verb of their following — *ittabaʿū* — is verse 100's verb: the following-in-virtue, performed here under the worst trade-conditions, which is why the tradition reads Tabūk as the community's graduation exercise. Then the verse's most human clause: *min baʿdi mā kāda yazīghu qulūbu farīqin minhum* — "after the hearts of a group of them had *almost deviated*" — the verb zāgha (to incline, swerve) in the kāda-construction (had almost): the near-miss; the exegetes, with the reports' support, identify the group as the ones shaken by the Tabūk extremes — the poor who wept at having nothing to give, the seven whose mosque-project was innocent but whose nerves were not, the rank-and-file whose hearts, at the edge of the desert, wobbled between the summons and the heat — *and God held them*: the tradition reads the omitted agent as the divine steadying (the 3:151-type *He established them*: cf. 8:11's *He sent down tranquility upon them*), and the verse says what steadied them: *thumma tāba ʿalayhim* — "then He turned unto them" — the double turn (first the hold, then the relenting) of a Lord who governs the heart's weather.

**Theology of the Almost**

The verse's kāda-clause deserves the tradition's full doctrine, because it is the believer's own biography in one word: *almost deviated.* The near-miss is real — the heart *did* reach the edge; the confession is in the verse, and the exegetes do not soften it; but the verse's placement of the confession (inside the sentence of *mercy*, not the sentence of verdict) teaches the doctrine the sūrah has been building since verse 102: wavering, honestly owned, is the material of God's turning, not its bar; the sealed heart of verse 93 is the one that stopped *confessing* the wavering and started managing it — the group of verse 117 wobbled *toward* God and told Him so, and the turn met them at the edge. Al-Qurṭubī's pastoral rule: the believer's interior is a contested weather-system; what mercy audits in the near-miss is not perfection but direction — and God's mercy, this verse rules, takes the weather into account.

**"Kind, Merciful": The Names Over the File**

The verse closes with the twin Names that governed the sūrah's mercy-arc: *inna Allāha bihim raʾūfun raḥīm* — "truly God is Kind (raʾūf), Merciful (raḥīm) toward them" — and the raʾūf is verse 128's own word (the Prophet ﷺ described by the same root in the sūrah's penultimate verse): tenderness as a divine attribute first, a prophetic attribute second; the tradition reads the pairing's placement as the sūrah's last structural lesson: the chapter that opened with barāʾah (dissociation, 9:1–5) closes its historical register with raʾfah — the severity and the tenderness both true, both divine, and ordered: *the turn came after the hardness did its work* — the ḥarr of verse 81, the boycott of verse 118's next case, all held inside a mercy that was never absent — which is, the scholars note, the sūrah's whole doctrine of discipline in a single verse-order: God turned upon them *after* the hour of hardness — the hardness His instrument, the turning His intent.

**The Modern Believer's Near-Misses**

For the modern believer, verse 117 is the verse to read on the evening of the near-miss: the day the doubt almost won, the almost-sent message, the almost-taken compromise, the prayer almost skipped — the heart's gyro giving its worst reading of the year; and the verse's counsel is its grammar: report the wobble (to God, in the sūrah's own register of confession), hold the direction (toward Him, however faintly), and receive the verse's testimony about what the Kind One does with nearly-lost hearts that stay in the file: *thumma tāba ʿalayhim* — He turned unto them — the same Officer of Reception as verse 104's Tawwāb, the same desk, open the same hours; and the believer who doubts whether his particular hour-of-hardness qualifies for the turn should note the verse's beneficiaries' condition — *who followed him in the hour of hardship* — the qualification was not the survival of the hardness without a wobble; it was the *following*, kept, through it.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:118

> **And He also turned unto the three who were left behind, till the earth, vast as it is, became straitened for them, and their souls became straitened for them, and they came to know that there is no refuge from God, except in Him. Then He turned unto them, that they might repent. Truly God is the Relenting, the Merciful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Story the Sūrah Kept for Last**

Verse 118 is the sūrah's narrative summit, and the tradition transmits its case-file in full — the longest personal-narrative ḥadīth in the Ṣaḥīḥ collections: al-Bukhārī (2757) and Muslim carry, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Kaʿb ibn Mālik, the first-person account of his father Kaʿb ibn Mālik — the poet of the Anṣār, a Badr-era Muslim who failed at Tabūk and *told the truth about it.* The three: Kaʿb ibn Mālik, Murārah ibn al-Rabīʿ, and Hilāl ibn Umayyah — all Anṣārī, all veterans of Badr's season (the reports note Hilāl among the earliest), none a hypocrite: their failure was the pure procrastinated sincerity — Kaʿb's own diagnosis, sworn in the ḥadīth: *I never was stronger or wealthier than on the day I lagged behind the Messenger of God* — no excuse existed; the muster left, he walked Madinah's streets pierced that only hypocrites remained with him, he delayed his confession through the campaign's fifty days in the vain hope the Prophet ﷺ would return *and the case would dissolve* — and when the Messenger returned and the excuse-parade filed past him (the ḥadīth preserves the whole line of them, including a man whose eloquence the Prophet ﷺ excused to God), the three refused the genre: when his turn came, Kaʿb said — asked the same question as all — he answered: *by God, I had no excuse at all... I was indeed with you the day I lagged* — and the Prophet ﷺ delivered the suspension: *as for him, he has spoken the truth; go, until God decides concerning you.*

**The Earth Itself Became Narrow**

Then the sentence, and the verse's first great image: *ḥattā idhā ḍāqat ʿalayhim al-arḍu bimā raḥubat* — "till the earth, vast as it is (bimā raḥubat), became straitened for them" — the syntax itself is the psychology: the earth's *vastness* (raḥbah) is stated inside the clause of its *narrowness* (ḍīq) — the world's dimensions unchanged, the experience collapsed: the divine withdrawal of comfort (the tradition reads the constriction as God's own act — the word *ḍāqat* permitted by Him) turning Creation's widest room into a cell; Kaʿb's narrative confirms the phenomenology in the details the ḥadīth preserves: the boycotting community (commanded — v. 118's context — to withhold from them *even the greeting*: the Ṣaḥīḥ's text states the Prophet ﷺ ordered the people to shun them until God decided their case, and to withhold even the greeting), the sold-out markets, the King of Ghassān's letter of recruitment (the enemy's counter-offer, arriving precisely in the isolation — the file's last test, which Kaʿb burned), the fifty days of the sūrah's most complete earthly loneliness. And the verse's second clause deepens the image: *wa-ḍāqat ʿalayhim anfusuhum* — "and their souls became straitened for them" — the constriction turning inward: not only the world too small, but the *self* too small to live in; the tradition's masters read the clause as the psychology of guilt come full term: the unconfessed failure (the three had confessed to the Prophet — but the *hearts'* full settling awaited God) housing them in an interior narrower than the earth — the soul itself refusing to be a refuge from the soul.

**"No Refuge from God Except in Him"**

Then the case's turning-point, stated as doctrine learned: *wa-ẓannū an lā maljaʾa mina Allāhi illā ilayhi* — "and they came to know (the ẓann of settled conviction) that there is no refuge (maljaʾ — a place one flees *to* for safety) from God, except in Him" — the sentence the tradition ranks among the Qur'an's most complete spiritual theologies: the flight-instinct (every creature's, at every pain) is analyzed and its object corrected — one cannot flee the Judge; one can only flee *into* Him; there is no maljaʾ *from* God except *in* God; the paradox resolves as the fire-wall resolves: the only safety from the fire is the fire's Owner. Kaʿb's narrative stages the doctrine at the narrative's end: the fiftieth day's dawn, the verse descending in Madinah, the rider on a mount crying *Kaʿb ibn Mālik — good news!* — and Kaʿb, prostrating at once (he had been praying, he notes, atop his roof in the pre-dawn, the ṣalāh of the desperate), coming down to find the community restored by revelation; and the ḥadīth's most famous detail — the Prophet ﷺ said, at the Friday congregation: *God has turned unto... the three who were left behind* — and Kaʿb, hurrying through the crowd, was met by Ṭalḥah ibn ʿUbayd Allāh, who alone rose to greet him — *hastening, joyful, never forgetting Ṭalḥah's joy for him that day* — the tradition's permanent monument to the congratulations that arrive when the file closes in mercy.

**"That They Might Repent": The Ending's Grammar**

The verse's final clauses complete the doctrine: *thumma tāba ʿalayhim li-yatūbū* — "then He turned unto them, *that they might repent*" — the order the scholars never stop teaching: God's turning first, the creature's turning as its fruit; the tawfīq (the enablement) preceding the tawbah, as the sun precedes the thaw; and the closing Names — *inna Allāhu huwa al-Tawwābu al-Raḥīm* — the Relenting, the Merciful — verse 104's acceptance-desk, open, as the sūrah's last narrative act, over the file the whole community had watched suspended for fifty days. The three men's afterlives seal the case: Kaʿb fought at every front thereafter and carried the ḥadīth of his own file to the next generation; Murārah and Hilāl — the reports state the three's later conduct matched the repentance; and the tradition's verdict on the whole story became the Sunnah's doctrine of truthful failure: the Prophet ﷺ had said, at the confession: *he has spoken the truth* — and the scholars drew the line that governs every failure since: *the truthful failure ends in mercy; the successful lie ends in the sealing* — the sūrah's two ledgers, in one sentence.

**The Believer's Own Fifty Days**

For the modern believer, verse 118 is the Qur'an's most precise map of the repentance-territory, walked in real time by a real man whose excuselessness is the point. Its lessons, in the order the story teaches them: the delay of confession is the straitening's true cause (Kaʿb's fifty days were spent hoping the case would dissolve; the earth narrowed at the *waiting*, not the sin); the community has a role the tradition kept (the suspension's discomfort was prescribed, and its end prescribed too — absolution arriving by revelation; the modern community's discipline aims at restoration, never at exile forever); the enemy's letter always arrives during the isolation (the recruit-the-desperate pattern — Ghassān's king knew a shunned man when his scouts reported one); and the terminus is always the same sentence, available in every generation at the same address: *no refuge from God except in Him* — the believer's last-hour geography, learned best (as the three learned it) when every other address has been visited and found narrow: the markets, the friendships, the interior itself — all raḥbah collapsed to ḍīq — until the believer does what Kaʿb did on the roof before dawn: stops fleeing, turns around, and prays *toward* the One he was fleeing from — and the file, the tradition testifies on this verse's authority, closes the same way the three men's did: not with the sentence the fifty days feared, but with the Names the fifty days forgot were waiting: *al-Tawwābu al-Raḥīm.*

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:119

> **O you who believe, be mindful of God, and be with the truthful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Shortest Heavy Verse in the Sūrah**

Verse 119 is six words of Arabic carrying the sūrah's entire practical doctrine: *yā ayyuhā alladhīna āmanū ittaqū Allāha* — "O you who believe, be mindful of God" — *wa-kūnū maʿa al-ṣādiqīn* — "and be with the truthful." The occasion, which the reports and the verse's placement establish, is the three men's case just closed: Kaʿb's own testimony — the ḥadīth of his file, cited at verse 118 — preserves the tradition's most famous application, from al-Bukhārī's very narration: the verse's word ṣādiqīn is read by the exegetes with Kaʿb's confession in view — *as for truthfulness (al-ṣidq), it brought me salvation* — the line the ḥadīth's after-matter transmits from Kaʿb's own lips; and the Prophet's ﷺ reported words to him — the tradition's line that the verse descended regarding the three, that the truthful are such as they were — make the pairing explicit: taqwā (the God-consciousness that refuses the managed lie) plus the company of the ṣādiqīn (the community of those who tell the truth about themselves) is the sūrah's distillation of everything its one hundred eighteen verses of ledgers, exposures, confessions, and returns have demonstrated.

**Ṣidq as the Sūrah's Dividing Line**

The sūrah's whole architecture retroactively justifies the little verse's weight. Chapter nine has been, from its first oath to its last file, a sorting of truth from management: the managed reputations of verses 94–96 (sworn, priced, worthless); the confession of verse 102 (mixed, honest, received); the three men's truthfulness without excuse (v. 118, mercy); the ḥadīth the collections transmit on the verse's root-matter — the Prophet's ﷺ great triad:

> *"Truthfulness leads to righteousness, and righteousness leads to the Garden; and a man keeps telling the truth until he is written with God as a truthful one (ṣiddīq). Lying leads to wickedness, and wickedness leads to the Fire; and a man keeps lying until he is written with God as a liar."* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī (6094) and Muslim (2607) from Ibn Masʿūd)

The triad's grammar is verse 119's doctrine expanded: ṣidq is not a policy but a *career* — the practice compounding into identity (ṣiddīq: the superlative truth-teller, the rank of Abū Bakr) — and its opposite compounding likewise; the sūrah's sealed men and its saved men differ, at the last audit, on exactly this axis, which is why the verse, arriving after the file-closings, reads as the sūrah's signature-line: *be with the truthful* — the company whose file-titles verse 102, 117, and 118 have just certified.

**The Company-Clause: Why "With"**

The verse's second command is social in its grammar — *kūnū maʿa* — "be *with*" — and the tradition built its doctrine of spiritual companionship on the preposition: taqwā is commanded to the singular soul (it is the vertical axis, non-delegable), but its maintenance is assigned to *company* — the maʿiyyah (withness) of the ṣādiqīn; the masters' reasoning, which the Ṣufī tradition systematized and the jurists endorsed: the self's truthful self-assessment atrophies in isolation and in false company (the v. 65 campfire's mockery-circle is the sūrah's specimen: three men alone made each other worse); it is kept alive among people whose own standard is truth — the ṣaḥābah's circles of correction, the ḥalaqah's centuries, the believer's one honest friend whose presence is a mirror. The Prophet ﷺ gave the company-doctrine its corporate form in the ḥadīth of the good and bad companion:

> *"The example of the good companion and the bad companion is that of the bearer of musk and the blower of the bellows: the musk-bearer will either give you some, or you will buy from him, or you will find a good scent with him; and the blower of the bellows will either burn your clothes, or you will find a foul odor with him."* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī (2101) and Muslim (2628) from Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī)

— and 9:61's plantech passage (cited at that verse) supplies the positive: the believer's best company is the Prophet ﷺ himself, present now in his Sunnah, his Book, and his inheritors.

**The Verse as a Life-Design Tool**

For the modern believer, verse 119 is among the Qur'an's most operational commands, and the tradition's practice suggests its implementation as a *portfolio review of withness*: list the company — physical and digital — and for each entry ask the verse's two audit-questions: does this company raise my taqwā (the vertical test) and does it raise my truthfulness (the honesty test — the company where my excuses shrink, my confessions get easier, my managed self gets *more* managed, or less)? The answers sort the portfolio into the musk-bearers and the bellows-blowers, and the verse's command — *be with* — then becomes concrete scheduling: more hours with the truthful, fewer with the corrosive, with the digital feeds assessed as strictly as the living rooms; and the believer who finds the ṣādiqīn scarce in his geography has the tradition's standing fallbacks: the Book (the ṣaḥībah of the Qur'an), the dead truth-tellers' works (Ibn al-Qayyim's counsel: take your needs to the graves of the sincere scholars' books), and the Friday congregation, the remnant-of-the-ṣaḥābah assembly the Sunnah never let die — the withness, one way or another, is keepable, and the sūrah that ends its narratives with a saved liar's confession to his Prophet has earned the right to command it: *be with the truthful* — because it has just shown, in the fifty-day file, what the truthful company is *for*.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:120

> **It was not for the people of Madinah, and for the Bedouins around them, to stay behind the Messenger of God, nor to prefer themselves to him. That is because they are not afflicted by thirst, or fatigue, or hunger, in the way of God; nor do they tread a tread that enrages the disbelievers, nor do they gain anything from an enemy, but that it is written for them as a righteous deed. Truly God does not waste the wage of the virtuous.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Geography of Obligation**

Verse 120 states the sūrah's final communal ruling — the *why* behind the no-staying of the whole chapter, now formalized as law for the settled belt: *mā kāna li-ahli al-madīnati wa-man ḥawlahum mina al-aʿrābi* — "it was not for the people of Madinah, nor for the Bedouins around them" — *an yatakhallafū ʿan rasūli Allāhi* — "to stay behind the Messenger of God" — *wa-lā yarghabū bi-anfusihim ʿan nafsihi* — "nor to prefer themselves to him/it" — the idiom's grammar (r-gh-b *with* anfus, about the self) naming the root sin of the sūrah's stayers: the self-preference — the private calculus in which one's own ease outranks the Messenger's person, the community's need, the duty's rank. The verse's jurisdiction-clause is sociologically exact: the Madinan settled and their desert ring — the population within reach of the summons — are the obliged class; the tradition's jurists drew from the verse the fiqh of campaign-geography (obligation scales with capability and proximity) and, beyond it, the general law: *capability defines constituency* — the community's obligations are assigned by capacity to respond, and the verse censures, specifically, the ones whose capacity was real and whose response was not.

**The Great Reimbursement Clause**

Then the verse delivers the sūrah's most generous accounting — the tradition's treasury-doctrine of the expended life: *lā yuṣībuhum ẓamaʾun* — "they are not afflicted by thirst" — *wa-lā naṣabun* — "nor fatigue" — *wa-lā makhmaṣatun* — "nor hunger" — *fī sabīli Allāhi* — "in the way of God" — *wa-lā yataṭawwaʾūna mawṭiʾan yaghīẓu al-kuffāra* — "nor do they tread a tread that enrages the disbelievers" — *wa-lā yanālūna min ʿaduwwin naylan* — "nor do they gain anything from an enemy" — *illā kutiba lahum bihi ʿamalun ṣāliḥ* — "but that it is written for them as a righteous deed." Five cost-lines — thirst, fatigue, hunger, the enemy-enraging footprint, the gain from the foe — each converted, automatically, into credit; and the tradition's scholars read the clause's metaphysics with the delight it deserves: the believer's *costs* on the path are not merely compensated but *transmuted* — the thirst itself becomes the deed; the Book's accountants enter the pain in the asset column; the metaphor is not reimbursement but alchemy. Al-Qurṭubī's inventory expands the five lines to the whole cost-of-discipleship: every discomfort endured *with the right address* (fī sabīli Allāh — the addressing-clause that separates the martyr's thirst from the merchant's) is in the ledger; and the Prophet ﷺ had already guaranteed the smallest unit's conversion:

> *"Nobody is stricken with fatigue, or illness, or anxiety, or sadness, or hurt, or grief — even the thorn that pricks him — but that God expiates some of his sins by it."* (Recorded by al-Bukhārī (5641) and Muslim (2573) from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī and Abū Hurayrah)

**"God Does Not Waste the Wage of the Virtuous"**

The verse's closing seal — *inna Allāha lā yuḍīʿu ajra al-muḥsinīn* — "truly God does not waste the wage of the virtuous" — is the Qur'an's standing guarantee-phrase (the formula that closes 11:115's *establish the prayer... and the good deeds; for God wastes not the wage of the virtuous*), and its placement here completes the sūrah's economics seminar: the chapter that opened with wealth-warnings (vv. 34–35), audited the spending-classes (vv. 79–99), and notarized the bargain (v. 111) ends its legal register with the *wage-guarantee* — the commercial God who wastes nothing: no thirst, no mile, no thorn. The verb yuḍīʿa (to let be lost, misplace) is the promise's precision: the wage is not merely paid but *kept* — nothing misplaced in the filing, no entry dropped — the divine bookkeeping as the believer's deepest solace; and the tradition notes the sūrah's symmetry: the stayers' ledger of preserved comforts (v. 85's punishing assets) against the marchers' ledger of converted thirsts — the two portfolios' end-of-year statements being, respectively, verses 82's weeping and 89's rivers.

**The Modern Believer's Cost-Ledger**

For the modern believer, verse 120 is the Qur'an's charter for the *cost-side of the religious life* — and its most consoling bookkeeping rule. The believer's Tabūks are relational, vocational, communal: the fast's thirst at the workstation, the hijāb's or beard's hostile footprint, the honest deal that lost the client, the standing-with-the-oppressed that cost the circle — each is a cost with an address, and the verse's clause (fī sabīli Allāhi — the address is the whole classification) trains the believer to *check the address* before lamenting the price: costs on God's path are assets; costs on the ego's path are merely costs; and the verse's five-line inventory invites the believer's evening audit — *what did today cost me, and where was it filed?* — with the guarantee standing over every rightly-filed entry: written as a righteous deed, wage unrelinquished, by the Bookkeeper whose attribute is the verse's last line: He does not waste — *any* — the wage of those who do beautiful things, which, on the sūrah's accounting, includes every honest thirst.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:121

> **Nor do they spend, small or great, nor do they cross a valley, but that it is written for them, that God may recompense them for the best of what they used to do.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Complete Bookkeeping**

Verse 121 completes verse 120's reimbursement-charter with its companion-guarantee on the *resource* side: the marchers' costs (thirst, fatigue, hunger — v. 120) are credited; so are their *expenditures* — *wa-mā yunfiqūna min nafaqatin ṣaghīratin aw kabīratin* — "nor do they spend — an expenditure small or great" — *wa-lā yaqtaʿūna wādiyan* — "nor do they cross a valley" — *illā kutiba lahum* — "but that it is written for them" — the same divine accounting-verb (kutiba: it is written) as the cost-clause, now over the whole inventory of the striving life: the spending at every scale (the ṣaghīrah's date and the kabīrah's caravan — verse 79's two sneered-at classes both honored here), the geography of obedience (every wadi-crossing of every campaign: the ledger's entries measured in valleys), each entry made *li-yajziyahumu Allāhu aḥsana mā kānū yaʿmalūn* — "that God may recompense them for the *best* of what they used to do." The exegetes dwell on the final clause's generosity: the wage is computed on the *best* (aḥsan) — not the average, not the whole — the divine auditor's rule of maximum leniency built into the ledger's closing formula; and Ibn Kathīr links the rule to the Qur'an's parallel promises: *whosoever brings a good deed shall have ten times its like* (6:160).

**Nothing Too Small to Be Filed**

The verse's doctrine of the *small* (ṣaghīrah) is its pastoral heart. The sūrah has dealt in great sums — armies funded, estates liquidated, two-currency lives; verse 121 closes the register by certifying the ledger's resolution: the same bookkeeping that entered ʿUthmān's fleet-funding enters the single date of the night-laborer; the same pen that wrote the Tabūk march writes the wadi-crossing; the tradition's masters drew the corollary the Qur'an states elsewhere in its charity-mathematics — *the example of those who spend their wealth seeking God's pleasure... is as a grain that sprouts seven ears, in every ear a hundred grains* (2:261) — and the Prophet ﷺ fixed the scale's floor in the ḥadīth of the half-ṣāʾ already cited at verse 79, and its ceiling in the two-dirham rule: nothing lawful given is beneath the ledger; nothing given from earned wealth fails to grow. Al-Qurṭubī's summary reads the verse as the community's standing encouragement: the believer's reluctance at the smallness of his gift is a category-error — the ledger does not record amounts; it records *addressed expenditures* (fī sabīl Allāh) and *crossed valleys*, and the categories are denominated in faith, not volume.

**Valleys as Units of Devotion**

The verse's second cost-line — *yaqtaʿūna wādiyan* — has been loved by the tradition for its sheer concreteness: the Qur'an, whose Paradise-rivers are its most famous geography, takes a moment to canonize the *wadi* — the dry, hot, bedouin-boring ditch between two hills — as a unit of recorded devotion; every valley the army crossed toward Tabūk is in the Book; and the spiritualized readings (the valley as every low place of the soul's journey, every depression traversed on the way to obedience) are licensed by the verse's metaphor without displacing the literal, which is the point: *the physical miles of the religious life are themselves worship* — the bus to the mosque, the flight to the ḥajj, the walk to the reconciling-meeting — each one a wadi in the ledger; the tradition's extension became proverbial in the pilgrimage manuals: the traveler for God's sake is in worship until he returns.

**The Ledger's Style: Writing for the Best**

For the modern believer, verses 120–121 together supply the completed theology of the *accounted life*: every cost (v. 120) and every expense and every mile (v. 121) — written, kept, and recompensed on the aḥsan-standard. The practical effect the tradition intended is the inversion of the cost-benefit anxiety that paralyzes religious initiative: the believer's nightly arithmetic (*was the conference worth the airfare? did the year of service count?*) is answered by a Bookkeeping that files *everything* — the verse's two "nor" clauses are exhaustive by design — and computes the return at the maximum defensible reading of the entry; and the believer's remaining task, in the sūrah's last analytical verses, is therefore not accounting but *addressing*: to keep the expenditures and the crossings on the one path whose ledger this is — fī sabīli Allāh — the address that, as the whole sūrah has ruled, is the deed's entire classification, and the only line-item the divine auditor ever asks about first.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:122

> **It is not for the believers to go forth all together. So why does a party from every group of them not go forth, in order to become deeply versed in religion, and to warn their people when they return to them, that they may beware?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Division of Labor Revelation**

Verse 122 is the sūrah's — and arguably the Qur'an's — foundational statement of the community's functional differentiation, and its occasion, as the reports and the context establish, is the post-Tabūk demography: whole settlements had emptied into the army, and the verse redistributes the obligations: *mā kāna lil-muʾminīna an yanfirū kāffatan* — "it is not for the believers to go forth *all together*" — the kāffah (entirety) naming the error of total mobilization: the community is not a standing army but a civilization; and the verse's interrogative then establishes the positive structure: *fa-law lā nafara min kulli firqatin minhum ṭāʾifatun* — "so why does a *party* (ṭāʾifah) from every group (firqah) of them not go forth" — *li-yatafaqqahū fī al-dīni* — "that they may become deeply versed (tafaqqah — from the fiqh-family: the fiqh of v. 87's diagnosis!) in religion" — *wa-li-yundhirū qawmahum idhā rajaʿū ilayhim* — "and warn their people when they return to them" — *laʿallahum yaḥdharūn* — "that they may beware." The grammar of the mission is precise: a cadre (ṭāʾifah) deployed *from every community subgroup* (each locality's own — the local knowledge preserved), for the dual curriculum: *learning* (tafaqqah: the deep instruction, at the source — in the Prophet's ﷺ Madinah, in the first instance) and *teaching* (the warning-return: the cadre's knowledge is a trust with a delivery-address — *their people*, idhā rajaʿū — the homecoming as the mission's second half).

**Taḥṣīl and the Economy of Expertise**

The verse generated the tradition's entire doctrine of specialized religious learning — the scholars naming its principle *taḥṣīl al-ʿilm* (the acquisition of knowledge as a distinct communal assignment): not every believer becomes a faqīh; a cadre does, on the community's behalf, and the community is bound to support the cadre and receive the teaching. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī develop the fiqh: the verse is the proof-text for the community's obligation (farḍ kifāyah — the scholars' technical term, coined on this verse's pattern) that some, not all, carry: when the cadre functions, the obligation rests on the whole community as *discharged*; when none functions, the whole bears the sin of the vacancy — the doctrine that governed the centuries of the madrasah-systems and still governs the fiqh of professional religious study. And the verse's warning-clause defines the cadre's product: the homecoming instruction aims at *ḥadhar* — the practical wariness of the warned: knowledge's KPI is the community's caution before God, not the community's admiration of the knower — the tradition's rule: the learning exists for the warning, and the warning for the beware.

**The Command's Balance-Beam**

The verse holds a balance the tradition has had to keep consciously ever since, and both sides of the beam matter. Against the pacifist reading (no one need go forth): the verse *assumes* the going-forth — the kāffah-limit presupposes that *some* still must (the campaign-obligations of the sūrah stand; the verse redistributes, it does not retire them). Against the clericalist reading (the religious class does the religion): the verse's cadre is a *rotating function* of the groups (min kulli firqatin — from every group — the scholars are local sons, not a caste), and the warning-return keeps them servants of the warned, not their lords — the Prophet's ﷺ model of the scholar-as-steward standing behind the whole fiqh: *the scholars are the heirs of the prophets* (the Sunan's ḥadīth from Abū al-Dardāʾ — heirs, meaning inheritors of a trust to distribute, not of a rank to enjoy). And against the modern extreme — the community where *no one* goes forth to the deep learning, and the warning-classes are fed by whoever claims a microphone: the verse's interrogative (*fa-law lā?* — "why does not...?") is a rebuke-shaped question, and the tradition hears it as standing: the vacancy it scolds is always current somewhere.

**The Verse in the Modern Community's Structure**

For the modern believer, verse 122 is the constitutional text for the community's intellectual infrastructure, and its applications run from the institutional to the personal. Institutionally: the community needs its cadres — the institutions of deep instruction (the seminaries, the programs, the apprenticeships) and the delivery-networks that carry the learned home to warn their own; and the verse's *min kulli firqatin* is the diversity-clause: every subgroup, subculture, and generation must send and receive its own — the warning that arrives without local fluency failing the verse's own idhā rajaʿū. Personally, the verse addresses the believer's vocational discernment: the community's needs include the going-forth and the staying — the ṭāʾifah's specialized deployment and the settled community's faithful building — and the believer's task, in the verse's terms, is to know which his season is: to go forth for the tafaqqah when sent, to return with the warning when prepared, and to build, in the settled seasons, the ḥadhar-culture the whole structure exists to produce: a people at home in their Book, warned in their own tongue, wary before their Lord — which is, the sūrah is nearly ready to say in its closing verses, the entire point of everything since verse one.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:123

> **O you who believe, fight those of the disbelievers who are near you, and let them find harshness in you. And know that God is with the God-reverent.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command's Military Logic and Its Ethics**

Verse 123 is the sūrah's final fighting-command, and the tradition reads it as the strategic codicil to the campaign-chapter: *yā ayyuhā alladhīna āmanū qātilū alladhīna yalūnakum mina al-kuffāri* — "O you who believe, fight those of the disbelievers who are *near you*" (yalūnakum: from w-l-y, to be adjacent — the near neighbors, the bordering hostile polities, the Arab tribes whose violation of the treaties the sūrah's opening verses had already adjudicated) — *wa-l-yajidū fīkum ghilẓan* — "and let them find in you *ghilẓah*" — harshness, sternness, hardness — the same noun the sūrah used at verse 73's command (*ughluẓ ʿalayhim* — be harsh against the hypocrites and deniers), now applied to the external front of the late-Medinan situation: the era's remaining hostile coalitions, the lessons of Tabūk (the Byzantine frontier's proximity) defining the *near*. The exegetes' ethics-commentary is careful and cumulative: the verse's ghilẓah is a *deterrence-attribute, not a license* — the sūrah's own frame having already fixed the law of the covenant-honoring (v. 4, 7: no aggression against treaty-keepers), the war-conduct (v. 6's protection of the asylum-seeker), and the daʿwah's standing door (v. 6's escort to safety); al-Qurṭubī and the jurists read ghilẓah within the fiqh of jihād's conduct-rules: severity in *resolve and readiness* — the credibility that deters — never in treachery, mutilation, or the targeting the Sunnah explicitly forbade (the reports of al-Bukhārī and Muslim from Ibn ʿUmar preserve the Prophet's ﷺ prohibition: *do not kill women and children* — and his Sunnah's treatment of prisoners, from Badr onward, as the applied counterweight to this verse's sternness).

**"Near You": The Doctrine of Concentric Engagement**

The verse's geographic qualifier — *those near you* — carried a strategic doctrine the tradition's military-jurists (Ibn Kathīr's history, al-Ṭabarī's annals) trace through the Prophet's ﷺ actual remaining campaigns: the near threats first, the far in their order; and the scholars generalized the principle far beyond war: the believer's engagements follow a *concentric order* — the near obligations (family, neighbor, the adjacent wrong) precede the distant in both charity and confrontation; the man ghilẓah-hard on the far enemy while treasonously soft on the adjacent wrong inside his own house has inverted the verse's logic, since the verse's own grammar assumes the *near* is where resolve is tested first. And the closing clause — *wa-ʿlamū anna Allāha maʿa al-muttaqīn* — "and know that God is *with* the God-reverent" — disciplines the ghilẓah with the sūrah's master-attribute: the harshness is licensed, but the divine presence that decides battles is promised only to taqwā — not to force, numbers, or fury; the verse's last word, as al-Rāzī notes, is the whole sūrah's criterion wearing armor: God is with the *careful* — and the ghilẓah that forgets its taqwā-conditions forfeits the withness it was commanded for.

**Ghilẓah Already Governed: The Verse 73 Connection**

The sūrah's internal architecture must be held in view here, because verse 123's ghilẓah is verse 73's word returning at the chapter's end: at verse 73 the harshness was commanded *on two fronts* — the external deniers and the internal hypocrites — with the tongue and the hand distributed as the reports specify; and the intervening fifty verses have been the sūrah's demonstration of the *internal* front's method: exposure by revelation (not massacre — the hypocrites were boycotted, demoted, sorted, and *prayed for* at their graves, v. 80–84), mercy to the repentant (vv. 102–118), and withdrawal from the obstinate (v. 95). Verse 123 completes the system: the external front receives the ghilẓah-command the internal received — while the internal, as the sūrah showed, receives the *sharper* instrument: truth itself. The tradition's summary — the sword for the declared enemy, the argument for the concealed, and taqwā as the warrant of both — is the sūrah's final fighting-doctrine, stated in the verse's own two clauses.

**The Modern Believer's Near Fronts**

For the modern believer, verse 123 arrives stripped of its late-Medinan battlefield but intact in its structure, and the tradition's transfer-method is the concentric logic above: the believer's *near* fronts are named — the consumer of the household's peace (the anger, the screen, the absence), the exploiter next door, the wrong inside one's own project or congregation — and against these the verse's ghilẓah-as-resolve is due: firmness, clarity, the refusal of drift — while the war-conduct's Sunnah governs the manner (no treachery, no targets of the innocent, the door of the v. 6 escort never closed); and over every engagement, the verse's closing knowledge is the audit: *God is with the muttaqīn* — the withness conditionally attached to the careful, not to the loud; which returns the believer, at the sūrah's final fighting-word, to the sūrah's first condition (v. 4's covenant-keeping God-lover), and prepares the ear for the closing verses' register-change: after the ghilẓah, the Book finishes chapter nine with hearts — increasing and decreasing (v. 125), straying and staying (v. 127), and finally, mercy upon mercy, the tenderest portrait of the Messenger in the Qur'an (v. 128) and the believer's one-line creed of refuge (v. 129) — the architecture's sermon plain: the fighting-chapter ends by telling the fighters what it was all *for*.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:124

> **And whenever a sūrah is sent down, some of them say, "Which of you has this increased in faith?" As for those who believe, it increases them in faith, and they rejoice.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Congregation's Weekly Barometer**

Verse 124 preserves, with the intimacy of an overheard remark, the community's reception-hall at the late-Madinan revelations: *wa-mā unzilat sūratun* — "and whenever a sūrah is sent down" (the *whenever*-construction of verse 86 returning) — *fa-minhum man yaqūlu ayyukum zādat-hādhīhi īmānan* — "some of them say: which of you has *this* increased in faith?" — the verse's quotation debated in its parsing: on the strong reading, the speakers are the hypocrites, and the line is the sneer of measurement — did that one move your needle? — revelation recited as an event of no consequence, its hearers audited for a change that (the sneer implies) will not be found; on the companion reading, the question is genuinely posed within the mixed congregation — who among you finds increase? — and the verse's answer then sorts the room by its responses. Either way the occasion is liturgical: the sūrah-descents of the era were congregational events — the Prophet ﷺ reciting the new passage, the community receiving it — and verse 124 records the room's barometric split: the same recitation, falling on the assembly, *increases some and (v. 125's clause) decreases others* — the Qur'an as the instrument that measures the room by how the room receives it, the Book's own doctrine of its two-edged reception:

> *"And We send down of the Qur'an that which is a healing and a mercy for the believers; and it increases the wrongdoers in nothing but loss."* (Qur'an 17:82)

**The Increase-Doctrine: Faith as Variable Capital**

The verse's theology of *ziyādah* — increase — settled one of the tradition's great doctrinal questions before it could fully open: is īmān constant or variable? Verse 124's answer, which the Ahl al-Sunnah formalized from it and its siblings (*that they may grow in faith with it* — 74:31; *We increased them in guidance* — 18:13; *and God increases those who are guided in guidance* — 19:76): faith is *dynamic capital* — it grows and it shrinks; the creed's ḥadīth-transmissions confirm the practical register: the Companions knew the felt differences — the Prophet ﷺ describing faith's seventy-odd branches (al-Bukhārī and Muslim from Abū Hurayrah), and the Sunnah's counsels to its renewal (*renew your faith... by the testimony there is no god but God*, the Sunan's report from Abū al-ʿĀliyah's generation); and the verse names the increase's instrument: the *sūrah itself* — recitation, not merely information: the believer's exposure to the descending word is the growth-event, which is why the tradition's spiritual physicians prescribed the Book as the faith-tonic's active ingredient — the believer who feels the needle stagnant is not instructed to feel harder; he is instructed to *recite more*, and to do so where the verse says the increase lands: in company, in the congregation, in the *whenever*.

**"And They Rejoice": The Response's Thermodynamics**

The believer's response-pair is diagnostic: *fa-ammā alladhīna āmanū fa-zādathum īmānan* — "as for those who believe, it increases them in faith" — *wa-hum yastabshirūn* — "and they rejoice" — the verb of the bargain-verse (v. 111's *fa-stabshirū*) in its continuous participle: the increase experienced as *good news in progress* — joy as faith's felt temperature; and the pairing teaches the reception's inner mechanics: the believer hears the sūrah as a *letter from the Beloved* (increase) and as a *share in the coming good* (rejoicing); the hypocrite hears measurement-talk (v. 124's sneer) and hardening (v. 125). The two thermodynamic profiles are the sūrah's final portrait-pair of the mixed congregation — the room of verses 101–106 at its last recorded meeting — and the tradition's pastoral application is the standing self-audit of the listening heart: *when the Qur'an is recited, which of the two profiles is mine today — the increase-with-rejoicing, or the measuring sneer that asks the question to avoid the answer?*

**The Believer's Needle**

For the modern believer, verse 124 personalizes instantly, because the instrument still functions: the believer's *recitation-response* remains the most honest barometer of his interior state — the passage that warmed him for years and now leaves him cold is not (the verse rules) evidence about the passage; it is a reading of the room, and the room is him; and the verse's prescribed response to a low reading is the whole tradition's program in miniature: return the instrument to the conditions of increase — congregation (the sūrah was received in company), consistency (the whenever-grammar), and expectation of address (the believer comes to be spoken to, not to have read); and the rejoicing-clause guards against the increase's misuse as spiritual one-upmanship — the believer who has received increase holds it as the v. 111 vendor holds his bargain: with joy, with gratitude, and with the quiet understanding that the Increase-Giver is under no obligation to continue except His promise — which is, the sūrah is nearly at its close, exactly what the next verse examines: the hearts that received the same recitations and went the other way.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:125

> **And as for those in whose hearts is a disease, it adds defilement to their defilement, and they die while they are disbelieving.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Diagnostic Complement**

Verse 125 completes verse 124's split-screen with its dark half: *wa-ammā alladhīna fī qulūbihim maraḍun* — "and as for those in whose hearts is a disease" — the sūrah's standing medical term for the corrupted interior (2:10's founding case: *in their hearts is a disease, so God increased them in disease*) — *fa-zādathum rijsan ilā rijsihim* — "it added defilement (rijs) to their defilement" — the same recitation that increased the believers' faith increasing the diseased heart's impurity — the Qur'an as the universal reagent: dropped into any heart, it reveals the medium; in the alkaline, faith; in the acid, defilement; the chemistry of reception determining what the revelation *does* there. And the verse's terminal clause — *wa-mātū wa-hum kāfirūn* — "and they died while they were disbelieving" — states the disease's endpoint on the occasion's actual cohort: the diagnosed men of the sūrah's exposures, whose hardness continued to death; the tradition's scholars read the clause as both history (the file of the ḍirār-party and the sealed askers closed at their graves) and warning-generic (the disease's *tendency*, unchecked, is terminal — every maraḍ-case is a candidate for the dying-in-it unless the v. 117-style turn intervenes).

**How One Recitation Feeds Both Outcomes**

The exegetes press the mechanism, because the verse's paradox — the same words as growth-agent and corruption-agent — is the sūrah's deepest psychology. Al-Rāzī's analysis, which the tradition kept: the revelation is *information about reality* — God, the Return, the accounting — and information does not act on a heart neutrally: it must be *integrated* (the v. 87 fiqh-function) or *defended against*; the believing heart integrates — the new sūrah is added to the existing structure, faith grows by absorption; the diseased heart defends — each new revelation is a new threat to the settled misalignment, and the defense-act itself (the sneer, the nit-pick, the measurement-talk of v. 124) *deepens the misalignment* — the heart hardening by the very exercise of its hardness, the rijs compounding like scar tissue around a refused surgery. Ibn Taymiyyah's dictum — the punishment of sin is further sin — is the verse's engine stated as law; and the sūrah's case-files have already displayed the natural history in stages: the campfire mockery (v. 65, the early lesion), the oath-cycle (vv. 74–96, the chronic phase), the seal (vv. 87, 93, 110, the sclerosis) — verse 125's clause the autopsy.

**The Verse's Mercy: Diagnosis Before Prognosis**

Yet the tradition insists the verse is placed as *diagnosis*, not doom, and the placement is the argument: verses 117–118 have just demonstrated — in the same sūrah, the same season, the same community — that the diseased-heart category is not a caste but a *stage*, and that the divine medicine (the constriction, the company of the truthful, the confession) exists precisely for its earlier phases; the verse's terminal cohort (the died-in-it men) is the *untreated* series, and the sūrah's own repentance-series (vv. 102, 106, 117, 118) is the treated. Al-Qurṭubī's pastoral rule distills the reading: the believer should treat every felt resistance to the recitation as the maraḍ's early report — the passage that irritates, the verse that occasions the mental rebuttal — and respond not with avoidance (the defense that deepens) but with the opposite exposure: more recitation, slower, with the v. 119 company, until the reagent's work turns from defilement-compounding to faith-increase; the disease's name is not its verdict, and the verse's last clause is a warning precisely so that its readers need not appear in it.

**The Believer's Reagent-Test**

For the modern believer, verse 125 supplies the sūrah's last self-test, and it is honesty's sharpest instrument: the believer's *response to being addressed* — not the verses about the ancient enemy (everyone enjoys those), but the verses about *him*: the giving-verses when the wallet is tight, the truth-verses when the excuse is load-bearing, the marriage-verses, the tongue-verses, the quiet-verses — the believer's reflex at the addressed passage is the reagent's reading, and the reading has three possible trends: integration (increase, rejoicing — v. 124's profile), defended integration (the modern believer's commonest case: agreement *in principle*, postponed in practice — the maraḍ's chronic but reversible stage), and defense (the measurement-sneer's refined descendant: the exegetical shrug that files the verse under *historical context* precisely where it touches the self). The verse's counsel to the second and third profiles is the sūrah's whole mercy-arc compressed: *turn now* — while the turning still metabolizes recitation into growth; the window is wide open at every reading; and the verse's terminal clause, standing as it does between v. 124's rejoicing and v. 126's clock, is best read as the Book's own urgent foot-note under the open window: *some closed it; they are the cautionary dead.*

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:126

> **Do they not see that they are tried once or twice a year? Yet they do not repent, nor do they remember.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Clock Verse**

Verse 126 is the sūrah's final diagnostic question, and it points at a *schedule*: *a-wa-lam yaraw annahum yuftanūna fī kulli ʿāmin marratan aw marratayn* — "do they not see that they are tried once or twice every year?" — the trials, in the occasion's register, being the sūrah's own events: the campaigns, droughts, scarcity-years, alarms — the late-Medinan calendar's recurring adversities (the reports of the era record the ḥijrah-year's famines and the frontier's emergencies); and the exegetes read the *marratan aw marratayn* (once or twice) as the divine speech's measured understatement: the adversities were frequent enough to constitute a *curriculum* — a scheduled series of the same lesson — delivered annually, and the lesson's two aims are the verse's final clauses, both absent in the addressees: *thumma lā yatūbūna* — "yet they do not repent" — *wa-lā yadhdhakkarūna* — "nor do they remember" — the two responses trial is *for*: the turn (tawbah: the adversity read as summons) and the remembrance (tadhdhakkur: the adversity read as reminder — the v. 122 ḥadhar-culture's interior root).

**Trial as Curriculum: The Sūrah's Theodicy-in-Miniature**

The verse contains, in one schedule, the Qur'an's whole doctrine of adversity's *purpose* — the theodicy the tradition built from the trial-verses: testing is not arbitrary harassment but *instruction with a delivery-cadence*; the same lesson, once or twice a year, each iteration a fresh chance to repent and remember; and the scholars' commentary on the verse's tragic core — the *thumma lā* — reads it as the curriculum's failed student, not the curriculum's flaw: the trials performed; the responses never came; and the tradition's classic texts on trial (al-Bukhārī's and Muslim's corpus: the Prophet's ﷺ *the affair of the believer is wondrous — all of it is good... prosperity is gratitude and adversity is patience*, from Ṣuḥayb's narration; and *never is a believer stricken by fatigue, illness, anxiety, grief... even the thorn, but that God expiates sins by it*, from the verse 120 section's citation) state what the verse's subjects lacked: the *reading* of the event — every trial a letter, addressed, signed, arriving annually, unread.

**"Nor Do They Remember": The Memory-Deficit**

The verse's second deficit — tadhdhakkur — deserves its own weight, for it is the sūrah's closing diagnosis of the unrepentant heart: not intellectual failure (*they knew*) but *remembrance-failure* — the event never connected to the memory of God, the Return, the covenant; the Qur'an's tadhdhakkur-clan (the dh-k-r root of dhikr) names the heart's core faculty: the capacity to let experience *recall* the truths already known, and its atrophy — the sūrah's subjects' condition — is the world's most complete anesthesia: trial upon trial arriving as noise, the letter returned unopened, the curriculum wasted on a student who no longer remembers being enrolled. Ibn Kathīr's application carries the verse's edge to every generation: the recurring adversity is *visible* — drought, loss, alarm, pandemic — and the verse's question (*a-wa-lam yaraw* — *do they not see?*) indicts the non-seeing of the obvious; the modern reader supplies his own calendar; the diagnosis is checked against it.

**The Believer's Annual Review**

For the modern believer, verse 126 converts the personal adversity-history into the sūrah's final accounting instrument, and its use is mechanical: *the trial-schedule review* — the believer lists his year's marratan aw marratayn (the health scare, the professional setback, the loss, the fright — the events that stopped the clock for a week), and for each asks the verse's two questions: *did I turn* (the tawbah-response: what did that season correct?) *and do I remember* (the dhikr-response: what does that event now recall when remembered — and is it recalled at all, or filed as bad luck?). The review's possible findings are humbling and hopeful in equal measure: some trials will show their fruit immediately; others will show their unread status — the letter still sealed — and these are the verse's urgent referrals: re-read, repented, remembered, *now*; because the verse's schedule stands — the trials will come again, once or twice, next year — and the sūrah, nearly finished, is about to state what the unread letters' final warning looks like (v. 127) before closing, with two verses of pure mercy (vv. 128–129), the account of the God who sent all the letters in the first place — *that they may remember* — and who, the tradition has always read in this verse's tragic cadence, keeps sending them anyway.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:127

> **And whenever a sūrah is sent down, they look at one another: "Does anyone see you?" Then they turn away. God has turned their hearts, for they are a people who understand not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Glance That Indicts**

Verse 127 is the sūrah's most cinematic verse — a single freeze-frame of the hypocrites at the recitation: *wa-mā unzilat sūratun* — "and whenever a sūrah is sent down" (the third *whenever* of the closing triptych, after vv. 86, 124) — *naẓara baʿḍuhum ilā baʿḍ* — "they look at one another" — the congregation's sideways glance; and the quoted interior line, half-whispered: *hal yarākum min aḥadin* — "does anyone see you?" — the question of the watched: not *is this true?* but *are we observed?* — the hypocrite's entire theology in four words: God's seeing discounted, men's seeing decisive; then the exit: *thumma iṣraffū* — "then they turn away" — the verb ṣ-r-f naming the deliberate swerve, the turned shoulder — and the divine comment's double verdict: *ṣarafahu Allāhu* — "God has turned their hearts away" (their swerve reproduced in heaven's grammar: they turned; God turned their hearts — the judicial iḍlāl of v. 115's doctrine, the reciprocated gesture) — *bi-annahum qawmun lā yafqahūn* — "because they are a people who understand not" — the fiqh-deficiency again (vv. 81's, 87's diagnosis), now terminal: the understanding-faculty's last failure performed publicly at the recitation's edge: they heard the sūrah and computed only the audience-question.

**The Hearing Without Attending**

The exegetes reconstruct the scene's sociology with care, because the verse's pathos is in its smallness: the Prophet ﷺ reciting; the believers gathered close; the diseased at the assembly's rim; a new sūrah begins — perhaps one of the exposure-serials — and the rim-exchange happens in glances: *is he watching us? does anyone see you?* — the counsel of early departure: the ṣaḥābah's reports note the type — the men who left the prayer before its end, who stood at the mosque's door and did not enter (v. 54 of the canon's other register: *and when they stand for prayer, they stand lazily, to be seen of men*), who attended only when attendance was counted.
