And al-Rāzī's psychological commentary fixes the verse's horror at its exact depth: the question *does anyone see you?* is the total inversion of the believer's question at the same event (*does the One who sees me see what He wishes in me?*) — the two parties at the same recitation asking opposite optics-questions; and the divine clause's answer (God turned their hearts) closes the loop the sūrah has been tracing since verse 8: those who took God's seeing lightly lost the capacity to be reached by what He says; the hearing-organ disconnected from the heart-organ; attendance without attending; the Book read *to* them with no *them* present to receive it.

**"Does Anyone See You?": The Modern Theatre**

The verse's four-word quotation has been called, in the tradition's modern preaching, the sharpest sentence in the Qur'an about religious life in the age of audiences — and the sūrah's closing diagnosis transfers without translation: the *hal yarākum min aḥadin* of the modern believer is the metrics-check — the glance at the views, the followers, the room's approval — the question that audits every act's worth by its witnesses; and the verse's exposure of the question is not that witnesses exist (the believer is commanded to *be* seen doing good — the v. 71 community's public office) but that the question *replaces* the truth-question at the moment of address: the sūrah descends — the duty is named — and the first computation is optical. The verse's tragic grammar completes the warning: the optics-first heart receives its judicial mirror (God turns the heart to match its own priority) — the man who lived by the audience-question is finally given an audience-shaped heart, incapable of hearing what no one applauds; which is why, the tradition notes, the verse's last clause is the fiqh-word one final time: they understand not — the understanding that dies last, dead now: the faculty that would have told them what the recitation was *for*.

**The Believer's Refusal of the Exit**

For the modern believer, verse 127's pastoral use is the closing triptych's mirror-audit: the believer places himself at the recitation of his own life's sūrahs — the sermon, the passage, the counsel that names his duty — and watches his own first motion: the lean *toward* the word (v. 124's profile: increase, rejoicing) or the glance *around* the word (v. 127's profile: who's watching; is there an exit; is attendance being taken). The verse's mercy, one last time, is in its placement *before* the end: the sūrah's very next verses (128–129) are the Book's tenderness in its purest register — the Messenger's compassionate portrait and the believer's creed of sufficiency — as if the Book itself, having photographed the exiting glances, turns to the exiting reader and says, in effect: *before you go — look what you are turning from.* The believer who stays for the last two verses — who does not take verse 127's exit — will hear the chapter close not with the sealed and the turned-away, but with the two most beloved portraits in the revelation: the Messenger who *grieves over you* (v. 128), and the God who *suffices* (v. 129) — the whole religion, offered again, to anyone still in the room.

---

## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:128

> **There has come unto you a Messenger from among yourselves. Grievous to him is your suffering; he is concerned for you, and gentle and merciful toward the believers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Portrait After the Exit**

Verse 128 is the Qur'an's most tender description of its own Messenger, and its placement — after one hundred twenty-seven verses of exposure, sorting, and command — is the sūrah's final structural sermon: *la-qad jāʾakum rasūlun min anfusikum* — "there has come unto you a Messenger *from among yourselves*" — the min anfusikum doing double work: genealogy (one of you: Makkah's own, the trusted one of the hills, known forty years before the revelation) and solidarity (from your own *nafs* — of your very selves: one who knows the life from inside — its costs, its dialects, its griefs — the Qur'an's own commentary on the phrase at 6:9 had stated the counterfactual: had We made him an angel, the embassy would have failed). Then the four-clause portrait, each clause a chapter of the sīrah: *ʿazīzun ʿalayhi mā ʿanittum* — "grievous to him is what you suffer" — ʿanitta: what burdens, hurts, embitters *you* — the messenger whose own distress-meter is calibrated to *his people's* readings: the reports' inventory — his sleeplessness over the ummah (*if they knew what was with God, they would weep much and laugh little*), his weeping at the dead of Uḥud a full year, his prayer at night *for his people* (the ḥadīth of the Sunan from Abū Hurayrah: *O God, my people... my people* — weeping as a child weeps, reciting the verse of the servant-prophets' plea: *if Thou punish them, they are Thy servants*); *ḥarīṣun ʿalaykum* — "he is deeply concerned *for you*" — the root ḥ-r-ṣ naming the intense wanting (the covetousness-word turned inside out: his avarice is *your* salvation — he covets your good the way the miser of v. 128's neighbors covets gold); *bi-l-muʾminīna raʾūfun raḥīm* — "toward the believers gentle (raʾūf) and merciful (raḥīm)" — the twin divine Names of v. 117 (God's own turning-attributes) loaned, in the Book's grammar, to the man: the tenderest compliment in revelation — the Messenger described in the Father's vocabulary.

**The Mercy That Structured the Sūrah**

The tradition reads v. 128 as the sūrah's self-disclosure: everything chapter nine has done — including its severities — was the work of *this* man, and the verse arrives to say what the work cost him and how he bore it: the man who commanded the ghilẓah (v. 123) is the man who grieved at the recitation's rim; the man who boycotted the three (v. 118) is the man whose mercy kept praying at the graveside of his chief slanderer (v. 80's shirt — the reports of the occasion); and the Sunnah's own commentary on the verse's key term stands in the Qur'an's method-verse: it was this raʾfah that was *commanded as the manner of command*: *"It was by the mercy of God that thou wast lenient with them; hadst thou been harsh and hard-hearted, they would have dispersed from around thee"* (3:159 — the verse cited at this sūrah's v. 73). And the Prophet ﷺ enacted the verse's solidarity at the level of bodily instinct: the reports of the congregational prayer preserve his concern for the individually burdened... and al-Bukhārī preserves the era's emblematic case: the woman who used to sweep the mosque, whose absence the Prophet ﷺ noticed, and of whom, upon learning she had died, he asked to be shown her grave — and went and prayed over her. The tradition's memory of the verse 128 man is the memory of a leader who noticed corners.

**The Verse's Address to the Turned-Away**

Placed after verse 127's exit-glance, the verse does its tenderest work on the ones at the rim: the Messenger they were glancing away *from* is described as the man for whom their own suffering is grievous — their sufferings, the hypocrites' included: the portrait holds even its subjects' welfare as his concern, which the exegetes read as the Book's final invitation — the same mercy that grieved at the rim's exits still stands at the door of the exited; and the tradition's homiletic line on the verse has stood for fourteen centuries: every hearer of the Qur'an finds himself *in* the verse — *from among yourselves*: the Messenger is not a foreign judge imported to condemn but your own kinsman, whose mission's entire energy is ḥirṣ — covetous concern — *for you*; the believer's love of the Prophet ﷺ, which the ḥadīth makes the condition of faith's completion (*none of you believes until I am more beloved to him than...*, the citation of v. 114's section), is not the cult of a distant hero but the reciprocity of the addressed: he grieves over you; you love him back; the relationship the Qur'an has described in a servant-verb and two divine Names.

**The Modern Believer's Relationship-Check**

For the modern believer, verse 128 is the standing audit of his *relationship with the Prophet ﷺ* — the sūrah's last assigned self-examination, and its questions are the verse's own clauses turned personal: *Does my Prophet's concern reach me as concern* (do I receive his Sunnah as the min-anfusikum counsel of one who knows my life from inside — or as a foreign code?), *and does his grief move me to ease his grief's causes* — the reports' specific: he ﷺ wept that his people would meet God unpardoned (the ḥadīth of ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr: the Sun's drawing-near, the believers' sweating to the ankles, and the Prophet's *my ummah, my ummah* — and God's threefold *enter, whoever prayed... whoever fasted...*)? The believer who answers honestly finds the verse performing its office: the chapter of barāʾah closing with raʾfah — severity's whole architecture revealing, at its final verse, that it was love's instrument all along — and the reader is left at the threshold of the last verse holding the Qur'an's most personal gift: a Messenger from among yourselves, grieving, concerned, gentle, merciful — to whom the proper response, the sūrah will now say in its final breath, is not complicated: it is trust.

---
## Sūrah al-Tawbah 9:129

> **So if they turn away, say, "God suffices me. There is no god but He. In Him I trust, and He is the Lord of the mighty Throne."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Last Word**

Verse 129 closes the longest sūrah of the middle Makkan-Madinan revelation with the Qur'an's most personal creed — and the tradition reads the placement as the architecture's final seal: *fa-in tawallaw* — "so if they turn away" — the verb of the exits (v. 127's turners, the v. 98 averted, the whole sūrah's turning-class) reaching its ultimate case: if even this — the exposure, the sorting, the mercy, the portrait of the grieving Messenger — is turned away from, then the Prophet ﷺ is given his answer, and it is not an argument but an anchor: *fa-qul ḥasbīya Allāhu* — "say: *God suffices me*" — the sufficiency-creed (ḥisbah: the accounting-word — He is my *enough*, my complete resource: what the tradition's scholars gloss as the full treasury in one Name); *lā ilāha illā huwa* — "there is no god but He" — the tawḥīd's core, the whole religion's center, returned at the end as the believer's last standing-place; *ʿalayhi tawakkaltu* — "in Him I trust" — the tawakkul-doctrine's personal verb: the reliance that the sūrah has been building since v. 51's *ḥasbunā Allāh* (the community's version, deployed there) now given in the first-person singular, the leader's own creed at the emptied hall; *wa-huwa rabbu al-ʿarshi al-ʿaẓīm* — "and He is the Lord of the Mighty Throne (ʿArsh)" — the Throne: creation's greatest thing, the divine command's seat (the ʿarsh of 7:54 and 23:86), named at the close to scale the sufficiency: the One who is enough is the Lord of the largest thing there is; the trust calibrated to the Trustee's actual size.

**The Ḥasbī Verse's Companions**

The verse entered the Sunnah's devotional life at its highest frequency — the tradition's reports make it, with its sister-phrases, the believer's portable fortress: the Prophet ﷺ taught the closing-of-the-day couplet that carries this verse — the ḥadīth of Abū al-Dardāʾ, in the collections: *whoever says, upon entering the evening and the morning: God suffices me — there is no god but He; in Him I trust, and He is the Lord of the Mighty Throne — seven times, God suffices him in what concerned him of the affairs of this world and the next* (recorded by Ibn al-Sunnī and others in wording the tradition has received; the Ṣaḥīḥ's parallel family includes Muslim's report from Abū Hurayrah on the morning-evening sufficiency-prayers) — and the sūrah's own sister-verse stands in the Qur'an's earlier parallel (the ʿarsh-mention's companion text): *"God — there is no god but He — and in God let the believers trust"* (64:13). The believer's whole portfolio-logic — the sūrah's wealth-thesis, its people-thesis, its fear-thesis — resolves here in seven words: the sufficiency of the Throne's Lord over every other holding.

**The Twice-Given Sufficiency**

The careful reader of chapter nine has met this creed before — at verse 51, in the plural: *ḥasbunā Allāhu wa-niʿma al-wakīl* — "God suffices us, and what an excellent Trustee" — spoken by the community; verse 129 gives the singular form to the Messenger at the chapter's close, and the tradition savors the pair as a designed signature: the sūrah that opened with barāʾah — the public severance, the community's hardest commands — ends by returning each party to the private anchor that survives every public storm: the community's *we* at the center (v. 51), the leader's *I* at the end (v. 129), the same sufficiency — because the sūrah's two audiences (the believer who must act in the storm, and the believer who must keep speaking after the room empties) each need it in their own grammar. And the reports of the Prophet's ﷺ own usage complete the doctrine: the ḥadīth of the Ṣaḥīḥ collections preserves the creed's deployment at the ḥudaybiyah-treaty's crisis-hour — *God suffices us and what an excellent Trustee*, said by Ibrāhīm in the fire and by the believers at the trials — the creed's genealogy running from the fire-cooled confessors to the Prophet's household to the ummah's morning-evenings.

**The End of the Exposure: Trust**

And so the sūrah that never named itself — the tradition's two titles (al-Tawbah, the Repentance, from its mercies; and Barāʾah, the Disavowal, from its opening word, the only sūrah to begin without bismillah, in the scholars' standard account, because the disavowal itself is unaccompanied by the greeting) — closes by describing the *alternative to both titles*: the man who, standing at the emptied recitation-hall of verse 127, does not chase the exits and does not curse the exiters, but turns — the sūrah's final tawbah in miniature, the verb reversed one last time — *toward* the Throne's Lord, and says the creed: enough, One, trusted. For the modern believer, verse 129 is the chapter's bequest — the sentence to carry out of the long reading: every ledger the sūrah opened closes in the hands of the Sufficer; every audience that turned away is answered by the Audience that cannot; and the believer's task, after the one hundred twenty-nine verses of sorting, is not to master the files (v. 101: thou knowest them not) but to file himself — the ḥasbī-grammar's first person — in the only ledger that ends well: *God suffices me. There is no god but He. In Him I trust, and He is the Lord of the mighty Throne.*

---
